<meta>This page serves as a standardized preface for a digitized volume, explaining the provenance of the digital file and the terms of its use.</meta>

<image-desc>The multi-colored "Google" company logo.</image-desc>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain <note>A legal status where a work is no longer protected by intellectual property laws and is free for public use.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia <note>Notes written in the margins of a book by previous readers or owners.</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

### **Usage guidelines**

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>The technology used to convert images of typed or handwritten text into machine-encoded text.</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

### **About Google Book Search**

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to the edition of Theon of Smyrna's mathematical commentary. It establishes the manuscript tradition and the editorial methods used by the translator/editor. Catchword from previous page: "from".</meta>

# PREFACE.

---

The commentary of Theon of Smyrna <note>A 2nd-century philosopher and mathematician who wrote this "handbook" to help students understand the mathematical passages in Plato's works.</note> concerning mathematical matters useful for reading Plato has reached our age divided into two parts; the first of which (pages 1—119) exists in the Venetian manuscript 307 of the Marcian Library (**A**), and the second (pages 120—205) in manuscript 303 of the same library (**B**). I examined these books at Bonn, where they had been sent to me through the intervention of my most refined teacher, Otto Jahn; afterwards, I inspected many passages again in Venice.

Manuscript **A**, made of parchment and dating to the 11th or 12th century, is square in shape and contains nothing besides the first part of Theon. The scribe committed many errors in matters pertaining to prosody <note>The patterns of stress and intonation in language; here referring to Greek accents and breathing marks.</note>; for example, he often wrote—to cite a few instances—*νητή* [nete], *ὄγδοος* [ogdoos], *τετρακτῦς* [tetraktys], *ἶσος* [isos], *δυᾶδος* [dyados], *μεταβάσα* [metabasa] <note>The editor is noting incorrect accentuation in the original Greek technical terms for musical notes and numbers.</note>. Prepositions often lack accents. Where the form of a reflexive pronoun is required, either *ἑαυτοῦ* [of himself] or *αὐτοῦ* [of him] is placed; never, unless I am mistaken, *αὑτοῦ* [of himself]. The forms *αἰεί* and *ἀεί* [always] are read indiscriminately. The letter *iota* is never written underneath <note>Referring to the "iota subscript," a small mark under certain Greek vowels.</note>, though it is sometimes written alongside. Numbers are almost always rendered in red ink.

Those errors which that scribe committed in prosody were corrected for the most part, though not everywhere, by another hand (**A**^2), which also correctly restored *ἰδέα* [form/idea] for *εἰδέα*, *λεῖμμα* [remainder/limma] for *λίμμα*, and similar words. Several more serious changes are also found to have been made by the same hand. However, those readings were not from—

<column-break/>

<summary>This preface introduces the two primary Greek manuscripts (Codex A and B) used to reconstruct Theon of Smyrna's mathematical guide to Plato, detailing the physical condition and scribal habits of the 11th-century parchment.</summary>
<keywords>Theon of Smyrna, Plato, Marcian Library, Otto Jahn, paleography, Greek manuscripts, mathematical commentary, scribal errors, prosody, codicology</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's technical preface regarding the manuscript tradition and the relationship between the primary sources used for this edition.</meta>

# PREFACE

must be sought from another manuscript: for all of them are of such a nature <note>original: "ita sunt comparatae"</note> that they could easily have been devised by conjecture, whether true or false. Furthermore, since even the scribe who wrote out the manuscript corrected many things and wrote over erasures, in several places it cannot be accurately determined whether a change should be attributed to the first hand or to a second hand.

Regarding manuscript B, compare what I have discussed in volume 31 of the journal *Philologus*, page 172 and following. It is made of paper, in the largest format, written in the 14th or 15th century. From folio 9a to 16b, it contains the second part of this work with figures <note>illustrations or diagrams</note> very carelessly executed.

The chapter headings provided by manuscripts A and B differ so greatly from a proper method of indicating the subject matter, and are sometimes placed so absurdly, that they cannot be attributed to the author himself, despite his mediocre talent. Therefore, I have decided that they should be assigned a place in the critical apparatus <note>the technical notes at the bottom of the page</note> rather than in the main body of the text. *Compare Martin, Theo on Astronomy, page 36.*

From manuscripts A and B are derived all the handwritten books of which I have knowledge that contain either the whole work or the first or second part; I will soon prove this to be the case with certain arguments in another place. Using copies derived from A or B, *Bullialdus* (Paris, 1644) edited the first part, and *Martin* (Paris, 1849) edited the second—the verses of Alexander (page 139 and following), which *Isaac Vossius* had transcribed from a copy of manuscript B, had already been printed several times before; see the note to page 139—and finally, *de Gelder* (Leiden, 1827) edited the first small section (pages 1 to 46, line 19). For my part, manuscripts of this kind had to be mentioned especially in those places where they show corrections of errors in the ancient writing. To avoid burdening the notes uselessly with a multitude of different signs, I did not wish to indicate individually those books from this group in which improved readings are found, but I have designated the manuscripts originating from A or B by the common name of *apographs* <term>apographum: a transcript or copy of an original manuscript</term>.

<summary>The editor describes the physical characteristics and poor quality of Manuscript B and explains his decision to move unreliable chapter headings to the critical notes. He also lists previous partial editions of the text and defines his use of the term "apographs" for derivative copies.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript tradition, codex, Philologus, Bullialdus, Martin, Isaac Vossius, de Gelder, apograph, paleography, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's technical preface, moving from the description of the primary manuscripts (A and B) to secondary sources, fragmentary copies, and the editorial methods used to reconstruct the text of Theon of Smyrna.</meta>

->VII<-

# PREFACE

Furthermore, several manuscripts provide a separate excerpt of that small portion of this book found on pages 46, 20 to 57, 6 (regarding the spirit <note>original: "τοῦ πνεύματος"</note>). These excerpts seem to have been transcribed into their archetype <note>the common ancestor manuscript from which others are copied</note> from a book different from manuscript A. Examples of this type include the *Venetus Marcianus* 512 manuscript from the 13th or 14th century, the *Riccardianus* K 2, 2, 41 from the 15th century, the *Neapolitanus* 260 (or III C 2) from the 15th or 16th century, the *Vaticanus* 221 from the 16th century, the *Vaticanus Urbinas* 77 from the 16th century, and the *Barberinianus* II 86 from the 16th century. Hermann Diels, with remarkable kindness, transcribed the fragment of Theon from the Marcianus manuscript for my use; Friedrich von Duhn and Georg Kaibel noted several details for me from the Neapolitanus; I personally compared the others with the edition by *Bullialdus*. The manuscript used by Manuel Bryennius was of this same type: compare his *Harmonics*, page 393 and following. Thus, by comparing these books with one another, what was written in their common ancestor (Z) can be easily identified.

To correct the verses of *Alexander* <note>Alexander Aetolus, a Hellenistic poet</note>, in addition to manuscript Marcianus B, I have used manuscript 203 (C) from the same library. This is a paper manuscript from the 13th century, on the last leaf (230) of which that poem exists under the title *Alexander Aetolus on the Planets* <note>original: "’Αλεξάνδρου Αἰτωλοῦ περὶ τῶν πλανητῶν"</note>. The scribe appears to have used the same model <note>exemplar</note> from which manuscript B was copied.

In several places, *Chalcidius's* commentary on Plato’s *Timaeus* proves useful for restoring the words of Theon. This is because both writers adapted the commentary of *Adrastus the Peripatetic* for their own use and accurately reproduced significant portions of it: see the journal *Rheinisches Museum*, volume XXVI, page 582 and following.

Traces of the gaps <note>lacunae</note>, for which I have placed signs within the flow of the text, exist in the manuscripts only in those places where I have explicitly indicated it. I have used angled brackets < > to enclose text that I believe should be added to the traditional reading, and square brackets [ ] for those things which I believe should be deleted. To indicate the writing of the first hand in manuscript A that was later changed by a second hand (A²), I have used the mark A¹. An asterisk * has been placed...

<summary>This page discusses the secondary manuscript tradition for specific sections of Theon's work and describes the editorial symbols used to indicate additions, deletions, and corrections in this edition.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript tradition, Alexander Aetolus, Chalcidius, Adrastus the Peripatetic, editorial methods, philology, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the editor's preface, detailing the internal logic of the editorial corrections, the difficulty of handling Theon’s quotations of Plato, and acknowledgments of scholarly assistance before concluding in Halle, 1878.</meta>

->VIII<-

# PREFACE

...the writings of manuscript B corrected by Martin. In all those places where I have not indicated the author or source of a correction—whether proposed in a note or accepted into the word order of the text—it should be known that the correction was discovered by my own conjecture <note>An "emendation by conjecture" is a correction made by an editor based on logic or linguistic patterns when the manuscript itself is clearly broken or nonsensical</note>; there are about 450 such instances. Furthermore, one had to proceed cautiously in changing the traditional reading; for there is no doubt that many things which appear to be said perversely or negligently must be attributed to the ignorance and superficiality of Theon himself. Particularly the passages from Plato included in the preface (pages 2–14) so far exceed the rest of the work in the multitude and foulness of their defects that it is completely incredible that all of them should be attributed to the errors of scribes. In these cases, therefore, because it is impossible to judge accurately what the scribes did wrong, what Theon did, or finally what was already corrupted in the copies of Plato used by Theon, I preferred to leave the errors untouched rather than engage in the easy but useless task of restoring Plato's genuine words here and there. I have borrowed the notations for the manuscripts of the *Republic* from Schneider’s edition, and those of the *Epinomis* <note>The Epinomis is a dialogue attributed to Plato, though its authorship was debated even in antiquity</note> from Bekker’s edition.

It remains for me to give the greatest thanks to the most distinguished *Friedrich Hultsch*, who, having most carefully read through these proof sheets, shared several corrections with me—some certain, others probable—which I have mentioned in the additions (compare also the notes to pages 15, 17; 22, 17; 25, 20).

->Written at Halle in the month of October, 1878.<-

<summary>The editor explains his use of personal conjecture for 450 corrections and justifies leaving errors in Theon's quotations of Plato as reflections of the author's own sources or limitations. The preface concludes with thanks to Friedrich Hultsch and a signature from Halle, 1878.</summary>

<keywords>textual criticism, conjecture, Plato, Theon of Smyrna, Friedrich Hultsch, Halle, Schneider, Bekker, manuscript errors</keywords>

Particularly the passages from Plato included in the preface (pages 2–14) so far exceed the rest of the work in the multitude and foulness of their defects that it is completely incredible that all of them should be attributed to the errors of scribes. In these cases, therefore, because it is impossible to judge accurately what the scribes did wrong, what Theon did, or finally what was already corrupted in the copies of Plato used by Theon, I preferred to leave the errors untouched rather than engage in the easy but useless task of restoring Plato's genuine words here and there. I have borrowed the notations for the manuscripts of the *Republic* from Schneider’s edition, and those of the *Epinomis* <note>The Epinomis is a dialogue attributed to Plato, though its authorship was debated even in antiquity; it focuses on the study of mathematics and astronomy as a path to wisdom</note> from Bekker’s edition.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This is the opening page of Theon of Smyrna’s treatise, a 2nd-century CE introductory manual designed to help students of philosophy understand the complex mathematical passages in Plato’s dialogues.</meta>

-># Theon of Smyrna<-
->### On the Mathematical Knowledge Useful for Reading Plato<-

### Preface

# 1

Everyone would likely agree that it is impossible to understand the mathematical discussions found in the works of Plato without having been trained in this field of study. <margin>5</margin> Furthermore, Plato himself seems to show through many examples that experience in these subjects is neither useless nor without benefit for other areas of knowledge. Now, if someone were to encounter the writings of Plato after having already become an expert in all of geometry, music, and astronomy, that would be a blessed thing; however, it is neither easy nor common, but requires a great deal of effort <margin>10</margin> starting from childhood. 

Therefore, for the sake of those who missed out on being trained in the mathematical sciences, but who nevertheless desire to understand his writings and do not wish to fail entirely in their pursuit, we shall provide a summary and concise presentation of the necessary mathematical theorems. These are the ones most needed by those who intend to read Plato, including arithmetic, <margin>15</margin> music <note>original: "μουσικῶν" — here referring to "Harmonics," the mathematical study of musical intervals and ratios.</note>, geometry (including <term>stereometry</term> <note>The study of three-dimensional or solid figures, like cubes and spheres.</note>), and astronomy, without which...

---

**Inscription:** Theon of Smyrna, the <term>Platonist</term> <note>A follower of Plato’s philosophy; Theon lived during the Middle Platonic period.</note>: Concerning the mathematical topics useful for the reading of Plato. <note>In the source, the word "the" is written above the line.</note> **Heading:** That the mathematical sciences are necessary (Found in manuscript A). **Line 2:** *trained*: ending corrected from *-on* to *-on* in manuscript A. **Line 4:** *nor the other things*: written as *neither the other things* in manuscript A. **Line 5:** *the* corrected from *then*. <margin>5</margin> **Line 6:** *geometry*: "and arithmetic" was added by a later hand in a copy, which is perhaps correct. **Line 10:** <margin>10</margin> *the goal of the book* noted in the margin of manuscript A.

<summary>Theon of Smyrna explains that because Plato’s philosophy is deeply rooted in mathematics, a student must understand arithmetic, music, and geometry to fully grasp his writings. He proposes this book as a concise "short-cut" for those who did not receive a rigorous mathematical education in childhood.</summary>

<keywords>Theon of Smyrna, Plato, mathematical education, arithmetic, harmonics, geometry, stereometry, astronomy, pedagogical summary</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Theon of Smyrna’s manual. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on the necessity of the four mathematical sciences—arithmetic, music, geometry, and astronomy—as a foundation for Platonic philosophy.</meta>

-># ON UTILITY<-

...he says it is not possible to achieve the best life, having shown through very many arguments that one must not neglect mathematical studies.

For Eratosthenes <note>Eratosthenes of Cyrene (c. 276–194 BCE) was a famous polymath and head of the Library of Alexandria, best known for calculating the Earth's circumference.</note> says in the work titled *Platonic* that when the god <note>The god Apollo at Delphi.</note> gave an oracle to the Delians [telling them] to <margin>5</margin> construct an altar double the size of the existing one in order to be delivered from a plague, the architects fell into great confusion as they sought how a solid might be made double a solid <note>This refers to the "Delian Problem" or "doubling the cube." If you simply double the sides of a cube, the volume increases eightfold; the problem requires a sophisticated geometric construction to double only the volume.</note>. They went to Plato to ask him about this. He told them that the god had not given the oracle because he was <margin>10</margin> in need of a doubled altar, but rather he was challenging and rebuking the Greeks for neglecting mathematical studies and for having disregarded geometry.

Following the advice of the Pythian <note>The Pythian refers to the Oracle of Apollo at Delphi.</note>, he [Plato] himself also details many points regarding the utility of mathematical studies. For <margin>15</margin> in the *Epinomis* <note>A dialogue attributed to Plato, intended as an appendix to his *Laws*.</note>, urging [the reader] toward these studies, he says: "For without these, no nature in a city will ever become happy; rather, this is the way, this is the upbringing, these are the studies—whether they be difficult or easy, one must proceed by this path; for it is not lawful to neglect the gods." And in <margin>20</margin> what follows, he says that such a person, having become one out of many, will be happy, most wise, and blessed all at once.

In the *Republic*, he says: "From the age of twenty-five <note>The standard text of Plato’s *Republic* actually specifies the age of twenty for this stage of education.</note>, those who have been pre-selected shall receive honors greater than the others, and..."

---

3 Eratosthenes: Bernhardy, *Eratosthenica* page 168. Compare *Philologus* XXX page 67. 12 "having disregarded" [Greek: *ōligōrēkosin*] in Manuscript A, corrected in the transcript. 15 *Epinomis* page 992 A: "For never without these (the word 'at least' [*ge*] is omitted by Nicomachus in *Introduction to Arithmetic* I.3, 5) will a nature become happy in cities (two manuscripts read 'of the happy ones' [*eudaimonōn*], one reads 'being happy' [*eudaimonōn*]); rather, this is the way, this (Ast adds 'the') upbringing, these studies, whether difficult or easy, one must travel (Nicomachus says 'go' [*iteon*]) this way; for it is not lawful to neglect the gods; 'being happy' [*eudaimonōn*] in Manuscript A. 17 'nature' [*phys*] in A, then two letters erased. 20 *Epinomis* page 992 B. 22 *Republic* VII page 537 B: 'twenty-five'] Plato says 'twenty'—see Schneider. 23 'honors greater than the others'—Plato's word order.

<summary>This page recounts the famous "Delian Problem" of doubling the cube as a divine mandate to study geometry and quotes Plato's Epinomis to emphasize that mathematics is a sacred path to happiness and wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Eratosthenes, Delian Problem, doubling the cube, Plato, Epinomis, Republic, geometry, oracle of Apollo, mathematical education</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the five mathematical sciences—arithmetic, geometry, solid geometry, astronomy, and music—as outlined in Plato’s Republic. It emphasizes the "purifying" effect these studies have on the human soul.</meta>

->### MATHEMATICS<-

...the studies encountered in a general way <note>The Greek "chydēn" implies studies taken up randomly or without a systematic order.</note> by everyone during their education must be brought together into a single overview, showing the kinship of these studies with one another and with the nature of reality. He [Plato] advises, first, to become experienced in arithmetic; second, in geo-
<margin>5</margin> metry; third, in stereometry <note>Stereometry is the study of three-dimensional solids, such as cubes or spheres.</note>; fourth, in astronomy—which he describes as the observation of a solid in motion—and fifth, in music. Demonstrating the utility of these mathematical studies, he says: "You are charming, for you seem to fear that I might be
<margin>10</margin> prescribing useless studies. But this is not at all a trivial thing; rather, it is difficult for everyone to believe that in these mathematical studies an organ of the soul in each person is purified and rekindled—an eye that was blinded and extinguished by other pursuits, though it is more worthy of being saved than ten thousand physical eyes; for by it alone
<margin>15</margin> is truth seen." <note>This famous passage is from Plato's Republic, Book VII, 527D.</note>
In the seventh book of the *Republic*, speaking about arithmetic, he says it is the most necessary of all...

***

1 **children** <note>original: παισιν</note> Plato. See H. Heller’s *Critical Notes on Plato’s Republic*, p. 16. **education** <note>original: παιδεία</note> corrected from **play** <note>original: παιδιᾷ</note> in Manuscript A (three manuscripts of Plato read **play**). 2 **kinship of one another** Plato (three manuscripts read **and the kinship of one another**). 3 See *Republic* p. 525 and following. **He advises you** in Manuscript A, corrected in the copy. 8 *Republic* p. 527 D: **"You are charming," I said, "because you seem to fear the many, lest you seem..."** (Nicomachus, Book I, 3, 7 reads: **"because you seem to fear, lest perhaps..."**) **useless studies** (Manuscript Paris K reads **the studies**; Nicomachus reads **these studies**) **to prescribe** (Nicomachus reads **I might prescribe**). **But this is not a trivial thing, but difficult to believe** (Nicomachus reads **very difficult**), **that in these studies an organ of the soul in each person is purified and rekindled** (the phrase **eye of the soul** also appears in Nicomachus, where these sections are compressed; see also Alcinous 27, p. 180 Hermann edition; Iamblichus, *On the Pythagorean Life* § 70; Boethius, *Introduction to Arithmetic* Book I, 1, p. 1 Friedlein edition) **being destroyed and blinded by etc.** 9 **the** and **I might prescribe** 
                                  **perhaps added by the second scribe of Manuscript A**. **I might prescribe** in Manuscript A. 10 **trivial**: the letter 'i' is written over an erasure in Manuscript A. 11 See Cobet, *Mnemosyne* XI p. 177. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the mathematical sciences from Plato’s Republic, moving from the general list of subjects to a specific defense of arithmetic (the study of numbers) and calculation. It uses a literary example from Greek tragedy to show why these skills are necessary for leaders.</meta>

->### ON UTILITY<-

...is necessary for all arts, all ways of thinking and sciences, and especially for the art of war. Indeed, in the tragedies, Palamedes always represents the general Agamemnon as an utterly ridiculous figure. For he says that, having discovered number, he [Palamedes] 
<margin>5</margin> established the ranks for the army at Ilium <note>Ilium is another name for the city of Troy.</note> and counted the ships and all other things, as if before this they were uncounted and Agamemnon, it seems, did not even know how many feet he had, if indeed he did not know how to count. It is likely, then, that this is one of the things that by nature leads toward understanding; yet no one
<margin>10</margin> uses it correctly as something that draws the soul toward true being <note>In Platonic philosophy, "true being" or "essence" refers to the eternal, unchanging reality of the Forms, as opposed to the shifting world of physical objects.</note> and summons the intellect. For those things that simply stimulate the senses do not awaken or summon the intellect—for example, seeing that a finger is a finger, or whether it is thick or thin, large or small. But those things that stimulate the senses in an opposite way
<margin>15</margin> are awakeners and summoners of thought—for example, when the same thing appears to be both large and small, light and heavy, or one and many. Thus, the "one" and number are summoners and awakeners of thought, since the "one" sometimes appears as many. Calculation <note>original: λογιστικὴ (logistikē). In this context, it refers to the practical art of computation.</note> and arithmetic <note>original: ἀριθμητικὴ (arithmētikē). For the Greeks, this was the theoretical study of the properties of numbers.</note> are a pull and a 
<margin>20</margin> guide toward truth. One must take up calculation not in an amateurish way...

---

**2** **utterly ridiculous** <note>original: παγγέλοιον</note>: the letters "gg" were corrected from "ng" in Manuscript A (the word is found in Plato’s Vienna Manuscript F). **3** **Palamedes** <note>A mythical Greek hero credited with inventing the alphabet, numbers, and dice.</note> in Manuscript A. **4** **shows. Or have you not noticed that he says...** <note>The apparatus here notes a longer version of the sentence found in Plato's original text.</note> **7** **did not know how many feet he had, if indeed he did not know how to count.** Plato. The word **know** <note>original: ἠπίστατο</note> was corrected from a misspelling in Manuscript A. *Republic*, page 523 A: **It is likely to be one of those things we are looking for that by nature leads to understanding, though no one uses it rightly, despite it being a thing that draws one entirely toward true being.** **10** **summons the intellect**: see *Republic*, page 523 D. **19** **indeed** <note>original: δὴ</note> instead of **but** <note>original: δὲ</note> as suggested by the scholar Stallbaum regarding Plato’s *Republic*, page 523 A. **20** *Republic*, page 525 B: **"It would be appropriate then, Glaucon, to legislate this study and persuade those who are going to share in the highest offices of the city to turn to calculation and take it up, not as amateurs, but until they reach the contemplation of the nature of numbers by the intellect itself—not for the sake of buying and selling, like merchants or shopkeepers..."**

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato’s Republic, specifically focusing on the educational curriculum for the ideal guardians. It transitions from the basic utility of arithmetic to the higher philosophical purpose of the mathematical sciences.</meta>

->### ON MATHEMATICS<-

...but rather so that they might reach the vision of the nature of numbers through the intellect, not practicing for the sake of buying and selling like merchants or petty traders, but for the sake of the soul and its path toward truth and essence <note>original: οὐσίαν (ousian) — refers to the fundamental reality of things, as opposed to mere appearance.</note>. For this leads the soul upward and 
<margin>5</margin> compels it to reason about the numbers themselves, not accepting it if anyone tries to reason with it by bringing forward physical bodies or visible things that possess numbers. And again in the same work he says: "Furthermore, those who are naturally skilled in calculation <note>original: λογιστικοὶ (logistikoi) — those proficient in the art of computation.</note> prove to be quick at all subjects, and even those who are slow, if trained, become quicker 
<margin>10</margin> than they were before." Again in the same work he says: "And in war it is useful for setting up encampments, the seizing of positions, and for the gathering and reviewing of the army." In the following passages, praising the pursuit of such studies, he says: "Geometry deals with the study of the plane, while astronomy deals with the motion 
<margin>15</margin> of the solid; this latter compels the soul to look upward and leads it from the things here to the things there." And indeed concerning music, he says in the same work that it requires two [realms...]

...but for the sake of war and for the ease of the soul itself and its turning from the world of becoming <note>original: γενέσεως (geneseōs) — the world of change and physical birth.</note> toward truth and essence— (the word "and" is omitted in many manuscripts) — 4 *Republic* p. 525 D: "This, at least—how strongly it leads the soul upward and compels it to discourse about numbers themselves, in no way accepting it if anyone offers for discussion numbers attached to visible or tangible bodies" (two manuscripts have "or from those," one has "or those of the"); "possessing" (three manuscripts have "possessing" in the singular) — 8 *Republic* p. 526 B: "Have you already noticed this, that those who are naturally skilled in calculation are, so to speak, quick at all subjects, and those who are slow, if they are educated and exercised in this, even if they benefit in no other way, nonetheless all improve by becoming quicker than they were before?" — 10 *Republic* p. 526 D: "As much of it," he said, "as pertains to war, it is clear that it is appropriate; for it is useful for encampments, the seizing of positions, the gathering and the drawing out of an army" — 16 *Republic* p. 529 A: "For it seems clear to everyone that this at least compels the soul to look upward and leads it from the things here to the things there." 18 "Two" corrected from "duoin" in manuscript A.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the higher mathematical curriculum for the guardians in Plato's Republic. The previous page concluded by introducing music as the sister science to astronomy, both requiring a transition from physical observation to intellectual understanding.</meta>

-># ON UTILITY<-

...contemplation, of astronomy and of harmony; and these sciences are sisters, as the Pythagoreans say. Therefore, those who measure audible harmonies and notes against one another labor to no end. By pressing
<margin>5</margin> their ears close, as if hunting for a voice from the neighbors, some say they can hear a certain sound in the middle and that this is the smallest interval <note>original: διάστημα (diastēma) — the "space" or distance between two musical pitches.</note> by which one must measure, while others argue that it sounds the same as the previous note—both groups putting their ears before their intellect. They trouble the strings, twisting them upon the tuning
<margin>10</margin> pegs <note>original: κολλάβων (kollabōn) — the cross-bars or pegs of a lyre used to tighten strings.</note>. But the good arithmeticians <note>The "good arithmeticians" here refers to mathematical music theorists who look for numerical ratios rather than relying on their ears.</note> seek, through observation, which numbers are harmonious with other numbers and which are not. And this is useful for the search for the Good.

---

1 *Republic, page 530 D* — "and these sciences are in some way sisters to one another, as the Pythagoreans say" — 2 *Republic, page 531 A* "For they, just like the astronomers, labor in vain by measuring audible harmonies and sounds against one another." "By the gods," he said, "and it is quite ridiculous, naming certain 'densities' <note>original: πυκνώματ’ (pyknōmat') — technical term for a condensed series of small musical intervals.</note> and pressing their ears close, as if hunting for a voice from the neighbors; some say they can still <gloss>the word "still" is omitted in three manuscripts</gloss> hear a certain sound in the middle and that this is the smallest interval by which one should measure, while others argue that the sound is already the same <gloss>the word "sounding" appears as a singular in two manuscripts</gloss>, both putting their ears before their intellect. "You," I said, "are speaking of those fine fellows who trouble the strings and torture them, twisting them on the tuning pegs" <gloss>marginal note: β</gloss> <note>The text notes various spellings for tuning pegs in the manuscripts: kolabōn, kallopōn, kollopōn; compare with Timaeus' Lexicon.</note> 4 "to no end" <note>original: ἀνήνυτα (anēnyta)</note> — the letter *u* was corrected from *oi* in Manuscript A. 5 "from" <note>original: ἐκ (ek)</note> — corrected from *eg* in Manuscript A. 7 "must be measured" <note>original: μετρητέον (metrēteon)</note> — the letter *ē* was corrected from *i* in Manuscript A. 11 *Republic, page 531 C* "For they seek the numbers within these audible harmonies, but they do not ascend to the level of problems to examine which numbers are harmonious and which are not, and for what reason in each case." "And I think," I said, "that the method concerning all these things we have discussed, if it reaches toward their common association and kinship, and if it infers how they are related to one another, contributes something to what we desire for our pursuit." — *compare with the edition by Schneider*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the higher mathematical curriculum for the guardians in Plato's Republic. This section transitions from the utility of music and harmony into the necessity of dialectic—the art of logical discourse—and the foundational role of arithmetic as a divine gift.</meta>

-># MATHEMATICS. 7<-

...and the search for the beautiful <note>original: καλοῦ (kalou) — referring to the Platonic ideal of Beauty or the Good.</note>, but otherwise it is useless. And the method of all these studies, if it should reach their mutual kinship <note>original: κοινωνίαν (koinōnian) — a communal relationship or fellowship between the different branches of mathematics.</note> and if it is reasoned out how they are related to one another, the pursuit of them bears fruit. But those who are skilled in these matters are
<margin>5</margin> dialecticians <note>original: διαλεκτικοί (dialektikoi) — masters of dialectic, the highest form of philosophical reasoning through dialogue.</note>; for they must be able to both give and receive a rational account <note>original: λόγον (logon) — a foundational Greek term meaning word, reason, or a logical justification.</note>. And this is not possible for one who has not passed through those mathematical studies; for through them is the path toward the contemplation of beings <note>original: τῶν ὄντων θέαν (tōn ontōn thean) — the "vision of things that are," or true reality.</note> in the practice of dialectic.

Again, in the *Epinomis* <note>An ancient dialogue often attributed to Plato, serving as an appendix to his "Laws."</note> he discusses many other things concerning
<margin>10</margin> arithmetic, calling it a gift of God, and saying it is not possible for anyone to become truly excellent without it. Going a bit further, he says outright: "For if we were to take number out of human nature, we would surely no longer be prudent, nor would the soul <note>original: ψυχή (psychē)</note> of this animal," he says,
<margin>15</margin> "ever attain all virtue; this would be roughly his meaning." And an animal that did not know "two" and "three," nor "odd" nor "even," and was altogether ignorant of number, would never be able to give an account of those things of which it possessed only sensations and memories.

---

**4 Republic, page 531 D** — "For surely those skilled in these things do not seem to you to be dialecticians?" "No, by Zeus," he said, "except for a very few whom I have met." "But then," I said, "if they are not able to give and receive an account, will they ever know anything of what we say they must know?" **6 the words "upon it" [ep' autō] appear after "those" [ekeinōn] in manuscript A, but dots indicate they should be deleted.** **8 see Republic, page 532 C** **10 see Epinomis, page 976 D–E** **11 "going further" [hypobas] corrected from "overstepping" [hyperbas] in manuscript A** **12 Epinomis, page 977 C** — "If we were to take number out of human nature, we would never become prudent in any way. For the soul of this animal would never again attain all virtue, roughly speaking, if the rational account [logos] were absent. And an animal that did not know two and three, nor odd nor even, but was altogether ignorant of number, would never have the power to give an account of things of which it possessed only sensations and memories; but as for the other virtues, courage and moderation, nothing prevents it from having them. However, being deprived of a true account [logos], it could never become wise." **15 the word "for" [gar] was added above the line after "roughly" [schedon] by the second hand of the manuscript [A²].**

<meta>This is the opening page of Theon of Smyrna's treatise. It establishes the author's intent to provide a mathematical handbook for students of Plato who lack a formal background in the sciences.</meta>

# **Theon of Smyrna, the Platonist: On the Mathematical Knowledge Useful for Reading Plato**

<margin>The necessity of mathematics</margin> That it is not possible to understand the mathematical discussions found in Plato without being trained in this field of study is something everyone would surely agree upon. Furthermore, Plato himself seems to demonstrate in many places that experience in these matters is neither useless nor unprofitable for other pursuits. 

<margin>5</margin> To encounter the writings of Plato after already becoming an expert in the whole of geometry, music, and astronomy would be a blessed thing for anyone to whom it might happen. However, this is neither a convenient nor an easy task; rather, it requires a great deal of toil beginning from childhood. 

<margin>10</margin> <margin>The purpose of the book</margin> Therefore, for the sake of those who have missed the opportunity to be trained in these <term>mathematical sciences<note>The Greek *mathēmata* refers to the "four ways" of learning: arithmetic, music, geometry, and astronomy.</note></term>, yet who still yearn for the knowledge contained in his writings, we shall provide a summary and concise presentation of the necessary principles. We will focus on those mathematical theorems most required by those who intend to read Plato: specifically, arithmetic, music, and geometry—including both <term>solid geometry<note>original: *stereometria*</note></term> and astronomy—without which 

---

**Title: Of Theon of Smyrna, the Platonist: On the mathematical matters useful for the reading of Plato.** <note>Recorded in Manuscript A. The words "the" were added above the line.</note>
**1** Title: *That the mathematical sciences are necessary.* <note>Margin note in Manuscript A.</note>
**2** "Being trained": <note>the ending of the Greek word was corrected in Manuscript A.</note>
**4** "Neither the other things": <note>alternative Greek phrasing for "neither/nor" found in Manuscript A.</note>
**5** "The": <note>corrected from a masculine to a neuter article in Manuscript A.</note>
**6** "Geometry": <note>A more recent hand added "and arithmetic" in a later copy, which is likely correct.</note>
**10** *Purpose of the book.* <note>Note in the margin of Manuscript A.</note>

<summary>In this introduction, Theon of Smyrna argues that mathematics is an essential prerequisite for understanding Platonic philosophy and announces his plan to provide a concise summary of arithmetic, music, and geometry for students.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the necessity of mathematical education, citing Plato's belief that a "best life" is unattainable without it. ### ON UTILITY

[Plato] says it is not possible to attain the best life, having shown through many instances that one must not neglect the <term>mathematical sciences<note>original: *mathemata*</note></term>.

For Eratosthenes <note>Eratosthenes of Cyrene (c. 276–194 BCE) was a famous polymath and head of the Library of Alexandria.</note> says in the work titled *Platonikos* that when the god gave an oracle to the inhabitants of Delos regarding
<margin>5</margin> deliverance from a plague, he commanded them to construct an altar double the size of the existing one. This threw the architects into a state of great confusion as they sought how to make a solid double that of another solid <note>This is the famous "Delian Problem" or "doubling the cube." It is a classic problem of ancient geometry.</note>. They went to Plato to inquire about this.
He told them that the god had not given this oracle to the Delians because he actually
<margin>10</margin> needed a doubled altar, but rather he was setting a challenge before them and reproaching the Greeks for neglecting the mathematical sciences and treating geometry with such little regard.

In accordance with this advice from the <term>Pythian<note>Referring to the Oracle of Apollo at Delphi.</note></term> god, Plato himself also recounts many things concerning the usefulness of mathematical studies.
<margin>15</margin> For in the *Epinomis*, while exhorting students toward these studies, he says: "For without these, no nature of man in a city will ever become happy; rather, this is the way, this is the training, these are the studies—whether they be difficult or easy—by which one must go; and it is not permitted by the gods to neglect them."
<margin>20</margin> And in the following passages, he says that such a person, having become one among many, will be happy, most wise, and blessed all at once.

And in the *Republic*, he says: "From the age of twenty-five <note>The standard text of Plato actually says twenty; the manuscript here says twenty-five.</note>, those who have been pre-selected shall receive honors greater than the others, and the..."

---

3 **Eratosthenes**: Bernhardy, *Eratosthenica* p. 168; see also *Philologus* XXX p. 67.
12 **neglecting**: Reading in Manuscript A; corrected in the transcription.
15 ***Epinomis* p. 992 A**: "For without these, never shall any nature in cities become happy (one manuscript reads 'being happy'). But this is the way, this is the training, these are the studies, whether difficult or easy; by this path one must travel (Nicomachus uses 'go'). And it is not permitted by the gods to neglect them."
17 **nature**: Manuscript A originally had "nature" (phys), then two letters were erased.
20 ***Epinomis* p. 992 B**.
22 ***Republic* VII p. 537 B**.
22 **twenty-five**: Plato says "twenty." See Schneider.
23 **greater than the others**: Plato’s word order is "greater of the others."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plato's curriculum from the previous page, moving from the Delian Problem to the specific sequence of the five mathematical sciences (arithmetic, geometry, stereometry, astronomy, and music) as described in Book VII of the Republic.</meta>

### MATHEMATICAL SCIENCES

The subjects that are learned haphazardly by everyone during their early education must be brought together into a unified view of their relationship to one another and to the nature of true being. He [Plato] recommends that one first become experienced in arithmetic, then geometry,
<margin>5</margin> thirdly <term>stereometry<note>The study of three-dimensional shapes or solid geometry.</note></term>, fourthly astronomy—which he says is the theory of a moving solid—and fifthly music. Demonstrating the utility of these studies, he says: "You are an amusing person, for you seem to fear that I might be assigning useless subjects." But this is not
<margin>10</margin> a trivial matter; rather, it is difficult for all to believe that in these subjects, as if by instruments, the eye of the soul in each person is purified and its spark rekindled—an eye that had been blinded and extinguished by other occupations. It is more vital to save this eye than ten thousand physical eyes, for by it alone
<margin>15</margin> is truth perceived.
In the seventh book of the *Republic*, while speaking about arithmetic, he says it is the most necessary of all subjects, and then...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plato's educational curriculum, specifically focusing on why the study of numbers (arithmetic and calculation) is essential for developing the human intellect and moving from the physical world to the world of true being.</meta>

->### ON UTILITY<-

...is necessary for all arts, all thought, and all sciences, as well as for the art of war. Indeed, in the tragedies, <term>Palamedes<note>A Greek hero often credited with inventing counting, the alphabet, and currency; he is frequently contrasted with Agamemnon in drama.</note></term> always represents Agamemnon as a completely ridiculous general. For he says that, having invented number, he
<margin>5</margin> established the ranks for the army at Troy and counted the ships and everything else—as if before this they were uncounted and Agamemnon, it seems, did not even know how many feet he had, if he did not know how to count. It is likely, then, that this study is among those that by nature lead toward understanding, yet no one
<margin>10</margin> uses it properly, even though it is a thing that draws us toward true being and serves to summon the intellect. For whatever merely moves the senses does not stimulate or summon the intellect—for example, seeing that something is a finger, or that it is thick or thin, or large or small. But whatever moves the senses
<margin>15</margin> in opposite ways *is* stimulating and summons the mind—for instance, when the same thing appears to be both large and small, light and heavy, or one and many. Therefore, both "the one" and number are summoners and stimulators of thought, since the same "one" sometimes appears as many; and <term>logistic<note>The art of calculation and the study of the relationships between numbers, as opposed to simple counting.</note></term> and arithmetic are a pull and a
<margin>20</margin> guide toward truth. One must take up logistic not in an amateurish way...

---

**2** *completely ridiculous* <note>original: "παγγέλοιον"</note>: the double gamma is corrected from 'ng' in Manuscript A (this spelling also appears in Plato’s Codex Vindobonensis F).
**3** Palamedes A. **4** *represents*. Or have you not considered that he says, having invented number, he established the ranks for the army at Troy...
**Plato 7** *knowing how many feet he had, if indeed he did not know how to count*. **Plato**; "did not know" was corrected from a misspelling in A. **Republic p. 523 A**: It is likely that of the things we seek, this is by nature one of those that lead toward understanding, though no one uses it correctly, even though it is entirely drawn toward being.

### MATHEMATICS

...but so that they might arrive at the contemplation of the nature of numbers by means of the intellect, practicing not for the sake of buying and selling like merchants or petty traders, but for the sake of the soul and the path toward truth and <term>being<note>In Platonic philosophy, 'being' (ousia) refers to the eternal, unchanging reality of the Forms, as opposed to the 'becoming' of the physical world.</note></term>. 
<margin>5</margin>For this study leads the soul upward and compels it to discuss numbers themselves, not accepting it if someone tries to discuss them while presenting physical bodies or visible things that have numbers. And again in the same work [Plato] says: "Furthermore, those who are naturally gifted in <term>logistic<note>The art of calculation and mathematical reasoning.</note></term> prove to be sharp in all subjects, while 
<margin>10</margin>those who are slow improve and become sharper than they were before." Further, in the same work he says: "And it is also useful in war for setting up camps, capturing territories, and for the gathering and reviewing of an army." And in the following sections, praising the dedication to such studies, he says: "Geometry is concerned 
<margin>15</margin>with the theory of the plane, and astronomy with the motion of the solid; this study compels the soul to look upward and leads it from things here to things there." And regarding music, he says in the same work that two things are needed for the study of things that are...

---

...but for the sake of war and the ease of the soul itself and its turning away from the world of <term>becoming<note>The physical world of change and decay.</note></term> toward truth and being — **4 Republic p. 525 D** "this at least" <note>original: τοῦτό γε</note> — "how it leads the soul quite powerfully upward and compels it to discuss numbers themselves, in no way accepting it if someone tries to discuss them while proposing visible or tangible physical bodies that possess numbers."

# MATHEMATICS

Then he demonstrates the way in which one might learn piety <note>original: "theosebeia" — meaning religious devotion or reverence for the divine</note>. He says that one must first learn astronomy. For if lying about men is a terrible thing, it is much more terrible to lie about the gods. But a person who holds false opinions <margin>5</margin> about the gods would be lying about them; and someone who has not even investigated the nature of the visible gods—that is, astronomy—would hold false opinions about the gods. He says it is unknown to the many that the true astronomer must necessarily be most wise—not one who practices astronomy according to Hesiod <note>The early poet Hesiod used the stars as a calendar for farming; here, the author distinguishes this practical use from deep scientific study</note>, for example, by having looked into the settings and <margin>10</margin> risings of stars, but rather one who has studied the orbital periods of the seven [planets], a feat which not every nature could easily ever be capable of contemplating. And for the one who prepares such natures, it is necessary to teach many things in advance by habituating them while they are children and young men through mathematics—of which the greatest is...

1 <term>Epinomis</term> page 989 E — [concerning] the way in which one will learn piety. 
7 <term>Epinomis</term> page 990 A: You are ignorant that the true astronomer must necessarily be most wise, not the one practicing astronomy according to Hesiod and all such people, such as one who has looked into settings and risings, but the one who tracks the seven periods of the eight [celestial spheres], each one traversing the same circle, in such a way as not every nature could easily ever be capable of contemplating. 
12 <term>Epinomis</term> page 990 C: ...preparing such natures, through which it is necessary to be capable of teaching many things in advance and habituating them; one must work through these as a child and a young man. Therefore, there would be a need for mathematics; and the first and greatest is numbers themselves, not having bodies, but concerning the whole generation and power of the odd and the even, as much as it provides toward the nature of reality. Having learned these, next is what they call by the very ridiculous name of <term>geometry</term> <note>original: "geometria" — literally 'earth-measurement'; the author finds the name too lowly for a divine science</note>, which is clearly an assimilation of numbers not naturally similar to one another according to the category of surfaces; this marvel is not human but a divine occurrence that would become clear to one able to understand. After this, those numbers increased three times <note>referring to cubic or three-dimensional numbers</note> and made similar to the solid nature, and those made dissimilar by another art similar to this, which those who encountered it called geometry (<term>stereometry</term> <note>the study of three-dimensional shapes</note> in manuscript Z); which is both divine and wonderful—

...the knowledge of numbers themselves—not as having physical bodies—and of the very origin and power of the "even" and the "odd," and how much this contributes to the nature of reality. It is actually the making of numbers—which are not naturally similar to one another—similar, in relation to the realm of surfaces. He also mentions another experience and art, which he calls "stereometry" <note>The study of three-dimensional "solid" geometry</note>, which occurs if one <margin>10</margin> takes the three numbers from which surfaces are increased and makes them similar and dissimilar, as I said before, into solid bodies; and this is something divine and wonderful.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plato's educational theory, specifically moving from the study of mathematics to the moral and aesthetic influence of music on the soul. It follows the previous page's excerpt from the Republic regarding the training of the guardians.</meta>

# MATHEMATICS

...until we recognize all the forms of temperance and courage and nobility and magnificence and all their sisters and <unclear>●</unclear> their opposites, which are carried about everywhere, and we distinguish them and perceive them where they are present <margin>5</margin> both the forms themselves and their images, and we do not despise them in small things or great, but consider them to belong to the same art and study? For through these things and those that came before them, he shows what the benefit of music is, and that only the philosopher is truly a musician, while the wicked person is unmusical <note>In the Greek sense, "unmusical" refers to someone who is out of tune with the cosmic and moral order, not just someone lacking technical musical skill</note>. For he says that "good-heartedness" <margin>10</margin>—which is truly the virtue of having a character well-disposed—is followed by "good speech" <note>original: *eulogia*</note>, that is, using speech well; and good speech is followed by grace, rhythm, and harmony. For grace <note>original: *euschēmosunē*, literally "goodness of form"</note> concerns melody, harmony concerns the musical scale, and rhythm <margin>15</margin> concerns the beat. But bad character—that is, a wicked disposition—he says is followed by "bad speech" <note>original: *kakologia*</note>, that is, the use of bad speech; and bad speech is followed by gracelessness, lack of rhythm, and lack of harmony in all things produced or imitated. So that only the one who is truly good-hearted would be a musician, and this <margin>20</margin> would be the philosopher. The things already stated also show this. For since music instills rhythm, harmony, and grace into the soul—entering it from a young age because it has harmless pleasure mixed with utility—he says it is impossible to become a perfect musician without knowing the nature of grace in everything, <margin>25</margin> and the qualities of grace, liberality, and temperance...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato’s educational theory from the Republic. While the previous page focused on the early musical training of the guardians and the recognition of "good-heartedness," this page transitions into the metaphysical "Forms" or "Ideas" that underpin those virtues, eventually linking Platonic thought to Pythagorean cosmic harmony.</meta>

# ON UTILITY

...not recognizing the types of [virtues], that is, the **Forms** <note>original: *ideae*. In Platonic philosophy, these are the perfect, eternal patterns of which physical things are mere shadows.</note>. He naturally adds: being carried about in everything—that is, the Forms—and not despising them in small things or great. Now, the knowledge of the Forms belongs to the philosopher; for no one
<margin>5</margin> could know what is decent, temperate, and graceful if they were themselves ungraceful and unrestrained. For gracefulness, rhythm, and being well-attuned in one's life are images of true grace, attunement, and rhythm; that is, things perceived by the senses are images
<margin>10</margin> of the **intelligible things** <note>original: *noēta*; things that can only be understood by the mind, not the senses</note> and the Forms.
And the **Pythagoreans** <note>A school of philosopher-mathematicians who believed the universe was structured according to numerical ratios and musical harmony.</note>, whom Plato follows in many ways, say that music is a harmony of opposites, a unification of the many, and a shared mindset of those who think differently. For music is not just a composer of rhythms and melody, but is simply the organizer of every system; for its goal is to unite 
<margin>15</margin> and to harmonize. Indeed, **God is the harmonizer** of the discordant, and this is the greatest work of God: to make enemies into friends, whether through the art of music or the art of medicine. In music, they say, lies the concord of things, and even the "aristocracy" <note>Used here in its literal Greek sense: "the rule of the best."</note> of the universe. For this music is naturally 
<margin>20</margin> expressed as **harmony** in the cosmos, as **lawfulness** <note>original: *eunomia*</note> in a city, and as **temperance** <note>original: *sōphrosynē*</note> in homes; for it is the power that brings together and unites the many. And the activity and use of this science, he says, occurs in four human spheres: the soul, the body, the home, and the city; for these
<margin>25</margin> four require harmony and order.
And in the *Republic*, Plato speaks regarding these studies...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plato’s educational program. While the previous page touched on the harmony of the soul and the role of the "Forms," this page provides a specific passage from the Republic regarding how education must be "dyed" into the character of the student to withstand the trials of life.</meta>

# MATHEMATICS <note>original: *MATHEMATICAE*</note>

And he said these things: "The good man is whoever preserves the correct opinion regarding those things that came to him from his education, both in pains and pleasures, and in desires and fears, and does not cast it out. And to what this seems to me to be similar, I am willing to liken it. Dyers nowadays, when they want to dye wool so as to be **sea-purple** <note>original: *halourga*; a prestigious and permanent purple dye derived from murex snails</note>, first choose from many colors a single kind, that of the white wools. Then they prepare it with no small preparation, treating it so that it might receive the **bloom** <note>original: *anthos*; the vibrancy or "flower" of the color</note> as much as possible, and only then do they dye it. And whatever is dyed in this manner, the dyed thing is roughly..."

---

1 The Republic, page 429 C <note>original: *Civ. p. 429 C*. Referring to Plato’s *Civitas* or *Republic*</note> (compare also the preceding passages): "And I used to call this 'preservation' throughout: the staying safe while in pains and in pleasures and in desires and in fears, and not casting [this opinion] out. To what this seems to me to be similar, I am willing to liken it, if you wish." "But I do wish." "Do you not know, then," I said, "that the dyers, whenever they want to dye wool so as to be **sea-purple**, first choose from so many colors a single kind, that of the white ones, then they prepare it beforehand with no small preparation, treating it so that it will receive <note>original: *dexetai*; a variant reading found in manuscript A of Stobaeus and several manuscripts of Plato</note> the **bloom** as much as possible, and thus indeed they dye it. And whatever is dyed in this way, the dyed thing becomes **fast-colored** <note>original: *deusopoion*; a technical term for a dye that is ingrained and will not wash out</note>, and the washing—whether without soaps or with soaps—cannot take away their bloom. But what is not [prepared], you know what happens to them, whether one dyes other colors or even these without first treating them." "I know," he said, "that they are washed out and ridiculous." "Suppose then," I said, "that we also, according to our power, were working toward such a thing when we were choosing the soldiers and educating them in music and gymnastics; think that we were devising nothing else than how they might receive the laws from us as beautifully as possible, just like a dye, so that their opinion might become **fast-colored** regarding both fears and other things, because they have the appropriate nature and training. We did this so that these **soaps** <note>original: *rymmata*; detergents or cleansing agents</note> might not wash out their dye—for they are terrible at rinsing things away—whether it be **pleasure**, which is more powerful at doing this than any **nitre** <note>original: *chalestraiou*; a mineral salt from Chalastra used for cleaning</note> or **lye**, or pain, or fear, or desire, which are more powerful than any other soap..."

<summary>This page quotes a famous passage from Plato’s Republic comparing the education of a citizen to the technical process of dyeing wool. Just as wool must be pre-treated to hold its color, a person's character must be prepared by education to ensure their virtues are "fast-colored" and cannot be washed away by the "detergents" of pleasure or pain.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Republic, education, dyeing metaphor, fast-colored, sea-purple, virtue, character formation, Stobaeus, apparatus criticus</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy from Plato’s Republic (Book IV, 429d–430b) comparing the education of the soul to the process of dyeing wool. It transitions into a discussion of philosophy as a spiritual initiation, likely drawing from Theon of Smyrna or a similar Neoplatonic source that parallels the stages of education with the stages of the ancient Mystery religions.</meta>

# ON UTILITY <note>original: *DE UTILITATE*</note>

...nature, and neither without detergents <note>original: *rhymmaton*; cleaning agents or lye used in the dyeing process</note> nor with detergents can their **bloom** <note>original: *anthos*; the vibrancy or "flower" of the color</note> be taken away. But for those things that are not so prepared, you know what happens if one dyes them without first treating them: the color is washed out and faded and not **colorfast** <note>original: *deusopoia*; a technical term for a dye that has deeply "soaked in" and become permanent</note>.

<margin>5</margin> We must consider that we are working in a similar way, according to our ability. For we educate children in **music** and **gymnastics**, and in **letters**, **geometry**, and **arithmetic**, devising nothing else than that we—having first cleansed and treated them beforehand with these studies as if with certain **mordants** <note>original: *styptikois*; astringent chemicals like alum used to fix dyes to fabric</note>—

<margin>10</margin> might then instill the teachings about every virtue they learn later, just like a dye. Our goal is that their **conviction** <note>original: *doxa*; often translated as "opinion," but here referring to a deeply held moral belief</note> might become colorfast because they have received the proper nature and upbringing. We do this so that these "detergents" do not wash out their dye—and these are formidable at scouring:

<margin>15</margin> **pleasure**, which is more powerful than any lye or solution, as well as **pain**, **fear**, and **longing**, which are stronger than any other detergent.

Furthermore, one might also say that **philosophy** is an **initiation** <note>original: *myēsin*; the process of becoming a "mystes" or initiate</note> into a true rite and the handing down of mysteries that truly exist. There are five parts to this initiation.

<margin>20</margin> The first part is **purification** <note>original: *katharmos*</note>. For the sharing of the mysteries is not open to everyone who wishes; rather, there are those who are publicly warned to stay away—such as those who do not have "clean hands" or an "intelligible voice." And even those who are not excluded

<margin>25</margin> must necessarily undergo a certain purification first. After the purification, the second part is the **transmission of the rite** <note>original: *teletēs paradosis*</note>.

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion of education as a transformative process. The previous page used the analogy of a dyer preparing wool to accept a permanent color; this page transitions from that metaphor into a formal comparison between the stages of Platonic education and the stages of initiation into the ancient Mystery religions.</meta>

### MATHEMATICS

The third stage is the so-called **vision** <note>original: *epopteia*; the highest stage of initiation in the Greek mysteries, where the initiate directly beholds sacred objects</note>; the fourth, which is the very goal of the vision, is the **binding of the head** and the **placement of wreaths**, so that one is able to pass on the rites they have received to others—having attained the office of **torch-bearer** <note>original: *daidouchia*</note> or **hierophant** <note>original: *hierophantia*; the high priest who "shows the sacred things"</note>
<margin>5</margin> or some other priesthood. The fifth stage is the **happiness** <note>original: *eudaimonia*; a state of flourishing or "blessedness"</note> that arises from these, resulting in a state of being dear to the gods and living in their company. In the same way, the transmission of Platonic discourses first involves a certain **purification** <note>original: *katharmos*</note>, such as the training from childhood in the appropriate mathematical studies. For
<margin>10</margin> Empedocles <note>A Pre-Socratic philosopher who wrote about nature and purification</note> says that one must "cleanse oneself, drawing from five springs with tireless bronze." Plato, however, says that one must perform this purification through **five mathematical subjects**: these are **arithmetic**, **geometry**, **solid geometry** <note>original: *stereometria*</note>, **music**, and **astronomy**. The transmission of the
<margin>15</margin> philosophical theorems—the logical, political, and physical—resembles the **initiation rite** <note>original: *teletē*</note>. He gives the name **vision** to the study of **intelligible things** <note>original: *noēta*; realities known by the mind rather than the senses</note>, of **true reality**, and of the **Forms** <note>original: *ideōn*</note>. We should consider the **binding** and **crowning** to be the ability to lead others
<margin>20</margin> to this same contemplation from what one has learned oneself. The fifth and most perfect stage would be the **happiness** that arises from these things.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of education as a sacred journey. Having compared the five mathematical subjects to the stages of purification in the Mystery religions on the previous page, the author now transitions into the specific curriculum intended for students of Plato.</meta>

### ON THE INTENTION OF THE WRITER <note>original Latin: *DE CONSILIO SCRIPTORIS*</note>

and, according to Plato himself, this is the **becoming like God** <note>original: *homoiōsis theō*; a central Platonic concept from the *Theaetetus*, p. 176 B</note> as far as is possible for a human.

One could certainly say many other things to demonstrate how useful and necessary these mathematical studies are. However, so as not to seem to dwell tastelessly on the praise of mathematics, we must now turn to the transmission 
<margin>→ (Hand-drawn arrow)</margin> of the necessary theorems within these subjects. We will not include everything that might turn a reader into a perfect **arithmetician**, **geometer**, **musician**, or **astronomer**; for this is not the primary purpose or the goal for everyone who reads Plato. Instead, we will transmit only those things that are sufficient to enable one to understand his writings. For Plato himself does not think it right that one should reach old age still drawing geometric diagrams and composing melodies; rather, he considers these subjects to be for **childhood** <note>In the sense of "introductory" or "elementary"</note>, serving as a preparation and a **purification** for the soul, making it fit for the study of philosophy. 

Therefore, it is especially necessary for someone who intends to study what we are presenting, as well as the writings of Plato, to have at least gone through the first elementary principles of **geometry** <note>original: *grammikē stoicheiōsis*; literally "linear elements"</note>. By doing so, they will more easily follow the things we transmit. Nevertheless, our presentation will be written in such a way that it is intelligible even to those who are entirely **uninitiated** <note>original: *amyētō*; continuing the metaphor of the Mystery religions where the "uninitiated" are those who have not yet received the sacred rites</note> in mathematics.

First, we will mention the theorems of **arithmetic**, which are joined to the principles of **music in numbers** <note>This refers to the mathematical ratios that govern musical harmony, rather than the performance of music</note>. We do not have an absolute need for the music played on instruments, just as Plato himself explains when he says that one should not, like... <note>This refers to a passage in the *Republic*, Book VII, 531 A, where Plato critiques musicians who favor their ears over their intellects</note>

<summary>The author explains that this text is an introductory guide to the mathematics necessary for understanding Plato's philosophy, serving as a "purification" of the mind rather than training for professional mathematicians.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, becoming like God, mathematics as purification, curriculum, arithmetic, geometry, music theory, initiation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the curriculum of mathematical study. Having established that mathematics is a purification for the soul, the author now defines the specific order in which these subjects should be learned, distinguishing between the order of instruction and the "natural" order of the universe.</meta>

# ON THE ORDER OF THE MATHEMATICAL DISCIPLINES

...hunting for sounds from neighbors <note>A reference to those who try to tune musical instruments by ear alone, through trial and error, rather than by mathematical ratio</note> and giving trouble to the strings. But we desire to understand the **harmony** within the cosmos and the **music** <note>original: *mousikē*; in the Platonic sense, this refers to the mathematical laws of proportion and concord, not just audible sound</note> within it. This cannot be perceived unless one has first become a **theorist** <note>original: *theōrētikous*; one who contemplates the underlying principles</note> of numbers. 

<margin>5</margin>For this reason, Plato says that music is the fifth discipline, speaking of the music in the cosmos, which exists in the motion, order, and concord of the stars moving within it. But for us, it is necessary to rank it second after **arithmetic**, even according to Plato himself, since cosmic music cannot be grasped without the music that is numbered and understood. 

<margin>10</margin>Thus, if the music found in numbers is joined to the study of pure numbers, it would be ranked second for the ease of our instruction. 

However, according to the **natural order**:
<margin>15</margin>1. First would be the study of numbers, called **arithmetic**.
2. Second would be the study of surfaces, called **geometry**.
3. Third would be the study of solids, which is **stereometry**.
4. Fourth would be the study of solids in motion, which is **astronomy**.

<margin>20</margin>The fifth, which concerns the specific relationship of motions and intervals, is **music**. This cannot be grasped unless we have first understood it through numbers. Therefore, for the purpose of our instruction, let music in numbers be ranked after arithmetic; but according to nature, the music that contemplates the **harmony of the cosmos** is fifth. 

<margin>25</margin>According to the **Pythagoreans**, we must honor...

<meta>This page transitions from the general curriculum of mathematical sciences to the specific study of arithmetic, the first of the disciplines. -># ON THE ONE<-

<note>original: *DE UNO*. This Latin title introduces a section on the nature of unity and the number one.</note>

<warning>Heavy black ink mark or strikeout obscuring parts of lines 7 and 8.</warning>

...the nature of numbers as the principle, fountain, and root of all things. 

A **number** is a collection of **monads** <note>original: *monas*; the fundamental unit or "one" from which all numbers are built</note>, or a progression of a multitude beginning from a monad and a regression ending back into a 
<margin>5</margin> monad. A **monad** is a limiting quantity—[the principle and element of numbers]—which, as a multitude is diminished through the subtraction of every number, is deprived of further reduction and reaches a state of stability and rest. For it is not 
<margin>10</margin> possible for the division to go any further. For even if we divide the "one" found in sensible things <note>Physical objects perceived by the senses, rather than abstract mathematical concepts</note> into parts, that "one" will conversely become a multitude and many; yet it will end in "one" again as each of those parts is subtracted. And if we divide those parts again into 
<margin>15</margin> further pieces, the parts will become a multitude, and the conclusion of the subtraction of each part will again be "one." Thus, the One, insofar as it is "one," is impartible and indivisible. 

Indeed, while any other number is lessened when divided and split into parts smaller than itself—for example, six is divided 
<margin>20</margin> into three and three, or four and two, or five and one—the "one," if it is divided among sensible things, is lessened as a **body** <note>A physical object takes up less space when cut</note>, but as a **number** it increases; for instead of one, it becomes many. 

Thus, in this respect also, the One is without parts. For nothing that is divided is split into parts 
<margin>23</margin> larger than itself; yet the ⟨One⟩...

***

<note>The following notes refer to the scholarly apparatus provided in the transcription, identifying the sources of this text.</note>

**Line 3:** Title: "On the One and the Monad." From the *Excerpts* of Stobaeus, Book I, chapter 1, section 8: "From the works of **Moderatus the Pythagorean** <note>A philosopher of the 1st century AD who sought to recover original Pythagorean teachings</note>. A number is, to speak in general terms, a collection of units, or a progression of a multitude beginning from a unit and a regression ending in a unit; a monad is a limiting quantity which, as the multitude is diminished by the subtraction of every number, is deprived of further reduction and takes up stay and standing. For the quantity of the monad is not able to regress further than the monad."

<summary>This page defines number as a system of units and describes the "monad" as the indivisible, stable foundation of all mathematics. It contrasts how a physical object becomes "many" when divided, while the mathematical concept of the One remains an indivisible limit.</summary>

<keywords>arithmetic, monad, Moderatus the Pythagorean, Stobaeus, division, unit, sensible things, mathematical principle</keywords>
