### ON THE PARTS OF MELODY <note>original Latin: DE PARTIBUS MODULATIONIS</note>
Theo of Smyrna, 4

...neither should one note sound at the same pitch <note>original Greek: ὁμότονον (homotonon). Literally "same tension," referring to notes that are identical in pitch.</note> as another note, nor should it be discordant <note>original Greek: διάφωνον (diaphōnon). Sounds that clash or do not blend.</note>, but rather it should be at some recognizable, similar interval. Notes are discordant and not **concordant** <note>original Greek: σύμφωνοι (symphōnoi). Sounds that blend harmoniously, like an octave or fifth.</note> when their
<margin>5</margin>interval is a **tone** or a **diesis** <note>A diesis is a very small musical interval, such as a quarter-tone, used in ancient Greek scales.</note>; for the tone and the diesis are the beginning of concord, but they are not yet concord themselves.

The Peripatetic <note>A member of the philosophical school founded by Aristotle.</note> Adrastus, explaining the nature of harmony and concord more clearly, says: Just as in written language and all speech, the primary and whole parts are **verbs** and **nouns**, and of
<margin>10</margin>these the **syllables**, and these in turn are made of **letters**—and letters are the primary, elementary, indivisible, and smallest sounds (for speech is composed of primary letters and is resolved back into these as its final parts)—in the same way, in melodic and harmonious sound and
<margin>15</margin>all song, the whole parts are the so-called **systems** <note>original Greek: συστήματα (systēmata). A set of notes forming a scale or part of a scale.</note>, such as **tetrachords**, **pentachords**, and **octachords**. <note>Scales consisting of four, five, and eight notes respectively.</note> <gloss>These systems correspond to words, such as verbs and nouns, while intervals correspond to syllables, and notes correspond to letters.</gloss> These are made of intervals, and intervals are made of **notes**, which are themselves the primary, indivisible, and elementary sounds from which
<margin>20</margin>all melody is first composed and into which it is finally resolved. They differ...

<summary>This page uses a linguistic analogy by Adrastus the Peripatetic to explain that musical notes are the "letters" or fundamental building blocks of music, which combine to form intervals and larger systems like scales.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical elements by the Peripatetic philosopher Adrastus, focusing on the physical nature of sound and the mathematical ratios that distinguish musical notes from mere noise. It transitions from the classification of musical parts to the Pythagorean theory of acoustics.</meta>

### ON THE DIFFERENCE OF SOUNDS <note>original Latin: DE SONORUM DIFFERENTIA</note>

...notes <note>original Greek: φθόγγοι (phthongoi). Specifically refers to musical tones with a definite pitch, as opposed to general noise.</note> differ from one another in their **tensions** <note>original Greek: τάσεσιν (tasesin). Literally "tensions," referring to the tightness of a string which determines its pitch.</note>, since some of them are higher-pitched <note>original Greek: ὀξύτεροι (oxyteroi). Literally "sharper" or "more acute."</note> and others are lower-pitched <note>original Greek: βαρύτεροι (baryteroi). Literally "heavier" or "more grave."</note>; and their tensions are defined according to certain **ratios** <note>original Greek: λόγους (logous). Mathematical proportions that govern the relationship between pitches.</note>.

<margin>5</margin> He says that the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed that the universe and music were governed by number and geometry.</note> also provide a technical account of these matters in this way: since every melody and every note is a kind of **voice** <note>original Greek: φωνή (phōnē). Can mean "voice," "sound," or "articulate utterance."</note>, and every voice is a **sound**, and sound is a striking of the air that is prevented from being scattered, it is clear that if there were stillness in the air, there would be neither sound nor voice, and therefore no musical note. However, when a 
<margin>10</margin> striking and movement occur in the air, a high-pitched note is produced by **fast** movement, and a low-pitched note by **slow** movement; and a **loud** sound is produced by violent movement, while a **soft** sound is produced by gentle movement. These speeds of movement and their intensities are produced either in certain ratios or **irrationally** <note>original Greek: ἀλόγως (alogōs). Meaning without a measurable mathematical proportion.</note> relative to one another. From those that are irrational, 
<margin>15</margin> irrational and unmelodic noises are produced, which should not even be called "notes" in the strict sense, but only "sounds"; but from those that exist in certain ratios to one another—whether **multiple** <note>ratios like 2:1 (octave) or 3:1.</note> or **superparticular** <note>original Greek: ἐπιμορίοις (epimoriois). Ratios where the numerator is one greater than the denominator, such as 3:2 (a fifth) or 4:3 (a fourth).</note> or simply one number to another—come melodic and, in the strict and proper sense, **notes**. Of these, some are merely "tuned," but those produced 
<margin>20</margin> according to the primary, most recognizable, and most fundamental ratios—both multiple and superparticular—are also **concords** <note>original Greek: σύμφωνοι (symphōnoi). Sounds that blend together harmoniously.</note>. Notes are concordant with one another when...

<summary>This page explains the Pythagorean physical theory of music, arguing that pitch is determined by the speed of air being struck and that only specific mathematical ratios (multiple and superparticular) create harmonious musical notes rather than random noise.</summary>

<keywords>acoustics, Pythagoras, Adrastus, pitch, ratios, concords, music theory, vibration</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical acoustics, transitioning from the physical movement of air to the harmonic relationships between notes. It explores sympathetic resonance and defines the primary musical intervals—the fourth, fifth, and octave—based on their positions on the ancient eight-string lyre.</meta>

### ON CONSONANCES <note>original Latin: DE CONSONANTIIS</note>

...when a string is struck on one of the instruments with tension <note>original Greek: ἐντατῶν (entatōn). Refers to stringed instruments where pitch is determined by the tension of the strings.</note>, the rest of the strings also sound together <note>original Greek: συνηχεῖ (synēchei). This describes the phenomenon of sympathetic resonance, where one vibrating string causes another to vibrate.</note> due to a certain affinity and sympathy. When both are struck at the same time, a sweet and pleasant voice is heard from the blending <note>original Greek: κράσεως (kraseōs). Literally "mixing" or "tempering," referring to how two distinct pitches merge into a single harmonious sound.</note>. 

<margin>5</margin> Among the notes tuned in succession, the fourth ones in order are consonant with one another; they form the consonance called the **fourth** <note>original Greek: διὰ τεσσάρων (dia tessaron). Literally "through four." In modern music, a perfect fourth.</note> for this very reason. Next are the fifth notes, forming the **fifth** <note>original Greek: διὰ πέντε (dia pente). Literally "through five." In modern music, a perfect fifth.</note>. After these are the notes that encompass both consonances, 

<margin>10</margin> occurring eight notes apart from each other, called the **octave** <note>original Greek: διὰ πασῶν (dia pasōn). Literally "through all [the strings]."</note>. It was given this name because on the original eight-string lyre, the first and lowest-pitched note—called the **highest** <term>hypate</term> <note>original Greek: ὑπάτη (hypate). Paradoxically, "hypate" means "highest," but it refers to the string physically highest on the lyre's frame, which produced the lowest pitch.</note>—was found to hold the same consonance with the last and highest-pitched note—that is, the **lowest** <term>nete</term> <note>original Greek: νήτη (nete). Means "lowest," referring to the string physically lowest on the frame, which produced the highest pitch.</note>—according to the 

<margin>15</margin> **antiphonal** <note>original Greek: ἀντίφωνον (antiphōnon). A relationship where two notes are perceived as the "same" pitch but in different registers, like a man and a boy singing the same melody.</note> relationship. Although music has expanded and instruments have become many-stringed and many-toned—by adding more notes both above and below the original eight notes—the names of the primary consonances are still preserved: the **fourth**, the **fifth**, and the **octave**.

<meta>This page follows a discussion on the basic musical intervals—the fourth, fifth, and octave—and now expands on how these primary consonances can be combined to form larger intervals. It transitions into a discussion of the "range" of the human voice and the mathematical/logical "grammar" of melody.</meta>

->### ON CONSONANCES <note>original Latin: DE CONSONANTIIS</note><-

In addition to these primary intervals, several others have been discovered. For when any other interval—whether it be smaller, larger, or equal—is added to the **octave** <note>original Greek: διὰ πασῶν (dia pasōn), meaning "through all [notes]"</note>, another consonance is produced from the combination of the two. Examples of this include the **octave plus a fourth** <note>original Greek: διὰ πασῶν καὶ διὰ τεσσάρων</note>,

<margin>5</margin> the **octave plus a fifth** <note>original Greek: διὰ πασῶν καὶ διὰ πέντε</note>, and the **double octave** <note>original Greek: δὶς διὰ πασῶν</note>. Furthermore, if one of these combined intervals were again added to the octave—such as adding the double octave to a fourth—and so on with other combinations, the sequence continues as far as the human voice is capable of sounding or the listener is capable of judging. For there is a certain **range** <note>original Greek: τόπος (topos), literally "place" or "region"</note> of the voice which it

<margin>10</margin> traverses, starting from the very lowest **note** <note>original Greek: φθόγγου (phthongou)</note> and proceeding in order toward the high pitch, or vice versa. Regarding these ranges, some people extend them further, while others keep them narrower. However, making melodic and orderly progress within this range does not happen by chance, nor does it happen in just one simple way.

<margin>15</margin> Instead, it occurs according to certain defined **modes** <note>original Greek: τρόπους (tropous)</note>, through which the differences in the so-called **genera** <term>genera</term> <note>original Greek: γενῶν (genōn). In ancient Greek music theory, there were three "genera" or types of scales: the Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic, each defined by the tuning of the middle notes in a four-note group.</note> of melody are observed. Just as in the case of speech and written language, where not every letter joined to another results in a syllable or a word,

<margin>20</margin> so it is with song: within the tuned voice and its specific range, not every note placed after another creates a **melodic interval** <note>original Greek: ἐμμελὲς διάστημα (emmelès diastēma)</note>. Rather, as we have said, these notes must follow certain defined modes.

***

"If" was originally omitted, then added in A; it is missing in Manuscript Z. **8** "Similarly" (Manuscript A); "In this way" (Manuscript Z). **9** See Aristoxenus page 18, 27. Vetter's additions to the Stephanus Treasury, page 24: "The range of the voice is that which, starting from the lowest, passes through in order to the high, or conversely from the highest to the low." (Note in the margin of Manuscript A). **12** "Of these... they extend" (possibly deleted). **13** "The... melodic... progress" (Manuscript Z); corrected to "The... melodic... progression" in Manuscript A. **14** "But" (Manuscript A); "But also" (Manuscript Z). **17** See Aristoxenus pages 38 and 52. **19** "According to the" (Manuscript Z); omitted in Manuscript A.

It uses an analogy between music and language, arguing that notes must be arranged according to specific rules—just like letters in a word—to be considered melodic.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on musical theory from page 52, moving from the broad definitions of consonances (the fourth, fifth, and octave) to the specific "ruler" of musical space: the tone. It introduces the diatonic genus, the most common arrangement of notes in ancient Greek music.</meta>

->### ON THE DIATONIC GENUS <note>original Latin: DE GENERE DIATONO</note><-

Within the so-called range of the voice and all the intervals contained within it, the most recognizable part and measure is the so-called **tonal interval** <note>original Greek: τονιαῖον διάστημα (toniaion diastema)</note>. This is just as the **cubit** <note>a standard unit of length roughly the distance from the elbow to the fingertip</note> is the measure of the physical distance through which moving bodies <margin>5</margin> pass. The tonal interval is the most recognizable because it is the difference between the first and most well-known consonances; for the **fifth** <note>original Greek: διὰ πέντε (dia pente)</note> exceeds the **fourth** <note>original Greek: διὰ τεσσάρων (dia tessarōn)</note> by exactly one tone.

However, the **semitone** <note>original Greek: ἡμιτόνιον (hemitonion)</note> is not named as being exactly "half" of a tone, as **Aristoxenus** <note>A famous musical theorist (4th c. BCE) who argued that the ear, not math, should judge intervals; he believed a tone could be split into two equal halves</note> believes—in the same way that a half-cubit is <margin>10</margin> exactly half of a cubit—but rather it is named as a melodic interval that is simply smaller than a tone. In the same way, we do not call a **semivowel** <note>original Greek: ἡμίφωνον (hēmiphōnon), such as the letters S or L</note> "half of a voice," but rather a sound that does not have a complete voice on its own. It is demonstrated that the tone cannot be divided into two equal parts at all when it is viewed as a **9:8 ratio** <term>epogdoos</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπογδόῳ (epogdoō). In Pythagorean math, a tone is the ratio 9:8. Because 9 is not a perfect square, its "square root" (the half-tone) cannot be expressed as a simple ratio of whole numbers</note>, <margin>15</margin> just as any other **superparticular** <term>epimorion</term> <note>A ratio where the numerator is the denominator plus one (e.g., 3:2, 4:3, 9:8)</note> interval cannot be so divided. For it is impossible for the number nine to be divided into equal [whole] parts.

When, therefore, the voice in singing within its so-called range moves from a certain lower note to the next higher one, having made the so-called semitonal interval <margin>20</margin> and then after that having set an interval of a tone first...

<margin>Note: One must know that the diatonic does not always have this fixed structure, but it is possible for there to be two tones after the semitone on either side; from different arrangements, a different melody arises.</margin>

<summary>This page defines the "tone" as the fundamental unit of musical measurement, derived from the mathematical difference between a fifth and a fourth. It argues against Aristoxenus's view that a tone can be split into two equal halves, using the Pythagorean 9:8 ratio as proof.</summary>
<keywords>tonal interval, semitone, Aristoxenus, 9:8 ratio, epogdoos, superparticular, diatonic genus, musical mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of musical intervals from page 53. Having defined the "tone" as the standard measure, the author now describes how these tones and semitones combine to form the "tetrachord"—the fundamental four-note building block of Greek scales—specifically within the Diatonic and Chromatic genera.</meta>

->### ON THE CHROMATIC GENUS <note>original Latin: DE GENERE CHROMATICO</note><-

...should the voice arrive at another note, wishing to progress melodiously in sequence, there can be no other interval, nor can it produce another melodious and tuned note, except for a **tonal interval** <note>original Greek: τονιαῖον διάστημα (toniaion diastema)</note>, or rather a <margin>5</margin> note that defines this interval toward the higher pitch and forms a consonance with the starting note: the **consonance of the fourth** <note>original Greek: διὰ τεσσάρων συμφωνίαν (dia tessarōn symphōnian)</note>. A system thus melodized is called a **tetrachord** <term>tetrachordon</term> <note>original: τετράχορδον; literally "four strings." It is a series of four notes spanning the interval of a perfect fourth.</note>, consisting of three intervals—a semitone, a tone, and a tone—and of four notes, of which the outer ones (that is, the <margin>10</margin> lowest and the highest) immediately form the consonance which we said is called the "fourth," consisting of <margin>What the fourth is and how it occurs; what the diatonic genus of melody is and how it occurs.</margin> two tones and a semitone. This kind of melody is called the **diatonic genus** <note>original Greek: διάτονον (diatonon)</note>, either because it proceeds mostly through **tones** <note>original Greek: διὰ τῶν τόνων</note> or because it exhibits a certain majestic, robust, and **vigorous character** <term>eutonon ethos</term> <note>original: εὔτονον ἦθος. Ancient Greeks believed different scales could influence a listener's moral character or "ethos."</note>.
<margin>15</margin> If, however, the voice—having defined the first starting note and moved a semitone higher—reaches the second note, and then again, having spaced another semitone from there, defines a third note; and if, from <margin>20</margin> this point, it attempts to progress melodiously in sequence, it cannot create any other interval <margin>What the Chromatic is; and this also belongs to the fourth.</margin> except the remainder of the first tetrachord: the **uncomposite trihemitone** <term>trihemitoniaion asyntheton</term> <note>original: τριημιτονιαῖον ἀσύνθετον. An interval equal to three semitones (a minor third), but played as a single leap rather than divided into smaller steps.</note>. Nor can it define any other note...

->### ON THE ENHARMONIC GENUS <note>original Latin: DE GENERE ENARMONIO</note><-

...or the note encompassing the first tetrachord toward the higher pitch, sounding in consonance with the lowest note at the interval of a **fourth** <note>original Greek: διὰ τεσσάρων (dia tessarōn)</note>; so that such a melody is formed by a semitone, a semitone, and an uncomposite **trisemitone** <note>original Greek: τριημιτόνιον ἀσύνθετον (triēmitonion asyntheton); an interval of three semitones, equivalent to a minor third, played as a single leap.</note>. This genus of melody is again called <margin>5</margin> **chromatic** <term>chromatikon</term> <note>original: χρωματικὸν; meaning "colored."</note> because it has been diverted and altered from the former genus to exhibit a more mournful and **emotional character** <term>pathetikoteron ethos</term> <note>original: παθητικώτερον ἦθος. Ancient Greeks believed the chromatic scale was more expressive of sorrow or passion than the stable Diatonic.</note>.

There is also a third genus of melody called **enharmonic** <term>enarmonion</term>, <note>marginal note in manuscript A: "What the enharmonic is."</note> which occurs when the voice, proceeding from the lowest note through a **diesis** <term>diesis</term>, another <margin>10</margin> diesis, and a **ditone** <note>original: δίτονον (ditonon); an interval of two whole tones, similar to a major third.</note>, melodizes the tetrachord. Those following **Aristoxenus** <note>Aristoxenus of Tarentum (4th century BCE), a music theorist who argued that musical intervals should be judged by the ear rather than by mathematical ratios alone.</note> call the **diesis** <note>marginal note in manuscript A: "What is a diesis?"</note> the smallest interval, being a quarter of a tone, or half of a semitone, as the smallest interval that can be sung; whereas the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras who defined musical intervals through precise mathematical ratios of string lengths.</note> call what is now called a semitone a "diesis." <margin>15</margin> Aristoxenus says that this aforementioned genus is called **harmony** <note>original Greek: ἁρμονία (harmonia)</note> because it is the most excellent, having earned the [distinction] of all things harmonized...

At line 11, the text references Aristoxenus’s "Harmonic Elements" p. 30 and 66.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical genera from page 55. The text completes a sentence regarding the Enharmonic genus, comparing its difficulty with the natural simplicity of the Diatonic genus, before transitioning into the mathematical ratios discovered by Pythagoras.</meta>

->### ON INVENTION <note>original Latin: DE INVENTIONE</note><-

...category. It is the most difficult to sing and, as **that man** <note>original: ἐκεῖνος (ekeinos). Refers to the theorist Aristoxenus, mentioned on the previous page.</note> says, it is highly technical and requires great practice, which is why it does not easily come into common use. The **diatonic genus** <term>diatonon genos</term>, however, is something simple and noble, and more in accordance with nature; for this reason, <margin>5</margin> **Plato** adopts this one more readily <note>Plato uses the Diatonic scale in the *Timaeus* to describe the soul of the world.</note>.

| Semitone | Whole Tone | Whole Tone | **Diatonic** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Semitone | Semitone | Trisemitone | **Chromatic** |
| Diesis | Diesis | Ditone | **Harmonic** <note>Also known as the Enharmonic genus.</note> |

<margin>10</margin> **Pythagoras** <note>The famous 6th-century BCE philosopher and mathematician.</note> is thought to have been the first to discover the consonant notes in their ratios to one another: the **fourth** <note>original: διὰ τεσσάρων (dia tessarōn)</note> in a **four-to-three ratio** <term>epitritos</term>; the **fifth** <note>original: διὰ πέντε (dia pente)</note> in a **three-to-two ratio** <term>hēmiolios</term>; and the **octave** <note>original: διὰ πασῶν (dia pasōn)</note> in a **double ratio** <term>diplasios</term>, or two-to-one. He also identified the **octave-plus-fourth** in a ratio of eight to three, which is a **multiple-superparticular** ratio <term>pollaplasiepimerēs</term> <note>A complex ratio where one number contains the other multiple times plus some fraction; specifically here, $8:3$ is $2 + 2/3$.</note>, for it is <margin>15</margin> double and two-thirds. The **octave-plus-fifth** he found in a **triple ratio** <note>3:1</note>, and the **double octave** in a **quadruple ratio** <note>4:1</note>. Among the other tuned intervals, he found those containing the **whole tone** in a **nine-to-eight ratio** <term>epogdoos</term>, and those containing what is now called a **semitone**—but was then called a **diesis**—in a ratio of number to num- <note>The text breaks off here; the "ratio of number to number" refers to the complex Pythagorean limma, 256:243.</note>

<summary>This page explains why the Diatonic genus is preferred over the complex Enharmonic genus and lists the mathematical ratios for musical consonances attributed to Pythagoras.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Plato, music theory, Diatonic, Chromatic, Enharmonic, musical ratios, harmonics, fourth, fifth, octave</keywords>

# OF CONSONANCES <note>original Latin: CONSONANTIARUM</note>

...the ratio of 216 to 248 <note>original Greek: συϛ΄ πρὸς σμη΄. While these specific numbers are recorded in the transcription, they likely relate to the complex multiple-superparticular ratios discussed on the previous page, such as the 256:243 ratio (the Pythagorean "limma" or semitone).</note>, having examined the ratios through the length and the thickness of the strings, and further, through the tension produced by the turning of the **tuning pegs** <term>kollaboi</term> or, more familiarly, by the hanging of weights. <margin>5</margin> He also tested this on **wind instruments** <note>original: ἐμπνευστῶν (empneustōn), literally "blown instruments" like flutes or pipes.</note> both through the width of their hollow cavities and by the increasing and relaxing of the breath, or even by means of volumes and weights, such as metal disks or vessels. For whichever of these methods is chosen according to one of the stated ratios, provided the other factors are **equal** <note>The text emphasizes the "all else being equal" (ceteris paribus) scientific principle.</note>, it will produce the <margin>10</margin> **consonance** <term>symphonia</term> corresponding to that ratio.

->**GEOMETRIC PROOF OF MUSICAL INTERVALS**<-

(8) —————— **Octave** <note>2:1 ratio</note> —————— (4) ——— **Fourth** <note>4:3 ratio</note> ——— (3)
(6) —————— **Octave** <note>2:1 ratio</note> —————— (3) ——— **Fifth** <note>3:2 ratio</note> ——— (2)
 \________________________________________________/ \____________________________/
             **Octave plus Fourth** (8 to 3)              **Octave plus Fifth** (6 to 2)

(8) —————— **Octave** <note>2:1 ratio</note> —————— (4) —————— **Octave** <note>2:1 ratio</note> —————— (2)
 \______________________________________________________________________________/
                           **Double Octave** <note>4:1 ratio</note>

<note>The numbers in the diagram represent string lengths or weights. In the Pythagorean system, 8:4 and 6:3 represent the 2:1 ratio of the Octave. The Fourth is 4:3, and the Fifth is 3:2. The Double Octave is a 4:1 ratio (8:2).</note>

Let it suffice for us at present to demonstrate this using the length of strings on the so-called **kanon** <note>The "kanon" or monochord was a single-stringed scientific instrument used to mark and measure musical intervals mathematically.</note>. For when the single string upon this instrument is measured out into four equal parts, the sound produced by the whole string, compared to the sound produced by three parts... <note>This begins the proof of the Fourth, which has a 4:3 ratio.</note>

# ON DISCOVERY <note>original Latin: DE INVENTIONE</note>

<margin>1</margin> ...of **parts** <note>original: μερῶν (merōn). A scholarly note suggests a variant reading "measures" in another manuscript.</note>, when it exists in a **sesquitertian ratio** <term>epitritos</term> <note>A ratio of 4:3, where one value is 1 and 1/3 times the other.</note>, it will sound the consonance of a **fourth** <term>dia tessaron</term>. And the sound from two parts—that is, from the **half**—when it exists in a **double ratio** <term>diplasios</term> <note>A 2:1 ratio.</note>, it will sound the **octave** <term>dia pason</term>. When it comes from the fourth part, existing in a 

->**HARMONIC RATIO DIAGRAM**<-

| Ratio | Interval Name | Numerical Proportion |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| From 2 to 3 | **Fifth** <term>dia pente</term> | 3:2 ratio |
| From 3 to 6 | **Octave** <term>dia pason</term> | 6:3 (2:1) ratio |
| From 6 to 8 | **Fourth** <term>dia tessaron</term> | 8:6 (4:3) ratio |

| From 3 to 8 | **Octave plus Fourth** | 8:3 ratio |

<margin>5</margin> **quadruple ratio** <term>tetraplasios</term> <note>A 4:1 ratio.</note>, it will sound the **double octave** <term>dis dia pason</term>. Furthermore, the sound from the three parts compared to that from the two parts, existing in a **sesquialter ratio** <term>hemiolios</term> <note>A ratio of 3:2, where one value is 1 and 1/2 times the other.</note>, will sound the **fifth** <term>dia pente</term>. When compared to the sound from the fourth part, existing in a **triple ratio** <term>triplasios</term> <note>A 3:1 ratio.</note>, it will sound the **octave plus a fifth** <note>original: διὰ πασῶν καὶ διὰ πέντε.</note>. If the
<margin>10</margin> string is divided into nine sections, the sound of the whole string compared to that of the eight parts in a **sesquioctave ratio** <term>epogdoos</term> <note>A ratio of 9:8.</note> will encompass the interval of a **tone** <term>toniaion</term>.

The **Tetraktys** <term>tetraktys</term> <note>The "fourness," a sacred Pythagorean symbol representing the first four numbers (1, 2, 3, 4) which sum to 10 and define all primary musical harmonies.</note> encompasses all of these consonances. For it is composed of 1, 2, 3, and 4. Within
<margin>15</margin> these numbers, there exists the consonance of the **fourth**...

# OF CONSONANCES <note>original Latin: CONSONANTIARUM</note>

...consonance, as well as the **fifth** <term>dia pente</term> and the **octave** <term>dia pason</term>, along with the **sesquitertian** <term>epitritos</term> <note>A 4:3 ratio.</note>, **sesquialter** <term>hemiolios</term> <note>A 3:2 ratio.</note>, **double** <note>2:1</note>, **triple** <note>3:1</note>, and **quadruple** <note>4:1</note> ratios.

Some thinkers believed these consonances should be understood
<margin>5</margin> through **weights**, others through **lengths**, others through **motions** [and numbers], and others through **vessels** [and lengths/sizes].

**Lasus of Hermione** <note>A 6th-century BCE Greek lyric poet and music theorist, said to be the teacher of Pindar.</note>, as they say, and those associated with **Hippasus of Metapontum** <note>A Pythagorean philosopher who is often credited with discovering the mathematical nature of musical harmony.</note>, a Pythagorean man, believed that the speeds and slowness of **motions** coincided [with the pitches] through which the
<margin>10</margin> consonances . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . <note>The text here is fragmentary or contains a lacuna in the original manuscript.</note>

Guided by numbers, he applied such ratios to **vessels**. For when all the vessels were equal and identical, he left one empty and <insert>filled</insert> the other half-way with liquid; he then struck each one, and to him the consonance of the **octave** <term>dia pason</term> was
<margin>15</margin> produced. Again, leaving one of the vessels empty, he poured one part out of four <note>i.e., filled it 1/4 full</note> into the other, and when he struck it, the consonance of the **fourth** <term>dia tessaron</term> was produced. The **fifth** <term>dia pente</term> was produced <insert>when</insert> he filled one part out of three. In these cases, the ratio of the empty space <note>original: κενώσεως (kenōseōs). The pitch in a vessel is determined by the volume of air, or "emptiness," remaining.</note> of one to the other was,
<margin>20</margin> for the **octave**, as 2 to 1; for the **fifth**, as 3 to 2; and for the **fourth**, as 4 to 3. These same ratios are observed through the divisions of strings, as was said before, but not on a single string—as is the case with the **monochord** <term>kanon</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used by ancient theorists to measure mathematical intervals.</note>—but on two strings. For having tuned two strings to the same pitch, whenever
<margin>25</margin> he divided one of them by pressing it at the midpoint, the half-length...

## ON DISCOVERY <note>original Latin: DE INVENTIONE</note>

...he produced the other consonance, the **octave** <term>dia pason</term>.
But when he took away a third part, he made the remaining parts
relate to the other consonance, the **fifth** <term>dia pente</term> <note>A ratio of 3:2, where one string length is 1.5 times the other.</note>.
He did the same for the **fourth** <term>dia tessaron</term> <note>A ratio of 4:3.</note>; for on this
<margin>5</margin> single string, having taken away a fourth part, he joined the remaining
parts to the other. He also did this on the **panpipes** <term>syrinx</term>
according to the same ratio. Some theorists derived the
consonances from **weights**, hanging weights from two strings
according to the aforementioned ratios; while others derived them
<margin>10</margin> from **lengths**, and... <unclear>having taken different parts</unclear> ...of the strings,
they demonstrated the consonances within those strings.

. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .

->**WHAT IS A NOTE?**<-

A **note** <term>phthongos</term> is the falling of the voice upon a single pitch <term>tasis</term> <note>This definition is famously attributed to Aristoxenus, a student of Aristotle and a foundational music theorist.</note>.
For they say that the note must be consistent with itself and
<margin>15</margin> indivisible in its difference, not composed of different pitches
such as a mixture of low and high. Among voices, some are
high-pitched <term>oxeiai</term> and some are low-pitched <term>bareiai</term>; thus it is
with notes, of which the high is fast and the low is slow.
If someone were to blow into two **pipes** <term>auloi</term> <note>A double-reed wind instrument, often compared to an oboe or clarinet.</note> of equal thickness
<margin>20</margin> and equal hollowness, bored in the manner of a **panpipe** <term>syrinx</term>,
where the length of one is double the length of the other, the breath
from the half-length is reflected back using double the speed,
and there arises the consonance of the **octave** <term>dia pason</term> between
the low note produced by the larger pipe and the high note produced by the smaller.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical acoustics, transitioning from the discovery of intervals on strings and panpipes to their manifestation in the holes of flutes. # OF CONSONANCES <note>original Latin: CONSONANTIARUM</note>

...the smaller [pipe]. The cause is the speed and the slowness of the movement. They also produced consonances on a single instrument according to the distances of the finger-holes in the pipes. For when the whole pipe is divided into two parts and blown, the hole at the halfway point <margin>5</margin> completes the **octave** consonance <term>dia pason</term>. When it is divided into three parts, with two parts toward the reed-mouthpiece and one below, and the whole is blown through the two, the **fifth** consonance <term>dia pente</term> is produced. When the division is into four parts, with <margin>10</margin> three above and one below, and the three are blown through the whole, the **fourth** <term>dia tessaron</term> is produced.

The followers of **Eudoxus** and **Archytas** <note>Eudoxus of Cnidus and Archytas of Tarentum were famous 4th-century BC mathematicians and friends of Plato who sought to define music through geometry and mechanics.</note> thought that the ratio of consonances existed in numbers; they also agreed that these ratios exist in movements. They held that a fast movement is high-pitched <term>oxeia</term> insofar as it strikes <margin>15</margin> the air continuously and punctures it more swiftly, while a slow movement is low-pitched <term>bareia</term> insofar as it is more sluggish.

This concludes the discussion on the discovery of the consonances; let us return to the teachings handed down by **Adrastus** <note>Adrastus of Aphrodisias was a 2nd-century AD philosopher who wrote extensively on music theory and Aristotle’s work.</note>. He says that while these instruments were <margin>20</margin> prepared according to mathematical ratios for the discovery of the consonances, sensory perception bears witness to them, and once perception is grasped, mathematical reason fits it. How the notes encompassing the so-called **semitone** <term>hemitonion</term> relate to one another in the ratio of **256 to 243** <margin>25</margin> <note>original Greek: σνϛ΄ πρὸς σμγ΄. This specific ratio, known as the Pythagorean limma, is slightly smaller than a modern equal-tempered semitone.</note> will be discussed a little later.

# ON RATIOS <note>original: DE RATIONIBUS</note>

...it will become clear. It is evident that both the compositions and the divisions of the **consonances** <term>symphōniai</term> are observed to be consistent and in harmony with the compositions and divisions of the ratios corresponding to them, which we indicated previously. 

<margin>1</margin> <note>margin note: Concerning the compositions and divisions of consonances expressed in ratios.</note>

For example, since the **octave** <term>dia pasōn</term> is composed of the **fifth** <term>dia pente</term> and the **fourth** <term>dia tessarōn</term>, and is divided back into these, and since the ratio of the octave is **double** <term>diplasios</term> [2:1], that of the fourth is **sesquitertial** <term>epitritos</term> [4:3], and that of the fifth is **sesquialter** <term>hēmiolios</term> [3:2], it is apparent [that] the double ratio is also composed of the sesquitertial and the sesquialter and is divided into them. 

<margin>10</margin> For example, 8 is the sesquitertial of 6, and 12 is the sesquialter of 8; thus 12 becomes the double of 6.
->6 : 8 : 12<-
Conversely, the double ratio of 12 to 6 is divided into the sesquitertial ratio of 12 to 9 and the sesquialter ratio of 9 to 6.

<margin>15</margin> Furthermore, since the fifth exceeds the fourth by a **whole tone** <term>tonos</term>—for the fifth consists of three tones and a semitone, <note>The text here has a slight gap or error in the manuscript; it should ideally specify that the fourth consists of two tones and a semitone.</note> and the tone exists in a **sesquioctave** ratio <term>epogdoos</term> [9:8]—it appears that the sesquialter [3:2] also exceeds the sesquitertial [4:3] by a sesquioctave [9:8]. 

<margin>20</margin> For if the sesquitertial ratio of 8 to 6 is subtracted from a sesquialter ratio such as 9 to 6, the remaining ratio is the sesquioctave of 9 to 8. <note>In ancient mathematics, "subtracting" ratios is equivalent to what we call division: (3/2) ÷ (4/3) = 9/8.</note> And again, if a sesquitertial ratio is added to this [9:8] ratio...

<margin>18</margin> <note>marginal note: If the ratio of 9 to 6 were reduced by the ratio of 8 to 6: the third of 6 is 2; the smaller term is therefore increased by two, and the sesquioctave ratio of 9 to 8 will be produced.</note>

<summary>This page provides a mathematical demonstration of musical intervals, proving that an octave (2:1) is the sum of a fifth (3:2) and a fourth (4:3), and that the difference between a fifth and a fourth is a whole tone (9:8).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of harmonic theory, likely following the work of Adrastus of Aphrodisias. While the previous page established the primary ratios for the fourth, fifth, and octave, this page demonstrates how these ratios are mathematically multiplied and combined to form larger musical intervals, concluding with a reference to Plato's cosmological music theory.</meta>

# OF CONSONANCES <note>original Latin header: CONSONANTIARUM</note>

...the ratio of 12 to 9 <margin>1</margin> <note>original margin note: which is the value 3, by which 12 exceeds 9; if this is added to 9, it becomes 12, which is the sesquialter [ratio of 12 to 8].</note> completes the **sesquialter** <term>hēmiolios</term> ratio [3:2] relative to the ratio of 12 to 8. And indeed, since the **octave** <term>dia pasōn</term> is in a **double** <term>diplasios</term> ratio [2:1], and the **fourth** <term>dia tessarōn</term> is in a **sesquitertial** <term>epitritos</term> ratio [4:3], the interval resulting from both together exists in the ratio of 8 to 3; for the sesquitertial of 3 is 4, and the double of 4 is 8. 

Furthermore, the **octave plus a fifth** <margin>5</margin> is in a **triple** <term>triplasios</term> ratio [3:1]; for the sesquialter and the double ratios, when combined, produce this result. For example, the ratio of 9 to 6 is sesquialter, while the ratio of 18 to 9 is double; thus the ratio of 18 to 6 becomes triple. Similarly, the **double octave** <term>dis dia pasōn</term> exists in a **quadruple** <term>tetraplasios</term> ratio [4:1]; <margin>10</margin> for this is composed of two double ratios. For the double of 6 is 12, and the double of 12 is 24, and these 24 are the quadruple of the original 6. 

Or rather, as we showed at the beginning, a triple ratio combined with a sesquitertial ratio produces a quadruple one. The ratio of the **octave plus a fifth** is triple, <margin>15</margin> and that of the **fourth** is sesquitertial; the double octave consists of both of these intervals. It is reasonable, then, that this interval appears in a quadruple ratio; for the triple of 6 is 18, and the sesquitertial of 18 is 24, which is the quadruple of 6. And again, the sesquitertial of 6 is 8, <margin>20</margin> and the triple of 8 is 24, which is the quadruple of 6. Those intervals composed from these will be found in these same ratios, however far we may extend the systems of scales.

**Plato** <margin>25</margin> <note>Specifically referring to the *Timaeus*, 35b-36b, where Plato describes the mathematical proportions used by the Demiurge to craft the World Soul.</note> establishes both the **diatonic genus** <term>genos diatonon</term> and the magnitude of the scale reaching to **four octaves and a fifth** and...

<summary>This page provides a mathematical proof of musical consonances, explaining how ratios like the double (octave) and sesquialter (fifth) combine to form complex intervals like the double octave (quadruple ratio). It concludes by connecting these harmonic principles to Plato's Timaeus.</summary>
<keywords>harmonic theory, Pythagorean ratios, octave, fifth, fourth, sesquialter, sesquitertial, Plato, Timaeus, musical mathematics</keywords>

# ON THE NUMBERS OF THE WORLD-SOUL IN PLATO <note>original Latin header: DE NUMERIS ANIMI MUNDANI AP. PLAT.</note>

->The first figure is repeated twice with slight differences by a second or more recent hand<-

| **12 — 8 — 6** | **8 — 6 — 4 — 3** |
| :--- | :--- |
| <gloss>Inner arc 1: The **fifth** [is] **sesquialter** [3:2]</gloss> | <gloss>Inner arc 1: **Octave** [2:1]</gloss> |
| <gloss>Inner arc 2: The **fourth** [is] **sesquitertial** [4:3]</gloss> | <gloss>Inner arc 2: **Fourth** [4:3]</gloss> |
| <gloss>Outer arc: The **octave** [2:1]</gloss> | <gloss>Outer arc: The **octave and a fourth** [8:3]</gloss> |
| **Double** | **Double and two-thirds** |

| **18 — 9 — 6** | **24 — 12 — 6** |
| :--- | :--- |
| <gloss>Inner arc 1: **Octave** [2:1]</gloss> | <gloss>Inner arc 1: **Octave** [2:1]</gloss> |
| <gloss>Inner arc 2: **Fifth** [3:2]</gloss> | <gloss>Inner arc 2: **Octave** [2:1]</gloss> |
| <gloss>Outer arc: The **octave and a fifth** [3:1]</gloss> | <gloss>Outer arc: The **double octave** [4:1]</gloss> |
| **Triple** | **Quadruple** |

...has brought forward a tone. But if someone should say, as **Adrastus** <note>Adrastus of Aphrodisias, a 2nd-century Peripatetic philosopher who wrote on Plato's Timaeus and music theory.</note> says, that it is not necessary to extend [the musical system] to such a great extent—for **Aristoxenus** <note>A pupil of Aristotle and a highly influential music theorist who favored the judgment of the ear over strict mathematical ratios.</note> constructed the magnitude of his own complex <term>polytropos</term> diagram <note>The "diagram" refers to the "System" or scale structure used to organize musical pitches.</note> up to the **double octave plus a fourth**—while the <margin>5</margin> more modern theorists consider the **fifteen-stringed** <term>pentekaidekachordon</term> <note>This refers to the "Greater Perfect System," the standard two-octave range of Greek music theory.</note> scale as the greatest, extending as far as three octaves and a tone—it must be said, he says, that those earlier theorists acted this way with a view toward our practical use. They did so because they believed that neither could those competing in musical performance utter sounds beyond these, nor [could the human ear distinguish them].

<meta>This page transitions from the historical debate between Adrastus and Aristoxenus regarding the limits of the musical scale to a Platonic explanation of why the World-Soul’s harmony is structured through specific mathematical ratios, and how these ratios relate to the physical properties of sound and pitch.</meta>

# WHY A LARGER NUMBER IS ASSIGNED TO A LOWER SOUND <note>original Latin: "CUR MAIOR NUM. GRAVIORI SONO ATTRIBUATUR."</note>

...[allowing] those listening to judge with good sense. But **Plato**, looking toward nature—since it is necessary for the Soul, being constituted according to harmony, to advance as far as solid numbers <note>In Pythagorean mathematics, "solid numbers" refer to three-dimensional values (like cubes), representing the physical, three-dimensional world.</note> and to be joined by two means <note>Plato uses the "arithmetic" and "harmonic" means in the *Timaeus* to bridge the gaps between numerical intervals.</note>—did this so that by passing through the whole of the perfect solid cosmic body <note>The physical universe as a whole.</note>, it would become capable of perceiving all existing things. He has extended its harmony up to this point, which in a certain way, according to its own nature, is able to proceed toward infinity.

He says that it is also appropriate to assign the larger numbers to the lower-pitched <term>grave</term> <note>From the Greek *barys*, meaning heavy or low in pitch.</note> sounds, even if in some cases of tension it seems to be inconsistent, such as in the tension produced by the hanging of weights. For if there are two strings equal in both length and thickness and similar in all other respects, the greater weight will produce a higher-pitched <term>acute</term> <note>From the Greek *oxys*, meaning sharp or high in pitch.</note> sound because of the greater tension. 

For since the greater weight produces greater tension, it adds more external power to the higher sound associated with it, which consequently has less of its own internal strength relative to the hanging weight. It is clear that, conversely, the lower sound—possessing more of its own innate power than the external weight—is sufficient to preserve its own harmony and concord. Therefore, the larger number should be assigned to the greater [internal] power. Other factors also agree with these points; for, again, lengths and thicknesses [contribute to] difficulty of movement...

# WHY A LARGER NUMBER IS ASSIGNED TO A LOWER SOUND <note>original Latin: "CUR MAIOR NUM. GRAVIORI SONO ATTRIBUATUR."</note>

...[the lack of tension] applied to the strings produces a weakness, so that they do not move easily, nor strike and shape the surrounding air more quickly, as the air is greater in volume. It is clear, then, that the <term>lower sounds</term> <note>original Greek: φθόγγοι (phthongoi). In ancient music theory, a phthongos is a sound of a single definite pitch.</note> possess their own proper power according to the larger number. One can find similar results in the case of wind instruments as well. For among the sounds produced in these, the lower ones—because they shape more air due to the length and width of the finger-holes, or, by Zeus, because of the relaxation of the breath as in the case of a <term>trumpet</term> <note>original Greek: σάλπιγγος (salpinx), a straight metal trumpet used for military signals.</note> <margin>5</margin> or the <term>windpipe</term> <note>original Greek: ἀρτηρίας (artērias). Ancient Greeks used this term for the trachea or "rough artery."</note>—becoming more relaxed and weaker, naturally possess their own proper power in a larger quantity.

The most sovereign of all intervals, he says, is the **fourth** <note>original Greek: διὰ τεσσάρων (dia tessarōn), literally "through four."</note>; for from this, the rest are discovered. <margin>10</margin> The **fifth** <note>original Greek: διὰ πέντε (dia pente), literally "through five."</note> differs from the fourth by a **tone**. Indeed, they define the <term>tone</term> <note>original Greek: τόνος (tonos). In this context, it is the mathematical difference between the two primary consonances.</note> in this way: the interval by which the fifth exceeds the fourth. <margin>15</margin> Furthermore, the **octave** <note>original Greek: διὰ πασῶν (dia pasōn), literally "through all."</note> is found from the combination of the fourth and the fifth; for it is composed of the fourth and the fifth together.

The ancients took the tone as the first interval of the voice, and they did not recognize the semitone or the <term>diesis</term> <note>original Greek: δίεσιν. In early Greek theory, a diesis usually refers to a small interval, often a quarter-tone or the "leakage" remaining after calculating larger ratios.</note>. <margin>20</margin> The tone was found in the <term>sesquioctave ratio</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπογδόῳ λόγῳ (epogdoō logō), the ratio of 9:8.</note> in the construction of bronze discs, vessels, strings, flutes, suspended weights, and many other things; for the ratio of 8 to 9 resulted in the hearing of a tonal interval. It is for this reason...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals from the previous page, shifting focus from the whole tone to the smaller "remainder" or semitone. It explores the conflict between the mathematical school of Plato and the empirical school of Aristoxenus regarding how these intervals are measured.</meta>

# ON THE REMAINDER <note>original Latin: DE LIMMATE. The "limma" or "remainder" is the Pythagorean version of a semitone, calculated as the mathematical leftover when two whole tones are subtracted from a perfect fourth.</note>

The first interval is the **tone**, because the voice, in descending as far as this point, keeps the hearing free from error. <margin>Different opinions on how many tones are in a fourth.</margin> But regarding the interval after this, the ear is no longer able to grasp it with accuracy. Indeed, concerning the next interval, which is called the **semitone**, they disagree: some call it a "perfect semitone," while others call it a **remainder** <note>original Greek: λεῖμμα (leimma). In Pythagorean tuning, this "remainder" is slightly smaller than a true mathematical half-tone.</note>.

The **fourth** <note>original Greek: διὰ τεσσάρων (dia tessarōn). This is the "consonance of the four," a perfect fourth.</note>—which is a <term>four-to-three ratio</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπίτριτον (epitriton). A ratio of 4:3, such as the length of strings required to produce a perfect fourth.</note>—is filled up by the tone (that is, the <term>nine-to-eight ratio</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπογδόῳ (epogdoō). A ratio of 9:8, the mathematical definition of a whole tone.</note> interval) in the following way. For it is agreed by everyone that the fourth is larger than two tones, but smaller than three tones.

However, **Aristoxenus** <note>Aristoxenus of Tarentum (4th century BCE) was a music theorist who argued that musical intervals should be judged by the ear rather than strictly by mathematical ratios.</note> says that it is composed of exactly two and a half tones. But **Plato** says it consists of two tones and the so-called **remainder**. <margin>What is a remainder?</margin> He says that this remainder cannot be named by a single term, but exists in a ratio of number to number, specifically the ratio that **256** has to **243** <note>The OCR reads "σνζʹ" (257), but the mathematical context and the "difference of 13" mentioned immediately after confirm the intended number is 256 (σνϛʹ). 256 minus 243 equals 13.</note>. This is the interval, and the difference between them is 13.

<margin>How the remainder is found.</margin> It is discovered in this way: The number 6 would not be the first term, because it does not have an "eighth" part that could produce a <term>nine-to-eight ratio</term>. <margin>For this is how it works in the fourth: the second note is a nine-to-eight ratio of the first, and the third is a nine-to-eight ratio of the second.</margin> Neither, then, are 6 or 8 sufficient; for even if 8 has 9 as its nine-to-eight ratio, the number 9 itself does not have an eighth part. But one must be able to take a nine-to-eight ratio of a nine-to-eight ratio <note>To calculate two whole tones (9:8 x 9:8), the starting number must be divisible by 8 twice (i.e., divisible by 64).</note>, because the fourth—the four-to-three ratio—is larger than two whole tones. We take, then, the base nine-to-eight ratio of 8 and 9, and the 8...

<summary>This page discusses the mathematical definition of the "limma" (the Pythagorean semitone) and contrasts Aristoxenus's ear-based theory (2.5 tones in a fourth) with Plato's ratio-based calculation (256:243).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of the "remainder" (limma), providing the specific numerical ratios used by Pythagorean theorists to define the intervals of a perfect fourth. # ON THE REMAINDER <note>original Latin: DE LIMMATE. The "limma" is the mathematical leftover when two whole tones are subtracted from a perfect fourth.</note>

...[by multiplying] themselves, we find 64; then 8 times 9 becomes 72; then 9 times itself becomes 81. <margin>The progression of terms: 8, 9, 64, 72, 81.</margin> Then again let each of these be taken three times: 64 times three is 192; 72 times three is 216; and 81 times three is 243. The series then becomes: 8, 9, 64, 72, 81, 192, 216, 243.

Next, we add to the 243 a <term>sesquitertian</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπίτριτον (epitriton). A ratio of 4:3, which defines the perfect fourth.</note> of the 192, which is 256. This results in the following arrangement:

*   The primary <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπόγδοος (epogdoos). A ratio of 9:8, which defines a whole tone.</note> root is 9 and 8.
*   The secondary sesquioctaves are 64, 72, and 81.
*   The third set of sesquioctaves relative to each other are 192, 216, and 243.

Let the sesquitertian of 192—which is 256—also be set down. This sesquitertian interval <note>the perfect fourth spanning from 192 to 256</note> will then be perfectly filled up by two whole tones and the aforementioned **remainder**. Some, however, take 384 as the first term.

| Ratio | Calculation | Result |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Whole Tone** | 192 to 216 | 9:8 ratio |
| **Whole Tone** | 216 to 243 | 9:8 ratio |
| **Remainder** | 243 to 256 | The "Limma" |
| **Perfect Fourth** | 192 to 256 | 4:3 ratio |

<image-desc>A mathematical and musical diagram illustrating the division of a perfect fourth (the "dia tessarōn"). Four numbers are listed horizontally: 192 (ρϟβ΄), 216 (σις΄), 243 (σμγ΄), and 256 (σνς΄). Arcs connect 192 to 216 and 216 to 243, both labeled "sesquioctave" (whole tone). A third arc connects 243 to 256, labeled "remainder" (limma). A large semi-circle spans from the first number 192 to the last number 256, labeled "sesquitertian" (perfect fourth) and "through four" (dia tessarōn).</image-desc>

For in order to obtain two whole tones, they multiply the first term, 6, by eight to make 48, and these again...

---

<margin>Note on line 6: That is, after 243, we add another number, 256, which is the 4:3 ratio of 192.</margin>

<margin>Note on line 8: Since there are two consecutive whole tones in the sequence 64, 72, 81, and since 64 does not have a 4:3 ratio in whole numbers (which would be needed after 81 to complete the fourth), we naturally bypassed those smaller numbers and took the next set: 192, 216, and 243. This is because while 216 is the 9:8 ratio of 192, the number 64 cannot be divided by three to create a whole-number ratio.</margin>

It explains why large numbers are necessary to ensure all intervals can be expressed as whole-number ratios.</summary>

**Remainder** (λεῖμμα / limma): The small interval left over in a scale.
**Sesquitertian** (ἐπίτριτος / epitritos): A ratio of 4:3, a perfect fourth.

**Interval** (διάστημα / diastēma): The distance between two pitches.
**Semitone** (ἡμιτόνιον / hēmitonion): Half of a whole tone.
**Ratio** (λόγος / logos): The mathematical relationship between two numbers.
**Plato** (Πλάτων): The philosopher who described these musical ratios in his dialogue *Timaeus*.

# ON DIVISION <note>original Latin: DE DIVISIONE</note>

...but judging these matters by their hearing, they believe so. Certainly, in the base **sesquioctave** <note>original Greek: ἐπογδόου πυθμένος. In Pythagorean music theory, the "base" is the simplest whole-number expression of a ratio, here 9:8.</note> ratio—that is, the relationship of 9 to 8—the interval is a **unit** <note>original Greek: μονὰς (monas). The difference between 9 and 8 is 1.</note>, and a unit cannot be divided. Now, if someone were to ask what the so-called **remainder** <note>original Greek: λεῖμμα (leimma). Often spelled "limma," this is the small "leftover" interval in a scale.</note> is a remainder of, <margin>5</margin> one must know that it belongs to the **fourth** <note>original Greek: διὰ τεσσάρων (dia tessarōn). The musical interval of a perfect fourth.</note>. For the fourth falls short of being two and a half perfect **tones** <note>original Greek: τόνων (tonōn). A tone is the 9:8 ratio.</note>.

The tone was discovered in this way: Since the **fourth** was shown to be in a **sesquitertian** <note>original Greek: ἐπιτρίτῳ (epitritō). A 4:3 ratio.</note> ratio, and the **fifth** <note>original Greek: διὰ πέντε (dia pente). The musical interval of a perfect fifth, a 3:2 ratio.</note> in a **sesquialter** <note>original Greek: ἡμιολίῳ (hēmioliō). A 3:2 ratio.</note> ratio, a first number was taken that possesses both a half and a third: <margin>10</margin> this number is 6. A sesquitertian of this is 8, and a sesquialter is 9. Thus: 6, 8, 9. The interval from the sesquialter [9] to the sesquitertian [8] was found to be in a **sesquioctave** ratio; for 9 is one-eighth more than 8. This "tension" <note>original Greek: τάσις (tasis). This refers to the pitch or musical tension of the string.</note> was called a **tone**.

It is clear that the tone cannot be divided exactly in half for the following reason: <margin>15</margin> First, the base sesquioctave ratio has an interval of a single unit [9-8=1], which is indivisible. Furthermore, in terms of number, a sesquioctave interval is not always cut into equal parts. For example, in the case of 243 compared to 216, the **excess** <note>original Greek: ὑπεροχή (hyperochē). The mathematical difference between two numbers.</note> is 27, which cannot be divided into equal [whole] parts, but rather into 13 and 14; <margin>16</margin> <gloss>A note in the margin points out that while the difference between 72 and 64 is 8 (which can be split), the difference between 81 and 72 is 9, which cannot be split into equal whole numbers.</gloss> for the unit is not divided. <meta>This page continues the discussion of musical ratios and the mathematical impossibility of dividing certain intervals. While the previous page focused on the "limma" (the remainder of a perfect fourth), this page addresses the physical and conceptual difficulties of dividing a musical "tone" (the 9:8 ratio) into two equal halves.</meta>

# ON THE TONE <note>original Greek: TONI. In this context, it refers to the interval of a whole step, or the 9:8 ratio.</note>

A **tone** is understood in one way by thought, in another by numbers, in another by spatial intervals, and in another through hearing in voices. Neither the tone expressed in **numbers** is ever cut into equal parts—as has already been demonstrated <note>The ratio 9:8 cannot be divided into two equal whole-number ratios (like 3:2 or 4:3), which leads to the discovery of the "semitone" being slightly less than half a tone.</note>—nor is the tone expressed in **sensible** and visible intervals. For <margin>5</margin> on the **rule** <note>original Greek: κανόνος (kanonos). This refers to the monochord, a single-stringed instrument used by ancient theorists to measure musical intervals geometrically.</note>, the **slider** <note>original Greek: ὑποβολεὺς (hypoboleus). The movable bridge placed under the string to change its pitch.</note>, being a physical object, will certainly have some width. It will not be so entirely without thickness that it does not take up some space during the division of the tone, occupying the boundary of the first part and the start of the second. Because of this, some portion of the <margin>10</margin> tone will be "consumed" or lost.

Furthermore, in all divisions, there are three things: the two parts being divided, and the third thing which is the "extracted" space between them. Among things being divided, just as with a **saw** in a cut, a certain amount is consumed by the act of cutting itself. Just as <margin>14</margin> <margin>This contradicts the principle that a given line can be cut exactly in half. For if something is always consumed in every division, then a line is not cut in half, nor is the specific portion truly cut off. The falsehood of this [the author's] claim is clear from experience. For if I break a hair, for example, without any loss occurring, nothing of its length is wasted. The same applies to similar cases.</margin> some portion is removed in certain sensible objects, <margin>15</margin> so it is in all things; even if it escapes our senses, something will certainly be consumed in the cut. For instance, if you measure a **spear** or a **reed** or any other physical length before dividing it, and then divide it into many parts, you will find that the common measure of all the divided parts combined is less <margin>20</margin> than the whole was before it was divided. 

Furthermore, if you divide a **string** and then cut it, the tension "runs back" <note>This refers to the recoil or loss of tension that happens when a taut string is severed.</note> after the cutting; and if you stretch the cut pieces again, it is necessary that some part of the tone has been lost. <margin>22</margin>

<summary>This page argues that a musical tone cannot be perfectly divided in practice because physical tools—like the bridge of a monochord or a saw—occupy space and "consume" a small portion of the material being measured. A marginal note from a later reader disputes this, arguing that breaking something like a hair results in no loss of material.</summary>

# ON THE DIVISION OF THE TONE <note>original Latin: DE DIVISIONE TONI. A "tone" in ancient Greek music theory refers to the interval of a whole step, mathematically defined by the ratio 9:8.</note>

...magnitude into the attachments of the contact points on either side of the <margin>1</margin> string being stretched. <note>original Greek: ἀφῶν (aphon), meaning points of contact or touch. The scribe corrected this from an earlier error.</note> And because of this, there will not be two perfect **semitones**. Moreover, the division of the tone into equal parts is not found even in voices. For having sung a tone and another tone, <margin>5</margin> I sing again the two **semitones** of the single tone using three **pitches** <note>original Greek: φθόγγοις (phthongois), the discrete sounds or notes in a scale.</note> and two intervals, ascending in **tension** <note>original Greek: τάσει (tasei), referring to the tightening of a string or vocal cords to raise pitch.</note>. Now, the third pitch will be higher than the second; it stands at a distance of a full tone from the first, but from the second it appears to be a semitone—yet it is not an identical semitone, <margin>10</margin> nor such as the second is from the first. For it is not possible for the lower [interval] to be identical to the higher. <note>In ancient acoustics, higher pitches were understood to require different "quantities" of motion or string length than lower pitches, meaning a 9:8 ratio cannot be split into two equal 3:2.828... ratios using whole numbers.</note> For even if we wish to sing the same pitch twice, breaking the voice [between them], we will [not] produce the exact same sound; rather, there must be some difference that escapes the notice of our hearing. <margin>15</margin> For it is not possible to prick the same spot twice in an identical and similar way, nor to strike the same string twice in the same manner—it will be either looser or more forceful—nor can one dip twice into the same liquid in the same way, nor having dipped, bring up the same amount [of liquid] on a finger, whether it be ink, or honey, or pitch. But the <margin>20</margin> **tone** grasped by **thought** <note>original Greek: νοήσει ληπτὸς (noesei leptos). The author distinguishes between "sensible" things we hear/touch and "intelligible" things we calculate with the mind.</note> can indeed be conceived as being divided into equal parts.

Next, we must speak concerning **harmony** in **numbers**, stating that a **definition** <note>original Greek: ὅρος (horos), which can mean a mathematical term, a boundary, or a definition.</note> is that which reveals the particular characteristic of each thing spoken of—such as number, magnitude, power, bulk, or weight. **Ratio** <margin>25</margin> <note>original Greek: λόγος (logos). This fundamental term means "word," "reason," and "mathematical ratio" simultaneously.</note>, according to the **Peripatetics** <note>The followers of Aristotle, who studied at the Lyceum in Athens.</note>, is spoken of in many ways: there is the "outward" ratio expressed with the voice by the more recent philosophers...

<summary>This page argues that while a musical tone can be divided equally in mathematical theory, it is physically impossible to do so in practice due to the limitations of human performance, the width of physical tools, and the nature of sound. The author then transitions into a philosophical discussion on the definitions of harmony and ratio as taught by the Peripatetic school.</summary>

<keywords>tone, semitone, pitch, Peripatetics, ratio, acoustics, monochord, harmony, musical measurement, philosophy of sound</keywords>

# WHAT LOGOS SIGNIFIES <note>original Latin: QUID SIGNIFICET ΛΟΓΟΣ. The word "Logos" is one of the most complex in Greek, meaning word, reason, account, or mathematical ratio.</note>

...spoken of as the internal [reason] and that which resides in the mind <note>original: ἐν διανοίᾳ (en dianoia), referring to unspoken thought.</note> without sound or voice; and that of mathematical ratio, according to which this thing is said to have a "ratio" <note>original: λόγον (logon)</note> to that thing; and the explanation of the elements of speech; and that regarding those who honor or <margin>5</margin> are honored, according to which we say a person "takes account" or "does not take account" of someone; and the banker’s account; and the oration found in a book by Demosthenes or Lysias; and the definition signifying the "whatness" and the essence, being definitive; and furthermore, the syllogism, induction, the Libyan tale, the myth, the riddle, <margin>10</margin> and the proverb; and even further, the *logos* of "form" and the "seminal principle" <note>original: σπερματικὸς (spermatikos), a Stoic term for the generative power in nature.</note> and many others. But according to Plato, *logos* is used in four ways: thought without sound, the stream of voice [flowing] from thought, the explanation of the elements of the whole, and that of mathematical ratio. Now, <margin>15</margin> it is proposed to investigate the *logos* of mathematical ratio.

A ratio is the specific relationship of two homogeneous terms to each other according to an analogy, such as "double" or "triple." For Adrastus says it is impossible to know how heterogeneous things relate to one another: for example, a cubit <note>A unit of length.</note> <margin>20</margin> compared to a mina <note>A unit of weight.</note>, or a dry quart <note>original: χοῖνιξ (choinix).</note> compared to a half-pint <note>original: κοτύλην (kotylen).</note>, or white compared to sweet or hot; these are incomparable and cannot be contrasted. But those things that are of the same...

# ON RATIOS <note>original Latin: DE RATIONIBUS</note>

...[to be of the same] genus is possible, such as lengths to lengths, and planes to planes, solids to solids, weights to weights, liquids to liquids, pourable things to pourable things, dry things to dry things, numbers to numbers, time to time, <margin>5</margin> motion to motion, voice to voice, flavor to flavor, color to color, and whatever things, being of the same genus or species, have some relationship to one another. 

We call "terms" <term>horoi; the individual values or objects being compared in a ratio</term> those things of the same genus or species taken for comparison; for example, when we examine what ratio a <margin>10</margin> talent <note>a unit of weight, approximately 26 kilograms</note> has to a mina <note>a smaller unit of weight, 1/60th of a talent</note>, we say the talent and the mina are "homogeneous terms," because the "genus" of both is weight. And the same reasoning <note>original: logos</note> applies to the other examples. Now, an "analogy" <term>analogia; in Greek mathematics, this specifically refers to a "proportion" or an equality of two ratios</term> is a certain relationship of ratios to one another, such as: as 2 is to 1, so 8 is to 4. 

<margin>15</margin> Of ratios, some are greater, some are smaller, and some are equal. Now, the "equal" is one and the same ratio; it precedes all other ratios and is fundamental <note>original: στοιχειώδης (stoicheiodes), meaning acting as a primary element</note>. Those ratios are equal which are examined against one another according to the same quantity, such as 1 to 1, 2 to 2, 10 <margin>20</margin> to 10, and 100 to 100. Of the greater ratios, some are "multiple" <note>where the larger number is a multiple of the smaller, like 2:1</note>, others are "superparticular" <note>original: epimorioi; where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part of it, like 3:2</note>, and others are neither. Likewise, of the smaller ratios, some are "submultiple," others are "sub-superparticular," and others are neither. Of these, some are in consonance <term>symphonia; a musical agreement of sounds</term>, and others are not. Now, the consonances of the...

<summary>This page defines mathematical ratios and proportions, explaining that only "homogeneous" things (like weight to weight or time to time) can be compared. It introduces the classification of ratios into equal, multiple, and superparticular, which forms the basis for musical harmony.</summary>

<keywords>ratio (logos), proportion (analogy), terms (horoi), homogeneity, musical consonance, superparticular, multiple ratios</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ratios (logoi) from the previous page, specifically focusing on how mathematical ratios correspond to musical intervals and consonances in the Pythagorean tradition.</meta>

# ON RATIOS <note>original Latin: DE RATIONIBUS</note>

...Among "multiple" ratios are the double, the triple, and the quadruple; among "superparticular" <term>epimorioi; ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term> are the sesquialter <note>original: hemiolios; a ratio of 3:2</note> and the sesquitertial <note>original: epitritos; a ratio of 4:3</note>. In neither of these categories <margin>5</margin> are the sesquioctave <note>original: epogdoos; a ratio of 9:8</note> and the ratio of 256 to 243, as well as their opposites: the sub-double, the sub-triple, the sub-quadruple, the sub-sesquialter, the sub-sesquitertial, the sub-sesquioctave, and the ratio of 243 to 256. 

The double ratio is found in the "octave" <note>original: dia pason</note> consonance, as was demonstrated above. The triple ratio is found in the <margin>10</margin> "octave plus a fifth" <note>original: dia pason kai dia pente</note>, and the quadruple in the "double octave" <note>original: dis dia pason</note>. The sesquialter (3:2) is found in the "fifth" <note>original: dia pente</note>, and the sesquitertial (4:3) in the "fourth" <note>original: dia tessaron</note>. The sesquioctave (9:8) is a "whole tone" <term>tonos; the standard musical step</term>, and the ratio of 256 to 243 is a "remainder" <term>leimma; the small mathematical gap left over when fitting tones into an interval</term>. The same applies to their opposites. 

The sesquioctave and the ratio of 256 to 243 are in neither category <note>i.e., they are neither multiples nor superparticulars of the primary consonances</note> <margin>15</margin> because they are neither within the primary consonances nor entirely outside of them. For the tone and the remainder are the "principles" <note>original: archai</note> of consonance and the "complements" that fill out the consonances, but they are not yet consonances themselves. 

In arithmetic, certain ratios of numbers are called not only multiples and superparticulars, but also "superpartient" <note>where the larger number contains the smaller plus several parts of it</note> and <margin>20</margin> "multiple-superpartient," and many more, about which we shall provide a clearer account in the following sections. 

The interval of a fourth <note>original: dia tessaron</note> is composed of two tones and a remainder; the fifth <note>original: dia pente</note> is composed of three tones and a remainder; and the octave <note>original: dia pason</note> is composed of a fifth and a fourth. From these are derived the primary <margin>25</margin> proportions <note>original: analogiai</note>.

<summary>This page defines the mathematical ratios that correspond to the fundamental musical intervals of the Greek system, such as the octave, fifth, and fourth. It explains that while tones and "remainders" (semitones) build these harmonies, they are considered the constituent parts rather than complete consonances themselves.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, Pythagorean intervals, octave, fifth, fourth, mathematical ratios, sesquialter, sesquitertial, Adrastus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the classification of mathematical ratios from the previous discussion, moving from the specific musical applications of ratios (like the octave and fifth) to a formal arithmetic categorization cited from the scholar Adrastus.</meta>

### ON THE MULTIPLE RATIO <note>original Latin: DE RATIONE MULTIPLICI</note>

Again, according to the arithmetic tradition, the **ratios** <note>original Greek: logoi; the term means both "ratio" and "reason"</note> of numbers are named—as Adrastus <note>Adrastus of Aphrodisias, a 2nd-century Peripatetic philosopher whose works on music and math were widely cited in antiquity</note> also teaches—as follows: some are **multiples**, some are **superparticulars** <term>epimorioi; ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term>, some are **superpartients** <term>epimereis; ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus several parts of it, such as 5:3</term>, some are **multiple superparticulars**, some are **multiple superpartients**, and some are **neither** <note>The scholar editing this text notes that "neither" is used awkwardly here; see page 80, line 8</note>. Among the smaller terms, there are **sub-multiples**, **sub-superparticulars**, and the rest which correspond inversely to the larger ratios.

<margin>20</margin> **What is a multiple ratio?**

A **multiple** ratio, then, occurs when the larger term contains the smaller term several times; that is, when the larger term is measured by the smaller term exactly, so that nothing is left over from it. Specifically, each multiple ratio is named after the number of times the larger term is measured by the smaller: for example, if it is twice, it is **double**; if three times, **triple**; if four times, **quadruple**, and so on in order. Conversely, the smaller term is called a part of the larger, sharing the same name as the ratio: in the case of the double, it is a **half**; in the case of the triple, it is a **third** <note>original: tritemorion</note>. The ratio is then called "half" or "third," and the same applies to the others.

<margin>21</margin> **What is a superparticular ratio?**

A **superparticular** ratio occurs when the larger term contains the smaller term once, plus one part of the smaller term—that is—

### ON THE SUPERPARTICULAR RATIO <note>original Latin: DE RAT. SUPERPARTICULARI</note>

...is whenever the larger number has this excess over the smaller: an amount which is one part of the smaller number. For instance, the number four compared to three; for it exceeds it by a unit, which is a third part of three. And the number six compared to four <margin>5</margin> exceeds it by two, which is half of four. For this reason, each of the **superparticulars** <term>epimorioi; ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus exactly one part (1/n) of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term> obtained its own specific name from the naming of its parts. For the ratio exceeding the smaller by a half-part is called **hemiolic** <term>hemiolios; literally "a whole and a half," known as the 3:2 ratio or the musical fifth</term>, as <margin>10</margin> three is to two and six is to four. For it contains the whole of the smaller number plus its half. In the case of three, it contains two plus its half, which is the unit; in the case of six, it contains four plus its half, which is the number two. Again, those exceeding by a third part of the smaller are called **epitrites** <margin>15</margin> <term>epitritos; literally "one-and-a-third," the 4:3 ratio or the musical fourth</term>, as four is to three. Those exceeding by a fourth part are called **epitetartoi** <term>the 5:4 ratio</term>, as five is to four and ten is to eight. Proceeding similarly, they are called **epipemptoi** <term>6:5</term>, **ephektoi** <term>7:6</term>, and **ephebdomoi** <term>8:7</term>—all of these being superparticulars. Because of this, the smaller numbers corresponding to these larger ones are called **sub-superparticulars** <margin>20</margin> <term>hypo-epimorioi</term>. Just as three was called the hemiolic of two, so also two will be called, proportionally, the **sub-hemiolic** <note>original Greek: hyphhemiolios</note> of three; and similarly, three is the **sub-epitrite** <note>original Greek: hypoepitritos</note> of four.

There is a ranking among the **multiple** ratios: the first and <margin>25</margin> smallest is the **double**, after this the **triple**, then the **quadruple**, and so on following in order forever.
