### ON THE SUPERPARTIENT RATIO <note>original Latin: DE RAT. SUPERPARTIENTE</note>

...the larger [numbers]. Among the **superparticular** <term>epimorioi; ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus exactly one part of it</term> ratios, the first and greatest is the **hemiolic** <term>hemiolios; the 3:2 ratio</term>, because the half part is indeed the first and greatest [fraction] and is closest to the whole. After this comes the **epitrite** <term>epitritos; the 4:3 ratio</term>, and the **epitetartos** <term>the 5:4 ratio</term>, and thus again <margin>5</margin> the progression goes on to infinity, always approaching smaller [fractions].

A **superpartient** <term>epimerēs; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part of it, such as 5:3</term> ratio occurs whenever the larger term contains the smaller term once and additionally several parts of it [the smaller term], whether these parts are the same and uniform or different and various.
The parts are the same in cases such as two-thirds or two-fifths and any <margin>10</margin> others of this sort. For instance, the number five is to the number three a **twice-epitrite** <note>original Greek: dis epitritos; usually called a super-bi-partient ratio today</note>, while seven is to five a **twice-epipemptos** <note>1 + 2/5</note>, and eight is to five a **thrice-epipemptos** <note>1 + 3/5</note>, and so on similarly.
The parts are different and various in cases such as when the larger term contains <margin>15</margin> the smaller term plus its half and its third—for example, the ratio that eleven has to six <note>11 = 6 + 3 (half) + 2 (third)</note>—or again a half and a fourth, which is the ratio of seven to four <note>7 = 4 + 2 (half) + 1 (fourth)</note>, or by Zeus! a third and a fourth, which is the ratio of nineteen to twelve <note>19 = 12 + 4 (third) + 3 (fourth)</note>.
The remaining superpartient ratios should be considered in a similar way, whether they exceed by two parts, or three, or more, and whether those parts are <margin>20</margin> similar or dissimilar. A **sub-superpartient** <term>hupepimerēs; the inverse of a superpartient ratio, where the smaller is compared to the larger</term> is the reverse: the smaller number in the aforementioned ratio being compared to the larger.

A **multiple-superparticular** <term>pollaplasi-epimorios; a ratio where the larger contains the smaller multiple times plus one extra part, e.g., 2½ or 5:2</term> ratio occurs whenever the larger term contains the smaller twice or more times and additionally one part...

<summary>This page defines the superpartient ratio, explaining how a larger number can contain a smaller one plus multiple fractional parts (either identical or diverse), and introduces the multiple-superparticular ratio.</summary>
<keywords>superparticular ratio, hemiolic, epitrite, superpartient ratio, sub-superpartient, multiple-superparticular ratio, mathematical proportions, Greek mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the classification of mathematical ratios, moving from simple superparticular and superpartient ratios to "multiple" versions. These occur when the larger number contains the smaller number several times over, plus one or more fractional parts.</meta>

## MULTIPLE SUPERPARTICULAR AND MULTIPLE SUPERPARTIENT RATIOS

...of it, just as the number 7 contains 3 twice and also a third of it, and is called its **double-superthird** <term>diplasiepitritos; the ratio 2 + 1/3 or 7:3</term>. Similarly, 9 contains 4 twice and also a fourth of it, and is called **double-superfourth** <term>diplasiepitétartos; the ratio 2 + 1/4 or 9:4</term>. Furthermore, 10 contains 3 three times and a third of it, and is called **triple-superthird** <term>triplasiepitritos; the ratio 3 + 1/3 or 10:3</term>. <margin>5</margin> Let the remaining **multiple-superparticular** <term>pollaplasiepimorioi; ratios where the larger number contains the smaller multiple times plus exactly one part of it</term> ratios be considered in a similar way. This occurs when, given two numbers, the smaller one measuring the larger is not able to measure it exactly as a whole, but leaves a part of the larger, which is a part of that smaller number itself. For instance, <margin>10</margin> 26 is called the multiple-superparticular of 8, because 8, measuring 26 three times, did not finish the count exactly as a whole, but reaching as far as 24, left two out of the 26, which is a fourth of 8 <note>Since 2 is 1/4 of 8, the ratio is 3 + 1/4</note>.

A **multiple-superpartient** <term>pollaplasiepipemerēs; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller multiple times plus more than one part of it</term> <margin>15</margin> ratio is when the larger term contains the smaller twice or more times, and additionally two or more parts of it, whether these parts are the same or different. For example, 8 contains 3 twice and two-thirds of it, and is called **double and twice-superthird** <note>original: diplasios kai dis epitritos; 2 + 2/3 or 8:3</note>. Also, 11 is to 3 <margin>20</margin> **triple and twice-superthird** <note>3 + 2/3 or 11:3</note>, while 11 is to 4 is **double and hemiolic and superfourth** <note>2 + 1/2 + 1/4</note> or **double and thrice-superfourth** <note>2 + 3/4</note>. It is easy to put forward many other various multiple-superpartients. This occurs when the smaller <margin>25</margin> number, measuring the larger, is not able to complete the count exactly, but leaves behind some number which consists of parts of it.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of complex ratios, specifically the multiple-superpartient ratio, before moving into the definition of "ratio of number to number" and the concept of "root" ratios in their lowest terms.</meta>

->### ON THE RATIO OF NUMBER TO NUMBER<-
<note>original: "DE RAT. NUMERI AD NUMERUM"</note>

...as the number 14 is to 3; for the number three, when measuring the number fourteen, was not able to complete it exactly, but advancing four times as far as 12, it left behind from the 14 a remaining two, which is two-thirds of 3, which are indeed called **two-thirds** <note>original: "dyo trita"</note>. Opposite to the multiple-superpartient is the **sub-multiple-superpartient** <term>hypopollaplasiepipemerēs; a ratio where the smaller number is contained within the larger multiple times plus several fractional parts</term>.

<margin>24</margin> A **ratio of number to number** <note>original: "arithmou pros arithmon logos"</note> occurs whenever the larger number is related to the smaller in none of the previously mentioned ratios. This will be shown also in the ratio containing the **remainder** <term>leimma; in ancient music theory, the "leftover" interval when two whole tones are subtracted from a perfect fourth, commonly known as the Pythagorean semitone</term>, having its terms in the lowest numbers as 256 is to 243. It is also clear that the ratios of the smaller terms to the larger ones are addressed inversely by those names, as has already been shown.

### CONCERNING ROOT RATIOS
<note>original: "peri pythmenōn logōn"</note>

Of all the ratios mentioned by species, those that are in the lowest terms and are **prime to one another** <term>prōtoi pros allēlous; numbers that share no common divisor other than 1</term> are called the **primary** <note>original: "prōtoi"</note> of those having the same ratio, and the **roots** <term>pythmenes; literally "bases" or "foundations"</term> of those of the same kind. 

For example, among **double** <term>diplasios; a 2:1 ratio</term> ratios, the primary and root is 2 to 1; for after this, in larger and composite numbers, there are double ratios such as 4 to 2, and 6 to 3, and likewise to infinity. Among **triple** <term>triplasios; a 3:1 ratio</term> ratios, the primary and root is 3 to 1; while the others always proceed to infinity in larger and composite numbers. It is the same for the other multiples, and likewise...

<summary>This page concludes the description of multiple-superpartient ratios and introduces the "root" (pythmēn), which refers to a ratio expressed in its simplest, lowest numerical terms. It also identifies the musical "limma" (256:243) as a specific type of number-to-number ratio.</summary>

<keywords>multiple-superpartient, root ratios, pythmen, lowest terms, limma, mathematical proportions, Pythagorean tuning, coprimality</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of root ratios, specifically addressing superpartient ratios (where the larger number contains the smaller plus a fraction) before defining the distinction between a "ratio" and an "interval," citing Eratosthenes.</meta>

-># ON RATIO AND INTERVAL<-
<note>original: "DE RATIONE ET INTERVALLO"</note>

...and among the **superpartient** <term>epimorioi; ratios where the larger number contains the smaller once plus one part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term> ratios. The first and the **root** <note>original: "pythmēn"</note> of the **hemiolic** <term>hemiolios; the 3:2 ratio, or "one-and-a-half"</term> ratios is that of 3 to 2; of the **epitritic** <term>epitritos; the 4:3 ratio, or "one-and-a-third"</term>, it is 4 to 3; and of the **epitetartic** <term>epitetartos; the 5:4 ratio, or "one-and-a-fourth"</term>, it is 5 to 4. Those found in <margin>5</margin> larger terms and composite ones are, in turn, infinite in number. The same principle is observed in the other types of ratios.

An **interval** <term>diastēma; in music and mathematics, the "space" or distance between two points or pitches</term> and a **ratio** <term>logos; the mathematical relationship or proportion between two values</term> differ from one another, since an interval is the space between terms that are of the same kind but unequal, while a ratio is simply the relationship of terms of the same kind to one another. Therefore, between equal terms there is no <margin>10</margin> interval, yet there is a ratio to one another—one and the same, the ratio of equality. Between unequal terms, the interval is one and the same from either <insert>direction</insert>, from one to the other, but the ratio is different and the opposite for each toward the other. For example, from 2 to 1 and from 1 to 2, the interval is one <margin>15</margin> and the same, but the ratio is different: of 2 to 1 it is a **double** ratio, while of 1 to 2 it is a **half** ratio.

Eratosthenes, in his work *The Platonic*, says that interval and ratio are not the same thing, because a ratio is a certain kind of relationship of two magnitudes to one another; and this relationship occurs both in things that differ <insert>and in things that do not differ</insert>. For example, in <margin>20</margin> whatever ratio the **sensible** <note>aisthēton; things perceived by the physical senses</note> stands to the **intelligible** <note>noēton; things grasped only by the mind or intellect</note>, in that same ratio stands **opinion** to **scientific knowledge**; and the intelligible differs from the object of knowledge just as opinion differs from the sensible. An interval, however,

<meta>This page continues the technical differentiation between ratio and interval, transitioning into a formal definition of mathematical proportion (analogia). It specifically defines continuous and disjunct proportions using numerical examples like 4:2:1 and 6:3::4:2.</meta>

### ON PROPORTIONS.
<note>original: "DE PROPORTIONIBUS"</note>

...they differ only in size, or in quality, or in position, or in some other way. It is also clear from this that a **ratio** <term>logos; the relationship between two values</term> is different from an **interval** <term>diastēma; the "distance" or space between terms</term>; for the half ratio compared to the double <insert>and the double compared to the half</insert> does not have the same <margin>5</margin> ratio, but it does have the same interval.

**Proportion** <term>analogia; the equality or similarity of ratios</term> <margin>On proportion and equality</margin> is the similarity or identity of several ratios; that is, a similarity of ratios among several terms, when the first has the same ratio to the second as the second has to the third, or as some other term has to another.

<margin>10</margin> One kind is called **continuous proportion** <term>synechēs analogia; where the middle term is shared</term>, and the other **disjunct** <term>diērēmenē; also called discrete, where the ratios are separate</term>. The continuous is that which exists in at least three terms, and the disjunct is that which exists in at least four. For example, after the proportion found in equal terms, a continuous one in the fewest terms according to the **double** ratio is 4, 2, 1; for as 4 is to 2, so <margin>15</margin> 2 is to 1. <margin>For the two ratios are not joined by a single common middle term, as in 4, 2, 1 where 2 is the middle and common term; for as 4 is to 2, so 2 is to 1.</margin> A disjunct one is 6, 3, 4, 2; for as 6 is to 3, so 4 is to 2. The same applies to other **multiples** <term>pollaplasioi; ratios like 3:1 or 4:1</term>. In a certain way, the continuous proportion can also exist in four terms, when the middle term is taken twice. <margin>For example, as 4 is to 2, so 2 is to 1; for the middle term 2 is taken twice—with 4 as the half, and with 1 as the double.</margin>

The same logic applies to **superparticulars** <term>epimorioi; ratios where the larger term contains the smaller plus one part of it, like 3:2</term>: <margin>20</margin> a continuous proportion in the **hemiolic** <note>original: "hēmioliō"; the 3:2 ratio, or "one-and-a-half"</note> ratio is 9, 6, 4; a disjunct one is 9, 6, 15, 10. The same logic holds for the other ratios.

**Eratosthenes** <note>Eratosthenes of Cyrene (c. 276–194 BCE), the famous librarian of Alexandria and polymath</note> says that the [nature] of proportion begins with ratio and <unclear>..........................</unclear> first...

<summary>This page defines proportion (analogia) as a similarity of ratios and distinguishes between continuous proportions (3 terms) and disjunct proportions (4 terms). It provides numerical examples for both, including double and hemiolic ratios.</summary>

<keywords>proportion, analogia, continuous proportion, disjunct proportion, ratio, interval, Eratosthenes, hemiolic, mathematical terms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical ratios, specifically moving from the definition of discrete and continuous proportions to the metaphysical and mathematical "elements" that compose them. It establishes "Equality" as the fundamental building block of all ratios.</meta>

### ON EQUALITY.
<note>original Latin header: "DE AEQUALITATE"</note>

...and it is the first cause of generation <note>The text suggests that equality is the "parent" from which all other mathematical relationships are born.</note> for all things that do not come into being in a disordered way. For every **proportion** <term>analogia; a relationship of ratios</term> is composed of **ratios** <term>logoi</term>, and the principle of ratio is equality. This is clear from the following: in each of the genera <note>The "genera" here refer to the categories of existence, such as quantity and quality.</note> there is a specific **element** <term>stoicheion; the smallest fundamental unit</term> <insert>and principle</insert> into which other things are analyzed, <margin>5</margin> but which itself is not analyzed into any of them. It is necessary, then, for this to be indivisible and atomic; for that which admits of division and cutting is called a "syllable" <note>In Greek, the word for "element" (stoicheion) also means "letter of the alphabet." The author is using a metaphor: letters are atoms of language, while syllables are already complex and divisible.</note> and not an element. Thus, the elements of substance are indivisible according to substance, those of quality according to quality, and those of quantity according to <margin>10</margin> quantity. Generally, each thing is atomic and "one" in this respect: insofar as it is an element of some composite or mixture. 

<margin>Note: What is the element and principle of each genus, and how generation occurs from these for the things derived from them.</margin>

Therefore, the element of quantity is the **unit** <term>monas; the Monad or the number one</term>, the element of magnitude is the **point** <term>stigmē</term>, and the element of ratio and proportion is **equality** <term>isotēs</term>. For one can no longer divide the unit into further quantity, nor the point into <margin>15</margin> magnitude, nor equality into more ratios. 

**Number** arises from the unit, the **line** from the point, and **ratio** and **proportion** from equality—though each of these arises in a different manner. The unit, when multiplied by itself, generates nothing as other numbers do, for "once one" <margin>20</margin> is one; but it increases through addition unto infinity. The point increases neither through multiplication nor addition; but having flowed <note>This refers to the "fluxion" theory where a moving point creates a line.</note> and been carried along continuously, it completes a line, the line completes a surface, and the surface completes a body. Furthermore, the ratio of equals does not increase when <margin>25</margin> added together; for when several equals are placed in sequence, the ratio...

***

<note>original Latin: "ciderunt aequalitatis commemoratio fuisse videtur"</note> The mention of equality seems to have been omitted: compare pages 107, 10 and 111, 12. <note>original: "ἀρχή post πρώτη add. A²"</note> The word "principle" was added after "first" by scribe A². <note>original: "καὶ ante πᾶσι add. A²"</note> The word "and" was added before "all" by scribe A². <note>original: "post καὶ una lit. er. A"</note> One letter was erased after "and" in manuscript A. <note>original: "μικτοῦ corr. ex μικτὸν A"</note> "Mixture" corrected from "mixed" in A. <note>original: "ὁ ἐστιν ἓν ante κατὰ del. A"</note> The phrase "which is one" was deleted before "according to" in manuscript A.

**Vocabulary:**
**Proportion** <gloss>analogia</gloss>, **Ratio** <gloss>logos</gloss>, **Element** <gloss>stoicheion</gloss>, **Principle** <gloss>archē</gloss>, **Atomic/Indivisible** <gloss>atomon</gloss>, **Substance** <gloss>ousia</gloss>, **Quality** <gloss>poion</gloss>, **Quantity** <gloss>poson</gloss>, **Unit/Monad** <gloss>monas</gloss>, **Point** <gloss>stigmē</gloss>, **Line** <gloss>grammē</gloss>, **Surface** <gloss>epiphaneia</gloss>, **Body** <gloss>sōma</gloss>, **Equality** <gloss>isotēs</gloss>.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of mathematical foundations, transitioning from the concept of equality as a "principle" to its role in proportion and the distinction between a mathematical "proportion" and a "mean." It cites Plato’s Epinomis to emphasize mathematics as a unifying bond of the cosmos.</meta>

### ON PROPORTION AND THE MEAN.
<note>original Latin header: "DE PROPORTIONE ET MEDIETATE"</note>

...the ratio of the encompassing limit remains in equality. Therefore, it follows that the point is not a part of a line, nor is equality [a part] of a ratio, just as the unit <note>original: "monas"</note> is a part of number; for only the unit, when added to itself, produces an increase. <margin>5</margin> The cause of what has been said is that equality is devoid of distance <note>Meaning it has no extension or "space" between its terms.</note>, just as the point is devoid of magnitude.

Plato seems to believe that there is one unifying bond of the mathematical sciences, which is derived from **proportion** <term>analogia; a relationship where two ratios are equal, e.g., 2 is to 4 as 4 is to 8</term>. For in the *Epinomis* <note>A dialogue attributed to Plato or his student Philip of Opus, discussing the path to wisdom through astronomy and math.</note> he says: "Every geometric diagram, every system of number, every construction of harmony, <margin>10</margin> and the entire revolution of the stars—which is a single proportion for all things—must be revealed to one who learns in the proper way. And it will be revealed, if one learns while looking correctly at what we are saying; for a single natural bond of all these things will be revealed." <note>original: "Epinomis 991E"</note>

<margin>15</margin> However, a **mean** <term>mesotēs; a middle value or "median" in a series</term> differs from a proportion. While every proportion is also a mean, it is not the case that every mean is immediately a proportion. For it is possible for something to be a "middle" by its position in an ordered sequence without being in proportion to the extremes. For example, the number two is the middle by position <margin>20</margin> between one and three, and between one and ten, the numbers three, four, and five [are middle terms]; for from the one it is not possible...

<summary>This page discusses the metaphysical nature of mathematical equality and units, citing Plato to argue that proportion is the unifying bond of all sciences. It also begins a technical distinction between a "mean" (a middle term in a sequence) and a "proportion" (a specific relationship of ratios).</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Epinomis, proportion, mean, equality, geometry, unit, mathematical harmony</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical means and proportions, specifically addressing how a simple sequence of numbers (a mean) differs from a true geometric proportion. It concludes a sentence from the previous page regarding the necessity of passing through intermediate numbers to reach a total.</meta>

### ON THE RATIOS OF CONSONANCES.
<note>original Latin: "DE RATIONIBUS CONSONANTIARUM"</note>

...to reach ten without first coming to two, three, and four. But none of these stands in proportion to the extremes. For the unit is not in the same ratio to two as two is to three; likewise for the numbers <margin>5</margin> two, three, and four. However, things that exist in the same ratio would also be means, such as one, two, and four; for this is a **proportion** <term>analogia; here referring specifically to a geometric progression</term> of doubling, and two is the mean between one and four.

Regarding proportions, Thrasyllus <note>A Platonist philosopher and mathematician, also the court astrologer to Emperor Tiberius</note> says there are three primary types: **arithmetic**, **geometric**, and **harmonic**. <margin>10</margin> **Arithmetic** is that which exceeds and is exceeded by the same number, <insert>such as 1, 3, 5, 7, 9</insert>; **geometric** is that which exceeds and is exceeded by the same ratio, such as double or triple, as in 3, 6, 12; **harmonic** is that which exceeds and is exceeded by the same part of the extremes, <margin>15</margin> such as by a third or a fourth—for example: 6, 8, 12. <note>In 6, 8, 12, the middle term 8 exceeds 6 by 2 (one-third of 6) and is exceeded by 12 by 4 (one-third of 12).</note>

Each of these is observed in numbers in another way as well: of 6, 12 is the double, 18 is the triple, 24 is the quadruple, 9 is the **hemiolic** <term>hemiolios; the ratio of 3:2, where one value is 1.5 times the other</term>, and 8 is the **epitritic** <term>epitritos; the ratio of 4:3, where one value is 1.33 times the other</term>. 9 is the **sesquioctave** <margin>20</margin> <term>epogdoos; the ratio of 9:8, representing a whole tone in music</term> of 8. 12, compared to 9, is epitritic [4:3]; compared to 8, it is hemiolic [3:2]; and compared to 6, it is double [2:1]. 18 is the double of 9, and 27 is the hemiolic [3:2] of 18. And 8 occurs in the interval of the **fourth** <note>original: "dia tessaron"</note> in relation to 6; 9 occurs in the **fifth** <note>original: "dia pente"</note>; 12 in the **octave** <note>original: "dia pason"</note>; and 18 in the **octave plus a fifth** <note>original: "dia pason kai dia pente"</note>. For <margin>25</margin> 12 is the double of 6 in the octave, and 18 is [the hemiolic] of 12...

<summary>This page defines the three primary mathematical proportions (arithmetic, geometric, and harmonic) and demonstrates their application to musical intervals like the fourth, fifth, and octave.</summary>
<keywords>Thrasyllus, arithmetic proportion, geometric proportion, harmonic proportion, musical intervals, ratios, hemiolic, epitritic, octave, perfect fifth, perfect fourth</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 85, which introduced Thrasyllus’s definitions of arithmetic, geometric, and harmonic means. This page applies these mathematical principles specifically to music theory, explaining how musical intervals are constructed from ratios.</meta>

### ON THE RATIOS OF CONSONANCES.
<note>original Latin: "DE RATIONIBUS CONSONANTIARUM"</note>

Hemiolic ratios <note>original: "ἡμιόλιά"; the ratio 3:2, where one number is one-and-a-half times another</note> are found in the **fifth** <term>dia pente; the musical interval of a perfect fifth</term>, such as in the sequence 6, 12, 18 <note>Here, 18 to 12 is a 3:2 ratio</note>. The ratio of 24 to 6 is found in the **double octave** <term>dis dia pason; a 4:1 ratio</term>. The ratio of 9 to 8 is in a **tone** <term>tonos; a major whole tone</term>. The ratio of 12 to 9 is in the **fourth** <term>dia tessaron; a 4:3 ratio</term>. The ratio of 12 to 8 is in the **fifth**. The ratio of 18 to 9 is in the **octave** <term>dia pason; a 2:1 ratio</term>. <margin>5</margin> The ratio of 27 to 18 is in the **fifth**.

The octave (12 to 6) is composed of the hemiolic (9 to 6) and the **epitritic** <term>epitritos; the ratio 4:3, where one number is one-and-a-third times another</term> (12 to 9). <note>This explains that an octave is mathematically the sum of a perfect fifth and a perfect fourth.</note> And again, the octave is composed of the hemiolic (12 to 8) and the epitritic (8 to 6). Likewise, the octave of 18 to 9 is composed of the hemiolic 18 to 12 and the epitritic 12 to 9. The <margin>10</margin> octave of 24 to 12 is composed of the epitritic 24 to 18 and the hemiolic 18 to 12. The fifth (9 to 6) is composed of the **epogdoic** <term>epogdoos; the ratio 9:8, representing a whole tone</term> (9 to 8) and the epitritic (8 to 6). The hemiolic (fifth) of 12 to 8 is composed of the epitritic (12 to 9) and the epogdoic (9 to 8).

<margin>15</margin> The **leimma** <term>leimma; literally "the remnant," referring to the Pythagorean minor semitone</term> occurs in the ratio of 256 to 243. It is found in this way: two epogdoic ratios (whole tones) are taken, and these are tripled, and a fourth is added to the two epogdoic ratios. For example, one epogdoic ratio is that of 9 to 8. From these, two <margin>20</margin> epogdoic ratios are formed as follows: 9 multiplied by itself becomes 81; then 9 times 8 becomes 72; then 8 multiplied by itself becomes 64. Thus, 81 is the epogdoic of 72, and 72 is the epogdoic of 64. If we triple these, 81 becomes 243, while 72 becomes 216, and 64 <insert>becomes 192</insert>.

<summary>This page details the mathematical ratios behind musical intervals—the octave, fifth, fourth, and whole tone—demonstrating how larger intervals are composed of smaller ones. It also provides the complex calculation for the 'leimma' or Pythagorean semitone.</summary>
<keywords>musical theory, ratios, octave, perfect fifth, perfect fourth, whole tone, leimma, Pythagorean tuning, Thrasyllus</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 85, where the ratios of musical consonances (the fifth, fourth, and octave) were established through numerical relationships. This page concludes the mathematical demonstration of the "leimma" (the small remainder in a scale) and begins Thrasyllus’s practical instructions for dividing the monochord string based on the sacred Pythagorean number four.</meta>

# THE DIVISION OF THE CANON
<note>original Latin: "SECTIO CANONIS"</note>

...thrice becomes 192 <note>original: ρϟβ΄</note>. The **epitritic** <term>epitritos; the ratio 4:3</term> of these is 256 <note>original: σνϚ΄</note>, which has the ratio of the **leimma** <term>leimma; the "remnant" or Pythagorean semitone</term> when compared to 243 <note>original: σμγ΄</note>, and this ratio is greater than the eighteenth part <note>original: "ἐποκτωκαιδέκατος"; the ratio 19:18. The author is noting that the leimma (256:243 ≈ 1.0535) is slightly larger than the ratio 19:18 (≈ 1.0556) or similar fractional approximations used by ancient theorists.</note>.

<note>This diagram illustrates how a perfect fourth (4:3 ratio, 256:192) is composed of two whole tones (9:8 ratios, 243:216 and 216:192) and the remaining small interval called the leimma (256:243).</note>

->The Fourth<-
<note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων"</note>

<margin>5</margin> The division of the **canon** <term>kanon; a one-stringed musical apparatus, also called a monochord, used to demonstrate mathematical ratios</term> is achieved through the **tetractys** <term>tetraktys; a mystical triangular figure of ten points, representing the first four numbers</term> within the decad <note>the number ten</note>, which is composed of the unit, the dyad, the triad, and the tetrad: 1, 2, 3, 4. For it contains the epitritic [4:3], hemiolic [3:2], double [2:1], triple [3:1], and quadruple [4:1] ratios. **Thrasyllus** <note>A 1st-century CE scholar and court astrologer to the Emperor Tiberius, known for his work on music theory and Plato.</note> divides it in the following manner:

<margin>10</margin> If one divides the length [of the string] in two, the middle point produces the **octave** <term>dia pason; the interval between the first and eighth notes of a scale</term> in the double ratio [2:1]. This occurs **inversely** <term>antipeponthotōs; a reciprocal relationship</term> regarding the movements [vibrations], having a double tension <note>meaning pitch</note> toward the high end. This "inverse proportion" is as follows: however much of the length you take away from the whole string on the canon, <margin>15</margin> that same amount is added to the pitch; and however much you add to the length of the string, that same amount is subtracted from the pitch. For the half-length <insert>the middle note compared to the two parts</insert> has double the pitch toward the high; but the double length has half the pitch toward the low.

<summary>This page concludes the mathematical calculation of the Pythagorean leimma and introduces Thrasyllus's method for dividing the monochord string using the Tetractys. It explains the inverse relationship between string length and musical pitch.</summary>
<keywords>leimma, monochord, Thrasyllus, Tetractys, pitch and length, inverse proportion, octave, epitritos</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of the "Division of the Canon" (the monochord). The previous page established the ratios for the tone and the leimma (semitone). Here, the text moves from general ratios to specific divisions of the string to locate the primary notes of the Greek musical system: the Hypate, Mese, and Nete.</meta>

# SECTION
<note>original Latin: "SECTIO"</note>

When the division [of the string] is made into three parts, both the **hypate of the middle notes** <term>hypate meson; the lowest note of the middle tetrachord, traditionally the E below middle C</term> and the **nete of the disjoined notes** <term>nete diezeugmenon; the highest note of the "disjoined" tetrachord, traditionally E an octave above the hypate meson</term> are produced. The **nete of the disjoined notes** stands in relation to the **mese** <term>mese; the "middle note" of the system, the central A</term> at the interval of the **fifth** <note>original: "διὰ πέντε"</note>; for there are two parts compared to three. In relation to the **hypate** <note>specifically the hypate meson</note>,
<margin>5</margin> it stands at the interval of the **octave** <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν"</note>; for it is one part compared to two. In relation to the **proslambanomenos** <term>proslambanomenos; the "added note," the lowest note in the entire Greek system, a low A</term>, it stands at the interval of the **octave and a fifth** <note>a compound interval of a twelfth</note>. For since the **proslambanomenos** stands at an **octave** interval in relation to the **mese**, the distance up to the **nete** is added to it, which is a **fifth** in relation to the
<margin>10</margin> **mese**. Moreover, the **mese** in relation to the **hypate** is at the interval of the **fourth** <note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων"</note>, and in relation to the **proslambanomenos** it is at the interval of the **octave**. The **hypate** in relation to the **proslambanomenos** is at the interval of the **fifth**. The magnitude <note>the length of the string</note> from the **hypate** to the **mese** (the **fourth**) becomes equal to the magnitude from the **mese** to the
<margin>15</margin> **nete** (the **fifth**). And likewise, the numbers of the vibrations <note>original: "κινήσεων"; literally "movements," referring to the frequency of sound</note> are in reciprocal proportion to the division of the magnitudes.

When the division is made into four parts, there is established both the so-called **hyperypate** <term>hyperypate; the note "above the hypate," also known as the **diatonic of the hypates** (diatonos hypaton)</term> and the **nete of the highest notes** <term>nete hyperbolaion; the highest note of the highest tetrachord</term>. The **nete of the
<margin>20</margin> highest notes** <margin>added note, hypate of the middle, nete of the disjoined</margin> in relation to the **nete of the disjoined notes** stands at the interval of the **fourth**; in relation to the **mese**, it is at the interval of the **octave**; in relation to the **hypate**, it is at the interval of the **octave and a fourth** <note>an eleventh</note>; in relation to the **hyperypate**, it is at the interval of the **octave and a fifth**; and in relation to the
<margin>25</margin> **proslambanomenos**, it stands at the interval of the **double octave** <note>original: "δὶς διὰ πασῶν"</note> toward the bass. The ratio for the **hyperypate**...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of the "Division of the Canon" (the monochord). The previous page ended while describing the harmonic relationships of the Hypate. Here, the author further defines the spatial and mathematical positions of the notes within the Greater Perfect System using a string divided into 12 units.</meta>

->OF THE CANON.<-

...is <note>referring to the note being discussed, likely the Hypate</note> in relation to the **proslambanomenos** <term>proslambanomenos; the "added note," the lowest note in the Greek system, two octaves below the Nete</term> at the interval of a **fourth** <note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων"</note> toward the lower register, and in relation to the **mese** <term>mese; the "middle note," the central A of the system</term> at the interval of a **fifth** <note>original: "διὰ πέντε"</note> toward the higher register, and it exceeds the **hypate** <note>hypate meson; the principal note of the middle tetrachord</note> by a **tone** <note>original: "τόνῳ"</note> in the lower register. And the tone-sized magnitude of the **hyperypate** <term>hyperypate; here likely referring to the Parhypate, the note just above the Hypate</term> in relation to the **hypate** is equal to the [interval of the] **fourth** of the **nete of the disjoined notes** <term>nete diezeugmenon; the highest note of the disjoined tetrachord</term> in relation to the **nete of the extreme notes** <term>nete hyperbolaiōn; the highest note in the entire system</term>. <note>Mathematically, the author here seems to swap the labels: the ratio of 4:3 (a fourth) and 9:8 (a tone) are applied to these positions in the numerical example that follows.</note> And likewise, the numbers of the vibrations correspond inversely to the magnitudes of the intervals [of the string].

What has been said will become clear through the use of numbers. For if the length of the canon <term>kanon; the measuring rod or the monochord itself</term> is 12 measures of any kind, the **mese** will be located when <insert>the entire string is divided in two, and it will have</insert> 6 [measures] on either side. The **hypate of the middle notes** will be 4 [measures] from the beginning; the **nete of the disjoined notes** will be 4 from the end; and the space between them will be 4. The **hyperypate** will be 3 measures from the beginning and 1 from the **hypate**; the **extreme note** <note>original: "ὑπερβολαία"</note> will be 3 from the end and 1 from the **disjoined note**. Between these two there are 6 [measures], so that each is 3 from the **mese**. Thus, the entire division is as follows: from the beginning to the **hyperypate**, 3; from there to the **hypate**, 1; from there to the **mese**, 2; then from the **mese** to the **disjoined note**, 2; from there to the **extreme note**, 1; and from that to the end, 3. The total becomes 12. Therefore, the <insert>ratio</insert> of the **nete of the disjoined notes** to the **extreme note** will be 4 to 3, an epitritic <term>epitritic; a ratio of 4:3, which produces the musical interval of a fourth</term>...

<summary>This page provides a practical mathematical model for the monochord, dividing a string of 12 units to precisely locate the Mese, Hypate, and Nete. It demonstrates the Pythagorean principle that musical intervals correspond to specific numerical ratios of string length.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof of the "Division of the Canon," moving from general principles to specific numerical ratios. The previous page introduced a string length of 12 units to represent the Proslambanomenos. Here, the author calculates the ratios between specific notes (Nete, Mese, Hypate, etc.) based on these lengths to demonstrate how the musical intervals of the fourth, fifth, and octave are formed.</meta>

### SECTION

...is the third [ratio], that of the **fourth** <note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων" (dia tessarōn)</note>. In relation to the note **three** <note>The highest note, Nete Hyperbolaiōn, is represented here by the number 3</note>, the **mese** <term>mese; the "middle note," the central pitch of the Greek system</term> at length **6** is double, forming the interval of the **octave** <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" (dia pasōn); the ratio 6:3 reduces to 2:1</note>; <insert>the **hypate** at length **8** compared to 3 is a two-and-one-third ratio, forming the interval of the **octave plus a fourth**</insert> <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν καὶ διὰ τεσσάρων" (dia pasōn kai dia tessarōn); the ratio 8:3</note>; the **hyperypate** <term>hyperypate; here referring to the Parhypate Meson, the note just above the Hypate</term> at length **9** compared to 3 is a triple ratio, forming the interval of the **octave plus a fifth** <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν καὶ διὰ πέντε" (dia pasōn kai dia pente); the ratio 9:3 reduces to 3:1</note>; and the total length of the **proslambanomenos** <term>proslambanomenos; the "added note," the lowest pitch in the system</term> at length **12** compared to 3 is a quadruple ratio, forming the interval of the **double octave** <note>original: "δὶς διὰ πασῶν" (dis dia pasōn); the ratio 12:3 reduces to 4:1</note>.

In relation to the **nete of the disjoined notes** <term>nete diezeugmenon; the note at the top of the fourth tetrachord, represented here by length 4</term>, the ratio of the **mese** (6 to 4) is one-and-a-half, which is the interval of the **fifth** <note>original: "διὰ πέντε" (dia pente); the ratio 6:4 reduces to 3:2</note>; that of the **hypate** (8 to 4) is double, forming the **octave**; that of the **hyperypate** (9 to 4) is <insert>two-and-one-quarter</insert>, forming the interval of the **double fifth** <note>Wait, the text says "double fifth" (dis dia pente) for 9:4. In Greek theory, 9:4 is indeed two fifths (3/2 * 3/2 = 9/4)</note>; and the total length of the **proslambanomenos** (12 to 4) is <insert>triple</insert>, forming the interval of the **octave plus a fifth**.

In relation to the **mese** (length 6), the ratio of the **hypate** (8 to 6) is one-and-a-third, forming the **fourth**; that of the **hyperypate** (9 to 6) is one-and-a-half, forming the **fifth**; and the total length of the **proslambanomenos** (12 to 6) is double, forming the **octave**.

In relation to the **hypate** (length 8), the **hyperypate** (9 to 8) is in the one-and-one-eighth ratio <term>epogdoos; the 9:8 ratio which defines the whole tone</term> of the **tone**; and the total length of the **proslambanomenos** (12 to 8) is one-and-a-half, <insert>the interval of the **fifth**</insert>. In relation <insert>to</insert> the **hyperypate**, the total length of the **proslambanomenos** (12 to 9) is in the one-and-a-third ratio <insert>of</insert> the **fourth**.

The remaining movements are in reciprocal relation according to the division of the **whole-tone** and the **fourth**...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals on the monochord (the "canon"). The previous page established the lengths for the primary intervals (octave, fifth, fourth). This page explains how the whole tone is the difference between a fifth and a fourth and begins the step-by-step division of the string to locate specific notes in the "Hyperbolaion" (highest) and "Diezeugmenon" (disjoined) tetrachords.</meta>

### OF THE CANON

...and the **sesquialter** <term>sesquialter; a ratio of 3:2, which produces the interval of a perfect fifth</term> **fifth** <note>original: "διὰ πέντε" (dia pente)</note> of the canon. Since the sesquialter fifth exceeds the **sesquitertial** <term>sesquitertial; a ratio of 4:3, which produces the interval of a perfect fourth</term> **fourth** <note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων" (dia tessarōn)</note> by a **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctave; a ratio of 9:8, which defines a standard musical whole tone</term> tone—for example, if we take a number that has both a half and a third, such as **6**; 
<margin>5</margin>
the sesquitertial of this is **8**, and the sesquialter is **9**. The ratio of 9 to 8 is a sesquioctave: **6, 8, 9**. Thus, the excess of the [9] sesquialter over the [8] sesquitertial is in the sesquioctave ratio. The fourth, being sesquitertial, consists of two sesquioctave tones and the **diesis-leimma** <term>diesis-leimma; the "leftover" semitone that remains when two whole tones are subtracted from a perfect fourth</term>. One must fill these in with the sesquioctave tones and the diesis-
<margin>10</margin>
leimmas. Let the filling begin with the **nete hyperbolaiōn** <term>nete hyperbolaiōn; the highest note of the highest tetrachord in the Greek system</term>. If we take an eighth of the distance from that point to the end of the string and extend it, we will find the **diatonos hyperbolaiōn** <note>The note a whole tone lower than the Nete</note>, which is a tone lower in pitch. Then, taking an eighth of the distance from this new point to the
<margin>15</margin>
end and extending it, we will find the **tritē hyperbolaiōn** <note>The third note of the highest tetrachord</note>, which is a tone lower than the diatonos. What remains, reaching to the **nete diezeugmenōn** <term>nete diezeugmenōn; the "disjoined" nete, the highest note of the next tetrachord down</term>, will be the diesis-leimma, completing the interval of a fourth relative to the nete hyperbolaiōn.
<margin>20</margin>
Again, starting from the nete diezeugmenōn to the end of the distance: if we take one-ninth and move it back <note>meaning to shorten the string/raise the pitch</note>, we will have the **chromatic hyperbolaiōn** <note>a note used in the chromatic scale variation</note>, which is a tone higher than the nete diezeugmenōn. If we extend the length by an eighth, we will have the **paranētē diezeugmenōn**; this is the same as the diatonos [of that tetrachord] and the **nete synēmmenōn** <term>nete synēmmenōn; the "conjunct" nete</term>, and it is a tone lower
<margin>25</margin>
than the nete diezeugmenōn. Taking an eighth of the distance from this nete to the end and extending it...

***

<summary>This page provides a mathematical proof that a musical tone (9:8) is the difference between a fifth (3:2) and a fourth (4:3), and begins describing the physical measurement of the monochord string to locate high-register notes.</summary>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical construction of the Greek musical system on a monochord. Having previously defined the highest notes (Nete), the author is now using the 9:8 ratio (the whole tone) to find the middle and lower notes of the "Disjoined" and "Conjunct" tetrachords, eventually completing the full scale known as the "Great Perfect System."</meta>

### DIVISION OF THE CANON

...we will have the **third of the disjoined** <note>original: "τρίτην τῶν διεζευγμένων" (tritē tōn diezeugmenōn); this is the note called Trite in the Disjoined tetrachord</note> a tone lower in pitch; and this same note is also the **diatonic of the conjunct** <note>original: "διάτονος συνημμένων" (diatonos sunēmmenōn)</note>. Similarly, by extending an eighth part of the interval from this point to the end of the string <note>Adding 1/8 to the length of a string lowers the pitch by a 9:8 whole tone</note>, we will have the **third of the conjunct** <note>tritē sunēmmenōn</note> a tone lower in pitch.
<margin>5</margin>
The remainder reaching to the **mesê** <term>mesê; the "middle" note and central anchor of the Greek scale</term> will be the **diesis-leimma** <term>diesis-leimma; the small semitone (256:243 ratio) that remains when two whole tones are subtracted from a fourth</term> for the completion of the **octave** <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" (dia pasōn)</note>. 

From the *mesê*, in the same manner, by shortening the string by <insert>the ninth</insert> part, we will have the **paramesê** <term>paramesê; the note "next to the middle," often the first note of the higher tetrachord</term> or the **chromatic conjunct** <note>the chromatic version of the note in the conjunct tetrachord</note>, which is a tone higher in pitch than the *mesê*. 
<margin>10</margin>
By shortening this length again by a ninth, we will have the **chromatic disjoined** <note>chromatikē diezeugmenōn</note>. 

By extending the length of the *mesê* by an eighth part, we will have the **diatonic of the middle** <note>diatonos mesōn</note> a tone lower in pitch than the *mesê*; then, by extending by an eighth from that point, we have the **parhypatê** <insert>of the middle</insert> <term>parhypatê; the "next-to-lowest" note of a tetrachord</term> a tone lower than that. 
<margin>15</margin>
And the remainder reaching to the **hypatê of the middle** <term>hypatê mesōn; the "lowest" note of the middle tetrachord</term> is the diesis-leimma for the completion of the **fourth** <note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων" (dia tessarōn)</note> toward the *mesê*. 

From the *hypatê*, for those who shorten the string by a ninth, the **chromatic of the middle** <note>chromatikē mesōn</note> will be a tone higher in pitch. By extending the length by an eighth, it will result in the **hyperhypatê** <note>likely referring to the Lichanos or a specific tuning step</note>. 
<margin>20</margin>
By extending this by an eighth part, the **parhypatê of the lowest** <note>parhypatē hypatōn</note> will be produced. 

By reversal, starting from the **proslambanomenos** <term>proslambanomenos; the "added note," the lowest note in the Greek system, an octave below the Mesê</term>, if one divides the whole interval into nine parts and leaves one behind in the direction opposite the **netai** <note>the highest notes of the scale</note>, the **hypatê of the lowest** <note>hypatōn hypatē</note> will be produced, a tone higher in pitch than the whole string, 
<margin>25</margin>
closing the **tetrachord** <term>tetrachord; a four-note grouping that is the building block of Greek scales</term> of the lowest notes with the leimma toward the *parhypatê*. 

And thus the entire **Great Perfect System** <note>original: "πᾶν ἀμετάβολον σύστημα" (pan ametabolon systēma), the standard two-octave scale of antiquity</note> will be completed according to the **diatonic** and **chromatic** genus.

<summary>This page provides the final mathematical steps for dividing a monochord string to locate every note in the Greek musical scale. It details how to derive the "middle" and "lowest" tetrachords using whole-tone (9:8) ratios and semitone "remainders" (leimmas).</summary>
<keywords>monochord division, Great Perfect System, tetrachord, mesê, hypatê, paramesê, diatonic, chromatic, leimma, ratio, music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of the Greek musical scale. Having previously located the notes of the "Disjoined" and "Conjunct" systems on the monochord, the author now transitions from the specific measurements of Thrasyllus to the broader Pythagorean theory of the Tetraktys—the sacred "four-fold" group of numbers that governs both music and the cosmos.</meta>

# ON THE QUATERNIONS.

...the **enharmonic genus** <term>enharmonic; the most complex of the three Greek musical styles, characterized by very small quarter-tone intervals</term> is produced when the **diatonic notes** <note>original: "διατόνων" (diatonōn)</note> are removed and each **tetrachord** <term>tetrachord; a scale of four notes spanning the interval of a perfect fourth</term> is doubled in its melodic arrangement. We could find these notes in numerical form as well, starting from the **highest of the highest notes** <note>original: "νήτης τῶν ὑπερβολαίων" (nētēs tōn hyperbolaiōn); the highest note of the standard Greek scale</note>, assuming its value to be ten thousand three hundred sixty-eight <note>10,368; a number favored by Pythagorean mathematicians because its many factors allow for the calculation of the entire scale without fractions</note>. 
<margin>5</margin> The subsequent **sesquioctave** ratios <term>sesquioctave; the 9:8 ratio, equivalent to a musical whole tone</term> and the remaining notes are taken according to the ratios previously mentioned. It would be tedious to set them all out here, but it is easy for anyone who has followed the preceding discussion.

The division of the **canon** <note>original: "κανόνος" (kanonos); the monochord, a single-stringed instrument used to measure musical ratios</note> handed down by **Thrasyllus** <note>Thrasyllus of Mendes (d. 36 AD), the court philosopher and astrologer to the Roman Emperor Tiberius</note> is as follows. 
<margin>10</margin> In what manner this system is also applied to the **sphere of the universe** <note>A reference to the "Music of the Spheres," the idea that planets move according to musical proportions</note>, we shall demonstrate later when we set forth the principles of astronomy. For now, let us return to the account of the remaining **proportions** <term>analogia; a mathematical relationship between three or more terms</term> and **means** <term>mesotēs; a middle value in a sequence, such as the arithmetic or geometric mean</term>. As we previously stated <note>Referencing page 84 of the original manuscript</note>, every proportion is a mean, though not every mean is a proportion. 
<margin>15</margin> Insofar as a proportion is indeed a mean, the discussion of proportions and means should naturally follow.

### ON THE TETRAKTYS AND THE DECAD
<note>This heading is preserved from a marginal note in the primary manuscript</note>

Since all the ratios of the musical **concords** <note>original: "συμφωνιῶν" (sumphōniōn)</note> were found, as has been shown <note>Referencing pages 58 and 87</note>, within the **Tetraktys of the Decad** <term>Tetraktys; the "Four-fold" group of numbers (1, 2, 3, 4) which were sacred to Pythagoreans because they sum to 10 (the Decad)</term>, we must speak of these first. 
<margin>20</margin> For the **Decad** <note>The number ten</note> is constituted by the Tetraktys; for 1 and 2 and 3 and 4 make 10. Within these numbers, we find the concord of the **fourth** <note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων" (dia tessarōn)</note> in the 4:3 ratio, the **fifth** <note>original: "διὰ πέντε" (dia pente)</note> in the 3:2 ratio, the **octave** <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" (dia pasōn)</note> in the 2:1 ratio, and the **double octave** <note>original: "δὶς διὰ πασῶν" (dis dia pasōn)</note> in the 4:1 ratio. 
<margin>25</margin> From these elements, the **unchangeable scale** <term>the "Great Perfect System" of Greek music, a fixed two-octave scale used as the theoretical standard</term> is completed. Such then is the Tetraktys in music...

<meta>This page transitions from the practical measurements of the monochord scale attributed to Thrasyllus to the metaphysical foundations of Pythagorean mathematics. It begins a detailed explanation of the "Tetraktys"—the sacred four-fold group—which the Pythagoreans viewed as the blueprint for both music and the cosmos.</meta>

# ON THE QUATERNIONS.

<note>original: "DE QUATERNIONIBUS"; Latin for "On the groups of four," here referring to the Tetraktys.</note>

...the **Tetraktys** <term>Tetraktys; a sacred Pythagorean symbol consisting of four rows of dots (1, 2, 3, and 4) totaling ten, representing the organization of the universe</term> exists by way of addition, since all the **musical concords** <note>original: "συμφωνίαι" (symphōniai); the fundamental intervals of the fourth, fifth, and octave</note> are found within it. However, it was not favored by all the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras who combined mathematics, music, and mysticism</note> for this reason alone, but because it also seems to hold together the **nature of the universe** <note>original: "τὴν τῶν ὅλων φύσιν" (tēn tōn holōn physin)</note>. Therefore, they even swore an oath by it:
<margin>5</margin>
> "Nay, by him who delivered to our soul the Tetraktys,
> which contains the fountain and root of ever-flowing nature."
<note>This is a famous excerpt from the "Golden Verses" of Pythagoras; the "him" refers to Pythagoras himself.</note>

They say that the one who delivered it was **Pythagoras**, since the doctrine concerning it is considered to be his discovery.
<margin>10</margin> This aforementioned Tetraktys, then, is formed by the **summation** <note>original: "ἐπισύνθεσιν" (episynthesin); the process of adding 1+2+3+4 to reach the "perfect" number 10</note> of the first numbers. But there is a second Tetraktys consisting of numbers increased by **multiplication** starting from the **unit** <term>unit; the "Monad" or number one, which Pythagoreans saw as the source of all things</term>, proceeding by both the even and the odd. Of these, the unit is taken first,
<margin>15</margin> since it is the beginning of all even, odd, and **even-odd** <term>even-odd; a classification for even numbers which, when divided, eventually yield an odd number</term> numbers, as has been stated before, and the principle of this unit is simple. The three following numbers are taken according to the even and the odd. They adopt this composition because...

***

It also discusses minor spelling variations in the Greek manuscripts regarding the words for "soul" (psychai) and "head" (kephalai).</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Pythagorean Tetraktys. While the previous page focused on the "additive" Tetraktys (1+2+3+4=10), this page introduces the "multiplicative" Tetraktys, often visualized as a Lambda shape, which explains the mathematical structure of musical intervals and the physical dimensions of the universe.</meta>

# ON THE QUATERNIONS.
<note>original: "DE QUATERNIONIBUS"; Latin for "On the groups of four."</note>

...and the whole of number is neither only even nor only odd. For this reason, two **Tetraktyes** <term>Tetraktys; a sacred Pythagorean grouping of four numbers</term> are taken according to multiplication: one even and one odd. The even one is in a **double ratio** <note>original: "λόγῳ διπλασίῳ" (logō diplasiō); a 2:1 ratio, which in music produces the octave</note>, for 2 is the first of the even numbers and is itself increased from the **unit** <term>unit; also called the "monad," the number 1 which the Pythagoreans viewed as the source of all numbers</term> by doubling. The odd one is increased in a **triple ratio** <note>original: "λόγῳ τριπλασίῳ" (logō triplasiō); a 3:1 ratio</note>, since 3 is the first of the odd numbers and is itself increased from the unit by tripling. 

Consequently, the unit is common to both, being both even and odd. 
<margin>10</margin> The second number among the even and doubled numbers is 2, while among the odd and tripled numbers it is 3. The third among the even numbers is 4, and among the odd numbers it is 9. The fourth among the even numbers is 8, and among the odd numbers it is 27.

->**1**<-
->**2** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **3**<-
->**4** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **9**<-
->**8** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **27**<-
<note>This arrangement is known as the "Platonic Lambda," named for its shape and its appearance in Plato's *Timaeus* to describe the soul of the world.</note>

Within these numbers, the more perfect **ratios** <note>original: "λόγοι" (logoi)</note> of the **concords** <term>concords; "symphōniai," or musical intervals that harmonize, such as the fourth, fifth, and octave</term> are found; the **tone** <note>original: "τόνος" (tonos); the standard whole-step interval</note> is also included in them. 

The unit represents the principle of the **beginning**, the **sign**, and the **point** <note>original: "σημείου καὶ στιγμῆς" (sēmeiou kai stigmēs)</note>. The second numbers, 2 and 3, represent the **side** <note>original: "πλευρὰν" (pleuran); here meaning a one-dimensional line</note>, being uncompounded and prime, measured by the unit and naturally linear. 
<margin>20</margin> The third terms, 4 and 9, represent the **plane square** <note>original: "ἐπίπεδον τετράγωνον" (epipedon tetragonon)</note>, being "equal times equal" <note>i.e., 2x2=4 and 3x3=9, creating a two-dimensional surface</note>. The fourth terms, 8 and 27, represent the **cube** <note>original: "κύβον" (kybon)</note>, being "equal times equal times equal" <note>i.e., 2x2x2=8 and 3x3x3=27, creating a three-dimensional solid</note>. Thus...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "multiplicative" Tetraktys. The previous page established the two sequences of numbers branching from the unit—the even doubling series (1, 2, 4, 8) and the odd tripling series (1, 3, 9, 27)—which together form the seven numbers used by Plato to describe the composition of the World Soul.</meta>

# ON THE GROUPS OF FOUR.
<note>original: "DE QUATERNIONIBUS"; Latin title for a section concerning the Pythagorean Tetraktys.</note>

From these numbers and from this **Tetraktys** <term>Tetraktys; a "group of four," a sacred Pythagorean concept where numbers represent the fundamental levels of reality</term>, the progression from a mark and a point into a solid body takes place. For after the mark and the point comes the side <note>meaning a line or dimension</note>, after the side the plane, and after the plane the solid. It is with these numbers that 
<margin>5</margin> Plato also composes the soul in the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s dialogue (c. 360 BCE) regarding the creation of the universe; specifically referencing section 35B</note>. The last of these seven numbers <note>the number 27</note> is equal to the sum of all those preceding it; for 1 and 2 and 3 and 4 and 8 and 9 become 27. 

Now, these are the two *Tetraktyes*—the one according to addition <note>1+2+3+4=10</note> 
<margin>10</margin> and the one according to multiplication—which encompass the musical, geometric, and arithmetic ratios from which the harmony of the universe was composed. 

There is also a third *Tetraktys* which, following the same analogy, encompasses the nature of every magnitude. For what the **unit** <term>monad; the number 1</term> was in the previous 
<margin>15</margin> *Tetraktys*, the **point** is in this one. And what the numbers capable of forming a side <note>the numbers 2 and 3</note> were in that one, in this one is the dual form of the **line**: the curved and the straight. The straight line corresponds to the **even** <note>original: "ἄρτιον" (artion)</note> because it is bounded by two points; the curved line corresponds to the 
<margin>20</margin> **odd** <note>original: "περιττὸν" (peritton)</note> because it is contained by a single line that has no [distinct] end. And what the numbers capable of forming a square <note>the numbers 4 and 9</note> were in that one, in this one is the dual form of **planes**: the rectilinear and the curvilinear. And what the numbers 
<margin>25</margin> capable of forming a cube <note>the numbers 8 and 27</note> were in that one—there being two, one from an odd number and one from an even—in this one is the **solid**, which is dual: <the one> formed from a hollow surface like a sphere and...

# ON THE GROUPS OF FOUR.
<note>original: "DE QUATERNIONIBUS"</note>

...cylinder, while that which is composed of planes is like the cube or the pyramid. This is the third **tetraktys** <term>Tetraktys; a "group of four," a sacred Pythagorean concept where numbers represent the fundamental levels of reality</term> which completes every magnitude, consisting of the point, the line, the plane, and the solid.
<margin>5</margin> The fourth **tetraktys** is that of the simple <insert>bodies</insert> <note>original: "σωμάτων" (somaton); referring to the four basic elements of the physical world</note>: fire, air, water, and earth. It possesses a proportion that follows the numbers. For what the **unit** <term>monad; the number one as a starting point</term> is in that numerical series, fire is in this one; what the **dyad** <term>the number two</term> is, air is; what the **triad** <term>the number three</term> is, water is; and what the **tetrad** <term>the number four</term> is, earth is. For such is the nature of the elements according to their 
<margin>10</margin> subtlety and density, so that fire has the same ratio to air as 1 has to 2; to water, as 1 to 3; and to earth, as 1 to 4. The other elements also have a proportional relationship to one another. 
The fifth **tetraktys** is that of the shapes of the simple bodies. For the pyramid is the shape of fire, the 
<margin>15</margin> octahedron is the shape of air, the icosahedron of water, and the cube of earth. <note>These are known as the "Platonic Solids." Ancient scientists believed the elements were made of microscopic particles in these geometric shapes.</note>
The sixth is that of things that grow. The seed is analogous to the **unit** and the point; growth in length is analogous to the **dyad** and the line; growth in width to the 
<margin>20</margin> **triad** and the surface; and growth in thickness to the **tetrad** and the solid. 
The seventh **tetraktys** is that of communities. The beginning and "unit," as it were, is the individual person; the **dyad** is the household; the **triad** is the village; and the **tetrad** is the city. For a nation is composed of these parts. 
These, then, are the material and perceptible **tetraktyes**. 
<margin>25</margin> The eighth **tetraktys** is this one, which is a discerning and intelligible sort...

This illustrates the Pythagorean belief that the number four is a fundamental building block of the entire universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the systematic list of the eleven Pythagorean "Tetraktyes" (Groups of Four). Having previously discussed the first six groups—ranging from basic numbers to geometric dimensions and the elements—the text now details the cognitive faculties of the soul, the structure of living beings, the cycles of time, and the stages of human life, concluding with a summary of how these groups correspond to one another.</meta>

-># ON THE GROUPS OF FOUR<-
<note>original Latin title: "DE QUATERNIONIBUS"</note>

...being: **intellect**, **knowledge**, **opinion**, and **sensation**.
**Intellect** <term>nous; the highest faculty of the mind, capable of direct intuition of truth</term> is like the **monad** <term>the unit or number one</term> in its essence.
**Knowledge** is like the **dyad** <term>the number two</term>, since knowledge is always *of* something <note>The dyad represents a relationship between the knower and the known</note>.
<insert>**Opinion** is like the **triad**</insert>, since <unclear>...</unclear> and opinion exists as a middle ground between knowledge and ignorance.
<margin>5</margin> **Sensation** is like the **tetrad** <term>the number four</term>, because it is fourfold—since touch is common to all the senses, and all the senses operate through contact. <note>The author notes that while we usually count five senses, the Pythagoreans often grouped them into four or argued that they all derived from the "touch" of a stimulus. The footnote cites the "Theology of Arithmetic" which explains that touch underlies all sensation.</note>

The ninth **tetraktys** <margin>9th</margin> is that from which the living being is composed: the soul and the body. The parts of the soul are the **rational**, the **spirited**,
<margin>10</margin> and the **appetitive**, and the fourth is the body in which the soul resides. <note>This reflects the famous Platonic division of the soul into three parts—reason, emotion/anger, and desire—with the physical body completing the group of four.</note>

The tenth **tetraktys** <margin>10th</margin> is of the seasons, through which all things come to be: spring, summer, autumn, and winter.

The eleventh is of the ages of life <margin>11th</margin>: the infant, the youth, the man, and the 
<margin>15</margin> old man.

Thus, there are eleven **tetraktyes**. 
The first is according to the composition of numbers; 
the second is according to the multiplication of numbers; 
the third is according to magnitude; 
the fourth is of the simple bodies <note>the four elements: fire, air, water, and earth</note>; 
the fifth is of the shapes; 
the sixth is of things that grow; 
the seventh is of communities; 
the eighth is critical <note>referring to the "critique" or judgment of the mind: intellect, knowledge, opinion, and sensation</note>; 
the ninth is of the parts of the living being;
<margin>20</margin> the tenth is of the seasons; 
and the eleventh is of the ages of life.

All of these possess a proportion to one another. For what the **monad** <note>original: "μονὰς"</note> is in the first and second groups, that the **point** is in the third, **fire** is in the fourth, the **pyramid** is in the fifth, the **seed** is in the sixth, <and> **man** is in the 
<margin>25</margin> seventh, **intellect** is in the eighth, and the rest follow proportionally. For example, the first group consists of the monad, dyad, triad, and tetrad; the second...

<meta>This page continues the systematic list of the eleven Pythagorean "Tetraktyes" (Groups of Four) that began on the previous page. # ON THE GROUPS OF FOUR
<note>original Latin title: DE QUATERNIONIBUS</note>

...[second], the **monad**, **side**, **square**, and **cube**; the third, **point**, **line**, **surface**, and **solid**; the fourth, **fire**, **air**, **water**, and **earth**; the fifth, the **pyramid**, **octahedron**, **icosahedron**, and **cube** <note>These are the "Platonic Solids," the geometric shapes ancient Greeks believed formed the physical elements of the universe</note>; the sixth, **seed**, **length**, **width**, and **depth**; the seventh, **man**, **house**, **village**, and **city**; the eighth, **intellect**, **knowledge**, **opinion**, and **sensation**; the ninth, the **rational**, **spirited**, and **appetitive** <note>The three parts of the human soul in Platonic-Pythagorean thought</note> and the **body**; the tenth, **spring**, **summer**, **autumn**, and **winter**; and the eleventh, the **child**, **youth**, **man**, and **elder**.

The world, which is composed of these **groups of four** <term>tetraktyes; mystical Pythagorean units of four that explain the structure of reality</term>, will be perfect and harmonized according to **geometry**, **musical harmony**, and **number**. It encompasses in its power the entire nature of number, every magnitude, and every body, whether simple or complex. It is perfect because all things are parts of it, but it is itself a part of nothing else. Because of this, the Pythagoreans were said to [use] the previously mentioned oath... <note>A gap exists in the manuscript here; scholars suggest words meaning "to use" or "to swear by" are missing</note> and they said:

->**All things are like unto number.**<-
<note>original Greek: ἀριθμῷ δέ τε πάντ’ ἐπέοικε (arithmō de te pant’ epeoike)</note>

<margin>About the Decade</margin>
And they held this to be the wisest thought: for they brought every number back to the **decade** <term>the number ten</term>, because no number exists beyond ten; for in our counting, we simply return again to the **monad** <term>the unit/one</term>, the **dyad** <term>two</term>, and the following numbers. They believed the **decade** is built upon the **tetrad** <term>the number four</term>; for one, plus two, plus three, plus four equals ten <note>1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10. This was the "holy" secret of the Pythagorean Tetraktys</note>. Thus, the most powerful numbers are seen to be contained within the **number four**.

<margin>About the Monad</margin>
The **monad** <term>the number one, viewed as the divine source of all things</term> is the beginning of all things and the most sovereign...

<summary>This page concludes a list of eleven "groups of four" that structure the universe, from geometry to human society, and explains the Pythagorean belief that the number ten is the perfect number because it is the sum of the first four numbers.</summary>

<keywords>Tetraktys, Monad, Decade, Pythagoreanism, Harmony, Number Theory, Platonic Solids, Four Elements</keywords>

# ON NUMBERS
<note>original: DE NUMERORUM</note>

...[numbers] and from which all things [come], but it itself comes from nothing;
indivisible and potentially all things, unchangeable, never de-
parting from its own nature through multi-
<margin>5</margin> plication; according to which every <term>intelligible; things known by the mind rather than the senses</term> and unbegotten thing [exists], and the
nature of the **Ideas**, and God, and **Mind**, and the **Beautiful**, and
the **Good**, and each of the intelligible essences, such as the
**Beautiful itself**, the **Just itself**, the **Equal itself**; for each of
these is conceived as one and by itself. <note>The author is describing the "Monad" or the number One as the source of all divine and abstract concepts.</note>

The first increase and change is from the monad to the **dyad** <note>the number Two</note>
<margin>10</margin> by the doubling of the monad, according to which [arise] **matter** and all
that is **sensible** <note>perceived by the senses</note> and **generation**, and **motion**, and **increase**,
and **composition**, and **association**, and **relation**.

But the **dyad**, coming together with the monad, becomes the **triad** <note>the number Three</note>,
which is the first to have a beginning, middle, and end. Therefore, it is
<margin>15</margin> also the first said to be "all things"; for things less than it
are not called "all," but rather "one" and "both," while in the case of
the three, they are called "all." And we make three **libations** <note>ritual drink offerings</note>, sig-
nifying that we ask for all good things; and we call those who are
in all respects miserable "**thrice-miserable**," and those who are
<margin>20</margin> in all respects blessed "**thrice-blessed**." The nature of the
**plane surface** also first arises from this. For the triad is like an image of the
plane, and its first manifestation is in the **triangle**; and
because of this, there are three types of them: equilateral, isosceles, and sca- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; it would complete as "scalene."</note>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and metaphysical exploration of the first ten numbers, moving from the definition of the Triad (3) to the Tetrad (4) and the Pentad (5). ### ON PROPERTIES

...[the triad]; and three angles being likened: the right [angle] to the nature of the **One**, being defined and consisting of equality and likeness; for this reason all right angles are equal to one another, being the middle between the acute and the obtuse, and between that which exceeds and that which is
<margin>5</margin> exceeded. <note>original: "ὑπερέχοντος καὶ ὑπερεχομένου" - a reference to how a right angle is a perfect mean between wider and narrower angles.</note> The remaining angles are infinite and indefinite, for they consist of excess and deficiency. And the **Triad**, from the Monad and the Dyad, makes six by addition, which is the first <term>perfect number; a number that is equal to the sum of its proper divisors (1+2+3=6)</term> being equal to its own parts. This perfect number [6], added to the first square,
<margin>10</margin> the **Tetrad** [4], makes the **Decad** <note>The number 10, considered by Pythagoreans to be the most sacred and complete number.</note>.

The **Tetrad** is the image of a **solid** <note>In Pythagorean geometry, 1 is a point, 2 is a line, 3 is a plane, and 4 is the first solid, the tetrahedron.</note> and is the first square number among the even numbers; and all the musical **harmonies** <note>Consonances like the octave (2:1), fifth (3:2), and fourth (4:3) utilize the first four numbers.</note> are completed according to it, as has been shown.

The **Pentad** [5] is the middle of the **Decad**. For if, according to
<margin>15</margin> any combination of two numbers you sum to ten, the five will be found as the middle according to **arithmetic proportion**; for example, 9 and 1, and 8 and 2, and 7 and 3, and 6 and 4; you will always make 10, and five will be found as the middle according to arithmetic proportion, as the diagram shows,
<margin>20</margin> according to every combination of the two numbers completing the ten [the five will be found as the middle according to the arithmetic proportion] <note>This bracketed phrase is a repetition in the original manuscript, likely a scribal error.</note> by exceeding and being exceeded by the same amount as the outer numbers. <note>For example, in the pair 9 and 1, 5 is 4 more than 1 and 4 less than 9.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the geometric and arithmetic properties of the Triad, Tetrad, and Pentad, highlighting the "perfect" nature of the number six and the Pentad's role as the arithmetic mean of the Decad.</summary>
<keywords>Triad, Tetrad, Pentad, perfect number, arithmetic proportion, Pythagorean geometry, right angles, musical harmonies</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and metaphysical exploration of the "first ten numbers" (the Decad). Having discussed the Tetrad (4) and began the Pentad (5) on the previous page, the author now explains how the Pentad serves as the "form of all number" and introduces the Hexad (6) as the first "perfect" number, detailing its unique role in musical and mathematical proportions.</meta>

# ON NUMBERS

| 1 | 4 | 6 |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | 5 | 8 |
| 3 | 6 | 9 |

<image-desc>A circular diagram with a central point labeled 5 (ε). Eight radii extend from the center to the circumference. The points on the circumference are labeled with Greek numerals. Starting from the top and moving clockwise: 1 (α), 6 (ϛ), 7 (ζ), 8 (η), 9 (θ), 4 (δ), 3 (γ), 2 (β). Lines connect opposite points through the center: 1 to 9, 2 to 8, 3 to 7, and 4 to 6.</image-desc>

First, the **Pentad** [5] encompasses the form of every number, both the even and the odd; I mean the **Dyad** [2] and the **Triad** [3]; for the **Monad** [1] was not yet considered a number <note>In Pythagorean thought, the Monad (1) was the 'mother of numbers' but not a number itself, as 'number' was defined as a multitude of units.</note>.
<margin>5</margin> The **Hexad** [6] is a <term>perfect number; a number equal to the sum of its divisors (1+2+3=6)</term>, as has been shown. For this reason, they also called it **Marriage**, since the work of marriage makes offspring similar to their parents <note>Ancient commentators often called 6 'Marriage' because it is formed by the first 'female' (even, 2) and 'male' (odd, 3) numbers: 2 x 3 = 6. It was seen as 'prolific' because its parts (1, 2, 3) regenerate the whole.</note>. And in this number, the **harmonic mean** was first established: if we take 6, it is in a <term>sesquitertial ratio; a 4:3 ratio</term> to 8, and a **double** ratio to 12. Thus: 6, 8, 12. For 8 exceeds the first term and is exceeded by the last term by the same **proportional part** <note>This is the definition of a harmonic mean. 8 exceeds 6 by 2 (which is 1/3 of 6), and 12 exceeds 8 by 4 (which is 1/3 of 12).</note>,
<margin>10</margin> that is, by the third.

Furthermore, an **arithmetic mean** is established when we take 6 as a <term>sesquialter ratio; a 3:2 ratio</term> to 9, and a **double** ratio to 12. For 9 exceeds the first term and is exceeded by the last by the same **number** <note>In an arithmetic mean (6, 9, 12), the difference is a constant value: 9 - 6 = 3, and 12 - 9 = 3.</note>.

Finally, the Hexad produces a **geometric proportion** when taken as the mean:
<margin>15</margin> for if we take its half, which is 3, and its double, which is 12, we will have the geometric proportion 3, 6, 12. For 6 exceeds and is exceeded by the extremes by the same **ratio**, that is, the **double** ratio <note>3 x 2 = 6, and 6 x 2 = 12.</note>.

Line 4: Manuscript A includes the heading 'Concerning the Hexad.' 
Line 5: 'as has been shown' refers to the previous page (p. 101, 8).
Line 8: The word 'ratio' was added carelessly here and in line 12.
Line 14: Possibly should read 'exceeded by a third' as in line 10.</note>

<summary>This page defines the Pentad (5) as the union of even and odd and introduces the Hexad (6) as a "perfect" number and "marriage." It demonstrates how 6 serves as a foundational mean for harmonic, arithmetic, and geometric proportions.</summary>
