--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the numerical properties of the first ten numbers (the Decad). Having previously discussed the Pentad (5) and Hexad (6), the author now transitions to the unique mathematical and mythological status of the Heptad (7), concluding with its relationship to the lunar cycle and the theories of Posidonius.</meta>

# ON THE PROPERTIES OF THE HEPTAD

### ->ON THE CHARACTERISTICS<-

The **Heptad** [7], being part of the **Decad** [10], possesses a marvelous power. For it alone among the numbers within the first ten neither "begets" any other number nor is "begotten" by any other <note>original: "οὔτε γεννᾷ ἕτερον οὔτε γεννᾶται" — In Pythagorean mathematics, "begetting" refers to multiplication. A number is "begotten" if it is the product of two other numbers within the Decad, and it "begets" if it can be multiplied to produce another number within the Decad.</note>. For this reason, the Pythagoreans called it **Athena**; just as the goddess had no mother and remained a virgin, so the number 7 is
<margin>5</margin> neither born of any "coupling" nor does it couple to produce another. For among the numbers within the Decad, some both beget and are begotten: for example, the **Tetrad** [4] begets the **Ogdoad** [8] when coupled with the **Dyad** [2], but is itself begotten by the **Dyad** [2x2=4]. Others are begotten but do not beget, such as the
<margin>10</margin> **Hexad** [6]; it is begotten by the **Dyad** and the **Triad** [2x3=6], but it begets no other number within the Decad <note>Since 6 multiplied by the smallest number, 2, results in 12, which is outside the first ten.</note>. Still others beget but are not begotten, such as the **Triad** [3] and the **Pentad** [5]; they are not begotten from any numerical coupling <note>They are prime numbers.</note>, but they
do beget: the **Triad** produces the **Ennead** [9] and the **Hexad** [with 2], while the **Pentad** begets the **Decad** [10] itself when coupled with the **Dyad**. But the **Heptad** [7] alone neither
<margin>15</margin> begets any number within the Decad through coupling, nor is it begotten from any coupling. Following this natural order, **Plato** also composed the Soul from seven numbers in his *Timaeus* <note>Referring to the 'World Soul' in Plato's dialogue, constructed using two geometric progressions: 1, 2, 4, 8 and 1, 3, 9, 27.</note>. Furthermore, as **Posidonius** says, day and night
<margin>20</margin> possess the nature of the even and the odd; and the month is completed through four **hebdomads** [weeks of 7 days]. In the first week, the moon is seen as a half-circle; in the second, it is the full moon; in the third, it returns to a half-circle; and in the fourth, it makes its conjunction with the sun, forming the beginning of another month.

---

<note>Critical Note: The Greek text at the beginning of the page contains a potential error. Some scholars suggest a missing phrase: "the Heptad... is the arithmetic mean between the Tetrad (4) and the Decad (10)," as 4+10=14, and half of 14 is 7.</note>

<summary>This page explores the unique status of the number 7 (the Heptad) in Pythagorean thought, noting that it is mathematically "sterile" within the first ten numbers and thus associated with the virgin goddess Athena. It also connects the number 7 to Plato's cosmology and the lunar cycle.</summary>

<keywords>Heptad, Seven, Athena, Pythagoras, Decad, begetting, prime numbers, Plato, Timaeus, Posidonius, lunar cycle, astronomy</keywords>

# ON THE PROPERTIES OF NUMBERS

...and growth occurs according to the **Heptad** [7]. For example, the fetus
seems to be fully formed in seven weeks, as **Empedocles** <note>Empedocles (c. 490–430 BCE) was a Pre-Socratic philosopher who proposed that all matter is composed of four elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note>
hints in his poem *Purifications*. Some others say that males
are fully formed in five weeks, but become viable in
<margin>5</margin> seven months; and once born at seven months, they begin to grow teeth,
and they shed these teeth at seven years of age. Semen
and puberty <note>original: "ἥβη" (hēbē) — referring to the onset of sexual maturity.</note> appear in the second week of years [age 14]; the beard generally appears in the
third week [age 21], which is also when one reaches full height;
attainment of full breadth occurs in the fourth week [age 28]. The crises of diseases
<margin>10</margin> also occur every seven days; the more severe stage in all
periodic fevers falls on the seventh day, even in

solstice is seven months <note>original: "μῆνες ἑπτά" — while there are six months between solstices, ancient inclusive counting or specific seasonal theories sometimes reckoned this as seven.</note>; the number of wandering stars [planets] is seven;
and from equinox to equinox is seven months; the
<margin>15</margin> passages [pores] of the head are seven; and the internal organs are seven: the tongue, heart,
lung, liver, spleen, and two kidneys. **Herophilus** <note>Herophilus (c. 335–280 BCE) was an Alexandrian physician often called the "father of anatomy" for his early dissections.</note>
says that the human intestine is 28 cubits long, which is
four weeks [four times seven]; and the tides in the straits
change at most seven times a day.

<margin>20</margin> The **Ogdoad** [8], which is the first **cube** [2x2x2], is composed
of the **Monad** [1] and the **Heptad** [7]. Some say there are eight...

<meta>This page transitions from the properties of the number seven (the Heptad) to the number eight (the Ogdoad), focusing on the theological and cosmological significance of the eightfold division in Orphic poetry and Egyptian inscriptions.</meta>

### ON PROPERTIES

...[that there are eight] gods ruling over all things, as can also be found in the Orphic oaths:

> Verily, the parents of the ever-living immortals are these:
> Fire and Water, Earth and Heaven, and the Moon,
<margin>5</margin> > And the Sun, and great Phanes <note>Phanes (original: "Φανῆς") is a primeval deity in Orphic cosmogony, often representing light and creation; in some traditions, he is equated with the Sun or Mithras.</note>, and black Night.

Evander <note>Evander refers to a historical or mythological writer often cited in late antiquity for "secret" Egyptian or Pythagorean lore.</note> says that on an Egyptian stele there is found an inscription belonging to King Kronos and Queen Rhea: "The eldest king of all, Osiris <note>Osiris is the Egyptian god of the afterlife, but here he is syncretized (blended) with Greek concepts of a primordial king.</note>, to the immortal gods—to Breath and Heaven; and Earth and Night and Day; and to the Father of all that exists and will exist, Eros <note>original: "Ἔρωτι" — here referring to the primordial force of attraction or desire that brings the universe into being.</note>—[dedicates these] memorials of his own excellence <insert>and</insert> the ordering of his life." ### ON THE PROPERTIES OF NUMBERS.

And eight moved in circles within spheres, traveling in a ring
................... around the earth, the ninth. <note>This verse fragment is attributed to Eratosthenes; it describes the ancient geocentric model where eight celestial spheres (the planets and stars) revolve around the Earth, which is the ninth body at the center.</note>
The number nine <note>original: "ἐννέα"</note> is the first square among the odd
numbers. For the first [counting] numbers are the Dyad and the Triad <note>The "Dyad" refers to the number 2, and the "Triad" to 3. In Pythagorean thought, 1 (the Monad) was often considered the "parent" of numbers rather than a number itself.</note>, the former
<margin>5</margin> of even numbers, the latter of odd numbers. For this reason, they
produce the first squares: the one being four (4), and the other nine (9). <note>A square number is the result of a number multiplied by itself: 2x2=4 and 3x3=9.</note>
However, the Decad <note>original: "δεκὰς" — the number ten.</note> limits every number,
containing all of nature within itself: of the even and the
odd, the moving and the immobile, and the good and the
<margin>10</margin> evil. Concerning this, both Archytas <note>Archytas of Tarentum (c. 435–360 BCE) was a Pythagorean philosopher and mathematician.</note> in his work *On the Decad*
and Philolaus <note>Philolaus of Croton (c. 470–385 BCE) was a Pythagorean who argued that the earth was not the center of the universe.</note> in his work *On Nature* discourse at length.
We must now return to the discussion of proportions and
means <note>term: "means" (original: "μεσότητες") — in mathematics, a value that lies between two extremes according to a specific rule, such as the average.</note>. There are several means: the geometric, the
arithmetic, the harmonic, the sub-contrary, the fifth, and the sixth.
<margin>15</margin> There are also said to be another six means that are contrary to these.
Of these, Adrastus <note>Adrastus of Aphrodisias (2nd century CE) was a Peripatetic philosopher who wrote commentaries on Plato and Aristotle.</note> says that only the geometric is strictly called
a "proportion" <note>term: "proportion" (original: "ἀναλογία") — a statement of equality between two ratios.</note> and is primary; for the others
depend upon it, but it does not require them, as he demonstrates in
the following sections. He says that more commonly, the other
<margin>20</margin> means are also called "proportions" by some. Of the strictly
defined proportions—that is, the geometric—some
consist of rational terms and ratios, such as 12, 6, 3, <note>In this geometric sequence, the ratio is 2:1 (12/6 = 2 and 6/3 = 2).</note>

---

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the properties of numbers, transitioning from the cosmological significance of the Decad into the specific mathematical definitions of "means" (medietates) and the foundational role of equality in the theory of proportions. ### ON THE MEANS.

...for they exist in double ratios, and such [causes are found in numbers], while others are in inexpressible <note>original: "ἀρρήτοις" — often translated as 'irrational' in modern mathematics, referring here to ratios that cannot be expressed as a ratio of two integers.</note> and irrational ratios <note>original: "ἀλόγοις"</note> [either in magnitudes or weights or times or some other double or triple or such multiples <margin>5</margin> or superparticulars <note>term: "superparticular" (original: "ἐπιμορίοις") — a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part of it, such as 3:2 (a whole plus a half) or 4:3 (a whole plus a third).</note>]. For the geometric mean, as we said, is that which exceeds one extreme and is exceeded by the other in the same ratio; <note>For example, in the sequence 2, 4, 8, the ratio 4:2 is the same as 8:4.</note> while the arithmetic mean is that which exceeds and is exceeded by the same number; <note>For example, in 2, 4, 6, the difference is always 2.</note> and the harmonic mean is that which exceeds and is exceeded by the same part of the extremes. <note>For example, in 3, 4, 6, the middle term 4 exceeds 3 by 1 (which is 1/3 of the extreme 3) and is exceeded by 6 by 2 (which is 1/3 of the extreme 6).</note>
<margin>10</margin> It is demonstrated that the ratio of equality <note>The ratio of 1:1.</note> is the leader and the first element of all the aforementioned ratios and the proportions based upon them; for from this first ratio all things are composed and into this all things are analyzed, both in the case of ratios and proportions.

<margin>15</margin> Eratosthenes <note>Eratosthenes of Cyrene (c. 276–194 BCE), the famous librarian of Alexandria known for calculating the Earth's circumference.</note> says that every ratio increases either by interval or by its terms; but it is a property of equality that it does not share in "interval"; it is clear, then, that it will increase by the terms alone. Having taken three magnitudes and the proportion within them, we shall move the <margin>20</margin> terms. And we shall show that everything in the mathematical sciences is composed from the proportion of certain quantities, and that the nature of proportion is their principle and element.

As for the proofs, Eratosthenes says he will omit them. However, Adrastus <note>Adrastus of Aphrodisias (2nd century CE), a Peripatetic philosopher whose commentaries on Plato’s Timaeus are a primary source for this text.</note> demonstrates more clearly that <margin>25</margin> when three terms are set out in any proportion whatever, if...

<meta>The Latin apparatus at the bottom of the page notes several textual variations: in line 2, the word "in" was added above the line in Manuscript A; in line 4, the French astronomer Ismaël Bullialdus suggested deleting the phrase about double and triple ratios; and in line 10, Manuscript A includes a specific sub-heading: "On equality, that it is the origin of proportions and how it becomes multiple."</meta>

<summary>This page defines the three classical mathematical means (geometric, arithmetic, and harmonic) and argues that the ratio of equality (1:1) is the foundational element from which all other mathematical proportions are derived.</summary>

### ON GENERATION
<note>original: "DE GENERATIONE" — Here meaning the "birth" or systematic derivation of mathematical ratios.</note>

...if three other terms are taken, formed from these: the first equal to the first, the second composed of the first and second, and the third from one first, two seconds, and the third, the terms taken in this way will again be in proportion. And from the <margin>5</margin> proportion in equal terms is generated the proportion in double ratios <note>original: "διπλασίοις" — ratios of 2:1.</note>, and from the double the triple <note>original: "τριπλασίοις" — ratios of 3:1.</note>, and from that the quadruple <note>original: "τετραπλασίοις" — ratios of 4:1.</note>, and so on with the other multiples. For example, let there be set out in three equal terms the smallest proportion of equality, that is, <margin>10</margin> three units. But let there also be taken three other terms in the aforementioned manner: the first from the first [1], the second from the first and the second [1+1=2], and the third from the first, two of the second, and the third [1+2+1=4]. The result will be 1, 2, 4, which are in a double ratio. Again, from these let others be constructed in <margin>15</margin> the same way: the first from the first [1], the second from the first and the second [1+2=3], and the third from the first, two of the second, and the third [1+4+4=9]. The result will be 1, 3, 9, which are in a triple ratio. Similarly, from these will be constructed 1, 4, 16 in a quadruple ratio, and from those 1, 5, 25 in a quintuple ratio, and <margin>20</margin> so on to infinity among the successive multiples.

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 1 |
| 1 | 2 | 4 |
| 1 | 3 | 9 |
| 1 | 4 | 16 |
| 1 | 5 | 25 |
| 1 | 6 | 36 |
| 1 | 7 | 49 |
| 1 | 8 | 64 |
| 1 | 9 | 81 |
| 1 | 10 | 100 |

Line 4 "of the" [τῆς] corrected from "of those" [τῶν] in A. Line 12 "the third from the first and twice the second" was added by Bullialdus to complete the sense. Line 17 "9" [θ] was corrected from "4" [δ] in manuscript A.</note>

It demonstrates the Neopythagorean belief that all complexity in ratios originates from the principle of equality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of how all mathematical ratios are generated from one another. While the previous page showed how multiple ratios (like 2:1 or 3:1) are born from equality (1:1), this page explains how "superparticular" ratios (like 3:2 or 4:3) are generated by reversing those multiples and applying the same additive rule.</meta>

# OF PROPORTIONS
<note>Latin: PROPORTIONUM</note>

From the multiple ratios, when they are arranged in reverse order [1, 1, 1] <note>original: "α' α' α'" — referring back to the three-term starting point.</note> and constructed in the same manner, the <term>superparticular ratios</term> <note>original: "ἐπιμόριοι λόγοι" — ratios where the larger number contains the smaller once plus one aliquot part (e.g., 3:2, 4:3).</note> and the proportions within them will be established. From doubles come <term>hemiolics</term> <note>original: "ἡμιόλιοι" — also known as sesquialter, the ratio of 3:2.</note>, from triples come <term>sesquitertians</term> <note>original: "ἐπίτριτοι" — the ratio of 4:3.</note>, from quadruples come <term>sesquiquartals</term> <note>original: "ἐπιτέταρτοι" — the ratio of 5:4.</note>, and so on in order. For example, <margin>5</margin> let there be a proportion in a double ratio of three terms, with the largest placed first, and let three others be constructed from these in the stated manner: 4, 2, 1. Those resulting from them will be 4, 6, 9; this becomes a proportion in hemiolic ratios. Next, let there be three terms in a proportion of <margin>10</margin> triples: 9, 3, 1. In the same way, three terms in a proportion of sesquitertians will be established from these: 9, 12, 16. From quadruples <note>Starting with 16, 4, 1</note> will be established sesquiquartals: 16, 20, 25; and so on forever, from the subsequent multiples come the next ratios of the same name.

| 4 | 2 | 1 |
|---|---|---|
| 4 | 6 | 9 |
| 9 | 3 | 1 |
| 9 | 12 | 16 |
| 16 | 20 | 25 |
| 25 | 30 | 36 |
| 36 | 42 | 49 |
| 49 | 56 | 64 |
| 64 | 72 | 81 |
| 81 | 90 | 100 |

From the superparticulars <margin>15</margin> come the <term>superpartient</term> <note>original: "ἐπιμερεῖς" — ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part, such as 5:3 (one plus two-thirds).</note> and the <term>multiple-superparticular</term> <note>original: "πολλαπλασιεπιμόριοι" — ratios combining a multiple and a fraction, like 2 1/2 (5:2).</note> ratios. And from the superpartients come other superpartients and <term>multiple-superpartient</term> <note>original: "πολλαπλασιεπιμερεῖς" — such as 2 2/3 (8:3).</note> ratios. Most of these should be omitted as they are not strictly necessary, though a few should be examined. For from the hemiolic proportion, <margin>20</margin> using the stated method and beginning from the largest term, a proportion in the <term>double-epitritic</term> <note>original: "δισεπιτρίτοις" — a specific superpartient ratio of 5:3, which contains the whole plus two-thirds.</note> superpartient ratios is established. <meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of the generation of mathematical proportions. While the previous page showed how superparticular ratios (like 3:2) are derived from multiple ratios (like 2:1), this page extends the method to more complex "mixed" ratios—multiple-superparticular and superpartient ratios—and concludes with the theorem that all mathematical inequality can be reduced back to equality.</meta>

# ON GENERATION
<note>Latin: DE GENERATIONE</note>

...9, 6, 4. From these, according to the stated method, 9, 15, 25 are constituted. However, when starting from the smaller term <note>The sequence 4, 6, 9</note>, a <term>multiple-superparticular proportion</term> <note>original: "πολλαπλασιεπιμόριος ἀναλογία" — a ratio that contains a smaller number multiple times plus one part of it.</note> will result, specifically the <term>double-hemiolic</term> <note>original: "διπλασιημιόλιος" — the ratio 5:2, or 2 1/2.</note>. For example, let 4, 6, 9 be set out; from these, <margin>5</margin> according to the same method, come 4, 10, 25. 

From the <term>sesquitertian</term> ratios <note>original: "ἐπιτρίτοις" — 4:3 ratios.</note>, when starting from the larger term, the proportion will be <term>superpartient</term> <note>original: "ἐπιμερὴς ἀναλογία" — where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part of it (e.g., 1 + 3/4).</note>, specifically the tri-sesquiquartal <note>original: "τρισεπιτέταρτος" — the ratio 7:4.</note>. For example, from 16, 12, 9 will come 16, 28, 49. But when starting from the smaller term, the proportion will be multiple-superparticular, <margin>10</margin> specifically the <term>double-sesquitertian</term> <note>original: "διπλασιεπίτριτος" — the ratio 7:3, or 2 1/3.</note> found in the numbers 9, 21, 49.

From the <term>sesquiquartal</term> ratios <note>original: "ἐπιτετάρτοις" — 5:4 ratios.</note>, starting from the larger term, the superpartient proportion will be the tetra-sesquiquintal <note>original: "τετράκις ἐπίπεμπτος" — the ratio 9:5, or 1 4/5.</note>; for example, from 25, 20, 16 will come 25, 45, 81. But when starting from the smaller term, it will be the multiple-superparticular known as the <margin>15</margin> <term>double-sesquiquartal</term> <note>original: "διπλασιεπιτέταρτος" — the ratio 9:4, or 2 1/4.</note>; for example, from 16, 20, 25 will come 16, 36, 81. This order proceeds in this manner to infinity. From these, others are fashioned according to the same logic, about which it is not necessary to speak at length.

<margin>5</margin> All such proportions and all the <margin>20</margin> ratios within them—just as they consist of the ratio of equality as their first principle—so also they are resolved back into this as their last. For if, from any proportion in three unequal terms, we subtract the smallest from the middle, and from the largest we subtract the smallest and two <margin>25</margin> of what remained when the smallest was subtracted from the middle... <note>This describes the "reverse" of the additive process to return any ratio back to 1:1:1.</note>

---

<note>The marginal note **ε̄** (5) marks the beginning of a new chapter or theorem: "That all proportions are resolved into equality."</note>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical discussion from page 110 regarding the relationship between ratios. While the previous page focused on how complex ratios are "synthesized" or built up from simple equality (1:1), this page describes the "analysis" or breaking down of these ratios back to their original state of equality. It also transitions from the study of numbers (arithmetic) to the study of shapes (geometry), using the same principles of derivation.</meta>

# OF PROPORTIONS
<note>Latin: PROPORTIONUM</note>

...of this smallest term, let us arrange the resulting numbers in order: first the smaller one itself, then the remainder left from the middle term, and finally the remainder left from the last term. The <term>proportion</term> <note>original: "ἀναλογία" (analogia)</note> dissolved in this way will be analyzed <margin>5</margin> back into the one before it from which it was composed. If this process is performed continually, the <term>analysis</term> <note>The reverse process of breaking a complex ratio down into simpler parts.</note> will arrive at the final proportion of <term>equality</term> <note>The ratio 1:1, considered the "mother" of all other ratios.</note>, from which all others were first composed. Equality itself is no longer reducible into any other, but only into the ratio of equality.

<margin>10</margin> **Eratosthenes** <note>Eratosthenes of Cyrene (c. 276–194 BCE), the polymath and librarian at Alexandria, famous for measuring the Earth's circumference.</note> demonstrates that even all geometric figures are composed of certain proportions, starting their composition from equality and being resolved back into equality; however, it is not necessary to speak of these things at present.

The same principles will be found to apply to figures as well. Of these, <margin>15</margin> the first is the **point** <note>original: "στιγμή" (stigme)</note>, which is a sign without magnitude or dimension, the limit of a line—functioning like a unit that has been given a position. Of magnitude, that which is extended and divisible in one direction is the **line**, which is length without breadth. That which is extended in two directions is the **surface**, having length and breadth. That which is extended in three directions is the **solid**, <margin>20</margin> having length, breadth, and depth. The solid is contained and bounded by surfaces, the surface by lines, and the line by points. Of lines, the **straight line** is right and, as it were, stretched; it is the shortest distance between two given points <margin>25</margin> among all lines having the same boundaries, and it lies equally with respect to its own points... <note>original: "ἐξ ἴσου τοῖς ἑαυτῆς σημείοις" — this mirrors Euclid's famous definition of a straight line.</note>

<summary>This page explains how mathematical ratios can be reduced back to a state of equality and introduces basic geometric definitions (point, line, surface, solid) as extensions of these proportional principles.</summary>
<keywords>analysis, equality, Eratosthenes, geometry, point, line, surface, solid, straight line, mathematical foundations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the basic elements of geometry—points and lines—introduced at the end of page 110. It moves from defining the nature of lines (straight vs. curved) into the definition of surfaces and planes, eventually categorizing two-dimensional shapes (polygons) and beginning the discussion on three-dimensional solids.</meta>

# ON FIGURES
<note>original: "DE FIGURIS"</note>

...placed <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing the definition of a straight line as one placed evenly between its points.</note> between points; while a **curved** line is that which is not so. A **plane** <note>original: "ἐπίπεδον" (epipedon)</note> also differs from a **surface** in a similar way. For a **surface** is the outward limit of every solid body, appearing in two dimensions: length and width.
<margin>5</margin> A **plane**, however, is a "straight" surface; it is that which, if a straight line touches two of its points, the line fits <note>That is, the line lies completely flat against the surface.</note> entirely upon it. **Parallel lines** are those which, being in the same plane and extended to infinity, do not meet in either direction, but maintain their distance from each other everywhere.
<margin>10</margin> Of figures, **plane figures** are those which have all their lines in the same plane; and **rectilinear** <note>original: "εὐθύγραμμα" (euthygramma), literally 'straight-lined'.</note> figures are those bounded by straight lines, while those not so formed are not rectilinear. Of plane and rectilinear figures, those bounded by three sides are called **trilateral** <note>Triangles.</note>,
<margin>15</margin> those by four sides are **quadrilateral**, and those by more are **polygonal**. Of the quadrilaterals, those having their opposite sides parallel to each other are called **parallelograms**. Of these, **rectangular** figures are those having their angles right; and **right angles** are those which
<margin>20</margin> a straight line, standing upon another straight line, makes equal to each other on both sides. Of these rectangular parallelograms, each is said to be bounded specifically by the sides that contain the right angle. Of these, those having all four sides equal are specifically called
<margin>25</margin> **squares** <note>original: "τετράγωνα" (tetragona)</note>, while those that are not so are called **oblongs** <note>original: "ἑτερομήκη" (heteromeke), literally 'other-length', referring to rectangles with unequal sides.</note>.
Similarly, of the **solids**, those bounded by plane parallelograms—which are six in total—are bounded...
<margin>26</margin> <insert>Concerning solids</insert> <note>This marginal insertion serves as a new section heading in the original manuscript.</note>

***

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on geometry. Having defined points, lines, planes, and polygons, the author now completes the categorization of three-dimensional solids before transitioning into the mathematical theory of "means" (proportions), which he describes as essential for understanding Plato’s philosophy.</meta>

# ON MEANS
<note>original: "DE MEDIETATIBUS"</note>

...are called **parallelepipeds** <note>original: "ἀλληλεπίπεδα" (allepipeda); solid figures bounded by six parallelograms, where opposite faces are parallel.</note>, and those among these formed by right angles are called **rectangular**. Of these, those that are equal on all sides—that is, having equal length, width, and depth, and bounded by six equal squares—are **cubes**. 
<margin>5</margin> Those that have equal length and width (meaning square bases) but a lesser height are called **tiles** <note>original: "πλινθίδες" (plinthides); also translated as "bricks".</note>. Those that have equal length and width but a greater height are called **beams** <note>original: "δοκίδες" (dokides); or "pillars".</note>. Finally, those that are unequal on all sides are called **scalene** <note>original: "σκαληνά" (skalena); a term used for solids where every dimension—length, width, and depth—is different.</note>.

A more precise account must be given regarding **means** <note>original: "μεσοτήτων" (mesoteton); often translated as "medieties" or "proportions".</note>, 
<margin>10</margin> since the study of these is absolutely essential for **Platonic** teachings. Simply put, a **mean** is when, between two unequal terms of the same kind, another term of the same kind is taken. This is done so that the ratio between the excess of the first and greater term over the middle term, and the excess 
<margin>15</margin> of the middle term over the lesser term, is the same as the ratio of the first term either to itself, or to one of the others—or conversely, the ratio of the lesser term to one of the others.

Specifically, the **arithmetic mean** is that which exceeds one of the extremes by the same number by which 
<margin>20</margin> it is exceeded by the other. For example: **3, 2, 1**. For the number **2** exceeds **1** by a unit, and is itself exceeded by **3** by a unit. It is a property of this mean that it is equal to half the sum of the extremes; for the **3** and 
<margin>25</margin> the **1** added together make **4**, which is double the middle number, **2**.

<summary>This page concludes a classification of three-dimensional solids (cubes, tiles, and beams) and introduces the theory of mathematical means, specifically defining the arithmetic mean through the example of the sequence 3, 2, 1.</summary>
<keywords>parallelepipeds, cubes, arithmetic mean, Platonic philosophy, solid geometry, mathematical proportions, Theon of Smyrna</keywords>

### ON MEANS
<note>original Latin: "DE MEDIETATIBUS"</note>

<margin>1</margin> The **geometric mean** <note>original: "γεωμετρικὴ μεσότης" (geometrike mesotes)</note> is that which is also properly called **proportion** <note>original: "ἀναλογία" (analogia)</note>. It is defined by exceeding and being exceeded by the same **ratio** <note>original: "λόγῳ" (logo)</note>, such as a **multiple** <note>original: "πολλαπλασίῳ"; a ratio like 2:1 or 3:1</note> or a **superparticular** <note>original: "ἐπιμορίῳ"; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part of it, like 3:2</note>. For example: **1, 2, 4**. Here, 4 is the double of 2, and 2 is the double of 1.
<margin>5</margin> Furthermore, the excess of 2 over 1 is 1 <insert>and the excess of 4 over 2 is 2</insert>; when these are examined similarly, they are found to be in a **double ratio** <note>1 is half of 2, just as 2 is half of 4</note>. It is a property of this proportion that the **product of the extremes** <note>the outer numbers in the sequence</note> is equal to the **square of the middle term**. For example, the extremes
<margin>10</margin> multiplied by each other produce 4 (for 1 times 4 is 4); and again, the middle term 2 multiplied by itself produces 4 (for 2 times 2 is 4). Thus, the product of the extremes becomes equal to the square of the middle: **1, 2, 4**.

The **harmonic proportion** <note>original: "ἁρμονικὴ ἀναλογία" (harmonike analogia)</note> occurs when, out of three
<margin>15</margin> set terms, the ratio that the first has to the third is the same ratio that the excess of the first over the second has to the excess of the second over the third. For example: **6, 3, 2**. For 6 is the **triple** of 2; and the excess of 6
<margin>19</margin> over 3 (which is 3) is triple the unit <note>1</note>,
<margin>20</margin> which is the excess of 3 when compared to 2. It is a property of this proportion that the middle term exceeds and is exceeded by the same **fraction** <note>original: "μέρει" (merei); literally "part"</note> of the extremes. For example: **6, 3, 2**. Indeed, 6 exceeds 3 by its own half <note>half of 6 is 3</note>, and 2 is exceeded by 3 by its own half <note>half of 2 is 1</note>.
<margin>25</margin> Also, if we add the extremes together and multiply them by the middle term, we find the result is double the product of the extremes...

### ON PROPORTIONS
<note>original Latin: DE MEDIETATIBUS</note>

...having become a multiple. For example, 6 and 2 [make] 8; these, when multiplied by 3, which is the mean <note>The "mean" is the middle term in the sequence 6, 3, 2</note>, become 24; and again, twice 6 is 12; and of these, 24 is double.

<margin>5</margin> A mean is called **sub-contrary** <note>original: "ὑπεναντία" (hypenantia)</note> to the harmonic when, as the third term is to the first, so is the excess of the first to the excess of the second. For example: **6, 5, 3**. Now, the 6 exceeds the 5 by a unit (1), and the 5 exceeds the 3 by two; and the 3 is half <note>original: "ὑποδιπλάσιά" (hypodiplasia); a 1:2 ratio</note> of the 6. But also the unit, 
<margin>10</margin> being the excess of the first number, is half of the two, which is the excess of the second number.

The **fifth mean** occurs when, among three terms, whatever ratio the third has to the second, this same ratio the excess of the first has to the
<margin>15</margin> excess of the second. For example: **5, 4, 2**. For 5 exceeds 4 by a unit (1), but 4 also exceeds 2 by two; and 2 is half of 4. And the 1 is half of the 2, which are the excesses of the first and 
the second numbers respectively.

<margin>20</margin> The **sixth** is called a mean when, three terms being set forth, as the second is to the first, so is the excess of the first to the excess of the second. For example: **6, 4, 1**. For 6 exceeds 4 by two, while 4 exceeds 1 by three. Now, 4 is two-thirds <note>original: "ὑφημιόλια" (hyphemiolia); a 2:3 ratio</note> of 6; and the two, which is the excess...

# HOW THEY ARE FOUND
<note>original Latin header: QUOMODO INVENIANTUR</note>

...[the excess] of the 6 and 4 is two-thirds <note>original: "ὑφημιολία" (hyphemiolia); a 2:3 ratio</note> of the 3, which is the excess of the 4 <note>the difference between the second term, 4, and the third term, 1</note>.

About these means and the six means opposite to them, much more has been said by the **Pythagoreans** <term>Pythagoreans: followers of Pythagoras who believed numbers were the fundamental reality of the universe</term>.
<margin>5</margin> But for our purposes, it is enough to have collected them in outline and summary form, according to the Pythagorean method, for the sake of a general overview of **mathematics**.

The means are found in the **arithmetic proportion** <note>original: "ἀναλογίαν" (analogian)</note> in this way: by adding half of the excess of the greater
<margin>10</margin> over the smaller to the smaller number, we will have the mean; or, by adding together the halves of each of the given numbers, we find the resulting mean; or, by taking half of the sum of both numbers [<insert>so as to find what is useful for Platonic matters as well</insert>]. Suppose it is
<margin>15</margin> required to find the middle term of two numbers, 12 and 6, according to the **arithmetic mean**. We take the excess of the greater over the smaller, which is 6; half of this is 3. Let us add this to the smaller number: it becomes 9, which is the mean of 12 and 6, exceeding [6] by three and 
<margin>20</margin> being exceeded [by 12] by three: **12, 9, 6**. Again, let us add the original extremes, 12 and 6: the sum is 18. Half of this is 9, which is the mean.

According to the **geometric proportion**, in the case of numbers, by taking the **square root** <note>original: "πλευρὰν τετράγωνου" (pleuran tetragonou); literally "the side of a square"</note> of the product of the extremes, we will have the middle term. For
<margin>25</margin> example, let two numbers be given, 24 and 6. Suppose...

# MEANS
<note>original Latin header: MEDIETATES</note>

...[it is] ordered to find the **middle term** <note>original: "μέσον ὅρος" (meson horos)</note> of these according to the **geometric proportion**. Let the given numbers be multiplied by one another: the result is 144. Let the **square root** <note>literally "square side" (πλευρὰ τετράγωνος)</note> of these be taken: it will be 12, which becomes the mean. For it is
<margin>5</margin> as 24 is to 12, so 12 is to 6, in a **double ratio** <term>double ratio: a ratio of 2:1</term>. But if the product of the extremes is a square number, the middle term found in this way becomes **rational** <term>rational: ῥητός (rhētos); a number expressible as a whole number or a ratio of whole numbers</term> and **commensurable in length** with the extremes, being found from whole units. If, however, the product
<margin>10</margin> of the extremes is not a square, the middle term will be **commensurable in power** <note>original: "δυνάμει μόνον ἔσται σύμμετρος" (dynamei monon estai symmetros); meaning the squares of the numbers share a common measure, though the lengths themselves are irrational relative to each other</note> only with the extremes.

This is more generally obtained in numbers, [and] rational values, and in ratios and magnitudes [and] commensurables, geometrically in this way: Let there be two terms for which it is necessary to
<margin>15</margin> find a **geometric mean** <note>literally "middle proportional" (μέσον ἀνάλογον)</note>; for example, AB and BC, and let them be placed in a straight line. About the whole line AC let a semicircle be drawn. From point B let a line BD be drawn up at right angles to AC as far as the
<margin>20</margin> circumference. This line BD becomes the mean of AB and BC according to the geometric proportion. For when AD and DC are joined, the angle at D becomes a **right angle**, since it is in a semicircle. And in the right-angled triangle ADC, DB is the **perpendicular** <note>original: "κάθετος" (kathetos)</note>; and the triangles around it are similar both to the whole and to one another. Consequently, the sides about their equal angles...

# HOW THEY MAY BE FOUND
<note>original Latin header: QUOMODO INVENIANTUR</note>

...they are proportional: as **AB** is to **BD**, so is **DB** to **BC**. Therefore, **BD** is the **mean proportional** <note>original: "μέση ἀνάλογον" (mesē analogon)</note> between **AB** and **BC**; which is what was to be shown.

->[Diagram: A semicircle is drawn on diameter AC (labeled αβγ). A vertical line BD (βδ) rises from the diameter to the circumference. Lines AD and DC are drawn to form a right-angled triangle inscribed in the semicircle.]<-

It remains to show how we might find the **middle term** <note>original: "μέσον ὅρος" (meson horos)</note> according to the **harmonic proportion**. 
<margin>5</margin> If, on the one hand, the extremes are given in a **double ratio** <term>double ratio: a ratio of 2:1</term> to each other, such as **12** <note>original: "ιβ'"</note> and **6** <note>original: "ς'"</note>, we take the excess of the greater over the lesser (which is **6**) and multiply it by the **6**; then, **applying** <term>applying: "παραβαλόντες" (parabalontes); a geometric method of division where an area is "applied" to a line to find the resulting width</term> the resulting **36** <note>original: "λς'"</note> to the sum of the extremes (which is 
<margin>10</margin> **18** <note>original: "ιη'"</note>), and adding the **width** <note>original: "πλάτος" (platos); the result of the division/application</note> of the **36** (which is **2** <note>original: "β'"</note>) to the lesser term (that is, to the **6**), we shall have the value sought. For the resulting **8** <note>original: "η'"</note> will exceed one extreme and be exceeded by the other by the same part of the extremes—that is, by a **third** of each extreme: **12, 8, 6**. <note>12 minus 8 is 4, which is 1/3 of 12; 8 minus 6 is 2, which is 1/3 of 6. This is the definition of a harmonic mean.</note> 
<margin>15</margin> If, on the other hand, the extremes are given in a **triple ratio** <term>triple ratio: a ratio of 3:1</term> to each other, such as **18** <note>original: "ιη'"</note> and **6** <note>original: "ς'"</note>, we shall multiply the excess of the greater over the lesser by itself: **12** times **12** becomes **144** <note>original: "ρμδ'"</note>. Half of these is **72** <note>original: "οβ'"</note>, which we **apply** to the sum of the extremes (which is **24** <note>original: "κδ'"</note>); the width of the application is **3** <note>original: "γ'"</note>...

<meta>This page concludes the specific numerical examples for finding the harmonic mean (providing the mean of 9 for the extremes 18 and 6) and then provides a general formula. It marks the transition from the mathematical (arithmetic and geometric) portion of the treatise to the section on astronomy.</meta>

# MEANS
<note>original Latin header: MEDIETATES</note>

...by adding [this width] to the smaller term, we shall have the sought-for **mean term** <note>original: "ὅρον μέσον" (horon meson)</note> between the terms given from the beginning, namely **9** <note>original: "θ'"</note>. This term will exceed [the smaller] and be exceeded [by the larger] by half of the extremes: **18, 9, 6**. <note>The difference between 18 and 9 is 9 (half of 18); the difference between 9 and 6 is 3 (half of 6).</note>

In a more general sense, for any two unequal terms given, the **harmonic mean** <note>original: "μέσον ἁρμονικῶς" (meson harmonikōs)</note> should be taken as follows:
<margin>5</margin> The excess [of the greater over the smaller] must be multiplied by the smaller term, and the product must be **applied** <term>applied: "παραβλητέον" (parablēteon); a Greek mathematical term for dividing an area by a line to find the width</term> to the sum of the extremes. Then, the **width** <term>width: "πλάτος" (platos); the result or quotient of the application/division</term> of this application must be added to the smaller term.

For example, let two terms be taken, **12** <note>original: "ιβ'"</note> and **4** <note>original: "δ'"</note>. The excess of 12 [over 4],
<margin>10</margin> which is **8** <note>original: "η'"</note>, should be multiplied by the smaller term, which is 4; this becomes **32** <note>original: "λβ'"</note>. This 32 must be applied to the sum of the extremes, which is **16** <note>original: "ις'"</note>. <insert>Then we must add the width of the application,</insert> which is **2** <note>original: "β'"</note>, to the smaller term, which is 4. The result will be **6** <note>original: "ϛ'"</note>, which is the harmonic mean of 12 and 4.
<margin>15</margin> It exceeds and is exceeded by the same part of the extremes—that is, by half of each extreme: **12, 6, 4**. <note>The difference between 12 and 6 is 6 (half of 12); the difference between 6 and 4 is 2 (half of 4).</note>

These things, then, are the most necessary and useful among the aforementioned mathematical subjects, presented as a summary tradition to assist in the **reading of the Platonic works** <note>original: "Πλατωνικῶν ἀνάγνωσιν" (Platōnikōn anagnōsin)</note>.
<margin>20</margin> It remains to mention, in an elementary way, the principles of **astronomy** <note>original: "ἀστρονομίαν" (astronomian)</note>.

-># ON THE SHAPE<-
<note>original Latin header: DE FORMA</note>

It is necessary to establish before all other things that the entire **cosmos** <note>original: "κόσμος" (kosmos); refers to the universe as an ordered, beautiful whole</note> is spherical, and that the earth is at its center, being itself spherical as well. In relation to the whole, the earth holds the ratio of a **center** <term>center: the mathematical point in the middle of a circle</term> in its position, and the ratio of a **point** <term>point: a magnitude so small it has no parts</term> in its size.
<margin>5</margin> A more precise account of these matters requires a longer investigation and many arguments; however, for a general overview of the teachings that are about to be handed down, it will suffice merely to recall the points provided in summary by **Adrastus** <note>Adrastus of Aphrodisias, a Peripatetic philosopher whose works on astronomy and music heavily influenced later writers like Theon</note>.
<margin>10</margin> It is clear that the cosmos is spherical and the earth is spherical—holding the position of a center and the size of a point relative to the whole—from the fact that all the risings <insert>and</insert> settings of the heavenly bodies, their circuits, and their subsequent risings, occur in
<margin>15</margin> the same places for those living in the same regions. This is also shown by the fact that from every part of the earth, half of the heaven (as far as our perception can tell) is seen raised high above us, while the rest remains unseen beneath the earth because the earth blocks our view. Furthermore, from every **vantage point** <note>original: "ὄψεως" (opseōs); referring here to the observer's line of sight</note>, all the
<margin>20</margin> straight lines falling upon the outermost heaven appear equal. Additionally, among the stars located diametrically opposite one another on the **great circles** <term>great circles: circles that bisect the sphere, like the celestial equator or the ecliptic</term>, as they are paired together, one is always seen at its rising while the other is at its setting. If the universe possessed a conical, cylindrical, pyramidal, or any other solid shape
<margin>25</margin> besides the spherical, these phenomena would not occur in relation to the earth; rather, the part of the heaven above the earth—and the lines extending toward it—would be found sometimes larger and sometimes smaller...

---

<note>Scholar’s notes on the text:
Line 1: The manuscript (B) includes the title "Theon of Smyrna: On the mathematical knowledge useful for reading Plato."
Line 10: Compare this argument to Chalcidius, sections 59 and following.
Line 18: A textual variant suggests adding "to be" [εἶναι] after "beneath the earth."</note>

<summary>Theon begins his astronomical section by asserting the sphericity of both the universe and the earth, citing the Peripatetic philosopher Adrastus. He provides several geometric and observational proofs, such as the visibility of exactly half the sky and the simultaneous rising and setting of opposite stars.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, cosmos, spherical earth, Adrastus, celestial mechanics, geometry of the universe, Theon of Smyrna</keywords>

# OF THE EARTH.
<note>original Latin: TERRAE</note>

...[surface] from the earth, the straight lines are of unequal magnitude. The **spherical shape** <term>spherical shape: a perfectly round, three-dimensional form like a globe</term> of the earth is demonstrated from East to West by the fact that the risings and settings of the same stars occur sooner for eastern **climates** <note>original: "κλίμασι" (klimasi); refers to latitudinal zones or geographical regions</note>, but later for those toward the West.
<margin>5</margin> Furthermore, one and the same **lunar eclipse** <term>lunar eclipse: an event where the moon passes into the earth's shadow</term>, which takes place at a single, brief moment and is seen by everyone simultaneously for whom it is possible, appears at different times according to the local hours; it always appears later to those further east. This is due to the **curvature** <note>original: "περιφέρειαν" (periphereian)</note> of the earth, as the sun does not shine upon all climates at once,
<margin>10</margin> and the shadow cast from the earth shifts accordingly, this occurring at night. It also appears curved from the **arctic** <note>original: "ἀρκτικῶν" (arktikōn); referring to the far North</note> and northern regions toward the southern and meridian ones. Indeed, for those traveling in this direction, many of the stars that are always visible around our **elevated pole** <note>The North Pole, which remains high in the sky for northern observers</note> are seen to rise and set as the traveler moves toward the
<margin>15</margin> southern regions; likewise, some of the stars that are always hidden in the region concealed from us appear to rise and set. This is seen with the star called **Canopus** <note>One of the brightest stars in the sky, visible only from southern latitudes in antiquity</note>; it is invisible in the regions further north than
<margin>20</margin> **Knidos** <note>An ancient Greek city in Asia Minor known for its school of medicine and astronomy</note>, but becomes visible to those south of it, and increasingly more visible the further south one goes. Conversely, for those arriving from southern regions to the north, many stars behind them that previously rose and set become entirely invisible,
<margin>25</margin> while some of those near **the Bears** <note>original: "ἄρκτους" (arktou); referring to the constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor, which mark the North</note> that similarly rose and set become permanently visible as they proceed, and even more so for those who advance further. Thus, seen from every side, the earth's surface is curved...

# ON SHAPE
<note>original Latin: DE FORMA</note>

...and the earth would be spherical. Furthermore, since those things which possess weight move naturally toward the center of the universe, if we were to imagine some parts of the earth, due to their great size, to be further away from the 
<margin>5</margin>center, these larger parts would necessarily squeeze the smaller parts. Being weighed down, these smaller parts would be overpowered and pushed toward the center until everything reaches a state of rest, having attained an equal distance, an equal power, and a state of balance—just as those who trade positions or those athletes <note>original: "ἀσκητῶν" (askētōn); refers to trained athletes or wrestlers</note> of equal strength who are positioned beneath one another. Since from every direction the
<margin>10</margin>parts of the earth are at an equal distance from the center, its shape must be spherical. Furthermore, since the downward tendency <note>original: "ῥοπή" (rhopē); the weight or inclination of a balance scale</note> of weights from every direction is toward the center, with all of them converging toward a single point, and since each of them falls perpendicularly <term>perpendicularly: falling at a perfect right angle (90 degrees) to a surface</term>—that is, making equal angles on either side of the line along which it falls
<margin>15</margin>relative to the surface of the earth—this also indicates that the surface of the earth is spherical.

But truly, even the surface of the sea and of all water in a state of calm becomes spherical in shape. This is clear to our senses from the 
<margin>20</margin>following: if you are standing on a shore and looking at something across the sea, such as a mountain, a tree, a tower, a ship, or the land itself, and then you crouch down and position your line of sight right at the surface of the sea, you will see either nothing at all or less of what previously appeared larger. This is because the 
<margin>25</margin>**curvature** <term>curvature: the rounded, outward-bulging shape of a sphere</term> of the sea’s surface obstructs your vision. This is also seen often when sailing, from...

---

**Vocabulary:**

# OF THE EARTH
<note>original Latin: TERRAE</note>

...thing like a ship. When land is not yet visible from the ship, or as a boat approaches, some who have climbed up the mast <note>original: "ἀναβάντες τινὲς ἐπὶ τὸν ἱστὸν"; a common ancient proof for the horizon</note> have seen it. By being at a height, they have, as it were, looked over the curvature of the sea that was blocking their vision. Both physically and mathematically, the surface of all water, <margin>5</margin> when it is at rest, is shown to be spherical in the following way:

---
**GEOMETRIC DIAGRAM OF THE WATER'S SURFACE**

[A horizontal line represents a hypothetical "flat" sea, with points **Alpha** (α) on the left, **Beta** (β) in the middle, and **Gamma** (γ) on the right. Below this, a point **Kappa** (κ) represents the center of the Earth. Three lines radiate from the center: **Kappa-Alpha**, **Kappa-Beta**, and **Kappa-Gamma**. This illustrates that in a flat model, the outer points are further from the center than the middle point.]
---

It is the nature of water to always flow from higher places toward lower ones. "Higher" places are those that are further distant from the center of the earth, while "lower" places are those that are closer to it. <margin>10</margin> Consequently, if we assume the surface of the water to be straight and flat—such as the line **αβγ**—and then from the center of the earth (point **κ**) we draw a perpendicular <term>perpendicular: a line falling at a right angle (90°) to another line</term> line **κβ** to the midpoint, and then connect straight lines **κα** and **κγ** to the ends of the surface, it is clear that both **κα** <margin>15</margin> and **κγ** are longer than **κβ**. Therefore, each of the points **α** and **γ** is further distant from **κ** than point **β** is, and will thus be "higher."

---

7 **flow**: spelling variation in B.
8 **lower**: spelling variation in B.
10 **of the surface of the water**: Manuscript B; "if we assume the surface of the water to be **flat and positioned in a straight line**" (Chalcidius 62).
12 **the middle**: reading from B.
13 **we draw... we connect**: spelling variations in B.
15 **is] will be?**: query regarding the future vs. present tense of the verb.
</meta>

<summary>This page uses both practical maritime observation (climbing a mast to see over the horizon) and geometric proofs to demonstrate that the surface of water must be spherical. It argues that if water were flat, its edges would be "higher" (further from the center of gravity) than its center, which contradicts the nature of fluids to seek the lowest point.</summary>

# ON THE FORM
<note>original Latin header: DE FORMA</note>

...toward **Beta** (**β**). The water will flow together from points **Alpha** (**α**) and **Gamma** (**γ**) toward **Beta** (**β**), because **Beta** is more concave <note>original: "κοιλότερον"; in this context meaning closer to the center of the earth</note> until such a point that **Beta**, as it is filled up, reaches a distance from **Kappa** (**κ**) equal to that of both **Alpha** and **Gamma**. And likewise, all points on the surface of the water <margin>5</margin> are equidistant from **Kappa** (**κ**). It is clear then that the surface itself becomes spherical. Consequently, the entire mass of earth and sea together is spherical. For one would not consider the height of mountains or the lowliness of the plains to be a sufficient cause of irregularity in proportion to the magnitude of the whole. <margin>10</margin> Indeed, <term>Eratosthenes</term> <note>Eratosthenes of Cyrene (c. 276–194 BC), the mathematician and librarian at Alexandria who famously calculated the Earth's circumference.</note> demonstrates that the total magnitude of the earth, measured along its greatest circle, is approximately 252,000 <term>stadia</term> <note>stadium: an ancient unit of distance; 252,000 stadia is roughly 25,000–29,000 miles, remarkably close to the actual polar circumference of 24,860 miles.</note>. <term>Archimedes</term> <note>Archimedes of Syracuse (c. 287–212 BC), the preeminent Greek mathematician and engineer.</note> shows that the circumference of a circle, when extended into a straight line, is greater than three times its diameter by approximately one-seventh part <margin>15</margin> [of the diameter] <note>This is the famous Archimedean approximation of Pi (π) as 22/7.</note>. Therefore, the entire diameter of the earth would be approximately 80,182 stadia; for the circumference of 252,000 stadia was three times this diameter plus one-seventh more. The vertical difference <insert>which is ten stadia</insert> between the <margin>20</margin> highest mountains and the lowest parts of the earth is very small, as <term>Eratosthenes</term> and <term>Dicaearchus</term> <note>Dicaearchus of Messana (c. 350–285 BC), a philosopher and geographer who was a student of Aristotle.</note> say they have found. And also by using instruments to measure the sizes of things from a distance...

# THE EARTH
<note>original Latin header: TERRAE</note>

...is observed to be of such a size using <term>dioptrae</term> <note>ancient surveying instruments used for measuring angles and altitudes</note>. It follows, then, that the protrusion of the highest mountain is approximately **one eight-thousandth** of the entire diameter of the earth. If we were to construct a sphere here with a diameter of one **foot** <note>original: "poús"; a Greek foot was roughly 30cm</note>, <margin>5</margin> since the span of a **finger** <note>original: "dáktylos"; 1/16th of a foot</note> is filled in length by approximately twelve **millet-seed** diameters <note>original: "kenkhriaias"; the millet seed was a standard ancient unit for very small dimensions</note> [with a surplus of a half], the one-foot diameter of our constructed sphere would be filled by a length of **two hundred** millet-seed diameters, or perhaps a little less. For one foot contains 16 fingers; <margin>10</margin> and one finger is filled by 12 millet-seed diameters; and 16 times 12 is **192**. Therefore, a fortieth part of a single millet-seed diameter <note>is larger than one eight-thousandth of the one-foot diameter</note>; for forty times two hundred is eight thousand. <margin>15</margin> But the highest mountain, measured by its vertical height, was shown to be approximately the **eight-thousandth** part of the earth’s diameter; thus, the fortieth part of a millet-seed diameter will have a greater ratio to the one-foot diameter of the sphere [than the mountain has to the earth]. And consequently, the <margin>20</margin> **solid** <note>meaning the three-dimensional volume</note> formed from the fortieth part of a millet-seed diameter, compared to the similar solid formed from the one-foot diameter, <note>will have a greater ratio than</note> the solid formed from a ten-**stadium** <note>a stadium was roughly 185 meters; 10 stadia is about 1.1 miles</note> vertical height compared to the similar solid formed from the diameter of the earth. And the spherical body formed...

# ON THE SHAPE
<note>original Latin header: DE FORMA</note>

...the **solid** <note>meaning the three-dimensional volume</note> derived from the fortieth part of the diameter of a **millet seed** <note>original: "kenkhriaias"; a tiny seed used by ancient mathematicians as a baseline for microscopic units</note> will be the one-64,000,000th part of the whole millet seed. Furthermore, a spherical mountain with a vertical height of ten **stades** <note>original: "dekastadiaias"; a stade was roughly 185 meters or 600 feet</note> has a volume of approximately **524** solid stades.
<margin>5</margin> Now, the whole earth, when calculated as a sphere, contains a volume of **269** myriads of "third-order" numbers, **9,410** myriads of "second-order" numbers, and **4,331** myriads of "first-order" numbers <note>these "orders" represent powers of ten-thousand (myriads) used to express astronomical figures</note>, plus an additional **7,821** stades and one-third of a stade. For again, it is demonstrated that the rectangular shape contained by the diameter and the circumference of a circle (when the latter is straightened out into a **line**) <margin>10</margin> is **four times** the area of a circle, which is the same as the area of the fourth part of a sphere. Because of this, the square of the diameter is found to have a ratio to the area of the circle of **14 to 11**. For since the circumference <margin>15</margin> is three times the diameter and one-seventh more <note>this is the classic Archimedean approximation of Pi as 22/7</note>, if the diameter is taken as **7** units, the circumference becomes **22**. A fourth of this circumference is **5 and 1/2**. Thus, where the square of the diameter is **49**, the area of the circle is **38 and 1/2**. To remove the fraction, we double these: where the square <margin>20</margin> is **98**, the circle is **77**. The ratio between these, expressed in the smallest and primary numbers, is **14 to 11**. For the greatest common measure of both is the number **7**, which measures 98 fourteen times and 77 eleven times. Therefore, [the ratio] of the square of the diameter...

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof concerning the insignificance of mountains relative to the earth's total volume. # THE EARTH
<note>original Latin header: TERRAE</note>

...the ratio of the cube to the cylinder circumscribing the circle <insert>is as 14 is to 11; and the cylinder circumscribing the circle</insert> is demonstrated by **Archimedes** <note>referring to his treatise "On the Sphere and the Cylinder," Book I, 37</note> to be **one-and-a-half times** the volume of the sphere contained within it. It follows then that if the cube formed from the diameter of the circle is 14 parts,
<margin>5</margin> the cylinder is 11 parts, and the sphere is **7 and one-third** parts. Because of this, the spherical volumes of both the earth and the greatest mountain are discovered using the previously mentioned numbers. Therefore, a spherical mountain having a vertical height of **ten stades** <note>original: "dekastadiaian"; about 1.1 miles or 1.8 kilometers</note> has a much
<margin>10</margin> smaller ratio to the whole earth than the **one-64,000th part** of a **millet seed** <note>original: "kenkhrou"; a tiny seed used as a standard for small measurements</note> has to a sphere with a diameter of one foot. Furthermore, a mountain that is not perfectly spherical, but shaped as we actually see them, has an even smaller ratio still. Such a tiny portion of a millet seed, whether added to the outside of a foot-wide sphere or
<margin>15</margin> taken away from it by hollowing it out, will not produce any noticeable difference. Therefore, a mountain that is ten stades high at its peak is not significant enough in proportion to prevent the entire surface of the land and sea from being a sphere. [The circumference of the earth is **252,000 stades**,
<margin>20</margin> its diameter is **80,182 stades**, the square of the diameter is **6,429,153,124**, and the cube of the diameter is **515,419,309,178,568**. One-fourteenth of this cube is **36,815,664,941**.] <note>The bracketed text contains calculations using the Greek system of "myriads of myriads" to express very large numbers; these figures represent the raw data for the earth's volume in cubic stades.</note>

---

<note>original Latin: DE TERRA</note>

The earth is spherical and lies in the center of the cosmos.

every part of it one half of the heavens would be above and the

the whole, but holds the rank of a point, is also shown by the

# ON THE CELESTIAL CIRCLES
<note>original Latin: DE CIRCULIS CAELESTIBUS</note>

The whole earth <insert>holds the rank of a point</insert> in relation to the entire sphere of the sun; and much more so in relation to the sphere of the <term>fixed stars<gloss>stars that maintain a constant position relative to one another, unlike the wandering planets</gloss></term>. For this reason, it is always the case that exactly half of the cosmos is seen above it <insert>to within a very small fraction of a degree</insert>.
<margin>5</margin> Concerning the shape of both the universe and the earth, and furthermore the central position of the latter and its imperceptible size relative to the whole—although much more could still be said—the demonstrations provided by <term>Adrastus<gloss>Adrastus of Aphrodisias, a 2nd-century Peripatetic philosopher whose astronomical summaries are preserved here</gloss></term> in the manner already described will suffice for the tradition that follows.
<margin>10</margin> In the following sections, he says: as the celestial sphere is carried around its own fixed <term>poles<gloss>the two points in the sky around which everything appears to rotate</gloss></term> and the axis connecting them—at the center of which the earth is firmly fixed—all the stars being carried along with it, and simply all points in the heavens, describe
<margin>15</margin> <term>parallel circles<gloss>circles on the celestial sphere that never intersect, similar to lines of latitude on a globe</gloss></term>. That is to say, they are equidistant from one another and are formed at right angles to the axis, since they are traced around the poles of the universe. Now, while the <insert>circles traced</insert> by the stars are countable, and those traced by other points are nearly infinite, a few have
<margin>20</margin> obtained <term>famous names<gloss>referring to the Equator, Tropics, and Arctic/Antarctic circles</gloss></term>, which it is useful to know for the study of the phenomena completed in the heavens. One of these is the circle which is high above us and always visible—

---

The following scholarly apparatus compares the Greek text with early Latin translations and commentaries.
</note>

Following that true solar midpoint, it is clear that the whole earth holds the place of a point when compared against the solar globe. <note>original Latin: "omnis terra puncti vicem habeat adversum solis globum"</note>. **Chalcidius 1** <note>A 4th-century translator of Plato</note> <insert>it is a point</insert>. **Martianus Capella 4** <note>A 5th-century encyclopedist</note> <insert>or</insert> a little bit less (or more). **Martianus 10** title: "Concerning the parallel circles in the <term>fixed sphere<gloss>the sphere of the fixed stars</gloss></term>." **Manuscript B** states: "But indeed with the celestial sphere turning itself around fixed and always remaining poles, and the axis connecting those same poles, to which the earth is attached as a center in the middle, the host of all the stars accompanies the rotation of the celestial sphere, etc." **Chalcidius 65**. <meta>This page continues the astronomical summary by Adrastus of Aphrodisias. Having established that the earth sits as a central point within the celestial sphere, the text now describes the specific "circles" or lines of latitude and coordinate systems used by ancient astronomers to map the movements of the stars and planets.</meta>

-># ON THE CIRCLES<-
-><note>original Greek: ΠΕΡΙ ΤΩΝ ΚΥΚΛΩΝ / Latin header: DE CIRCULIS</note><-

...[circles] being described around the visible pole, and this circle itself is always visible, called the **arctic** <note>original: *arktikos*</note> after the **Bears** <term><gloss>the constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor, which in the northern hemisphere never set below the horizon</gloss></term> placed among the stars within it. There is another circle
on the opposite side, equal in size to this one, located around the hidden pole; this circle is always invisible to us and is called the **antarctic**.
<margin>5</margin> In the middle of all these is the largest circle of all, which divides the entire sphere in half; it is called the **equinoctial** <note>original: *isēmerinos* — what we now call the celestial equator</note>. This is because, for the <term>climate<gloss>from *klima*, referring to a latitudinal zone or region of the earth</gloss></term> directly beneath it, all nights and all days are equal in length. Furthermore, for all other people, at every
turning point of the universe, whenever the sun rises and sets
<margin>10</margin> and happens to be upon this circle, it divides the day to be equal to the night. Between the
equinoctial and the arctic circles on either side is a **tropic** <note>original: *tropikos*, meaning a 'turning' circle</note>. The **summer tropic** is positioned toward our part of the world, north of the equinoctial, while the **winter tropic** is on the other side. These are so named because the sun turns its
<margin>15</margin> southern and northern passage back when it reaches them. Set at an angle to these is the **zodiac**, which is itself one of the <term>great circles<gloss>a circle that divides a sphere into two equal halves</gloss></term>. It touches each of the tropics at a single point: the summer tropic at the sign of **Cancer**, and the other at **Capricorn**. It cuts the equinoctial in half and is itself
<margin>20</margin> bisected by it at the points of the **Claws** <note>the 'Claws' of the Scorpion, now known as the constellation Libra</note> and the **Ram** <note>Aries</note>. Beneath this circle, the sun, the moon, and the remaining **planets** <note>original: *planētes*, literally 'wanderers'</note> travel. These include the one called **Phainon** <note>the 'Shining One'</note>—attributed to **Saturn**, though some say it belongs to the Sun—and **Phaethon** <note>the 'Radiant One'</note>, which belongs to **Jupiter**. Then there is
**Pyroeis** <note>the 'Fiery One'</note>, which they call the star of **Mars**, though others call it the star of **Hercules**...

---

**1** *clearly* B* **3** *to this* [masculine] ] *this* [neuter] B* **7** *nights and* ] *nights* [variant spelling] B* **12** *at the other* B, *on this side and that Chalcidius 66. compare Cleomedes page 15 Bak.: at each of these again two others are written, etc.* **16** **zodiac** *added by Martin; in B there is a blank space; Chalcidius's version reads: through which the diagonal sign-bearer is curved, and it is itself a great circle, etc. Title in B: Concerning the zodiac and the wandering zodiacs (the second 'zodiacs' deleted by Martin)* **22** *Phainon* ] *Phainonte* B*

->### [CONCERNING] THE CELESTIAL BODIES<-
-><note>original Latin header: CAELESTIBUS</note><-

...the **Light-bearer** <note>original: *phosphoros*</note>, which they say belongs to **Aphrodite** <note>the planet Venus</note>; they also call this same star the **Dawn-bearer** <note>original: *heōsphoros*</note> and the **Evening-star** <note>original: *hesperos*</note>. In addition to these is the **Gleaming-one** <note>original: *stilbōn*</note>, which they call the star of **Hermes** <note>the planet Mercury</note>.

| The Celestial Sphere |
| :--- |
| **Arctic** <note>at the top pole</note> |
| **Winter Tropic** |
| **Equinoctial** |
| **Summer Tropic** |
| **Antarctic** <note>at the bottom pole</note> |
| *Note: A diagonal line intersecting the middle horizontal lines is labeled **Zodiac**.* |

There is also a certain circle called the **horizon** <note>original: *horizōn*, meaning the "bounding" circle</note>, which is projected through our line of sight and, by the obstruction of the earth, <insert>5</insert> divides the entire heavens into two equal parts as far as our senses can perceive—that is, the visible hemisphere above the earth and the invisible hemisphere beneath the earth. It is likewise a "great circle" <term>great circle<gloss>a circle on the surface of a sphere that has the same circumference as the sphere itself, dividing it into two equal halves</gloss></term> and it bisects the other great circles, namely the **equinoctial** and the **zodiac**. Because of this, <insert>10</insert> of the stars located at opposite ends of a diameter, occurring in pairs, one is always seen at its rising while the other is at its setting. This circle also bisects the **meridian**. For there is also a so-called **meridian** <note>original: *mesēmbrinos*, meaning "midday" circle</note> which is a great circle, <insert>15</insert> drawn through both poles of the universe and conceived as being at right angles to the horizon.

<margin>5</margin> [Word variant in manuscript B].
<margin>12</margin> "It is divided" [variant in manuscript B]; *it could also be written as "it is divided in two by the meridian." Chalcidius says the meridian itself divides the horizon in half.*
<margin>13</margin> <note>Note on the title:</note> "Concerning the meridian" in manuscript B.

<note>9* — likely a printer's signature or manuscript mark</note>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial coordinate systems, transitioning from the definition of the horizon to the meridian and then distinguishing between astronomical circles that are fixed by nature versus those that are relative to the observer.</meta>

->### CONCERNING THE CIRCLES<-
-><note>original Latin header: DE CIRCULIS</note><-

...and the **meridian** <note>original: *mesēmbrinos*, from the words for 'mid-day'</note>, so called because the sun reaches its highest point in the sky upon this circle at midday. Some also call this the **colure** <note>original: *kolouron*, meaning 'docked' or 'clipped-tail'</note>, because the part of it extending toward the invisible pole remains unseen by us.

<margin>5</margin> Now, the **equinoctial** <note>the celestial equator</note> and the **tropics** on either side of it are "given" and fixed in both their magnitudes and their positions. Points and lines are said to be "given in position" if they always occupy the same place; areas, lines, and angles are said to be "given in magnitude" <margin>10</margin> when we are able to provide equals to them. 

The circle of the **equinoctial** and the **tropics** on either side always occupy the same place and are fixed; it is also possible to provide circles equal to them. To the **equinoctial**, the **zodiac**, the **horizon**, and the **meridian** are equal <note>these are all "great circles" of the sphere</note>; to the winter tropic, the <margin>15</margin> **summer tropic** is equal, and to the summer, the winter. These are always "given" through these properties because it is not within our power to establish them as being of such a kind or such a size; rather, they exist by nature as such and are "given" even if we do not designate them ourselves. But those things which are within our power to define as being of one sort <margin>20</margin> or another are not "given" by nature. 

Therefore, the **equinoctial** and the circles on either side are "given" and fixed by nature—that is, they are established and fixed—in both position and magnitude. The **zodiac**, however, is "given" in its magnitude and in its position relative to the heaven itself, but <margin>25</margin> relative to us, it is not "given" in its position; for its position shifts relative to us because of the **obliquity** <term>obliquity<gloss>original: *loxōsis*; the tilt or "slant" of the sun's path relative to the celestial equator</gloss></term> of the universe, standing now one way, now another...

<summary>This page defines the meridian and colure, while distinguishing between celestial circles that are fixed by nature (like the equator and tropics) and those whose positions shift relative to a terrestrial observer (like the zodiac).</summary>
<keywords>meridian, colure, equinoctial, tropics, zodiac, mathematical definitions, obliquity, celestial geometry</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial circles. Having previously defined the equator and the tropics as "given" in both size and position, the author now contrasts them with the horizon and meridian, which vary based on the observer's location, and introduces the variable polar circles and the unique width of the zodiac.</meta>

# ON CELESTIAL BODIES
<note>original Latin header: CAELESTIBUS</note>

...above us. The **meridian** and the **horizon** are "given" in their magnitude—for they are among the "greatest" circles <note>great circles divide a sphere into two equal halves</note>—but they shift in their position according to each **climate** <term>clima: a geographical latitude or belt of the earth</term>, differing from one place to another. For the same **horizon** does not exist <margin>5</margin> for everyone on earth, nor is the same **mid-heaven** <note>original: *mesouranisma*; the point where the ecliptic crosses the meridian</note> shared by all, nor does everyone have the [same] **meridian**. 

However, the circles near the poles—the **arctic** and the **antarctic**—are "given" neither in their magnitudes nor in their positions. Instead, according to the differences between the more southern and more northern climates, <margin>10</margin> they are seen as larger in some places and smaller in others. Indeed, at the very center of the earth—that is, in the zone called "under the equator," which is said to be uninhabitable because of the heat—these circles do not exist at all; for there, both poles appear on the horizon, and the horizon itself falls through them. <margin>15</margin> There are some who also call this the **right sphere**, as all the parallel circles are "upright" <note>at right angles</note> in relation to those specific places on the earth.

Furthermore, while each of the other circles is truly a circle bounded by a single line, the one called the **zodiac** appears with a certain width, just like the hoop of a **tambourine** <margin>20</margin> <note>original: *tympanon*; referring to the circular wooden frame of a hand-drum</note>, and upon this the constellations are fashioned. Of this zodiac, the line through the middle of the signs is called the [ecliptic], which is both a "great circle" and one that touches each of the **tropics** at a single point and bisects the **equator**. <margin>25</margin> But the circles on either side that define the width of the zodiac are smaller than the one in the middle.

---

<note>The following are scholarly notes regarding variations in the original manuscripts:</note>

2 **but they shift in position**: *but in position they shift* in Manuscript B and Martianus; *but they waver in position* according to Chalcidius 67.
5 **mid-heaven**: possibly *culmination*?
**nor**: *according to* in Manuscript B; *and* in Martianus.
16 **in relation to those**: Manuscript B reads *it belongs as*.
17 **Heading**: *Concerning the Zodiac* in Manuscript B.
**Furthermore**: *if someone* in Manuscript B and Martianus; *also that must be added* in Chalcidius 68.
21 **the one [cutting through the width]**: *the circle called the 'middle of the signs' further cuts through its width* in Chalcidius.

<summary>This page explains how the meridian and horizon change based on an observer's latitude and describes the arctic/antarctic circles as variable in size. It also introduces the zodiac as a band with physical width rather than a single line.</summary>
