<meta>This page continues the transition from describing the celestial circles to describing the bodies that inhabit them. Having previously introduced the width of the zodiac, the text now explains how the fixed stars and the planets (the "wanderers") move differently within the celestial architecture.</meta>

# ON THE STARS
<note>original Latin header: DE STELLIS</note>

Now, the many stars—those we call the **fixed stars**—are carried along by the first and greatest sphere, which surrounds the whole universe from the outside. They follow a single, simple circular motion, as if they were firmly fixed within this sphere and carried by it. <margin>5</margin> They maintain one and always the same position within the sphere and the same arrangement relative to each other. They do not undergo any change whatsoever, whether in their pattern <note>their constellation or grouping</note>, their position, their size, or their color.

However, the **sun**, the **moon**, and all the remaining stars called <margin>10</margin> **planets** <term>planētes: literally "wanderers" in Greek</term> are carried along by the rotation of the whole universe from east to west every day, just like the fixed stars. But they are also seen to make many other varied motions of their own each day. For they move into the following signs of the **zodiac** <margin>15</margin> and not into the preceding ones along their own proper path. They move in a direction contrary to the whole universe—what is called their "motion in length" <note>this refers to celestial longitude, the movement through the signs of the zodiac over weeks and months</note>. They also turn from the north toward the south and back again, making a transition "in width" <note>this refers to celestial latitude, the slight north-south wobble of planets relative to the sun's path</note>. Simply <margin>20</margin> put, they are carried from the summer tropic toward the winter tropic and back again because of the tilt <note>obliquity</note> of the zodiac relative to those observers by whom they are always seen; and within that very width...

***

**1 heading.** *On the fixed stars* in Manuscript B.  **2** *and the* is written both at the end of the page and the beginning of the next in Manuscript B, but once in the copies.  **3 simple]** *wandering* in Manuscript B: compare page 149, line 1. Chalcidius [translates as]: *with one and simple motion around it*.  **4** *firmly fixed* in Manuscript B.  **5** *one and the same position always* in Chalcidius.  **6 any** [manuscript variation note].  **8 heading.** *On the planets* in Manuscript B.  **the sun however]** *and the sun* in Manuscript B.  **13 into]** [variant spelling] in Manuscript B.  **16 the whole]** *everywhere* in Manuscript B; *performing motions contrary to the motion of the whole globe* in Chalcidius.  **17 turn]** [singular verb form] in Manuscript B.  **18 width]** *of the universe* in Manuscript B and Martianus; compare line 21, page 135, line 12 and 173, line 11. *With a wider, perverse deflection* in Chalcidius.  **20 relative to those observers by whom they are always seen** perhaps to be deleted.

<summary>This page distinguishes between the "fixed stars," which move in a single uniform motion with the outermost sphere of the universe, and the "planets," which possess secondary, complex motions along the length and width of the zodiac.</summary>
<keywords>fixed stars, planets, celestial spheres, zodiac, longitude, latitude, obliquity of the ecliptic, ancient astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "wandering stars" (planets), moving from their general retrograde and north-south motions to the specific variations in their speed, distance from Earth, and the width of their paths within the zodiac belt.</meta>

# ON THE WANDERING STARS
<note>original Latin header: DE STELLIS ERRANTIBUS</note>

...of the zodiac, appearing sometimes further north of the midline <note>original: "dia mesou" — referring to the ecliptic, the sun's path</note> and being said to be "heightened," and sometimes further south and "lowered." Some planets do this more, and others less. Furthermore, they vary in their apparent size because they are sometimes further from the earth and sometimes very near to us in their depth <margin>5</margin>. Because of this, the speed of their movement through the zodiac signs appears irregular; they do not cross equal distances in equal times. Instead, they move faster when they also seem largest because they have moved closer to the earth <note>this point of closest approach is called perigee</note>, and slower when they are smaller <margin>10</margin> because they have moved far from the earth <note>this point of furthest distance is called apogee</note>.

As for the "width" of the transition within the zodiac itself: the sun is seen to move only a very small amount entirely, about one degree out of the 360. But the moon—as the ancients <margin>15</margin> say—and the **Morning Star** <term>Phosphorus: the planet Venus</term> move the most, about 12 degrees. **The Gleaming One** <gloss>Mercury</gloss> <term>Stilbon: the planet Mercury</term> moves about 8 degrees; **The Fiery One** <term>Pyroeis: the planet Mars</term> and **The Radiant One** <term>Phaethon: the planet Jupiter</term> move about 5 degrees; while **The Shimmering One** <term>Phainon: the planet Saturn</term> moves about 3 degrees. Now, the moon and the sun appear to move an equal amount on either side of the midline across the width of every zodiac sign. Of the others, each does not move equally, but in one sign it becomes most northerly, while in <margin>20</margin> another it becomes most southerly. Along the length of the zodiac circle, from one point back to the same point, toward the following signs...

<summary>This page explains the varying speeds and distances of the planets (apogee and perigee) and details the specific latitudinal "width" each planet covers within the zodiac belt.</summary>
<keywords>zodiac, planets, apogee, perigee, Venus, Mercury, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, celestial latitude, orbital speed</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary motions, moving from their latitude (north-south "width") to the specific duration of their orbits and their visibility relative to the Sun.</meta>

# ON THE STARS
<note>original Latin header: DE STELLARUM (concerning the stars)</note>

...moving [through the signs] and not toward the preceding signs, the Moon completes its circuit in approximately 27 days and a third of a day and night. The Sun completes it in a year, which is close to 365 and 1/4 days. **Phosphorus** <term>Venus</term> and **Stilbon** <term>Mercury</term> move irregularly in their individual paths, <margin>5</margin> differing slightly in their times, but to speak generally, they are **equal-running** <note>original: "isodromoi" — meaning they have the same average orbital period as the Sun</note> with the Sun, as they are always observed near it. For this reason, they both overtake the Sun and are overtaken by it. **Pyroeis** <term>Mars</term> completes its circuit in nearly two years, **Phaethon** <term>Jupiter</term> in <margin>10</margin> close to twelve years, and **Phainon** <term>Saturn</term> in just under thirty years.

Because of this, not all planets make their conjunctions <note>meetings with the sun</note>, phases, and occultations—which are also called "risings" and "settings"—in the same manner. The Moon, for instance, after its conjunction with the Sun, moves faster than the Sun <margin>15</margin> toward the following signs; therefore, it is always first seen appearing and rising as an **evening star** <note>appearing in the west after sunset</note>, but it becomes hidden and sets as a **morning star** <note>disappearing in the light of dawn</note>. 

On the other hand, **Phainon** <term>Saturn</term>, **Phaethon** <term>Jupiter</term>, and **Pyroeis** <term>Mars</term>, since they complete their circle through the zodiac more slowly than the Sun toward the following signs—as if they are being overtaken <margin>20</margin> and passed by it—always set as evening stars but rise as morning stars. 

**Phosphorus** <term>Venus</term> and **Stilbon** <term>Mercury</term>, being equal-running with the Sun and always seen around it, overtaking it and being overtaken by it, [behave] in both ways: having risen as evening stars, they are hidden again as evening stars; and having appeared as morning stars, they set and disappear as morning stars. <margin>25</margin> For while the other wandering stars [can be separated] from the Sun by any distance...

---

Line 17: Variant spelling of "slower."
Line 18: A repetition of the word "circle" in manuscript B; the scholar Martianus Capella suggests "on the contrary," while Chalcidius translates this as "as if overtaken by it."
Line 19: Variant spelling of "evening"; Martianus Capella and Chalcidius clarify the rising and setting times.
Line 20: A heading in manuscript B notes "concerning those that run equal to the Sun."

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary movements relative to the Sun, transitioning from the outer planets (which can be opposite the Sun) to the inner planets, Mercury and Venus, and defining the technical terminology for celestial "risings" and "settings."</meta>

->ON RISINGS AND SETTINGS. 137<-

...distance from the Sun and sometimes being diametrically opposite to it, these two <note>Mercury and Venus</note> are always seen near the Sun. **Stilbon** <term>Mercury, "The Gleaming One"</term> is seen at some 20 degrees—that is, nearly two-thirds of a zodiac sign <note>Each zodiac sign consists of 30 degrees</note>—at its furthest point becoming more easterly or westerly than the Sun. **Aphrodite** <term>Venus</term> moves away about 50 degrees toward its eastern or western points.

"Rising" <note>original: *anatole*</note> is spoken of in many ways. Properly and generally, for both the Sun and the other stars, it is the first ascent above the horizon. In another way, for the other stars, it is the first **appearance** <note>original: *phausis*</note> out of the Sun's rays, which is also properly called **manifestation**. The remaining type is called **acronychal** <term>rising at the edge of night</term>, which occurs when the star directly opposite the Sun is seen in the east just as the Sun sets. It is called **acronychal** because such a rising happens at the "edge of the night," that is, when night is beginning. 

Similarly, **setting** <note>original: *dysis*</note> is generally the first descent below the horizon. In another way, it is the first **disappearance** of a star under the Sun's rays, which is also properly called **occultation** <note>original: *krypsis*</note>. The remaining type is also **acronychal**, when the star directly opposite the Sun sets just as the Sun rises. 

Of the risings and settings described in relation to the Sun's rays—that is, appearances and occultations—some are **morning** events and others are **evening** events. A morning rising of a star occurs when, escaping the Sun's rays and rising before it, it is seen for the first time, just like the **rising of the Dog Star** <note>original: *kynos epitole*—the first appearance of Sirius in the morning sky after a period of invisibility</note>...

---

<note>Scholarly variants: 3. "somewhere" [pou] is "much" [poly] in Manuscript B. 5. "50" [n'] is "20" [k'] in Manuscript B. 7. Heading in Manuscript B: "In how many ways 'rising' is defined." 10. Suggested correction: "appearance" [phasis]. 20. Variant spelling: "sets opposite."</note>

<summary>This page defines the maximum elongation of Mercury and Venus from the Sun and explains the three types of celestial risings and settings: general (horizon-based), heliacal (sun-based), and acronychal (opposite the sun).</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Venus, rising, setting, acronychal, heliacal rising, Sirius, Dog Star, Greek astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of planetary "risings" and "settings" in relation to the Sun, moving from general definitions to specific types of evening and morning observations, before transitioning into the Pythagorean theory of the order of the planetary spheres.</meta>

# ON THE STARS
### Page 138

...is spoken of; but an **evening rising** <note>original: *hesperia anatole*</note> occurs whenever [a star] first appears after the setting of the Sun, just as we say the **Moon** rises during the new moons. In a similar way, there are also **morning settings** <margin>5</margin> <note>original: *dyseis heoai*</note>, whenever something that was rising before the Sun in previous days first disappears because the Sun has approached it, just as the **Moon** does; and an **evening setting** occurs whenever the Sun, having approached a star that is setting after it, first makes it invisible by means of its rays.

<margin>10</margin> ## ON THE POSITION OF THE PLANETS
Regarding the local position and arrangement of the spheres <note>or</note> circles in which the planets are situated and carried, some of the **Pythagoreans** <term>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras who believed the universe was ordered by mathematical and musical ratios</term> believe the following: that the circle of **Selene** <term>The Moon</term> is closest to the Earth; the second above this is that of **Hermes** <term>Mercury</term>; then that of **Phosphoros** <term>Venus, "The Light-Bringer"</term>; <margin>15</margin> and fourth that of **Helios** <term>The Sun</term>; then that of **Ares** <term>Mars</term>; then that of **Zeus** <term>Jupiter</term>; and finally, and closest to the fixed stars, that of **Kronos** <term>Saturn</term>. They wish the circle of the **Sun** to be in the middle of the planets, as it is the most authoritative and like the **heart of the universe** <note>This refers to the "Chaldaean" order, placing the Sun as a central vital force among the seven wandering stars</note>. **Alexander the Aetolian** <note>A 3rd-century BCE poet-scholar who wrote about astronomy</note> also indicates these things, speaking thus:

---

<margin>Chalcidius 71</margin> **1** "Whenever" (in manuscript B); **3** "Morning" (as in the Latin translation by Chalcidius, section 71, regarding morning settings). **4** "Something rising before" (manuscript B). **5** "Having approached" (manuscript B). **6** "Evening settings" (corrected by Martin). **7** "First" (manuscript B); "itself" (emended to "to it" in manuscript B). <margin>Chalcidius 72</margin> **9** The heading in manuscript B reads: *On the position of the planets*. The order of the wandering stars is illustrated with two figures in manuscript B; next to the second figure (similar to the description in Chalcidius's edition) is written **the Sirens** <note>Referring to the Platonic idea that a Siren sits upon each celestial sphere singing a single note, creating the "music of the spheres"</note>. "With" (in manuscript B); "or" (added by Martin). The Latin Chalcidius (72) reads: "the position and order of the placement of the globes or circles." **11** For the Pythagoreans, compare Zeller volume I. **17** "Planets" (manuscript B); "most authoritative" (Martin). **18** "Heart" (manuscript B; Martin). Chalcidius (72) explains: "namely, so that the Sun, located in the middle among the planets, may be understood to possess the preeminence of the heart, or rather of all vital things." Compare also Proclus's commentary on the *Timaeus*. **19** Compare *Rheinisches Museum* volume XXVI.

<summary>This page defines evening risings and morning/evening settings of celestial bodies and outlines the Pythagorean planetary order, which places the Sun in the fourth (middle) position as the "heart" of the cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Pythagoreanism, planetary order, Sun as heart, Alexander the Aetolian, Chalcidius, celestial spheres, Mercury, Venus, music of the spheres</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary arrangements from page 138, specifically detailing the "Chaldaean" or Pythagorean order of the celestial spheres and introducing the concept of the "Music of the Spheres."</meta>

->### ON THE ORDER OF THE WANDERERS<-
<note>original: "Errantium Ordine" — 'Wanderers' is the literal translation of the Greek word 'planētai,' from which we get 'planets.'</note>

### Page 139

High up, one after another, each obtained a higher circle:
Nearest to the Earth is the divine **Moon** <term>Selene</term>;
Second then is the gleaming star of **Hermes** <term>Mercury</term>, the carver of the tortoise-shell <note>The god Hermes was said to have invented the lyre by stretching strings over the shell of a tortoise</note>;
Above him is the most radiant **Light-bringer** <term>Phosphoros, or Venus</term> of the Cytherean goddess <note>Cythereia is a title for Aphrodite, who was said to have first stepped ashore at the island of Cythera</note>;
Fourth, himself above them, the **Sun** <term>Helios</term> is carried on his horses; <note>5</note>
Fifth then is the fiery star of murderous, Thracian **Ares** <term>Mars</term>;
Sixth then, the bright, shining star of **Zeus** <term>Jupiter</term> is stationed;
And seventh, the shining star of **Kronos** <term>Saturn</term> rises near the fixed stars.
And all of them, in unison with the notes of the seven-stringed lyre,
Pour forth a harmony, each at its own distance from the next. <note>10</note>

For this too is a **Pythagorean** doctrine: that the universe was discovered to be ordered according to harmony, and according to the ratios of things harmonized and in concord—

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Music of the Spheres" from page 139, moving from a general description of the planetary order to a specific poetic fragment by Alexander of Ephesus that assigns musical intervals to the celestial bodies.</meta>

->## ON THE SPHERES<-

...of the notes and their ratios, the heavenly bodies, separated by the force and speed of their motion, produce harmonized and concordant notes. For this reason, in the following lines, Alexander <note>Likely Alexander of Ephesus, also known as Alexander Lychnus, a scholar-poet of the 1st century BCE</note> says:

> <margin>5</margin> Earth, then, as the **highest string** <term>hypate</term> and deep-toned, dwells in the middle;
> The sphere of the fixed stars, joined to it, is the **lowest string** <term>nete</term>; <note>In ancient Greek music, the "highest" string (*hypate*) produced the lowest pitch, while the "lowest" string (*nete*) produced the highest pitch. This can be confusing for modern readers: Alexander is mapping the physical layout of a lyre onto the structure of the cosmos.</note>
> The Sun holds the middle position among the wandering stars;
> Away from it, the cold circle <note>The planet Saturn, associated with coldness due to its distance from the Sun</note> holds an interval of a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>;
> From that, the **shining one** <term>Jupiter</term> releases a **semitone** when relaxed,
> <margin>10</margin> And from that, the **radiant one** <term>Mars</term> is as far as the mighty star of Ares;
> Below these, the Sun, delighting mortals, holds a **whole tone**;
> From the brightness of the Sun, the **Cytherean** <term>Venus</term> is a **three-semitone** interval <note>A minor third</note>;
> Below that, the gleaming star of **Hermes** <term>Mercury</term> moves by a **semitone**;
> And of the same distance is the **Moon**, colored with a many-turning nature;
> <margin>15</margin> From the center of the Sun, the Earth obtained its position through a **fifth** <term>diapente</term>;
> The Earth itself has five zones, reaching from the air to the blazing fire.

---

5 **in the middle** <note>original: *messothi* or *mesothi*</note> in manuscript C] **in the middle** <note>original: *mesethi*</note> in B. **dwells** in C] **dwells** in B <note>Minor spelling variations in the Greek verbs</note>
6 **lowest string** <note>original: *nete*</note> in C, **of the south** <note>original: *notes*</note> in B. **middle** in B, **middle** in C. **Sun** <note>original: *eelios*</note> in C] **Sun** <note>original: *helios*</note> in B
7 **wandering** in BC, emended by Vossius. **holds** in C] **held above** in B, **held above** emended by Vossius
8 **away from** in B, **under** in C. **from that, away, indeed, the height** or **from that, away, indeed, the height** conjectured by Naeke
8 **fourth** <note>original: *tessara*</note>: the letters *ara* are lost in C, **of the fourths** <note>original: *tessaro*</note> in B. **of the circles** in C
10 **to that, the stretching, the radiant one as much as the mighty star of Ares** in B, **from that, so much the radiant one, mighty... star** in C, **from that, equal the radiant one, a tone the mighty star of Ares** suggested doubtfully by Bergk, *Journal of Classical Studies* 1850 page 175

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Alexander of Ephesus’s poem regarding the "Music of the Spheres." The previous page concluded with a description of the planetary intervals (Moon to Saturn). This page provides the concluding lines of the poem and a critical commentary by an anonymous author (likely Theo of Smyrna or a similar musical theorist) who finds the poet's astronomical-musical math to be inconsistent.</meta>

### ON THE CELESTIAL CONCORD
141

> ...fitted together by rays of fire and chilling frosts,
> it held the six-tone pitch of heaven, the **octave** <term>dia pason</term>.
> Such a Siren-song did Hermes, son of Zeus, tune
> upon the seven-toned lyre, an image of the divinely-counseled cosmos.

<margin>5</margin> In these lines, the poet has indicated the order of the spheres which he intended, but their distances and almost everything else appear to have been composed at random. For while he says that Hermes constructed the seven-stringed lyre as an image of the cosmos, he [actually] composes the whole as a nine-stringed system tuned in the **octave** <term>dia pason</term> concord, yet containing six [whole] tones.

<margin>10</margin> He assigns the note of the **highest string** <term>hypate</term> <note>The note with the lowest pitch but the highest physical position on the lyre</note> to the Earth, because it is the heaviest of all things; and yet, since it is immobile in the middle, it does not produce a sound at all. He assigns the note of the **lowest "joined" string** <term>nete synemmenon</term> <note>The highest pitch in a specific ancient Greek scale</note> to the sphere of the fixed stars, and this is 6 [units]... <margin>15</margin> and in between these he places the notes of the planets.

Again, he assigns the note of the **middle string** <term>mese</term> to the Sun, even though the *hypate* (Earth) does not harmonize with the *mese* at the interval of a **fifth** <term>dia pente</term>, but at a **fourth** <term>dia tessaron</term>; nor does it harmonize with the "joined" *nete* at an octave, but rather with the **"disjoined" string** <term>diezeugmenon</term>. <margin>20</margin> Furthermore, the entire system is not tuned according to the **diatonic genus** <note>The standard musical scale consisting of whole tones and semitones</note>; for in this genus, neither an uncomposed three-semitone interval <note>A minor third</note> nor more than one semitone in succession is played.

---

**1** *Fitted with rays* in manuscript B; *fitted by rays* in manuscript C. *Chilling* [neuter plural] in manuscript C; *chilling* [masculine/feminine plural] in manuscript B. **2** *Of heaven* [accusative] in manuscripts B and C. *Heaven of six tones* proposed by Schneider. *The* [singular] in C; *of the* [plural] in B. *Of all* in BC, emended by Schneider; Bergk thinks several words are missing at the end. **3** *Such a* in C; *Therefore* in B. *Siren* [masculine] in BC, emended to *Siren* [feminine] by Naeke. **4** *Divinely-counseled* in BC, emended by Meineke. **5** *In*] *If* in the first hand of B. **9** *Tuned* in the first hand of B. **10** *Containing* [misspelled] in B. **12** *Or*] *The* in B; *If* in Martianus Capella. **14** *To the* [dative]] *The* [accusative] in the first hand of B. **15** *Sphere* [accusative] in the first hand of B. *And between these he places 6...* in Martianus Capella, perhaps correctly; in B, there is a small empty space of a few letters after the '6'.

<summary>This page concludes Alexander’s poem comparing the cosmos to a seven-stringed lyre and begins a technical critique of his musical theory. The commentator argues that the poet's mapping of planets to musical notes is mathematically and harmonically inconsistent with ancient Greek musical standards.</summary>
<keywords>Music of the Spheres, Alexander of Ephesus, Hermes, lyre, octave, hypate, nete, mese, diatonic genus, planetary harmony</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical and astronomical systems, moving from the critique of Alexander of Ephesus’s poetry to the theories of Eratosthenes of Cyrene (c. 276–194 BCE), a polymath who famously calculated the Earth's circumference and wrote on the "harmony of the spheres."</meta>

## ON THE CONCORD OF THE SPHERES
142

...nor indeed according to the **chromatic genus** <term>chroma</term> <note>One of the three ancient Greek musical scales, characterized by the use of semitones</note>; for again, in the chromatic genus, an uncompounded whole tone is not melodic. And if anyone should say that the system is a mixture of both genera... <note>The text is fragmentary here</note> the arrangement of more than two consecutive semitones is entirely un-melodic. But these matters remain obscure to those uninitiated in the study of music.

Eratosthenes, however, demonstrates the **harmony** <term>harmonia</term> produced by the movement of the stars in a similar fashion, though he does not maintain the same order of the wandering stars <note>The planets</note>. Instead, he says that after the Moon, the Sun is the second body carried above the Earth. For he says that while Hermes was still young, having constructed the lyre, he then for the first time ascended into heaven and passed by the so-called wandering stars. He marveled at the **harmony** <term>harmonia</term> produced by the rush of their motion, finding it <insert>similar</insert> to the lyre he had constructed...

<margin>15</margin> In his epic verses, this man appears to leave the Earth immobile, but he creates sounds for the seven wandering stars beneath the sphere of the fixed stars. He sets them all in motion around the Earth and creates an eight-stringed lyre within the **octave** <term>dia pason</term> concord [the most musical...

---

<margin>1-20</margin> <note>Apparatus Criticus: 1. **chromatic genus**: B reads "and chromatic"; Mart. reads "in the chromatic." 7. See Eratosthenes, *Collected Fragments*, page 38. 11. **still**: B reads "is." 12. **first**: B reads "firstly"; Chalcidius 73 reads "at first." 13. **he marveled**: B reads "he might marvel"; Mart. reads "he would marvel"; Chalcidius reads "having marveled." 15. Mart. added "similar" after "harmony." Chalcidius adds: "marveling that the image of the work he had invented was found also in the heavens through the arrangement of the stars, which was the cause of their concord; he reviewed them, seeing that first from the Earth he had passed the globe of the Moon, after which he surpassed that of the Sun, then that of Mercury (Stilbon) and the others, up to the highest and most exalted sphere of the fixed stars." 17. See page 105. 20. B reads "the most musical Alexan-"; the derivative manuscripts read "the most musical Alexander"; Mart. reads "more musical than Alexander"; Bergk reads "not like the most musical Alexander."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary order, moving from general mathematical opinions to the specific cosmic model described by Plato in the "Myth of Er" at the end of his Republic.</meta>

# THE PLATONIC MYTH #

...Alexander]. However, the mathematicians do not all establish the same order for the wandering stars <note>The "wandering stars" are the planets, from the Greek *planētai*.</note>, nor even a single consistent one; rather, after the Moon they place the Sun, and above this, some place the "Gleaming One" <note>original: *stilbōn*, the ancient name for the planet Mercury</note>, then the "Light-Bringer" <note>original: *phosphoros*, the ancient name for the planet Venus</note>, <insert>while others place the "Light-Bringer"</insert> first, then the "Gleaming <margin>5</margin> One," and the others in the order already mentioned.

Plato, at the end of the *Republic*, while exhorting his readers toward justice and virtue, recounts a certain myth regarding the arrangement of the heavenly bodies. He describes a certain axis extending through the pole like a pillar, and another part like a distaff <margin>10</margin> and a spindle. He says there are certain hollow whorls fitted into one another around this spindle, which represent the spheres of the stars: seven belonging to the wandering stars, and one outer sphere for the fixed stars which contains the others within itself. He clarifies the order of these spheres by the size of each star, by the color of each, and further <margin>15</margin> by the speed of their motion in the direction opposite to the rotation of the whole universe, writing as follows:

> When seven days had passed for each of those in the meadow, they had to rise from there on the eighth day and set out, <margin>20</margin> and arrive on the fourth day at a place from which they could see...

---

**1** <note>original Latin: *mathematici tamen...*</note> However, the mathematicians do not all set down this same single arrangement of the wandering stars (Chalcidius). **4** "this" corrected from "of these" in Manuscript B. **5** "others [place] the Light-Bringer" added by Martin. "Beyond this, some place Mercury, others place some other of the remaining stars" (Chalcidius). **7** "The contents of the *Republic* concerning the Myth of Er" <note>The Greek text here uses "Pamphylos," referring to Er's origin as a Pamphylian.</note> in Manuscript B (these words are inserted without any distinction from the rest of the text). *Republic*: compare Book 10, page 614B and following. **9** Compare Boeckh, *Shorter Writings*, Vol. III, page 299 and following. **10** "the pole" in Manuscript B; "as if" or "which" in Manuscript B*; "distaff" misspelled in Manuscript B*. **11** "this" in Manuscript B. **13** Manuscript B*; "one" or "the first" in Manuscript B; "the first" according to Martin. **19** Plato's *Republic*, page 616B; "days" in Manuscript B. **20** "to travel" in Plato's original. **21** "on the fourth day" and "clear" in Manuscript B.

<meta>This page continues the translation of the "Myth of Er" from Plato's Republic. The previous page established the context of Er's journey and his arrival at the viewpoint of the celestial arrangement.</meta>

### STORY ###

...from above, extending through the whole of heaven and earth, a light stretched straight like a pillar, most like a rainbow, yet brighter and purer. They arrived there after a journey of one day, and they saw there, at the middle of the <margin>5</margin> light, the ends of the bonds of heaven stretched out; for this light is the bond of heaven, like the undergirding ropes of triremes <note>Ancient Greek warships; these ropes were heavy cables wrapped around the hull to prevent it from breaking apart in heavy seas.</note>, thus holding together the entire rotation. And from the ends extended the Spindle of Necessity <note>original: *Anankēs atrakton*</note>, through which all the rotations are turned. Of this, <margin>10</margin> the shaft <note>original: *ēlakatē*; the central rod of the spindle.</note> and the hook are made of adamant <note>A legendary, indestructible metal or diamond-like substance.</note>, while the whorl <note>original: *sphondylos*; the heavy, circular weight at the base of a spindle that provides momentum for spinning.</note> is a mixture of this and other materials. The nature of the whorl is as follows: its shape is like the kind used here on earth; but we must imagine it, according to his description, in this way: as if in one large <margin>15</margin> hollow whorl, carved out all the way through, another smaller one of the same kind were nested, fitting together like jars that fit into one another; and then a third one, and a fourth, and four more. For there are eight whorls in total, nested within one another, showing their rims <margin>20</margin> as circles from above, and forming the continuous surface of a single whorl around the shaft; and that through...

---

1 <note>original: *tetagmenon*</note> **arranged** in Manuscript B (likewise in the Vatican B manuscript of Plato) 2 <note>original: *empheres*</note> **resembling**] Plato’s standard text uses a slightly different word for **resembling** (as does the Venetian manuscript B) 3 **purer** in Manuscript B; Plato uses **to arrive** 4 **to see** in Plato] **must** in Manuscript B 5 **the ends of its bonds** in Plato (many manuscripts place **of its** at the end; one manuscript reads **the ends from the bonds**) **stretched out; for this light** in Manuscript B 7 **this whole holding together** (corrected from **continually**) in Manuscript B 8 **Necessities** (plural) in Manuscript B 10 **shaft** [spelling variant] in Manuscript B; **and**] **both and** in Plato 11 **from this and other kinds** in Plato 12 **of the** in Plato] **the** in Manuscript B 13 **just as the** in Plato 14 **as if in one large** in Manuscript B; **as if it were in one large** in Plato 15 **hollow and carved out** [spelling variants] in Manuscript B; **otherwise** in Manuscript B; **smaller** [spelling variant] in Manuscript B 16 **would be drawn** in Manuscript B; **the** before **into** in Plato (missing in the Vindobonensis B manuscript) 17 **indeed** in Plato 20 **whorls** in Manuscript B and two manuscripts of Plato 21 **but through** in Plato] **through however** in Manuscript B

<margin>15</margin> circles are carried around slowly in the opposite direction to the whole. <meta>This page continues Theon of Smyrna’s commentary on the "Music of the Spheres" and the "Spindle of Necessity" from Plato’s Republic. The previous page detailed the physical dimensions and colors of the eight planetary whorls. This page discusses the Sirens who sit upon these whorls, their harmonic voices, and the etymological link between "Sirens" and the "blazing" of the stars.</meta>

### ON THE SIRENS IN PLATO ###
->Theon of Smyrna<-

...a Siren carried around with each circle, emitting a single sound, a single tone; and from all eight of them, a single harmony sounds in unison. <note>original: "Σειρῆνα συμπεριφερομένην, φωνὴν μίαν ἱεῖσαν, ἕνα τόνον· ἐκ πασῶν ὀκτὼ οὐσῶν ἁρμονίαν συμφωνεῖν." This is the classic definition of the "Music of the Spheres."</note> These things, then, are what Plato says; we have provided an explanation of them in our commentaries on the *Republic*. <margin>5</margin> We have also constructed a model of the spheres <note>A mechanical model or celestial globe used for demonstration.</note> according to these descriptions; for Plato himself says that to attempt to teach such things without visual models <note>original: "δι' ὄψεως μιμημάτων"</note> is a "vain labor." Regarding the Sirens whom he says stand upon the circles, some <insert>say</insert> they are the planets <margin>10</margin> themselves, named from the word "to blaze" <term>seiriazein: to shine with a scorching or intense light</term>. For Adrastus says that the poets commonly call all the stars "Sirius-stars," just as Ibycus writes:

> ...flaming, just as through the long night the Sirius-stars shine brightly... <note>original: "φλεγέθων, ἅπερ διὰ νύκτα μακρὰν σείρια παμφανόωντα"</note>

<margin>15</margin> And by way of distinction, some call only the bright and eminent stars by this name, as Aratus says the star of the Dog <note>The star Sirius.</note> "blazes piercingly," <note>original: "ὀξέα σειριᾶν"</note> and the tragic poet <note>Likely Euripides in Iphigenia in Aulis.</note> says it regarding one of the planets:

---

<note>Critical Apparatus: 1. Plato reads "one tone," while Manuscript B reads "nine-toned." 4. Proclus in his commentary on the Timaeus (p. 26 A) mentions others identify the children of Callaeschrus as Critias and Glaucon, as perhaps Theon the Platonist does. 6. Plato, Timaeus (p. 40 D): "To speak of these things without visual models would be a vain labor." 11. Manuscript B reads "seiroous." 12. Ibycus: fragment 3. See also Hesychius regarding "in the manner of the Sirius-dog." 15. See Pseudo-Eratosthenes, Catasterismi 33: "It is great and bright; astrologers call such stars 'Sirius-stars' because of the movement of their flame." 17. Euripides, Iphigenia in Aulis, line 6.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial Sirens and the mechanics of planetary motion according to Theon of Smyrna. The previous page linked the term "Siren" to the "scorching" (seiriazein) light of the stars. Here, the text transitions from the poetic and Pythagorean concepts of the "Music of the Spheres" to the technical astronomical definitions of planetary progression and retrogression, citing both Adrastus and Plato.</meta>

# ON THE ERRATIC MOTIONS OF THE STARS #

->"What star is this that sails along?<-
->Sirius?"<-
<note>original: "τί ποτ' ἄρα ὁ ἀστὴρ ὅδε πορθμεύει σείριος;" These lines are from Euripides’ play *Iphigenia in Aulis*. Theon uses them to bridge the discussion of Sirens and the star Sirius.</note>

Some say that the "Sirens" do not refer to the stars themselves, but rather, according to the Pythagorean view, to the sounds and harmonious, consonant notes <margin>5</margin> produced by their motion, from which a single harmonious voice is perfected.

Regarding the wandering stars <note>The planets.</note>, Adrastus says that some are "always lagging" <term>hypoleptika: planets that move steadily through the zodiac without appearing to go backward</term>, such as the Sun and the Moon. For these never move into the preceding signs of the zodiac, <margin>10</margin> but are always seen moving into the following signs; for this reason, they perform neither "stations" <term>stērigmous: the points where a planet appears to stop in the sky</term> nor "retrogressions" <term>anapodismous: backward or "re-stepping" motion</term>.

The other planets both "precede" and "lag," just like the rest; for this reason, they necessarily appear at times to be stationary and <margin>15</margin> at other times to be moving backward. For "lagging" <term>hypoleipsis: the prograde motion of a planet through the zodiac</term> is the appearance of a wandering star as it moves toward the following signs of the zodiac and toward the east, as Adrastus says. But as Plato says, it is not merely an appearance, but is the actual movement of the wandering star into the following zodiac signs toward the east according to its own proper motion—for example, from Cancer into Leo. "Preceding" <term>proēgēsis: the apparent westward or retrograde motion</term> <margin>20</margin> is the appearance of a wandering star as it moves toward the preceding signs and toward the west—for example, from Cancer into Gemini...

---

<margin>1</margin> Euripides writes "τίς ποτ' ἄρ' ἀστὴρ" <note>Which star...?</note>. Bergk <note>Theodor Bergk, a 19th-century philologist.</note> thinks it should be written "τί ποτ' ἄρ' ἀστὴρ" <note>What star...?</note>. <margin>2</margin> **In the two existing manuscripts of this play, the words "What star is this that sails along?" are attributed to Agamemnon, and "Sirius," etc. are attributed to the Old Man; Ennius <note>An early Roman poet who adapted Greek plays into Latin.</note> seems to have read it no differently (Iphigenia fragment 1, Ribbeck). See also, besides the editors of Euripides, Bremi’s *Philological Contributions from Switzerland* I, pages 211 and following, and Dindorf in the *Journal for Philology*, 1868, page 410.** <margin>3</margin> **Manuscript B* reads "seirinas" <note>a variant spelling of Sirens</note>.** <margin>7</margin> **Heading in B: "What is lagging and preceding, station and retrogression?" (Martianus reads "hypolepsis").** <margin>9</margin> **"Never" in manuscript B* is written as "not at any time"; Chalcidius <note>A 4th-century translator of Plato into Latin.</note> also reads "numquam" (never) in section 74.** <margin>11</margin> **Heading in B: "What is lagging?"** <margin>19</margin> **Heading in B: "What is preceding?"**

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of planetary motion, following the introduction of Adrastus’s theories. Having established that the Sun and Moon move steadily forward, the text now defines the visual phenomena of "stations" and "retrogressions" seen in other planets, explaining them as optical illusions caused by the planets' specific orbits relative to the fixed stars and the Earth.</meta>

# OF THE STARS #

...[retrogressions]. <margin>1</margin> A station <term>stērigmos: the point where a planet appears to stop in the sky</term> is the appearance of a wandering star <note>A planet.</note> as if it were standing and remaining for a longer time alongside one of the fixed stars. A retrogression <term>anapodismos: a "stepping back" or backward motion</term> is the appearance of a wandering star turning back <margin>3</margin> from a station, as if moving in the opposite direction to its previous path.

<margin>5</margin> All these things appear to us to happen, though they are not truly performed in this way; the cause of this is that each of the wandering stars, being carried on its own specific circle or in its own sphere below the fixed stars, appears to us—due to projection <note>original: "epiprosthesis," referring to the way objects at different distances are projected onto a single background.</note>—to be carried along the zodiac circle that lies above <margin>10</margin> them. Adrastus also defines these matters so that the difference between the hypotheses concerning the planets might become clear, from which the observed phenomena follow.

He says that the whole cosmos is of such a nature and composed of such and so many parts as we <margin>15</margin> have distinguished, being carried in a circular motion proper to the spherical shape by the first cause <note>The "Prime Mover" or the outermost sphere of the universe.</note>; for this reason, it was constructed for the sake of the best and most excellent end. But for the numbering of time and the change of the perigees <term>perigeion: the point in an orbit closest to the Earth</term> and apogees <term>apogeion: the point in an orbit furthest from the Earth</term>, the motion of <margin>20</margin> the planets was established as a complex variety, so that things here on earth might follow it; for through the turnings of these planets as they approach and depart, the things here on earth change along with them in every way.

<meta>This page continues the transition from the general movement of the heavens to the specific dynamics of the "wandering stars" (planets). It moves from the optical definitions of stations and retrogressions to a broader philosophical and cosmological explanation of why the universe is structured with a stable center and moving parts.</meta>

->ON THE MOTIONS OF THE PLANETS<- <note>original: "ERRANTIUM MOTIBUS"</note>

For the movement of the fixed stars is simple and single in its circular path, being both ordered and uniform. But the movement of the other wandering stars <note>The planets.</note>, though circular, does not appear simple or single, nor uniform and ordered. Among the things below the moon—around us and reaching even to us—there is every kind of change and motion and, as it is said, <margin>5</margin>

> where strife and murder and tribes of other dooms [dwell]. <note>A quote from the Pre-Socratic philosopher Empedocles, describing the chaotic nature of the earthly realm.</note>

For here there is birth and decay, growth and decrease, every kind of alteration, and various types of movement from place to place. He says that the wandering stars are the causes of these things. <margin>10</margin> One might say that these more precious, divine, and eternal things—unbegotten <gloss>or: uncreated</gloss> and indestructible—did not come into being for the sake of the lesser, mortal, and perishable things; rather, while the heavenly bodies always remain as they are because they are most beautiful, best, and most blessed, <margin>15</margin> the things here on Earth follow them by consequence.

In order for the circular motion of the whole cosmos to remain forever the same—being a kind of divine activity and life—it is necessary for the Earth to remain at the center, <insert>around which</insert> <note>original: "hēi"</note> that which is carried in a circle will revolve. And if it is necessary for the Earth to remain below, <margin>20</margin> it is also necessary for fire to occupy the opposite place, situated just beneath the ethereal substance <term>aithēr: the "fifth element" or "quintessence" that makes up the heavens</term> that moves in a circle. Since these are positioned so far apart, it is necessary that the other elements—water and air—occupy the middle space according to proportion. And since these things exist... <margin>24</margin>

<summary>This page contrasts the perfect, uniform motion of the fixed stars with the complex movements of the planets, which are cited as the drivers of change and decay in the sublunary (earthly) realm. It also explains the physical necessity of a stationary Earth at the center of the universe to allow for the harmonious rotation of the elements.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 149 regarding the influence of celestial bodies on the earthly realm. While the previous page established the necessity of the Earth being at the center of the cosmos, this page explains how the "wandering stars" (planets) create the conditions for life and change on Earth through their complex motions.</meta>

->OF THE STARS<-

...change exists in things here, because their <note>original: *hylē*</note> matter is entirely mutable and possesses opposing forces. Change is brought about by the varied motion of the wandering stars <note>The "wandering stars" (*planōmenōn*) are the planets, as opposed to the "fixed stars" which do not move relative to one another.</note>. For if these were carried along parallel paths just like the fixed stars, the state of the whole universe would always remain the same, and there would be no alteration or change in our realm. 

But as it is, the solstices and equinoxes, the advances and retreats in both altitude and latitude—primarily of the Sun and Moon, though not excluding the others—produce the various seasons and effect all change, birth, and transformation here. 

The appearance of variety in the movement of the wandering stars occurs because they are fixed within their own specific circles and spheres. Moving through those, they seem to us to be carried through the <term>zodiac<gloss>The "circle of little animals," the belt of the heavens through which the planets appear to move.</gloss></term>, just as Pythagoras first understood: while their inherent motion is ordered, simple, and uniform, a certain varied and irregular motion arises as a byproduct of their arrangement.

Concerning the position of the spheres [or circles]... which will <term>save the appearances<gloss>A classic Greek astronomical principle (*sōzein ta phainomena*) of creating a mathematical model that matches observed data while maintaining the ideal of circular motion.</gloss></term>, he explains the following: 

It is natural and necessary that—just like the fixed stars—each of the other heavenly bodies moves according to a simple, single, and uniform motion in an orderly fashion. It is clear that...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from page 149 regarding the motions of the planets (the "wandering stars"). Having established that their seemingly irregular paths are actually part of an orderly system, the author now explains the geometric model—specifically the "double motion" theory—that allows astronomers to account for their behavior while maintaining the ideal of perfect circular motion.</meta>

# ON THE MOTIONS OF THE WANDERING STARS <note>original Latin: *Errantium Motibus*</note>

I say this will come about if, by mental conception, we set the world still and consider the wandering stars <note>The planets</note> beneath the zodiac, assuming them to be motionless for the sake of the hypothesis, yet moving. For in this way, their motion will no longer appear varied and irregular, but will be seen to be performed in an orderly fashion, as is demonstrated by us in our work on the <margin>5</margin> **Platonic sphere-making** <note>original: *sphairopoiia*; this refers to the construction of celestial globes or models to demonstrate planetary paths</note>. The cause of their seemingly overlapping and varied motion is their **double motion**: the sphere of the fixed stars is carried from east to west around the axis passing through the poles, <margin>10</margin> dragging the wandering stars along by its own force <note>The "diurnal motion" or the daily rotation of the heavens</note> and tracing all the parallel circles along which the fixed stars are carried. However, the wandering stars themselves, according to their own proper motion—which is slower—are carried from west to east over unequal periods of time beneath the <margin>15</margin> **zodiac**, which is tilted across the three parallel circles: the winter, the equinoctial, and the summer <note>These correspond to the Tropics of Capricorn and Cancer, and the Equator</note>. This motion occurs around another axis which is perpendicular to the zodiac, and which stands apart from the axis of the fixed stars by the length of the side of a **fifteen-sided polygon** <note>The side of a 15-gon subtends 24 degrees (360/15), which was the standard ancient Greek estimate for the obliquity of the ecliptic—the tilt of the Earth's axis</note>. This axis of the wandering stars is what Plato calls the **distaff** and the **spindle** <note>A reference to the Myth of Er in Plato’s *Republic*, where the mechanism of the heavens is described as a cosmic spindle</note>.

<margin>20</margin> Adrastus says <note>Adrastus of Aphrodisias, a 2nd-century Peripatetic philosopher</note> that to move **uniformly** <insert>What is uniform motion?</insert> means to traverse equal distances in equal times, and not for a body to sometimes slacken and at other times intensify its own speed. To move **orderly** <insert>What is orderly motion?</insert> is to never stop or turn back, but to be carried toward the...

---

2 **the world**] *Manuscript B reads "and the world"* &emsp; 8 *In Manuscript B, "sphere" appears after "west" rather than here, likely a scribal error corrected by a transposition mark* &emsp; 13 **slower**] *Manuscript B reads "shorter"* &emsp; 19 *See page 143, 10* &emsp; 20 **Heading:** *What is uniform motion? (found in B)* &emsp; 23 **Heading:** *What is orderly motion? (found in B)* &emsp; 24 **turn back**] *Manuscript B contains a minor spelling error here*

<summary>This page explains the "double motion" of planets: they are carried daily by the rotation of the fixed stars (East to West) while slowly moving in their own orbits (West to East) along the tilted zodiac. It also defines "uniform" and "orderly" motion according to the philosopher Adrastus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the movements of the celestial bodies. Having previously argued that the heavens move in an orderly, circular fashion, the author now addresses why the planets appear to move irregularly or "disorderly" from our perspective on Earth. This page introduces the geometric model of the Sun’s path through the zodiac as the primary example for understanding these complex appearances.</meta>

# ON THE MOTIONS OF THE WANDERING STARS <note>original Latin: *DE STELL. ERR. MOTIBUS*</note>

...these always move in the same way. It seems to us, however, that all the <term>wandering stars<note>original Greek: *planōmena*; this refers to the five planets known in antiquity—Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—along with the Sun and Moon, which all "wander" against the background of fixed stars.</note></term> share in <term>irregularities<note>original: *anōmalia*; the apparent changes in speed or direction (like retrograde motion) that seem to break the rule of perfect circular motion.</note></term>, and some even share in total disorder. What then is the cause of such an appearance? The first cause is that, while these bodies exist in different spheres and move on different circles along which they are carried, they only *appear* to be carried through the <term>zodiac<note>original: *zōdiakos*; the "circle of animals," the belt of the heavens through which the Sun, Moon, and planets move.</note></term>, as was said before. <margin>5</margin> By accident, as previously stated, although the seven <note>The seven celestial wanderers</note> perform their own simple motion, they appear to trace multiple and different circles. This would become clear to us if we consider a single case, that of the most visible and greatest of the wandering stars: the **Sun**.

->Aries<-
->α<-

A circular astronomical diagram representing the zodiac. A circle is intersected by two perpendicular diameters: a vertical one ending at points **α** (top) and **γ** (bottom), and a horizontal one ending at points **β** (left) and **δ** (right). The center point where they intersect is labeled **ϑ**. The exterior points are labeled with zodiac signs: "**Aries**" at **α**, "**Cancer**" at **β**, "**Libra**" at **γ**, and "**Capricorn**" at **δ**. Inside the four quadrants are numerical values for the length of the seasons in days: the top-left quadrant (Spring) is "**94 1/6**", the top-right quadrant (Winter) is "**90 1/8**", the bottom-left quadrant (Summer) is "**92 1/6**", and the bottom-right quadrant (Autumn) is "**88 1/8**".

->γ<-
->Libra<-

Let the zodiac be the circle **αβγδ**. Let its center—which is also the center of the universe, around which the **Earth** is said to be firmly fixed in the middle—be the point **ϑ**. Through this center, let the <margin>15</margin> diameters **αγ** and **βδ** be drawn at right angles to one another. Let point **α** be at the beginning of **Aries**, **β** at the beginning of **Cancer**, **γ** at the beginning of **Libra**, and **δ** at the beginning of **Capricorn**.

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of the Sun's path, moving from a general discussion of the "wandering stars" to the specific mathematical observations of the Sun's movement through the four quarters of the year.</meta>

### ON THE MOTION OF THE SUN <note>original Latin: DE MOTU SOLIS</note>

Thus, the Sun is seen to produce the **vernal equinox** <note>The spring equinox, when day and night are of equal length, occurring around March 21st.</note> when it reaches point **α**, the **summer solstice** <note>original: tropē; literally a "turning" of the Sun's path.</note> at point **β**, the **autumnal equinox** at point **γ**, and the **winter solstice** at point **δ**. Even though the four segments of its path—**αβ**, **βγ**, **γδ**, and **δα**—are equal quarters of the circle, the Sun passes through them <margin>5</margin> irregularly in unequal amounts of time. From the vernal equinox to the summer solstice, it arrives in **94 ½ days**; from the summer solstice to the autumnal equinox, in **92 ½ days**; from the autumnal equinox to the winter <margin>10</margin> solstice, in **88 ⅛ days**; and for the remaining distance from the winter solstice back to the vernal equinox, it takes **90 ⅛ days** <note>The Greek text here is slightly damaged or varies by manuscript; these figures were standard in Hellenistic astronomy to account for the ~365.25 day year.</note>. Consequently, the Sun completes the whole circle in a year of approximately **365 ¼ days**. It moves at its slowest when it is at the beginning of **Gemini**, at its fastest at the beginning of **Sagittarius**, and at a medium speed when <margin>15</margin> passing through **Virgo** and **Pisces**.

It is a natural principle, as we maintain, and a necessary one, that all celestial <note>original: theia; literally "divine" things.</note> bodies move uniformly and in an orderly fashion. It is therefore clear that while the Sun is carried uniformly and orderly upon its own specific circle, it only *appears* to us, who are watching from point **θ** <note>Point θ represents the Earth, positioned away from the actual center of the Sun's orbit.</note>, to be carried <margin>20</margin> through the circle **αβγδ** irregularly. If its circle shared the same center as the universe—that is, if it were centered on **θ**—the segments divided by the diameters **αγ** and **βδ** would result in equal times, because the angles around the center and the [arcs] would be equal...

<summary>This page explains the varying lengths of the four seasons and identifies the Sun's varying speed through the zodiac. It introduces the "eccentric" model, arguing that while the Sun moves at a constant speed, it only appears to move irregularly because the Earth (point θ) is not at the center of the Sun's orbit.</summary>

<keywords>Sun, seasons, equinox, solstice, Gemini, Sagittarius, Virgo, Pisces, uniform motion, eccentricity, Greek astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric explanation of the Sun's apparent motion. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how the Sun only *appears* to move irregularly because its path is not centered on the Earth.</meta>

->### ON THE MOTION OF THE SUN <note>original Latin: DE MOTU SOLIS</note><-

...[equality] of the arcs <note>Referring to the segments of the Sun's path discussed on the previous page.</note> would provide the same difficulty. It is clear, then, that the Sun moving in a different way—and specifically not around the center **ϑ** <note>The Greek letter theta (ϑ) is used here to represent the center of the Earth or the universe.</note>—is the cause of this appearance. Therefore, the Sun's path must either enclose **ϑ** within itself, or it must pass through it, or <margin>5</margin> it must leave **ϑ** outside of itself. 

Now, for the solar circle to pass through the point **ϑ** is impossible; for in that case, the Sun itself would eventually collide with the Earth. Furthermore, for those living on one side of the Earth, it would always be day, while for those on the other side, it would always be night. The Sun would not <margin>10</margin> be seen rising or setting, nor moving around the Earth at all—consequences which are clearly absurd. It remains, therefore, that the point **ϑ** is either enclosed within the solar circle or is left outside of it. 

Whichever of these two is assumed, he says, the "phenomena will be saved" <note>A famous phrase in ancient science (sōzein ta phainomena), meaning that a mathematical model successfully accounts for what we actually observe in the sky.</note>, and from this, the dispute among <margin>15</margin> mathematicians will be shown to be pointless. Some of them claim that the **wandering stars** <term>planēta: literally "wanderers," referring to the planets including the Sun and Moon</term> are carried only by **eccentric** circles, others by **epicycles**, and others around the same center as the sphere of the **fixed stars** <note>The outermost sphere of the universe which contains the stars that do not move relative to one another.</note>. It will be demonstrated that those who describe these three <margin>20</margin> circles—the one around the center of the universe, the **eccentric**, and the **epicycle**—are actually describing the same physical reality through different mathematical accidents. 

For if we assume, he says, that the center **ϑ** is enclosed within the solar circle, but not as its actual center, such a model is called **eccentric** <term>eccentric: a circular orbit whose center is offset from the Earth</term>. If, however, the center **ϑ** is left outside the circle, it is called an **epicycle** <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle</term>.

<summary>The author argues that the Sun's apparent irregular speed is caused by its path being offset from the Earth's center, explaining that both "eccentric" and "epicycle" models can mathematically account for this observation.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, solar motion, eccentric, epicycle, saving the phenomena, mathematical models, ancient cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>The previous page established that the Sun's observed irregularity must be caused by its path being shifted away from the Earth's center (point ϑ). It concluded that the "phenomena are saved" whether one uses an eccentric circle or an epicycle model, rendering the dispute between different mathematical schools academic rather than physical.</meta>

-># ON THE ECCENTRIC CIRCLE <note>Latin: DE CIRCULO ECCENTRO</note><-

Let it first be assumed that the sun's 
circle is **eccentric** <term>eccentric: literally "out of center," a circle whose center is shifted away from the Earth</term>, namely the circle **εζηκ**, inclined in such a way as to have its 
center beneath the **εζ** arc, for instance at point **μ**. When this circle is 
divided into 365 1/4 equal parts <note>original: τξε' δ''; this represents the number of days in a solar year</note>, let the **εζ** arc 
be 94 1/2 parts, the **ζη** arc 92 1/2, the **ηκ** arc 88 1/8, <margin>5</margin>
and the **κε** arc 90 1/8. It is clear, then, that when the sun is 
at point **ε**, it will appear to us—viewing it from point **ϑ** along a straight line—
to be at point **α**. Having traversed the arc **εζ**, which is the greatest of the 
four segments into which its own circle is divided, in 94 1/2 days 
(the same number as its <insert>degrees</insert>), moving at a uniform speed, and having reached <margin>10</margin>

# ON THE CIRCLE <note>Original: DE CIRCULO</note>

...at point **ζ**, it will appear to us to be at point **β**, and it will seem to have traversed the arc **αβ**, which is a quadrant <term>quadrant: one-quarter of a circle, or 90 degrees</term> of the zodiacal circle, in a different number of days, irregularly. Then again, after traversing the arc **ζη**—the second in size of its own circle—uniformly in 92 1/2 days <note>Original: ζβ' ϛ'; in this context, ϛ' signifies 1/2</note>, which was the number of its degrees, and having arrived at point **η**, it will appear to us at point **γ**. It will then seem to have traversed the arc **βγ**, a quadrant of the zodiac and equal in size to the previous one, in fewer days and irregularly. Similarly, having traveled through the arc **ηκ**, which is the smallest of the four segments of its own circle, consisting of 88 1/8 degrees <note>Original: πη' η''; η'' represents the fraction 1/8</note>, in that same number of days, and having arrived at point **κ**, it will appear at point **δ** to those viewing it from point **ϑ**. It will seem to have traversed the arc **γδ**, a quadrant equal to the previous ones, in the minimum number of days. Finally, following the same logic, having traveled the remaining arc **κε** in 90 1/8 days (the number of its degrees) and having returned to point **ε**, it will seem to have completed the arc **δα**, a quadrant and equal to the others, in 90 1/8 days, and to have returned to point **α**. Thus, while the sun travels through its own circle uniformly <note>at a constant speed</note>, it will appear to have traversed the circle of the zodiac irregularly. But if, joining the centers, we extend the line **ϑμ** in both directions in a straight line...

# ON THE ECCENTRIC <note>Original: ECCENTRO</note>

Since the center of the circle **εξ** is point **μ**, the distance **μν** will be equal to **μξ**. Consequently, when the sun is at point **ν**, it will be at its apogee <term>apogee: the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</term>; and to us looking from point **θ** <note>the Earth</note>, it will appear smallest in size and seem to be moving most slowly. This it appears to do when it is at approximately the fifth and a half degree <margin>5</margin> of Gemini <note>original: Διδύμων</note>. However, when it arrives at point **ξ**, it will be at its perigee <term>perigee: the point in an orbit closest to the Earth</term>, and because of this, it will appear largest in appearance and will seem to be moving most quickly. These phenomena it appears to do, in turn, at the 5 1/2 degree of Sagittarius <note>original: Τοξότου</note>. And reasonably, around the same degrees of both Pisces <note>original: Ἰχθύων</note> and Virgo <note>original: Παρθένου</note>, <margin>10</margin> it seems to be carried at a medium size and speed. And in this way, he says, all the observed phenomena will be "saved" <note>to "save the phenomena" was the classical goal of providing a mathematical model that matches observed reality</note>.

The circle **εξηκ** is found to be given in its position and size. For let the lines **οπ** and **ρσ** be drawn through point **μ** parallel to lines **αγ** and **βδ** and at right angles to one another, and let the lines **ζμ** and **με** be joined. <margin>15</margin> It is clear, then, that if the circle **εξηκ** is divided into 365 1/4 days, the arc **εξη** will consist of 187 of such days, while the arc **ηκε** will be 178 1/4 days. Therefore, each of the segments **εο**, **πη**, **ρζ**, and **σκ** are equal, while the arcs **σπ**, **πρ**, **ρο**, and **οσ** each consist of 91 1/4 1/16 <note>91 days, 6 hours, and approximately 1 hour 52 minutes</note> of such days. <margin>20</margin> Therefore, the given angle **ομν** will be equal to the angle **θμτ**; similarly, the angle **ρμν** will be equal to **υμθ**. # ON THE ECCENTRIC CIRCLE <note>Original: DE CIRCULO ECCENTRO</note>

And the center **θ** of the universe <note>original: τοῦ παντὸς, "of the all"</note> is given in relation to each of the points **ν** and **ξ**; for one defines the greatest distance <note>apogee</note>, and the other the least <note>perigee</note>. <margin>5</margin> And the line **θμ** is the distance between the center of the universe and the center of the solar circle. Moreover, the ratio of **θμ** <insert>to **μν**</insert> is found, through the treatise concerning distances and magnitudes <note>likely a reference to the work of Hipparchus or Aristarchus of Samos</note>, to be approximately as 1 is to 24.

# ON THE EPICYCLE <note>Original Latin: DE EPICYCLO</note>

...they say that the epicycle <note>literally "the circle upon another circle"</note> is of such a kind. Let there be again a zodiac circle <term>zodiac: the band of the sky through which the sun, moon, and planets appear to move from the perspective of Earth</term> marked **αβγδ**, and a solar circle **εξκ**, leaving outside of itself the point **θ**, which is the center of the universe. Now, as the sphere of the fixed stars <term>sphere of fixed stars: in the geocentric model, the outermost shell of the heavens that carries the stars in a fixed formation</term> is carried from the rising at **β** <note>the East</note> toward the <margin>5</margin> meridian at **α** <term>meridian: the highest point reached by a celestial body in its daily path; mid-heaven</term> and from **α** toward the setting at **δ** <note>the West</note>, the circle **εξκ** will either remain at rest or will itself also be moved, with the sun being carried around it. 

But if it remains at rest, it is clear that the sun will appear neither setting nor rising; rather, it will always create day for those above the earth, and night for those who, in relation <margin>10</margin> to us, are below the earth, and in one rotation of the universe it will seem to pass through all the signs of the zodiac; which things are absurd. Therefore, it too will be moved. And being moved, it will be carried either in the same direction as the universe or in the opposite direction. And <if> in the same direction as the universe, it will be either at equal speed, or faster <margin>15</margin> than it, or slower. 

But if it moves at equal speed—lines **θζν** and **θκλ** having been drawn tangent to the circle **ζε**—the sun will always seem to turn back within the arc **ναλ** of the zodiac. For when it is at **ζ**, it will appear at **ν**; and when at **ε**, at **α**; and having moved to **κ**, at <margin>20</margin> **λ**. Having traversed the arc **ζεκ**, it will seem to have traveled through **ναλ** toward the preceding signs of the zodiac <note>this refers to "retrograde" or westward motion, which contradicts the Sun's observed behavior</note>; and having passed through **κηζ**, it will seem to have been carried through **λαν** toward the following signs <note>eastward motion</note>; which things, again, are not observed. Therefore, the sun's circle **εξκ** does not revolve together with the universe at equal speed...

# ON THE EPICYCLE <note>original Latin: DE EPICYCLO</note>

...it will be carried. But surely it will not move faster, since in that way, outstripping the stars, it will seem to lead ahead of the fixed stars and traverse the zodiac in reverse—for instance, from Aries into Pisces and Aquarius—which is not what is observed. It is therefore clear 
<margin>5</margin> that the circle **εξηκ** <note>represented by the Greek letters epsilon, xi, eta, kappa</note> will either be moved in the same direction as the whole <term>the whole: the rotation of the entire celestial sphere from East to West</term>, but more slowly, and for this reason, being left behind, it will seem to pass into the following signs <note>the sun appears to move "backward" through the zodiac over the course of a year compared to the daily rotation</note>; or, it will be moved on its own account in a direction opposite to the whole, but will be carried along with the whole each day, being overpowered by the 
<margin>10</margin> motion from the east toward the west. For in this way also it will appear to move into the following signs as if being left behind.

->How then will he save the phenomena?<- <term>save the phenomena: a phrase used by ancient astronomers meaning "to find a mathematical model that matches observed reality"</term>

Let the center of the solar circle be **μ**, and let there be drawn with center **θ** and radius **θμ** a circle **μονξ**. Let it be assumed that 
<margin>15</margin> the circle **εξηκ** is now carried along with the whole in the movement from the east toward the west, either being left behind due to slowness, or even being carried in a direction opposite to the whole—which is the view preferred by Plato <note>Plato suggests this in the *Timaeus*, where the "Circle of the Different" moves opposite to the "Circle of the Same"</note>—so that the center, being carried along the circle **μονξ**, revolves 
<margin>20</margin> uniformly in a year, and in the <same> time the sun completes its own circle, likewise being carried uniformly. Again, the sun will be carried upon the circle **εξηκ** either in the same direction as the whole, or in the opposite direction, <but in the same direction> as its own circle, for instance from **κ** to **ε** 
<margin>25</margin> and from **ε** to **ζ**. I say then that as the circle **εξηκ** revolves...

# ON THE EPICYCLE <note>original Latin: DE EPICYCLO</note>

...along the circle **monx** <note>represented by the Greek letters mu, omicron, nu, xi</note> in a direction opposite to the whole, the sun on the circle **exek** <note>epsilon, xi, eta, kappa</note> will be carried in the same direction as the whole and will save the phenomena <term>save the phenomena: a classic goal of ancient astronomy—to find a mathematical model that perfectly matches observed movements in the sky</term>.

For let it first be moved in a direction opposite to the whole, 
<margin>5</margin> but in the same direction as its own circle—for instance, from **e** to **z** or from **z** to **h** or from **h** to **k**. Since, therefore, when the sun is at point **e**, it is at its greatest distance from us, it is clear that point **a** <note>the sun's apparent position on the zodiac</note> is at the 5 1/2 degree of Gemini; point **g** will therefore be at approximately the 5 1/2 
<margin>10</margin> degree of Sagittarius <note>Gemini and Sagittarius are opposite signs in the zodiac</note>. And let **m**, the center of the solar circle, be carried through a quarter-circle arc of the circle **monx**, moving uniformly—this is the arc **mo**—and let it have moved the circle **exek** to the position of circle **lp**. The sun, being carried in the same direction as this motion, let it likewise be carried through a quarter-circle 
<margin>15</margin> arc of circle **exek**, namely the arc **ez**. It will then be at point **p**, but it will appear to us to be at point **s**. Having traversed the quarter-circle **es** of its own circle, it will seem to travel a distance on the zodiac greater than or equal to the arc **abs**, and it will appear to move quickly away from point **a**. 
Again, let the center **o** be carried through the quarter-circle 
<margin>20</margin> arc **on**, and let it have positioned the circle **lp** at the position of circle **phy**. Let the sun have moved through the quarter-circle arc **py**; it will then be at point **y**, but it will appear to us to be at point **g**. It will then seem to have been carried through the arc **sg** of the zodiac, which is smaller than a quarter-circle, 
and to be approaching point **g**.

# ON THE EPICYCLE <note>original Latin: DE EPICYCLO</note>

[A geometric diagram illustrating the theory of epicycles in astronomy. A large central circle with its center labeled with the Greek letter theta ($\vartheta$) has a vertical axis $\alpha\gamma$ and a horizontal axis $\beta\delta$. Four smaller circles (epicycles) are positioned around the circumference of the main circle. The top epicycle is centered at $\alpha$ with points $\epsilon$, $\zeta$, $\eta$, and $\kappa$ marked on its circumference. The left epicycle is centered at a point $o$ on the main circle, with points $\lambda$, $\mu$, $\rho$, and $\sigma$ associated with it. The bottom epicycle is centered at $\upsilon$ with points $\tau$, $\phi$, and $\psi$ marked. The right epicycle is centered at a point near $\delta$ with points $\chi, \psi$, and $\omega$ marked. Various line segments and arcs connect these points, representing the motion of a celestial body (the Sun) as it moves on an epicycle that itself moves along the larger deferent circle.]

It remains then, while the epicycle is carried in a direction opposite to the motion of the whole universe, for the Sun to be carried upon the epicycle in the same direction as the fixed stars; for in this way the phenomena will be saved <note>This is a famous phrase in Greek astronomy—"saving the phenomena"—meaning to create a mathematical model that matches what we actually see in the sky</note>. For example, let the center of the epicycle have been carried 
<margin>5</margin> through a quarter-circle arc along the concentric circle <term>concentric circle: a circle that shares the same center as the observer, often called a deferent</term> **m-o** <note>Greek: mu-omicron</note>, and let it have moved the epicycle to the position **l-p** <note>Greek: lambda-pi</note>; and let the Sun have moved along a similar arc **e-k** <note>Greek: epsilon-kappa</note> of the epicycle; it will then be at point **l**, but to us it will appear to be at point **s** <note>Greek: sigma</note>, having moved through a quarter-circle arc of its own circle; 
<margin>10</margin> but on the zodiac it will seem to have been carried through a smaller arc **a-s** <note>Greek: alpha-sigma</note> and to be moving slowly as it departs from point **a**. Again, let the center **o** <note>Greek: omicron</note> have moved through the quarter-circle arc **o-n** <note>Greek: omicron-nu</note>, and the Sun through a similar arc of the epicycle **l-p**; it will then be at point **u** <note>Greek: upsilon</note>, but it will appear at point **g** <note>Greek: gamma</note>, and it will seem to have moved through the arc 
<margin>15</margin> **s-b-g** <note>Greek: sigma-beta-gamma</note> of the zodiac, which is greater than a quarter-circle, as it accelerates toward point **g**. Moreover...

# ON THE EPICYCLE <note>original Latin: DE EPICYCLO</note>

...let [the center] be carried from point $\nu$ to $\xi$ <note>The letters $\nu$ and $\xi$ represent points on the larger "deferent" circle that carries the epicycle.</note>, traversing the quarter-circle arc $\nu\xi$, and let it have brought the circle $\upsilon\phi$ into alignment with $\chi\psi$. Let the Sun, having moved along the arc $\upsilon\phi$ on the epicycle in a manner similar to those described before, be at point $\chi$. It will appear to be at $\omega$ <note>This indicates the Sun's projected position against the backdrop of the zodiac as seen from Earth.</note>, and will seem to have traversed the arc $\gamma\delta\omega$ of the zodiac, which is larger than a quarter-circle, and to be moving rapidly from $\gamma$ toward $\delta$.

Then, <insert>after the center has traveled</insert> the remaining motion $\xi\mu$, let it restore the circle $\chi\psi$ to the position of the epicycle $\epsilon\zeta\eta$. Then the Sun itself, having been carried through the remaining similar arc $\chi\psi$, is restored to point $\epsilon$. It will appear to be at point $\alpha$, and the Sun <insert>at point $\alpha$</insert> will seem to have traversed the arc $\omega\alpha$ of the zodiac, which is smaller than a quarter-circle, and to approach $\alpha$ slowly.

Thus, according to this hypothesis, the phenomena will be saved <note>In ancient science, to "save the phenomena" meant to develop a mathematical model that perfectly matched the observed physical movements of the stars and planets.</note>. For the Sun will seem to move slowest and to be smallest in size <note>The Sun appears smaller when it is further away at its "apogee."</note> around the 5th or 6th degree of Gemini, and it will appear to move fastest and be largest in size around the same degree of Sagittarius. This happens for a logical reason: for when it moves from $\epsilon$ toward $\kappa$, while the circle itself is moving from $\mu$ toward $o$, being carried in the opposite direction...
