# ON THE EPICYCLE <note>original Latin: DE EPICYCLO</note>

...toward $\pi$, as the epicycle moves from $o$ to $\upsilon$, running along with it <note>The Sun moves in the same direction as the epicycle’s center, making its cumulative speed appear faster to an observer on Earth.</note>, [the Sun] will seem to increase its motion on the zodiac, since the movement occurs in the same direction <insert>as the whole</insert> and, in a sense, coincides. And similarly, being carried from $\upsilon$ toward $\phi$ while the epicycle moves from $\upsilon$ to $\xi$—as if outstripping its own circle—it will seem to accelerate along the zodiac. Conversely, arriving from $\chi$ at $\psi$ while $\xi$ moves <insert>to</insert> $\mu$, moving in opposition to its own circle <note>Here, the Sun's motion on the epicycle is backwards relative to the center's forward motion, causing it to appear slower.</note>, it appears to make its motion along the zodiac slow.

The size of the epicycle is found once again, and the ratio of the distance between the centers to the diameter $\epsilon\eta$ of the epicycle $\epsilon\zeta$ <insert>diameter</insert> <note>This refers to the ratio between the radius of the main orbit (deferent) and the radius of the small circle (epicycle).</note>, inversely to what was shown before, as 24 to 1, by means of the treatise *On Distances and Magnitudes* <note>original Greek: περὶ ἀποστημάτων καὶ μεγεθῶν. This refers to a lost but famous work of ancient astronomy, likely by Hipparchus or Aristarchus, which used geometry to calculate the scale of the cosmos.</note>. For the greatest distance <term>apogee: the point where the Sun is furthest from Earth</term> of the Sun is $\theta\epsilon$, while the least distance <term>perigee: the point where the Sun is closest to Earth</term> is $\theta\upsilon$; and the difference between the greatest and the least becomes the diameter of the epicycle. For such an investigation is also carried out according to the epicycle [model].

# ON THE ECCENTRIC CIRCLE <note>original Latin: DE ECCENTRO CIRCULO</note>

...since the circle $\epsilon\zeta\eta$ of the planet is carried along another concentric <insert>homocentric</insert> circle, such as $\mu\nu o\xi$.

<note>
**Technical Vocabulary from this page:**
*   **Epicycle:** A small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one.
*   **Eccentric:** A circular orbit whose center is not the Earth, used to explain why planets seem to speed up or slow down.
*   **Zodiac:** The belt of the heavens divided into twelve signs (like Gemini and Sagittarius).
*   **Gemini / Sagittarius:** Specific signs used as fixed reference points for the Sun's position.
*   **Diameters:** Straight lines passing through the center of a circle.
*   **Degree:** A unit of measurement (1/360th of a circle).
</note>

# ON THE ECCENTRIC CIRCLE <note>original Latin: DE ECCENTRO CIRCULO</note>

...segment $\sigma\upsilon$ is equal to $\theta\nu$. Therefore, each of the segments $\epsilon\sigma$ and $\sigma\upsilon$ will be equal to the radius of circle $\mu o \nu \xi$. It was also shown that each of the segments $\lambda\sigma$ and $\sigma\chi$ is equal to the radius of that same circle. Thus, the four segments $\sigma\epsilon, \sigma\lambda, \sigma\upsilon, \sigma\chi$ are equal to one another and
<margin>5</margin> are at right angles. Therefore, a circle described with center $\sigma$ and a radius equal to one of these segments will pass through points $\epsilon, \lambda, \upsilon,$ and $\chi$; it will be equal <note>meaning equal in size/area</note> to circle $\mu o \nu \xi$ and will be divided into four equal parts by the diameters $\epsilon\upsilon$ and $\lambda\chi$. 

Let this circle be drawn, and let it be designated $\epsilon\lambda\upsilon\chi$. This will be the **eccentric circle**, 
<margin>10</margin> having its **apogee** <note>the point in an orbit furthest from the Earth; original Greek: ἀπογειότατον</note> at point $\alpha$, at the 5th and 1/6 degree of Gemini, and its **perigee** <note>the point in an orbit closest to the Earth; original Greek: προσγειότατον</note> at point $\gamma$, at the 5th and 1/6 degree <note>original: ε' ς' μοῖραν; ancient Greek notation for 5 1/6 degrees</note> of Sagittarius. 

I say then that the Sun, while moving (according to our hypothesis) upon the epicycle $\epsilon\kappa\eta\zeta$, will also incidentally <note>Greek: κατὰ συμβεβηκός; meaning the motion on one path naturally results in the tracing of the other</note> trace the eccentric circle $\epsilon\lambda\upsilon\chi$. 

For let the
<margin>15</margin> center of the epicycle be moved through the arc $\mu o$, which is a quarter-circle. In that same amount of time, let the Sun be moved through the similar arc $\epsilon\kappa$ of the epicycle; the Sun will then be at point $\lambda$. Thus, moving from $\epsilon$ to $\lambda$, the Sun will have traced the arc $\epsilon\lambda$ of the eccentric circle, which is a quarter-circle.

<margin>20</margin> the circle through the quarter-arc $o\nu$, and let the Sun move through a similar arc $\lambda\tau$ of the epicycle. # ON THE ECCENTRIC CIRCLE <note>original Latin: DE ECCENTRO CIRCULO</note>

In a direction contrary to the whole universe, the sun—being carried in the same amount of time along the epicycle $\text{E-K-H-Z}$ <note>Greek: ε̅κ̅η̅ζ̅</note> both uniformly and in a direction contrary to the epicycle's own rotation, but in the same direction as the universe—will by consequence also trace an <term>eccentric circle</term> <note>A circular path whose center is not the center of the Earth</note> that is equal to the <term>concentric circle</term> <note>A circle centered exactly on the Earth, the center of the universe in this model</note> $\text{M-O-N-X}$. <margin>5</margin> For let the center $M$ be moved through any given arc $MO$, and let it have positioned the <term>epicycle</term> <note>A small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger one</note> at the position $P-R-X$. The Sun, having started from point $E$ (that is, from $R$), shall have traversed the arc $RP$ in that same amount of time, which is similar to the arc $MO$. Let the line $Th-H$ be set as equal to the line $M-E$, <margin>10</margin> and let the lines $H-P$ and $Th-R$ be joined. Since, therefore, the arc $RP$ is similar to the arc $OM$, the angle $\phi$ <note>phi</note> is also equal to the angle $\tau$ <note>tau</note>. Therefore, the line $PO$ is parallel to $H-Th$, and it is also equal in length. Consequently, the line $PH$ is equal to $O-Th$ and is parallel to it. Furthermore, $Th-O$ is equal to $H-E$; thus $HP$ is also equal to <margin>15</margin> $H-E$. Therefore, the circle drawn with center $H$ and at the distance $H-E$ will also pass through point $P$ and will be equal to the circle $\text{M-O-N-X}$. Let this circle $E-P-L-Y-Z$ be drawn; this, then, shall be the eccentric circle. Since the line $PH$ is parallel to $R-Th$, the angle $\phi$ is equal to the angle $\tau$, which is to say, the angle $H-P-E$. <margin>20</margin> Therefore, the arc $EP$ is similar. Having started <insert>the sun</insert> from $E$, it will by consequence describe the similar arc $EP$ of the eccentric circle. <meta>This page concludes the first geometric demonstration regarding the equivalence of solar models and begins a new proof showing the "converse" case. In this section, the author demonstrates that a Sun moving on an eccentric path can also be understood as moving on an epicycle that is itself being carried around a concentric circle.</meta>

# AND ON THE EPICYCLE <note>original: ET EPICYCLO</note>

...it will describe the whole epicycle by means of the <term>concentric circle</term> <note>A circle centered exactly on the Earth</note> and the <term>eccentric circle</term>; which was to be demonstrated. <note>The classic "Q.E.D." concluding a proof.</note>

We must also demonstrate the converse. For let there be, once again, the zodiac circle $A-B-C-D$ <note>Greek: αβγδ</note>, and its diameter $A-C$ <note>αγ</note>, and <margin>5</margin> the center $Th$ <note>θ</note>, and the sun’s eccentric circle $E-L-U-X$ <note>ελυξ</note>. And let its <term>apogee</term> <note>The point in an orbit furthest from the Earth</note> be $E$ <note>ε</note> at $5 \frac{1}{6}$ degrees of the sign of the Twins <note>Gemini</note>, and let the center be $H$ <note>η</note> upon the line $A-Th$. Let there be drawn, with center $Th$ and distance $H-E$, a circle $M-O-N-X$ <note>μονξ</note>. Again, with center $M$ <note>μ</note> and distance $M-E$ <note>με</note>, <margin>10</margin> let a circle $E-Z-H-K$ <note>εξηκ</note> be drawn. It is then clear that this circle will be the same as the <term>epicycle</term> <note>A small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle</note>. I say, then, that the sun, moving uniformly along the eccentric circle $E-L-U-X$, will "incidentally" <note>An astronomical term meaning that the motion is a mathematical byproduct of other movements</note> also describe the epicycle $E-Z-H-K$ as it is carried uniformly along the circle $M-O-N-X$ and in equal time with the sun. <margin>15</margin> For let the sun be carried through any given arc $E-P$ <note>επ</note> on the eccentric circle, and let line $P-H$ <note>πη</note> be joined, and let $R-Th$ <note>ρθ</note> be parallel to it, and let $O-R$ <note>ορ</note> be equal to $Th-H$ <note>θη</note>, and let $P-O$ <note>πο</note> be joined. Since, therefore, the lines $H-Th$ and $P-O$ will be equal and parallel, and $Th-H$ is equal to $M-E$ (that <margin>20</margin> is, to $O-R$ and $O-P$), then the circle drawn with center $O$ <note>ο</note> and distance $O-R$ will also pass through point $P$, and it will be the same as the epicycle $E-Z-H-K$. Let the circle $P-R-X$ <note>πρχ</note> be drawn then. Since, therefore, because of the parallel lines, the angles $\tau$ <note>tau</note> and $\phi$ <note>phi</note> are equal <note>This refers to the interior angles created by the intersection of the radial lines and the parallels</note>...

### CONCERNING THE STARS <note>original: DE STELLARUM</note>

...they are to one another, and within circles, equal angles stand upon similar arcs; and in equal circles, they stand upon equal arcs, whether the angles are at the centers or at the circumferences. Therefore, the arcs $R-P$, $E-P$, and $M-O$ <note>original: ρπ, επ, μο</note> <margin>5</margin> will be similar to one another, and the arcs $E-P$ and $M-O$ will also be equal. In the time, therefore, that the sun moved through the arc $E-P$ of the **eccentric circle**, in this same time the center $M$ of the **epicycle**, being carried along the arc $M-O$, moved the epicycle $E-Z-H$ <note>original: εξη</note> to the position $P-R-Ch$ <note>original: πρχ</note>; and the sun, having traversed the arc $E-P$ on the eccentric circle, <margin>10</margin> starting from $E$ (that is, from $R$), also described the similar arc $R-P$ of the epicycle. The same result will be shown for every motion the sun makes; so that, having traversed the whole eccentric circle, the sun will also describe the whole epicycle; which was to be demonstrated.

<margin>15</margin> These things are also demonstrated for the other wandering stars <note>The planets</note>. However, the sun alone appears to do these things without variation according to both hypotheses <note>The eccentric and epicycle models</note>, because its <term>restorative periods</term> <note>original: apokatastatikos; the time it takes for a body to return to a specific starting point</note>—that is, the period of <term>longitude</term> <note>Position along the zodiac</note>, that of <margin>20</margin> <term>latitude</term> <note>North-south deviation from the ecliptic</note>, that of <term>depth</term> <note>The change in distance from the Earth</note>, and that of the so-called <term>anomaly</term> <note>original: anomalia; the apparent irregularity or "wobble" in the sun's speed</note>—are so close to one another that they seem equal to most mathematicians, each being 365 and 1/4 days. # ON THE STARS <note>original: DE STELLARUM</note>

...their unequal distances from the center of the universe, and furthermore because of the various <term>obliquities</term> <note>The tilt of a planet's orbit relative to the celestial equator</note> or unequal <term>inclinations</term> <note>The angle between the plane of an orbit and the plane of the zodiac</note> and positions relative to the circle through the middle of the zodiac <note>original: zōdiōn</note>. For this reason, the phenomena of <term>stations</term> <note>original: stērigmōn; when a planet appears to stop moving in the sky</note>, <term>retrogradations</term> <note>original: anapodismōn; when a planet appears to move backward against the stars</note>, <term>progressions</term> <note>original: proēgēseōn; forward motion</note>, 
<margin>5</margin> and <term>retardations</term> <note>original: hypoleipseōn; falling behind the expected motion</note> do not occur in the same way for all [celestial bodies]. Rather, these things appear to happen for the five [planets], even if not entirely in the same manner. However, for the Sun and Moon, they do not occur at all. For these two never appear to progress, stand still, 
<margin>10</margin> or retrograde. This is because the Sun appears to be carried nearly at the same time upon the same circle, and its <term>epicycle</term> <note>A small circular orbit whose center moves around the circumference of a larger circle</note> moves upon the <term>eccentric</term> <note>original: enkentrou; a circular path whose center is offset from the Earth</note>, just as we said. But the Moon’s epicycle is carried faster upon the eccentric and lags behind the circle of the zodiac, even as 
<margin>15</margin> the Moon itself passes through the epicycle.

**[Geometric Diagram]**
*This diagram illustrates the "Epicycle on a Deferent" model used in ancient astronomy to explain planetary motion. A large circle (the deferent) is centered on point Theta (θ), representing the Earth or the center of the universe. Along its circumference, a smaller circle (the epicycle) is centered on point Eta (η). As the large circle rotates, the smaller circle also rotates, carrying the planet (at points Zeta [ζ] or Kappa [κ]) in a looping motion. This explains why a planet might appear to slow down or move backward from our perspective on Earth.*

->### ON THE MOTIONS OF THE WANDERERS <note>Latin: ERRANTIUM MOTIBUS</note><-

It is clear that it makes no difference toward **saving the phenomena** <note>The classic astronomical goal of creating a mathematical model that matches observed observations</note> whether we say that the <term>planets</term> <note>original: planētas; literally "wanderers"</note> move along the circles as has been defined, or that the circles themselves, which carry these bodies, move around their own centers.
<margin>5</margin> I mean that the <term>concentric circles</term> <note>original: enkentrous; circles sharing a center with the Earth</note>, which carry the centers of the **epicycles**, move around their own centers in a direction opposite <**to the whole universe**>, while the **epicycles**, which carry the bodies of the wandering stars, move in turn around their own centers. For example: 
<margin>10</margin> let the concentric circle *MLNX* be carried around point *θ* <note>the Greek letter theta</note>, which is the center of the universe and of the circle itself, in a direction opposite to the universe; this circle carries on its circumference the center *μ* <note>the Greek letter mu</note> of the **epicycle**. Furthermore, let the epicycle *EXIK*, which holds the planet at point *ε* <note>the Greek letter epsilon</note>, move in turn around the center *μ*. In the case of the 
<margin>15</margin> Sun and the Moon, this motion is in the same direction as the universe, but in the case of the **other planets**, this also moves in a direction opposite to the universe; for in this way, the appearances are preserved.

At the bottom of the large circle is point ν. On the horizontal radius to the right is point μ, which serves as the center for the epicycle. The epicycle passes through point ξ on the far right. ->### ON THE STARS <note>Latin: DE STELLARUM</note><-

Regarding the **eccentric circle** <note>original: ekkentros; a circle whose center is shifted away from the Earth’s center</note> around center *K*: in the case of the Sun, this eccentric circle itself moves uniformly around center *K* over the course of a year. It carries the Sun fixed at point *E* and will **save the phenomena** <note>a phrase used by ancient astronomers meaning "to provide a mathematical explanation that matches observed movements"</note>.
<margin>5</margin> The center *K* itself does not move independently nor in a direction opposite to the whole universe; rather, it is carried along with the whole and traces the **epicycle** <note>original: epikyklos; a smaller circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger one</note> each day. This becomes equal to the circle described in the other part of this study. In this way, the Sun will always reach its
<margin>10</margin> greatest distances in the same regions of the sky, and again its least distances in other specific regions, and similarly its middle distances in others. The greatest distances <note>apogee</note> occur at the 5th or 6th degree of **Gemini**, as has been stated, while the least distances <note>perigee</note> occur at the same degree of
<margin>15</margin> **Sagittarius**, and the middle distances likewise at the same degrees of **Virgo** and **Pisces**. For since point *E* of the eccentric circle on which the Sun is positioned—when the circle is in this specific orientation—appears to be at its furthest point from the earth <term>apogee</term> <note>original: apogeiotaton; literally "furthest from earth"</note> when under Gemini; but when the circle is carried around
<margin>20</margin> center *K* and shifts to where point *U* is now, it will appear under Sagittarius and be at its closest point to the earth <term>perigee</term> <note>original: prosgeiotaton; literally "nearest to earth"</note>. Between these two points, in Virgo and 
Pisces, it will stand at a middle distance.

However, since the other **planets** <note>original: planēta; "wanderers"</note> produce their greatest, least, and middle distances and motions in every part of the zodiac, if we imagine the **epicycle** circle being drawn with center *Θ* <note>theta</note>
<margin>25</margin> of the universe and with the radius *ΘK*, and then this circle, being concentric...

->### ON THE MOTIONS OF THE PLANETS <note>Latin: ERRANTIUM MOTIBUS</note><-

Meanwhile, the eccentric circle moves around its own center *Kappa* in a different period of time, carrying the planet <margin>5</margin> fixed within it at point *E*. By taking the specific and appropriate times for each of the wandering stars <note>planets</note>, the observed phenomena will be **saved** <note>original: sotheshetai ta phainomena; a technical term meaning to provide a mathematical model that accounts for all observed movements</note>.

He goes further into these matters to reconcile the hypotheses and treatises of the mathematicians with one another. These mathematicians, <margin>10</margin> looking only to the phenomena and the accidental movements of the planets, observed them over long periods because of the favorable nature of their lands—namely the **Babylonians**, **Chaldeans**, and **Egyptians**. They eagerly sought out certain principles and hypotheses <margin>15</margin> to which the phenomena might fit, by which they could judge based on what was found before and anticipate what would occur in the future. Some, such as the Chaldeans, brought forward certain **arithmetical methods**, while others, such as the Egyptians, used **linear methods** <note>original: grammikas; likely referring to geometric or graphic constructions</note>. All of them, however, <margin>20</margin> made their methods incomplete by lacking **natural philosophy** <note>original: physiologia; the study of the physical nature and causes of the universe</note>, for it is necessary to examine these matters from a physical perspective as well. This is what the astronomers among the **Greeks** attempted to do, having received from those others the principles and the observations of the phenomena, just as **Plato**

->## ON THE STARS <note>Latin header: DE STELLARUM</note><-

He indicates this in the *Epinomis* <note>a dialogue attributed to Plato, serving as an appendix to the *Laws*</note>, as will be made clear a little later when his own words are set alongside this text.

And **Aristotle**, too, in his books *On the Heavens* <note>Aristotle's treatise *De Caelo*</note>, having shown generally through many arguments concerning the stars that they are neither carried through a stationary **etherial body** <term>aether; the "fifth element" which Aristotle believed made up the heavens, being changeless and circular in motion</term>, nor do they run along with a moving body as if they were detached and independent. Furthermore, he shows they do not move by spinning or rolling. Rather, the **fixed stars** <note>original: aplane; those stars that do not change position relative to one another</note>, being many, are carried by that single, common, outermost body. As for the **wandering stars** <note>original: planomenon; the planets</note>, each one is moved by several spheres.

Again, in Book 30 <note>The OCR notes 'L' or Lambda, which is Book 12 of the *Metaphysics*</note> of his *Metaphysics* <note>original: meta ta physika</note>, he says that **Eudoxus** and **Callippus** <note>famous 4th-century BCE Greek astronomers who proposed the system of homocentric spheres</note> move the planets by means of certain spheres. For the physical reality is that neither do the stars themselves move according to their own accord in certain circular or spiral lines—especially in a direction contrary to the whole universe—nor do certain circles themselves spin around their own centers while carrying the stars fixed within them, with some [seven] moving in the same direction as the whole and others in the opposite direction. 

For how would it even be possible for such massive bodies to be bound in **incorporeal circles** <note>mathematical lines without physical substance</note>? Rather, it is more appropriate that there are certain spheres made of the **fifth body** <note>the aether</note> situated and moving within the depth of the entire heaven. Some of these are higher, while others are arranged beneath them; some are larger, while others are smaller. Furthermore, some are hollow, while others within the depth of these are solid, in which the wandering stars are fixed just like the fixed stars.

It references the works of Plato and Aristotle to support the idea of a physical, rather than purely mathematical, celestial machinery.</summary>

->### ON THE SPHERES OF THE WANDERERS <note>original Latin header: ERRANTIUM SPHAERIS</note><-

...[the planets], although their own movement is simple, appear by accident to move in various ways because of their positions and unequal speeds. They seem to trace certain **eccentric circles** <term>eccentric: a circular orbit where the Earth is not at the exact center</term> or paths situated upon other circles, or even certain spirals,
<margin>5</margin>upon which the mathematicians believe they move, being deceived by the apparent reversal <note>original: *anastrophē*; referring to retrograde motion where a planet appears to move backward in the sky</note>. Since, therefore, the planets appear on the one hand to be carried along by the whole universe every day from the east to the west, and on the other hand to move in the opposite direction through the succession of the **zodiac** <term>zodiac: the "circle of animals," the path of the sun and planets through the twelve constellations</term> along an oblique path,
<margin>10</margin>and further to move somewhat in latitude, being seen now further north and now further south; and moreover to move in height and depth, being observed sometimes further from the earth <note>apogee</note> and sometimes closer to the earth <note>perigee</note>, Aristotle says that the earlier thinkers hypothesized that each planet was carried by several spheres. **Eudoxus** <note>Eudoxus of Cnidus, a student of Plato who developed the first mathematical model of the universe based on nested spheres</note> says that the sun and the
<margin>15</margin>moon are fixed by means of **three spheres**. One is the sphere of the **fixed stars** <note>original: *aplanōn*</note>, which spins around the poles of the universe and, by its great power, pulls all the other spheres along with it from east to west. A second sphere is carried around an axis perpendicular to the circle through the middle of the zodiac <note>the ecliptic, or the sun's annual path</note>,
<margin>20</margin>through which each planet again appears to make its common transition in longitude through the signs of the zodiac. A third sphere moves around an axis perpendicular to a circle inclined toward the middle of the zodiac’s width <note>latitude</note>, through which each planet performs its own particular motion in latitude, one in a longer time, another in <note>the sentence breaks off here</note>...

### OF THE STARS ###

Regarding the other wandering stars <term>wanderers: the planets, from the Greek *planētai*</term>, each is carried through four spheres,
<margin>5</margin> while another sphere is added <insert>if one should suppose sirens</insert> <note>This is a likely later addition by a scribe, confusing the "four spheres" with Plato's Myth of Er where sirens sit upon the heavenly spheres</note> for each, through which each planet also creates its depth of motion. But **Callippus**, while leaving the spheres of Saturn and Jupiter the same as before, said that one should add certain other spheres to the rest: two more for both the sun and the moon, and one more for each of the remaining planets. Then he [Aristotle] calculates that if they are to be combined to **save the appearances** <term>save the appearances: to create a mathematical model that matches the observed movements of the stars</term>,
<margin>10</margin> there must be other spheres for each of the wandering stars—one fewer than the spheres carrying the planet—which are **unrolling spheres** <term>unrolling: *anellittousai*; these are counter-rotating spheres intended to cancel out the motion of an outer planet's spheres so they don't interfere with the next planet inward</term>, whether this was his own opinion or he was explaining theirs. For since they thought it was according to nature for everything to be carried in the same direction, but they saw the wandering stars also
<margin>15</margin> changing to the opposite direction, they supposed there must be certain other spheres in between those carrying the planets—solid spheres, clearly—which by their own motion will "unroll" those spheres carrying them in the opposite direction. They do this by touching them, just like the so-called **little drums** <term>little drums: *tumpania*, the ancient term for gear-wheels</term> used in the **making of mechanical spheres** <note>original: *mēchanosphairopoiiais*; the construction of orreries or mechanical planetariums</note>.
<margin>20</margin> These gears, moving around the center with their own particular motion, move and "unroll" the underlying and interconnected parts in the opposite direction through the interlocking of their teeth. But the truly physical explanation is that all the spheres are carried in the same direction, being rotated by the outermost sphere, though each moves according to its own particular motion because of...

---

<meta>This page continues the technical description of planetary motion. The previous page ended with a discussion of Callippus and Aristotle’s theories on the "unrolling" spheres. ### ON THE SPHERES OF THE WANDERERS ###
<note>original Latin: ERRANTIUM SPHAERIS. This refers to the planets, which the Greeks called "planētai" or wanderers because they move against the background of the stars.</note>

[...everything follows an] order of position and the places and the sizes, with some [spheres] being carried faster and others more slowly in the opposite direction around their own axes, which are tilted relative to the sphere of the <term>fixed stars: the outermost shell of the universe where the stars do not move relative to one another</term>; so that the stars within them, while being carried by the simple
<margin>5</margin>and uniform motion of these spheres, appear by coincidence to perform composite, irregular, and varied movements. And they describe various circles: some <term>concentric: circles sharing a common center, usually the Earth</term>, some <term>eccentric: circles whose center is offset from the center of the Earth</term>, and some <term>epicycles: small circles whose centers move along the circumference of a larger circle</term>. For the sake of understanding what is being said,
<margin>10</margin> it is necessary to briefly set these out, according to the diagram we deem necessary for <term>sphere-making: the construction of physical or mathematical models of the heavens</term>.

->[Diagram Description]<-
<image-desc>A circular geometric diagram illustrating astronomical spheres and epicycles. A large outer circle is labeled with alpha at the top, beta at the left, gamma at the bottom, and delta at the right. A horizontal diameter connects beta and delta, and a vertical diameter connects alpha and gamma, passing through a central point labeled theta. Within the outer circle are several concentric circles. At the four cardinal points of one inner circle, small circles (epicycles) are drawn. Various points on the circles and epicycles are labeled with Greek letters, including epsilon, zeta, eta, theta, kappa, lambda, xi, pi, rho, sigma, tau, upsilon, phi, chi, psi, and omega.</image-desc>

Let the hollow sphere of the fixed stars be $\overline{alpha\ beta\ gamma\ delta}$ around the center $\overline{theta}$ of the universe at depth $\overline{alpha\ epsilon}$; its diameters are $\overline{alpha\ gamma}$ and $\overline{beta\ delta}$; and let the circle $alpha\ beta\ gamma\ delta$ be conceived as a "great circle"
<margin>15</margin>passing through the middle of the <term>zodiac: the band of sky through which the sun, moon, and planets appear to move</term>; and another [sphere] lies beneath it...

---

<note>14: The segment "alpha-epsilon" is replaced with the word "the" in manuscript B.</note>
<note>15: The phrase "a certain [sphere]" is replaced by "who/which" in manuscript B.</note>

->## ON THE SPHERES OF THE PLANETS<-
<note>original Latin: ERRANTIUM SPHAERIS</note>

...ordered, but being oblique and lagging behind the <term>fixed stars: the distant stars that appear stationary relative to one another</term> only because of its slowness; or, [being moved] by the single sphere that carries the solid one—that is, the <term>epicycle: a small circle on which a planet moves, while the center of the epicycle itself moves around a larger circle</term>—which, by moving in the opposite direction, produces an incidental result: the complex, composite, and irregular motion of the planet. Through <margin>5</margin> <note>lacuna in text; likely "the motion of the hollow sphere"</note> this [motion], which occurs either truly or by lagging behind toward the "following" signs of the <term>zodiac: the belt of constellations through which the planets appear to move</term>, and being seen within some latitude of the zodiac because of its obliquity; and because of the rotation of the solid sphere around its own axis, it appears sometimes at a great height and therefore seems slow, and at other times at a low depth and therefore <margin>10</margin> faster—and simply irregular. Because of these factors, while the motion actually occurs on the epicycle, it appears to happen on the <term>eccentric: a circular orbit whose center is shifted away from the Earth</term>. It is clear then, as one might expect, that the mathematical hypotheses for their motion—both the epicyclic and the eccentric models—follow and agree with each other, since <margin>15</margin> both correspond to the natural [motion] through an accidental one. This is what <term>Hipparchus: a famous Greek astronomer (c. 190–120 BCE) who laid the foundations for trigonometry and solar/lunar models</term> marvels at, especially regarding the Sun, because the motion of its spheres is perfectly completed in exactly the same amount of time. However, for the other planets, it is not so <margin>20</margin> precise, because the solid sphere of the planet is not restored <note>returned to its original starting position</note> in the same time as that in which the hollow sphere either lags behind or revolves in the opposite direction. Instead, for some planets it is faster, and for others slower, so that their corresponding motions, even according to...

<header>->## ON THE STARS<-</header>
<note>original Latin: DE STELLARUM</note>

...the specific points of the spheres do not meet in the same places, but always vary. The <term>obliquities: the tilting or inclination of the orbital paths</term> of the spheres occur across several latitudes. Because of this, the <margin>5</margin> time required for their <term>restoration: the period it takes for a planet to return to its original position</term> in longitude, latitude, and depth is unequal and various. <insert>And the maximum,</insert> minimum, and mean distances and motions occur at different times and places throughout all the signs of the zodiac. Furthermore, because the corresponding motions and the corresponding <margin>10</margin> points of the spheres vary, as we have said, the <term>planets: literally "the wanderers," as they appeared to move against the fixed stars</term> do not appear to trace circles through their incidental motions, but rather certain <term>spirals: complex, winding paths resembling a helix or coil</term>. Therefore, for each of the planets, one must suppose that there is a unique <term>hollow sphere: a transparent, encompassing outer shell</term> which carries within its own depth a <margin>15</margin> <term>solid sphere: the inner, physical sphere that directly supports the planet</term>; this solid sphere, in turn, carries the planet upon its own surface.

Regarding the Sun, <term>Phosphoros: the "Light-bringer," the ancient name for the planet Venus</term>, and <term>Stilbon: the "Gleaming One," the ancient name for the planet Mercury</term>, it is possible for each of them to have their own pair of spheres. <margin>20</margin> In this case, the three hollow spheres move at the same speed and travel around the sphere of the <term>fixed stars: the distant stars that appear stationary relative to one another</term> in the opposite direction in equal time. Their solid spheres have their centers on a single straight line. In terms of size, the Sun's sphere is the smallest; larger than it is the sphere of Stilbon [Mercury], and larger still than that is the sphere of <margin>25</margin> Phosphoros [Venus]. However, it is also possible that there is a single hollow sphere common to all three, while the solid spheres of the three...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary spheres from page 186. The previous page concluded by introducing the "solid spheres" of the Sun, Venus (Phosphorus), and Mercury (Stilbon). This page explains their specific spatial arrangement and introduces a biological analogy—comparing the Sun’s position in the cosmos to the heart’s position in a living body.</meta>

->## ON THE SPHERES OF THE PLANETS<-
<note>original Latin header: ERRANTIUM SPHAERIS</note>

...in the depth of this [outer sphere] and around the same center as one another, there is the smallest and truly <term>solid sphere: the physical inner sphere that carries a celestial body</term> belonging to the Sun. Around this is the sphere of <term>Stilbon: "the Gleaming One," the ancient name for Mercury</term>. Then, encompassing both of these and filling the entire depth of the hollow and common sphere, is the sphere of <term>Phosphorus: "the Light-Bringer," the ancient name for Venus</term>. Because of this arrangement, <margin>5</margin> these three celestial bodies perform their longitudinal motion through the signs of the zodiac—whether this is their "falling behind" <note>Greek: hypoleipsim; the apparent westward drift of planets relative to the stars</note> or their movement in the opposite direction—at an equal pace. However, they do not perform their other motions in the same way; they are always seen overtaking one another, being overtaken, or <margin>10</margin> passing in front of one another. <term>Hermes: the Greek name for the planet Mercury</term> stands at most about twenty degrees on either side of the Sun, toward the evening or toward the dawn, while the sphere of <term>Aphrodite: the Greek name for the planet Venus</term> stands at most fifty degrees away.

One might also suspect <insert>someone</insert> <note>The translator notes an addition here in the original manuscript to clarify the subject</note> that this is the truer position and order, so that <margin>15</margin> this place may be the seat of the <term>life-force: the animating principle or soul-power</term> of the cosmos, since the cosmos is a living being. In this view, the Sun is as if it were the heart of the universe, being intensely hot due to its motion, its size, and the company of the planets surrounding it. For in living creatures, the center of the thing itself—that is, the creature as a living being—is different <margin>20</margin> from the center of its physical size. For example, as we said of ourselves, as humans and living creatures, the center of our life-force is the region around the heart, which is ever-moving and intensely hot, and for these reasons is the source of every power of the soul—such as the soul's capacity for <note>original: "of the soul and..." possibly meaning the "nutritive" power as found in Aristotle</note> movement from place to place, desire, imagination, <margin>25</margin> and thought. However, the center of our physical size is different, such as the region around the navel. In the same way, for the cosmos...

<summary>This page describes the concentric arrangement of the spheres of the Sun, Mercury, and Venus, explaining why they appear to travel together. It famously argues that the Sun acts as the "heart" of the cosmic living being, serving as the vital center of the universe's soul rather than its geometric center.</summary>

<keywords>Stilbon, Phosphorus, Mercury, Venus, Solar Heart, life-force, cosmic biology, planetary motion, longitudinal motion, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary arrangement, moving from the specific order of the Sun, Mercury, and Venus to the underlying physical and philosophical principles governing their motion. It introduces the analogy of the Sun as the "heart" of the cosmos and compares the mathematical models of epicycles and eccentrics.</meta>

->## ON THE STARS<-
<note>original Latin header: DE STELLARUM</note>

...of the whole, just as one might judge the greatest, most precious, and divine things from things that are small, ordinary, and mortal. The center of its physical mass is the region around the Earth, which is cooled and motionless. However, as a <term>cosmos: an ordered universe</term>, and insofar as it is a cosmos and a living creature, the center of its vitality is the region around the Sun. <margin>5</margin> The Sun is, as it were, the heart of the whole universe, from which they say its soul begins and proceeds to reach through the entire body, stretched out from the extremities. 

It is clear that for the reasons mentioned, since both hypotheses follow from one another, the theory of the <term>epicycle: a small circle on which a planet moves, while the circle itself moves along a larger path</term> <margin>10</margin> seems more general, more universal, and closer to the actual nature of things. For the greatest circle of the <term>solid sphere: the physical crystalline shell carrying a planet</term>, which the wandering star describes by its own revolution upon it, is the epicycle. The <term>eccentric: a circular orbit whose center is not the Earth</term> is entirely detached from the natural center and is described <margin>15</margin> more by "accident" <note>Greek: kata symbebekos; meaning a mathematical result rather than a physical cause</note>. 

Observing this, *Hipparchus* praised the epicycle hypothesis as being consistent with his own views, saying it is more persuasive that all celestial bodies are situated in balance and joined in the same way relative to the center of the cosmos. However, because he was not equipped with <margin>20</margin> <term>natural philosophy: the study of the physical laws of nature</term> <note>original Greek: physiologia</note>, he did not know precisely which motion of the wandering stars was "according to nature" and true, and which was "accidental" and merely apparent. He also hypothesized that the epicycle of each planet moves along a concentric circle, while the planet itself moves along the epicycle. <margin>25</margin> It seems that *Plato* also considered the epicycle theory to be the more authoritative one.

<note>The following are scholarly notations regarding variations in the original Greek manuscripts.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the Sun acts as the "heart" and vital center of the living universe, and asserts that the mathematical theory of epicycles is more "natural" than the theory of eccentric orbits. It notes that while the astronomer Hipparchus favored epicycles for their symmetry, he lacked the physical philosophy to fully explain planetary causes.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Hipparchus, Plato, epicycle, eccentric, Sun as heart, cosmology, natural philosophy, planetary motion</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the Sun's central vital role and the general theory of epicycles to the specific cosmological models proposed by Plato and Aristotle. It focuses on whether the heavenly bodies are carried by circles or physical spheres.</meta>

->## ON THE SPHERES OF THE WANDERERS<-
<note>original Latin header: ERRANTIUM SPHAERIS; refers to the "wandering stars" or planets</note>

...an epicycle, and that the things carrying the wandering stars <note>the planets</note> are not spheres, but circles, just as he hints at the end of the *Republic* <note>original Greek: Politeia</note> with the <term>whorls: the rings of a spindle, used here as a mechanical model for planetary paths</term> fitted into one another. He uses these names quite generally, often calling the spheres "circles" and <margin>5</margin> "poles," while also calling the axes "poles."

Aristotle, however, says that there are certain spheres of the <term>fifth body: the aether or quintessence, the unchanging element of the heavens</term> situated and moving within the depth of the entire heaven. Some are higher, while others are arranged beneath them; some are larger and <margin>10</margin> others smaller. Furthermore, some are hollow, while inside these there are solid spheres in which the wandering stars are fixed, just like the fixed stars. By the simple motion of those spheres—though it appears to be of unequal speed due to their different locations—it seems by "accident" <note>Greek: kata symbebekos; meaning the appearance of complex motion is a mathematical byproduct of simple rotations</note> that they now move in various ways and trace certain <margin>15</margin> <term>eccentric circles: orbits whose center is not the Earth</term>, or circles placed upon other circles, or even certain spirals. Mathematicians believe the stars move along these paths, but they are deceived by the <term>reversal: the apparent backward or retrograde motion of the planets</term>.

---

<note>2 Plato, Republic, Book 10, page 616D.</note>
<note>3 "into one another"] "into others" in manuscript B.</note>
<note>5 "circle" in manuscript B. See Republic 616E, 617AB, and Timaeus 36D.</note>
<note>6 "and poles": see Cratylus 405C, Epinomis 986C. "the axes poles": see Timaeus 40B. Martin thinks a passage from the Epinomis is missing here.</note>
<note>7 Heading "The teachings of Aristotle" in manuscript B. Martin considers this entire passage (lines 7–18) to be spurious.</note>

<summary>This page compares Plato’s vision of concentric "whorls" or circles with Aristotle’s physical model of nested aetherial spheres, explaining how simple rotations can appear as complex eccentric or spiral paths.</summary>

### ON PROGRESSIONS, STATIONS, AND RETROGRADATIONS.

<image-desc>A geometrical diagram illustrating the motion of a planet on an epicycle relative to the zodiac. A large circle represents the zodiac with points α (alpha), β (beta), γ (gamma), δ (delta) at the cardinal positions. The center of this circle—representing the Earth or the observer—is marked θ (theta). At the top of the large circle, a smaller circle (the epicycle) is centered at point μ (mu). The epicycle is marked with points ε (epsilon), ξ (xi), η (eta), ν (nu). Two lines radiate from the center θ and are tangent to the epicycle at points ξ and ν; these lines extend to the zodiac circle at points κ (kappa) and λ (lambda). A vertical diameter connects points α, ε, μ, η, θ, and γ.</image-desc>

We must now explain how it is that those planets that appear to do so seem to <term>move forward: leading through the zodiac in the order of the signs</term>, to <term>stand still: making a station or appearing stationary</term>, and to <term>move backward: retrograde motion</term>. Let the <term>zodiac: the great circle of constellations through which the planets move</term> be represented by the circle αβγδ around θ, the center of the universe. Let the <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle</term> <margin>5</margin> of the planet be εξη, and from our point of sight at θ, let the lines θξκ and θνλ be drawn tangent to the epicycle. Through the center μ of the epicycle, let there be the line θμεα. Since we see in straight lines, it is clear that when the star <note>original: ἀστὴρ (astēr); in ancient astronomy, planets were called "wandering stars"</note> is at point ξ, it will appear to us to be at point κ on the zodiac. When it is carried through the arc ξε, it will seem to have <margin>10</margin> moved forward along the zodiac from κ to α into the <term>leading parts: the forward-moving direction of the zodiac signs</term> <note>original Greek: eis ta proegoumena</note>. Likewise, having completed the arc εν, it will seem to have moved forward through αλ.

Again, however, having traveled through the arc νη, it will seem to have <term>moved backward: retrograded</term> through the zodiac from λ to α and then back toward κ, into the <term>following signs: moving against the order of the zodiac</term> <note>original Greek: eis ta epomena</note>. When the planet is approaching point ξ and first departing from it, it will appear at point κ <margin>15</margin> for a longer duration of time and thus appear to be <term>standing still: making a station</term>. And more [away]... <note>the text breaks off mid-sentence</note>

### ON STATIONS AND MEAN DISTANCES.

...having stopped at point ξ, and moving forward again; then, as it approaches point ν and first departs from it, it will again seem to stand still and <term>retrograde: move backward against the background of stars</term>. However, each <term>planet: literally a "wanderer" (planēs)</term> <margin>5</margin> will perform these <term>stations: points where the planet appears to stop</term>, retrogradations, progressions, and lags at different times and in different signs of the <term>zodiac: the circle of constellations through which the planets appear to move</term>. This is because the <term>epicycle: the small circle carrying the planet</term> of each is always shifting forward into the subsequent signs, either passing through them or falling behind.

<image-desc>
A geometric diagram illustrating the epicycle model. A vertical axis contains the following points from top to bottom: ε (epsilon, at the peak of the epicycle), μ (mu, center of the epicycle), κ (kappa), ϑ (theta, the center of the universe/Earth), and ν (nu, at the bottom of the epicycle/larger circle). The epicycle is a small circle centered at μ with points ε (top), ξ (left), η (right), and ν (bottom). A larger circle (the deferent) is centered at ϑ and passes through μ, with horizontal points λ (left) and ξ (right) marking its width.
</image-desc>

It is useful for the matters currently before us to also observe the <term>mean distance: the average distance of a celestial body from the center of its orbit</term> of a planet, whatever that may be. <margin>10</margin> According to the study of epicycles, if we take the greatest distance of the star from us—such as the line $\vartheta\epsilon$—and then the least distance—such as the line $\vartheta\nu$—and take the difference between the greatest and the least—the line $\epsilon\nu$—and divide it in two at point μ, <margin>15</margin> it is clear that the mean distance will be the line $\vartheta\mu$. If we then use $\vartheta$ as the center and the distance $\vartheta\mu$ as the radius to draw the circle $\mu\lambda\nu\xi$ centered...

<note>The scribe notes various readings in the margin (the "apparatus"). For instance, at line 1, one manuscript (B) uses "and" (καὶ) instead of "again" (πάλιν). At line 2, there is a correction regarding whether the planet moves toward point ν or η.</note>

<summary>This page explains that planetary stations and retrogradations occur at different points in the zodiac because the epicycle itself is constantly moving. It also provides a geometric proof that the "mean distance" of a planet is the mathematical average of its furthest and closest points from Earth.</summary>

<keywords>epicycle, mean distance, retrogradation, stations, planetary motion, geometry, zodiac, Ptolemaic astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the geometric demonstration that both the epicycle model and the eccentric model produce identical results for calculating a planet's distance. It transitions from orbital mechanics to the study of planetary configurations such as conjunctions and eclipses.</meta>

### ON THE STATIONS AND MEAN DISTANCES.
<note>original: "DE ST. ERR. MEDIIS DISTANTIIS." This Latin header likely stands for "De Stationibus et Mediis Distantiis."</note>

...[with] μ as the center, and with center μ and distance <note>radius</note> με, [drawing] the epicycle εζνη, it is clear that as the star moves along the epicycle, when it reaches point ε it will be at its maximum distance from us, and at point υ <note>In the previous page's diagram, this was labeled ν; it represents the point closest to the Earth.</note> it will be at its minimum. <margin>5</margin> At each of the two points ζ and η, where the epicycle is intersected by the <term>concentric circle: the "encentron," a circle centered exactly on the Earth</term>, the planet will be at its **mean distance**, no matter where the epicycle has moved to.

According to the hypothesis of <term>eccentrics: circles whose center is shifted away from the Earth</term>, if the eccentric circle is ελυξ centered on point κ, while the center of the universe is θ, <margin>10</margin> and the line θκ between the two centers is extended in both directions—if we then draw a circle μλνξ around center θ equal in size to the eccentric, it is clear that this will be the concentric circle upon which the epicycle of the other hypothesis is carried, being drawn with center μ and radius με. The <term>planet: literally "the wanderer" (planēs)</term>, as it moves along the eccentric circle, <margin>15</margin> will be at its maximum distance from us when it reaches point ε (wherever that may be), and at its minimum distance at point υ. At the points λ and ξ where it bisects the concentric circle—wherever those points may fall as the eccentric shifts—it is at its **mean distance**. And <margin>20</margin> it is clear that under either hypothesis, the same results will agree regarding the maximum, minimum, and mean distances.

It remains to discuss briefly, for the sake of the topics at hand, conjunctions, <term>occultations: when one celestial body passes in front of another, hiding it from view</term>, <margin>25</margin> appearances, and eclipses. Since by nature we see in a straight line, and the highest part is the sphere of the <term>fixed stars: the outermost sphere of stars that do not move relative to one another</term>, and below it...

<summary>This page demonstrates that the epicycle and eccentric models are geometrically equivalent for determining a planet's maximum, minimum, and mean distances from Earth. It then begins a new section on celestial phenomena like conjunctions and eclipses.</summary>

<keywords>astronomy, epicycle, eccentric, mean distance, planetary motion, concentric circle, conjunction, occultation, fixed stars</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary arrangement. While the previous page focused on the geometry of distances and the mathematical equivalence of the eccentric and epicycle models, this page transitions into the physical consequences of the planets' relative positions—specifically, how they occult (hide) one another based on their proximity to Earth.</meta>

### ON THE OCCULTATIONS OF THE WANDERING STARS.
<note>original Latin: "DE ST. ERR. OBSCURATIONIBUS." This is an abbreviation for "De Stellarum Errantium Obscurationibus," referring to the eclipses or "hiding" of the wandering stars (planets).</note>

...[according to] this order of the <term>wandering stars: the "planets," from the Greek "planētai," meaning wanderers</term> as we have defined it. 
It is clear that the Moon, being the closest to the Earth, will <term>block the view: "epiprosthēsei," to stand in front of</term> everything above it. It hides all of the wandering stars and some of the fixed stars whenever it stands in a straight line between our <margin>5</margin> sight and any of them. However, the Moon itself is never hidden by any other star. The Sun is blocked by the Moon, but the Sun itself hides all other things (except the Moon)—first by approaching and outshining them with its glare, and then by being positioned in a straight line <margin>10</margin> between our sight and one of those other bodies. 

<term>Stilbōn: "The Gleaming," the ancient name for Mercury</term> and <term>Phōsphoros: "The Light-Bringer," the ancient name for Venus</term> hide the things above them when they similarly come into a straight line in front of our sight. They also seem to occasionally block <margin>15</margin> one another because they become higher or lower in the sky due to their sizes, the <term>inclinations: "loxōseis," the tilting or "slanting" of the planetary orbits</term> of their circles, and their positions relative to each other. However, the precise truth about them is unclear because they revolve around the Sun; especially Mercury, which is a small body in terms of size, is always close to the Sun, and is usually <margin>20</margin> invisible because it is outshone by the Sun's light. 

<term>Pyroeis: "The Fiery," the ancient name for Mars</term> occasionally hides the two wandering stars above it <note>Jupiter and Saturn</note>, and <term>Phaethōn: "The Radiant," the ancient name for Jupiter</term> hides <term>Phainōn: "The Shining," the ancient name for Saturn</term>. All the wandering stars hide whichever fixed stars lie along their individual paths. 

When the Moon is at the diameter <note>opposite the Sun</note> and falls into the shadow of the Earth, it <margin>24</margin> undergoes an eclipse.

---

<note>original: "1 διωρίσαμεν: p. 138 sqq."</note> 1 **As we have defined**: see page 138 and following. <note>original: "6 κρύπτε ante ἄστρου del. Mart."</note> 13 **in front**: Manuscript B reads "in front"; the word "but" was added by the editor Martin. <note>original: "15 ἄλλων B*, invicem Chalc. 87"</note> 15 **each other**: Manuscript B reads "others," while Calcidius (section 87) uses "in turn." <note>original: "16 αὑτῷ B*"</note> 16 **himself**: Manuscript B. <note>original: "20 δύο] αὖ B, duos supra se planetas Chalc."</note> 20 **two**: Manuscript B reads "again," but Calcidius reads "the two planets above him." <note>original: "23 inscr. περὶ ἐκλείψεως ἡλίου καὶ σελήνης B"</note> 23 **heading**: Manuscript B includes the title "Concerning the eclipse of the Sun and Moon." <note>original: "ἡλίου καὶ σελήνης γενομένων Mart."</note> **of the sun and moon**: Martin's reading. <note>original: "luna item diametro a sole distans et incurrens umbram terrae Chalc."</note> **likewise the moon being at a diameter from the sun and running into the earth's shadow**: reading from Calcidius.

<summary>This page describes the hierarchy of planetary occultations, explaining how closer celestial bodies (like the Moon) block our view of more distant ones. # ON THE ECLIPSE OF THE SUN
<note>original Latin: "DE SOLIS DEFECTU." The term "defectus" implies a failing or a "dropping out" of the sun's light.</note>

However, this does not happen in every month; for the sun does not suffer an eclipse at all of the so-called <term>conjunctions: "synodois," the meeting of the Sun and Moon at New Moon</term> and monthly gatherings, nor does the moon at all full moons. This is because their orbital circles <margin>5</margin> are greatly <term>inclined: "leloxōsthai," tilted or slanted</term> in relation to one another. For the circle of the sun, as we say, appears to be carried nearly along the middle of the <term>zodiac: "zōdiōn," the circle of animal constellations</term>, while its own circle is tilted slightly from this so as to deviate by half a <term>degree: "moiras," a unit of measurement in a circle</term> to either side. But the circle of the moon—as <margin>10</margin> <note>Hipparchus (c. 190–120 BCE) was a Greek astronomer who is often considered the founder of trigonometry and discovered the precession of the equinoxes.</note> Hipparchus finds—is inclined across a width of ten degrees, though most mathematicians believe it to be twelve degrees; as a result, the moon appears at times five or even six degrees more northerly or more southerly than the center line. Now, if we imagine the planes of both circles—that of the sun <margin>15</margin> and that of the moon—to be extended, their common intersection will be a straight line on which the centers of both are located. This straight line will be, in a way, a common diameter for both. The ends of this line, where the circles seem to cut <margin>20</margin> one another, are called <term>nodes: "syndesmoi," meaning bonds or links</term>; one is the <term>ascending node: "anabibazōn," literally "the one that brings up"</term> and the other is the <term>descending node: "katabibazōn," "the one that brings down"</term>. These nodes themselves shift forward into the subsequent signs of the zodiac. If, therefore, the conjunction of the sun and moon occurs at a node, with the bodies appearing close to each other, the moon will <margin>25</margin> <term>obstruct: "epiprosthēsei," to stand in front of</term> the sun from our point of view. Consequently, the sun will appear to us to suffer an eclipse, and indeed by as much of a portion as the moon stands in front of it. But if the monthly conjunction does not occur at the node, but rather...

XII p. 118 11 'twelve' B, corrected by editor; '6th' was '7th' in B* 12 'of the mid-point' B* 16 'is' was 'upon' in B* 18 'one another' B*</note>

-># ON THE ECLIPSE OF THE MOON.<-
<note>original Latin: "DE LUNAE DEFECTU." In ancient astronomy, "defectus" (Latin) or "ekleipsis" (Greek) refers to the "failing" or abandonment of light.</note>

...[if they are] at the same <term>degree: "moiran," a 1/360th division of a circle</term> of the zodiac’s length, but not at the same degree of its width <note>The "length" refers to celestial longitude along the ecliptic; "width" refers to celestial latitude, how far north or south an object is from that center line.</note>, so that one of the stars appears more northerly and the other more southerly, the sun will not be obstructed nor will it appear to undergo an eclipse.
<margin>5</margin> Regarding the moon, it would become clear in this way: for it has been stated many times that the moon undergoes an eclipse when it falls into the shadow of the earth; but why this does not happen in every month must be explained.

<image-desc>A geometrical diagram illustrating the casting of a cylindrical shadow. Two circles (spheres) of equal size are placed side-by-side. Horizontal parallel lines connect the top of the left circle (α) to the top of the right circle (γ) and the bottom of the left circle (β) to the bottom of the right circle (δ). These lines extend further to the right, labeled ε (top) and ζ (bottom), representing the boundaries of a cylindrical shadow.</image-desc>

Since, therefore, the rays and beams fall in a straight line from the illuminating bodies, and the shadows are continuous with them in a similar way, whenever the illuminating body and the body casting the shadow are equal, and both are spherical, the shadow becomes <term>cylindrical: "kylindrikē," a shadow that does not taper or widen because the light source and the object are the same size</term> and extends to infinity. For example, let the illuminating body be **αβ**, and the illuminated body be **γδ**, and let them be equal to each other and spherical. It is clear, then, that as the ray **αγ** and the ray **βδ** fall in a straight line—since the <term>diameters: "diametroi," the lines passing through the center of the spheres</term> **αβ** and **γδ** are equal to each other and at right angles to the <term>tangents: "ephaptomenais," lines that touch the circles at a single point</term> **αγε** and **βδζ**—they will be parallel, and the [shadow boundaries...]

-># ON THE MOON<-
<note>original Latin: "DE LUNAE." This is likely a fragment of "DE LUNAE DEFECTU" (On the Eclipse of the Moon).</note>

$\overline{\gamma \varepsilon}$ and $\overline{\delta \zeta}$, being extended to infinity, will not meet; and since this occurs on all sides, it is clear that the shadow of the $\overline{\gamma \delta}$ sphere will be <term>cylindrical: "kylindrikē," a shadow that does not taper or widen</term> and will extend to infinity.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating a cylindrical or expanding shadow. There are two circles; the left circle (representing the light source) is smaller than the right circle (the object). The left circle has a vertical diameter with points labeled η at the top and θ at the bottom. The right circle has a vertical diameter with points labeled κ at the top and λ at the bottom. Two tangent lines originate from the points η and θ, pass through points κ and λ respectively, and continue to the right, diverging away from each other towards points labeled μ and ν.</image-desc>

<margin>5</margin> If, however, the illuminating body is smaller, such as $\overline{\eta \theta}$, and the body being illuminated is larger, such as $\overline{\kappa \lambda}$, the <insert>shadow</insert> <note>The Greek word for "shadow" (skia) was missing in the original manuscript but is reconstructed from Latin translations by Chalcidius and Martianus Capella.</note> $\kappa \mu \lambda \nu$ will be <term>basket-shaped: "kalathoeidēs," a flared shape resembling a calathus (a Greek wool-basket)</term> in its form, and will similarly extend to infinity. Since the diameter $\overline{\kappa \lambda}$ is greater than $\overline{\eta \theta}$, the rays $\overline{\kappa \mu}$ and $\overline{\lambda \nu}$, as they extend to infinity, will always be at an
<margin>10</margin> increasing distance from each other, and this will be the case from all directions similarly.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating a converging shadow. There are two circles; the left circle is larger than the right circle. The left circle has a vertical diameter with points labeled ξ at the top and ο at the bottom. Right circle has a vertical diameter with points labeled π at the top and ρ at the bottom. Two tangent lines originate from the points ξ and ο, pass through π and ρ respectively, and converge to a single point labeled σ on the right side of the diagram.</image-desc>

<note>Though the text for this third diagram is not on this page, the geometry illustrates the scenario where the light source is larger than the object—as the Sun is to the Earth—creating a cone-shaped shadow that eventually terminates at a point (σ).</note>

### [ON THE] ECLIPSE
<note>original Latin: "DEFECTU." This header completes the title from the previous page, "DE LUNAE DEFECTU" (On the Eclipse of the Moon).</note>

If, on the other hand, the illuminating body is larger, such as $\overline{\xi o}$, and the body being illuminated is <insert>smaller</insert>, such as $\overline{\pi \rho}$, and both are spherical, it is clear that the shadow of $\overline{\pi \rho}$—that is, the area $\pi \rho \sigma$—will be <term>cone-shaped: "kōnoeidēs," tapering to a point</term> and finite in length. This occurs because the rays $\overline{\xi \pi}$ and
<margin>5</margin> $\overline{o \rho}$ are extended in a straight line and meet each other at point $\sigma$, since the diameter $\overline{\pi \rho}$ is smaller than $\overline{\xi o}$, and this convergence happens on all sides. 

Since, therefore, through his *Treatise on the Distances and Sizes of the Sun and Moon*, <term>Hipparchus: a famous Greek astronomer (2nd century BCE) often called the "father of astronomy"</term> shows that 
<margin>10</margin> the Sun is approximately 1,880 times the size of the Earth, and the Earth is roughly 27 times the size of the Moon, and that the Sun is much higher <note>In ancient astronomy, "higher" refers to being further away from the Earth.</note> than the Moon, it is clear that the shadow of the Earth will be cone-shaped and will fall along the
<margin>15</margin> common diameter of both the Sun and the Earth. Furthermore, the size of the Moon is, for the most part, smaller than the thickness of the shadow cast by the Earth. Whenever the Sun reaches one <term>node: "syndesmos," the two points where the Moon's path crosses the Sun's path</term> and the Moon reaches the other, and the Sun, the Earth,
<margin>20</margin> the shadow, and the Moon all stand upon a single straight line, then the Moon necessarily falls into the shadow of the Earth. Because the Moon is smaller than the shadow and possesses no light of its own, it 
becomes invisible and is said to suffer an **eclipse**. But when they are exactly diametrically opposed, so that they stand—as we said—upon the same straight line, and the center of the Sun...

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof that the Earth's shadow is a finite cone and explains how lunar eclipses occur when the Moon passes through this shadow at its nodes.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, lunar eclipse, Hipparchus, geometry, shadow cones, nodes, Earth, Sun, Moon</keywords>

...both that of the Earth and that of the Moon, the Moon passing through the middle of the shadow undergoes a total eclipse. However, when it is near [the center] but not perfectly aligned, sometimes it is not a total eclipse. For the most part, if the bodies of both 
<margin>5</margin> the Sun and the Moon are not positioned at the <term>nodes: "syndesmoi," the two points where the Moon's path crosses the Sun's path</term> during the full moon, the Earth's shadow will still lie on a straight line with the Sun, but the Moon—being either further north or south of the shadow and not falling into it at all—will not be eclipsed.

<page-num>199</page-num>
# ON THE PRINCIPLES OF ASTRONOMY
<note>original Latin: "DE ASTROLOGIAE PRINCIPIIS"</note>

...[Anax]imenes <note>Anaximenes of Miletus, a 6th-century BCE philosopher</note> recognized that the Moon receives its light from the Sun and explained the manner in which it is eclipsed. Others, building upon these discoveries, found further truths: that the <term>fixed stars: "aplanēis," the stars that do not change position relative to one another</term> move around the axis that remains fixed through the poles, while the 
<margin>5</margin> <term>planets: "planōmenoi," literally "the wanderers"</term> move around the axis of the <term>zodiac: the circle of animals/constellations through which the Sun and planets appear to travel</term>, which is at right angles to the zodiac’s own plane. The axis of the fixed stars and the axis of the planets are separated from one another by the side of a fifteen-sided polygon, which is an angle of 24 degrees. <note>The Greeks calculated the tilt of the Earth's axis (obliquity of the ecliptic) as 1/15th of a 360-degree circle, resulting in 24°.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the following sections, he says: just as in the fields of 
<margin>10</margin> geometry and music it is impossible to grasp the reasoning of the higher principles without first establishing the <term>hypotheses: "hypotheseis," the fundamental assumptions or starting points</term>, in the same way, one must first agree upon the hypotheses of astronomy. These are the foundations upon which the discourse regarding the motion of the planets proceeds. Above all, he says, for almost all 
<margin>15</margin> who handle the study of mathematics, the adoption of principles is taken as a matter of mutual agreement. First, that the constitution of the <term>cosmos: "kosmos," the universe as an ordered whole</term> is established in an orderly way, governed by a single principle, and that these existing and appearing things are stable. Therefore, one should not say that the cosmos 
<margin>20</margin> extends to infinity from our point of view, but rather that it exists within a defined boundary. Second, that the risings and settings of the divine bodies <note>referring to the stars and planets</note> do not occur by a "quenching" and "re-lighting" <note>This refutes an older belief, held by philosophers like Xenophanes, that the sun and stars were fiery clouds that were extinguished at night and relit in the morning</note>; for if they were not eternal...

<summary>This page discusses the historical discovery of the Earth's axial tilt (calculated as 24 degrees) and establishes the scientific "hypotheses" of astronomy, specifically arguing that the universe is finite and that celestial bodies are eternal rather than daily fires.</summary>
<keywords>Anaximenes, fixed stars, planets, zodiac axis, obliquity of the ecliptic, mathematical hypotheses, cosmic order, celestial eternity</keywords>

<page-num>200</page-num>

# CONCERNING THE STARS
<note>original Latin: "DE STELLARUM"</note>

...their permanence, the order in the universe would not be preserved. Third, that the <term>planets: "planōmenoi," literally "the wanderers"</term> are neither more nor less than seven; and this is clear from long-term observation. Fourth, since it is not reasonable for all existing things to be in motion, nor for all to remain at rest, but rather some move while others stay still, it must be agreed upon which things in the universe ought to remain still and which ought to move. 

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He says that one must believe that the Earth remains still—the <gloss>hearth of the house of the gods</gloss> according to <note>Plato, specifically in the "Phaedrus," page 247A</note> Plato—while the planets move along with the surrounding heaven that encompasses everything. He dismisses those who would make the moving things stationary and move the things that are stationary by nature and position as going against the <term>mathematical hypotheses: the foundational geometric principles used to model the heavens</term>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In these matters, he says that the planets move according to longitude, depth, and latitude in an ordered, uniform, and circular manner. <unclear>.....</unclear> if we follow this, we would not fail to reach the truth concerning them. Therefore, he rejects the "risings" and "co-risings" of the motion in longitude, as well as the <gloss>loose and lazy causes</gloss> of the so-called <term>falling behind: "hypoleipseōs," the phenomenon of retrograde motion where planets appear to lag behind or move backward</term> handed down from the elders. 

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He says it is correct to believe that, by avoiding everything irrational and disordered in such motion, the planets move gently in a direction opposite to the <term>fixed stars: "aplanē," the outer sphere of stars that move as a single unit</term>, as the inner motion is carried around by the outer. He does not think it right to consider <term>spiral lines: "helikoeideis grammas," curves that wrap around a sphere</term> as the primary causes of the planets' paths, nor those similar to the <term>horse-fetter: "hippikē," a figure-eight shaped curve (also called a hippopede) used by early astronomers like Eudoxus to explain planetary loops</term>. He argues that these occur only as a side-effect; but the first and primary...

<page-num>201</page-num>

<header>CONCERNING THE MOTIONS OF THE PLANETS.</header>
<note>original Latin: "ERRANTIUM MOTIBUS"</note>

...the cause of both the wandering and the <term>spiral: "helikos," a complex path resulting from combined motions</term> is the motion along the oblique <term>zodiac circle: "zōdiakou kyklou," the path the sun and planets appear to follow through the sky</term>. For the motion along the spiral is incidental and secondary, produced from the double nature of the motion around them. One 
<margin>5</margin> must say that the primary motion is the one leading along the oblique circle; for the spiral follows and is not the first.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Again, he rejects the idea that <term>eccentricities: "ekkentrotytas," orbits where the center is offset from the Earth</term> are the cause of motion in depth. Instead, he believes that there is one single center for both the planets and the universe for 
<margin>10</margin> all things carried through the heavens. This motion occurs "incidentally" by the planets, not as a primary action—as we demonstrated above <note>referring to pages 167 and following</note>—involving <term>epicycles: "epikyklous," small circles that move along the circumference of larger circles</term> and eccentric circles being traced through the depth of the <term>concentric spheres: "enkentrous," spheres sharing a common center with the Earth</term>. For 
<margin>15</margin> each sphere has two surfaces, the inner being concave and the outer being convex; between these, the stars move upon epicycles and concentric paths, and by this motion, they also trace eccentric paths incidentally. He says that according to our <term>perceptions: "phantasias," the way things appear to our eyes</term> the motions of the planets are irregular, but according to the underlying 
<margin>20</margin> truth, they are uniform. For all of them, the motion is <gloss>voluntary and unforced</gloss>, occurring through the fewest possible revolutions and within ordered spheres. He blames those philosophers who, by joining the stars to the spheres as if they were <gloss>inanimate things</gloss>, introduce a "multitude of spheres" for these circles—as 
<margin>25</margin> <note>Aristotle, specifically in "Metaphysics," book 12, chapter 8, page 1073b</note> Aristotle claims, as well as <note>Menaechmus, a Greek mathematician who studied conic sections</note> Menaechmus among the mathematicians.

***

<note>Note on line 5: "to say" — found as "this" in manuscript B or "of this" by Martinus. The word "leading" appears as "leading from" in manuscript B.</note>
<note>Note on line 8: "one thing the" — found as "in the [sphere] of the" in manuscript B.</note>
<note>Note on line 13: Manuscript B reads "eccentrics" instead of "concentric" and "width" instead of "depth."</note>
<note>Note on line 19: "and the truth [are] uniform" — manuscript B reads "uniformly and the truth."</note>

<summary>This page explains that planetary "spirals" are secondary effects of their motion along the zodiac and argues that planets move voluntarily within the thickness of concentric spheres, rather than being mechanically fixed to them. The author defends the idea of uniform motion against the appearance of irregularity and criticizes Aristotle’s theory of multiple celestial spheres.</summary>

# CONCERNING THE AXES OF THE ZODIAC
<note>original Latin: "DE AXIBUS ZODIACI"</note>

...Callippus, who introduced the spheres that "carry" and those that "unwind" <note>original: "pherousas" and "anelittousas"; these were a system of nested spheres used by astronomers Eudoxus and Callippus to explain why planets appear to slow down or move backward</note>. In addition to these agreed-upon points, he believes that the heaven, together with the stars, moves around the stationary Earth in uniform and circular motions that are as minimal <margin>5</margin> and harmonious as possible, following paths that share a common center and are not forced; he demonstrates that these hypotheses are also preserved in the works of <term>Plato: the philosopher whose "Timaeus" provides the foundational vision of an ordered, mathematical cosmos</term>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The fixed stars move around the fixed axis that passes through the poles, while the planets move around the <margin>10</margin> axis of the zodiac, which is at an angle to it. The axis of the fixed stars and the axis of the planets are distant from one another by the length of the side of a <term>fifteen-sided polygon: "pentekaidekagōnou"; the side of such a shape inscribed in a circle represents an angle of 24 degrees, which was the ancient measurement for the tilt of the Earth's axis</term>. The zodiac, being a "great circle," bisects the world-system; and while the circumference of the whole is divided into 360 <margin>15</margin> degrees, the zodiac marks off 180 degrees on either side. The axis of the zodiac, being at a right angle, divides these 180 degrees in half.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The zodiac is tilted from the winter parallel toward the summer parallel. According to <term>Hipparchus: often called the "father of astronomy," active in the 2nd century BCE</term>, there are 30 degrees <margin>20</margin> from the summer parallel to the <term>antarctic circle: most likely a scribal error for the "arctic circle," which is the boundary of the stars that never set</term>, and from the antarctic circle to the pole of the sphere of fixed stars, there are thirty-six degrees. Combined, from the summer parallel to the pole of the sphere of fixed stars, there are 66 degrees. To complete the 6 degrees <margin>24</margin> remaining to reach the pole of the axis of the planets, one must add degrees...

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical and geometric demonstration of the universe's structure.
