<page-num>203</page-num>

<header>AND OF THE FIXED STARS.</header>

...24 degrees, which is where the pole of the axis of the **planets** <note>original Greek: "planōmenōn," literally "the wanderers"</note> would be, since it stands at right angles to the zodiac. There remain 12 degrees from the pole of the axis of the planets to the summer parts of the antarctic circle <note>original: "antarktikou"; in this context, it refers to a specific celestial parallel</note>; for the total was 36, from which we subtract 24, leaving 12. To these we must add the 30 degrees from the antarctic to the summer circle again, and the 24 degrees from the summer circle to the **equator** <term>isēmerinos: the "equal-day" line where day and night are of equal length</term>, and the 24 degrees from the equator to the winter circle, where the zodiac touches once more. This results in 24 degrees out of the 360 degrees of the entire circle, which is a fifteenth part; for fifteen times 24 equals 360. It is for this reason that we say the side of a **fifteen-sided polygon** <term>pentekaidekagōnou: a geometric shape with fifteen equal sides inscribed within a circle</term> inscribed in a sphere represents the distance between the two axes—that of the fixed stars and that of the planets.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The wandering stars describe a **spiral** <note>original: "helika"; a winding, screw-like path</note> by coincidence, owing to the fact that they are moved by two motions that are contrary to one another. For while they are carried by their own proper motion from the summer to the winter and back again—circling slowly—they are simultaneously carried around very rapidly in the opposite direction every single day by the sphere of the fixed stars. Consequently, they do not travel in a straight line from one parallel circle to another, but are instead carried around the sphere of the fixed stars. Thus, in order to progress through the zodiac from point **a** to point **b**, because their movement is not merely in a straight line along the zodiac but also occurs in a circle around the fixed sphere, the path becomes a **spiral**...

<page-num>204</page-num>

<header>ON THE TWISTED MOTION OF THE WANDERING STARS.</header>

...they trace, in their passage from parallel to parallel, a shape similar to the **spiral** <note>original Greek: *heliki*, a winding curve like a vine tendril</note> of a vine; just as if one were to wind a leather strap around a **cylinder** <term>kylindrō: a solid tube or drum</term> from one end to the other, in the same way the **ephors** <note>high-ranking Spartan magistrates</note>
<margin>5</margin> used to wind straps around the **Laconian staves** <term>skytalais: a tool used for ancient cryptography; a message was written on a strap wound around a staff, readable only when wound around a staff of the same thickness</term> to write their letters. The wandering stars also trace another kind of spiral, not only as if around a cylinder from one boundary to the other, but also as if on a **flat plane**. For since they move through the ages from one parallel to the other
<margin>10</margin> and back again to the same one, and this occurs without interruption or end, if we were to imagine the parallels as **straight lines extending to infinity**, and the wandering stars traveling through them in the same ways—sometimes the winter path, sometimes
<margin>15</margin> the summer path—we would find them tracing a spiral to infinity. Because of the ceaseless and eternal nature of their journey around the sphere through the parallels, their path becomes similar to a journey along straight lines extended to infinity, as the accompanying
<margin>20</margin> diagrams demonstrate. Thus, they trace two spirals **by coincidence** <note>meaning as a secondary result of two combined motions: the daily rotation of the heavens and the planets' own movement</note>: one as if around a cylinder, and the other as if through a plane.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;These are the most essential and authoritative points from **astronomy** <note>original: *astrologias*; in the ancient world, astronomy and astrology were often treated as a single discipline</note> for the reading of the **Platonic works**. Since
<margin>25</margin> we have said that there is a **music and harmony** existing in instruments, another in numbers, and another in the **cosmos** <note>the ordered universe</note>, and...

<page-num>205</page-num>

### EPILOGUE. <note>original Latin: *Epilogus*</note>

**Concerning all the necessary matters within the cosmos, we promised to treat them in order,**
**after the tradition concerning astrology — for this,**
**Plato said, was the fifth among the mathematical sciences after**
**arithmetic, geometry, stereometry, and astronomy — ,**
<margin>5</margin> **which Thrasyllus also demonstrates in summary concerning these things; together with what we ourselves have previously elaborated, these things must be made clear.**

---

**1 promised:** <note>references to earlier sections of this work on pages 47 and 93</note> **2 astrology** <note>Manuscript B variant</note>
**3 Plato:** <note>referencing Plato's *Republic*, Book VII, page 530, where he discusses the education of the philosopher-king</note> **4 stereometry** <term>stereometrian: the study of three-dimensional solid figures</term> <note>Manuscript B variant spelling</note> **5 Thra-**
**syllus** <note>Thrasyllus of Mendes (died 36 AD), a grammarian and astrologer to the Emperor Tiberius, known for organizing Plato's works</note> <note>Manuscript B variant</note> **6 previously elaborated** <note>Manuscript B variant spelling</note>; **the end** <note>Manuscript B adds "the end" here</note>

<vocab>Πλάτων (Platon): Plato; Θράσυλλος (Thrasyllos): Thrasyllus; ἀστρολογία (astrologia): astrology or harmonics; γεωμετρία (geometria): geometry; στερεομετρία (stereometria): solid geometry; ἀριθμητική (arithmetike): arithmetic</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the astronomical section by categorizing "astrology" (often implying harmonics in this context) as the fifth and final mathematical science according to Plato's curriculum. It credits the scholar Thrasyllus for summarizing these essential cosmic principles.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Thrasyllus, mathematical sciences, stereometry, astronomy, astrology, cosmic order, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page serves as a technical appendix to the preceding epilogue. Following the classification of "astrology" (harmonics) as the fifth mathematical science in the Platonic tradition, this diagram illustrates the "Greater Perfect System," the foundational scale of Ancient Greek music theory which was believed to mirror the harmony of the cosmos.</meta>

### THE GREATER PERFECT SYSTEM <note>original Greek: *Systema Teleion*</note>

| Musical Intervals (Ratios) | Note Names |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Double Octave** <term>dis dia pason: literally "twice through all [notes]," spanning two octaves</term> <br/> **Quadruple Ratio** <note>original: *tetraplasion* (4:1 ratio)</note> | |
| ⎧ **Octave** <term>dia pason: the interval where the frequency doubles (2:1 ratio)</term> <br/> ⎪ **Double Ratio** <note>original: *diplasios*</note> | |
| ⎪ ⎧ **Fifth** <term>dia pente: an interval of five notes</term> <br/> ⎪ ⎪ **Sesquialter Ratio** <note>original: *hemiolios* (3:2 ratio)</note> | |
| ⎪ ⎪ ⎧ **Fourth** <term>dia tessaron: an interval of four notes</term> <br/> ⎪ ⎪ ⎪ **Sesquitertial Ratio** <note>original: *epitrito* (4:3 ratio)</note> | **The Added Note** <note>original: *proslambanomenos*</note> |
| ⎪ ⎪ ⎪ | **Lowest of the Low** <note>original: *hypate hypaton*</note> |
| ⎪ ⎪ ⎪ | **Next to the Lowest of the Low** <note>original: *parhypate hypaton*</note> |
| ⎪ ⎪ ⎩ | **Low Diatonic** <note>original: *hypate diatonos*; usually referred to as the "index finger" note of the lowest grouping</note> |
| ⎪ ⎩ | **Lowest of the Middle** <note>original: *hypate meson*</note> |
| ⎪ | **Next to the Lowest of the Middle** <note>original: *parhypate meson*</note> |
| ⎪ | **Middle Diatonic** <note>original: *meson diatonos*</note> |
| ⎩ | **Middle Note** <note>original: *mese*; the central pitch and functional sun of the Greek scale</note> |
| ⎧ | **Next to the Middle** <note>original: *paramese*</note> |
| ⎪ ⎧ | **Third of the Separated** <note>original: *trite diezeugmenon*</note> |
| ⎪ ⎪ ⎧ | **Separated Diatonic** <note>original: *diezeugmenon diatonos*</note> |
| ⎪ ⎪ ⎪ | **Highest of the Separated** <note>original: *nete diezeugmenon*</note> |
| ⎪ ⎪ ⎩ | **Third of the Highest** <note>original: *trite hyperbolaion*</note> |
| ⎪ ⎩ **Fifth** <note>original: *dia e'*</note> | **Highest Diatonic** <note>original: *diatonos hyperbolaion*</note> |
| ⎩ **Octave** <note>original: *dia pason*</note> | **Highest of the Highest** <note>original: *nete hyperbolaion*</note> |

---

This description exists, drawn with great negligence, in Manuscript A following the conclusion of the section concerning music (page 72). These notation signs have been added to the fifteen names: q ξ̅ R Φ C̅ P̅ u ι ζ ζ̅ ⊏ ε̅ ⊔ ϑ̅ ɥ u ᒣ K̅. <note>These are Alypian musical symbols, a complex system of notation used in antiquity to denote specific pitches.</note> Compare Boethius, *On the Fundamentals of Music*, pages 312 and following in Friedlein's edition; Westphal, page 324 and following.

<vocab>**Added Note** (proslambanomenos): the lowest pitch in the system; **Lowest** (hypate): literally "highest" in Greek, but referring to the longest string/lowest pitch; **Middle** (mese): the central fundamental note; **Highest** (nete): the shortest string/highest pitch; **Double Octave** (dis dia pason): the full range of the Greek scale; **Alypian notation**: the standard Greek musical script; **Manuscript A** (codex A): the primary source text for this diagram; **Boethius**: the 6th-century philosopher whose work preserved Greek music theory for the Middle Ages.</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a diagram of the Ancient Greek Greater Perfect System, a two-octave musical scale. It defines the names of the fifteen notes and their mathematical ratios, serving as a technical bridge between astronomical harmony and musical practice.</summary>
<keywords>Greater Perfect System, Ancient Greek music, harmonics, musical ratios, octave, fifth, fourth, Alypian notation, Boethius, Greek note names</keywords>

Page 48, line 15: regarding "of the highest strings" <note>original: ὑπατῶν (hypatōn); in ancient Greek music, the 'highest' strings were actually the lowest in pitch, named for their physical position on the lyre</note>: compare Karl von Jan in the journal *Philologus*, Volume 29, page 302, note 1.

Page 52, line 18: for "ratio" <note>original: λόγον (logon)</note> perhaps read "expression"? <note>original: λέξιν (lexin)</note>

Page 53, line 9: "half-cubit long" <note>original: ἡμιπήχειον</note> suggested by Hultsch.

Page 55, line 14: correct to "diesis" <note>original: δίεσιν; a very small interval in ancient Greek music, often roughly a quarter-tone</note>

Page 62, line 8: for "that" <note>original: ὅτι</note> read "therefore" <note>original: ἄρα</note> as suggested by Hultsch.

Page 71, line 1: for "certainly someone" <note>original: μέν τις</note> read "indeed by the" <note>original: μὲν τῇ</note> as suggested by Hultsch.

Page 84, in the footnote to line 15: correct the abbreviation for "inscription."

# ADDITIONS AND CORRECTIONS.

Page 100, line 14: "middle" <note>original: μέσα</note>: compare the note for page 46, line 15.

101, 18: correct to "always" <note>original: ἀεί</note>.

102, 6: "to make" <note>original: ποιεῖν</note> suggested by Hultsch <note>the editor, Friedrich Hultsch</note>.

103, 14: "begets" <note>original: γεννᾷ</note> seems it should be deleted.

114, 14 and 21: "proportion" <note>original: ἀναλογία</note> and "by proportion" <note>original: ἀναλογίᾳ</note> were carelessly placed instead of "mean" <note>original: μεσότης</note> and "by the mean" <note>original: μεσότητι</note>.

116, 12: "shall we find?" <note>original: εὑρήσομεν?</note>

124, 1: "will flow together [then?]" <note>original: συρρυήσεται 〈ἆρα〉</note> suggested by Hultsch.

124, 5: "clearly [therefore?]" <note>original: δῆλον 〈οὖν〉?</note>

127, 16: for "of the [things]" <note>original: τῶν</note> read "the" <note>original: τὸ</note>?

128, 12: for "in relation to the whole spheres" <note>original: πρὸς τὰς ὅλας σφαίρας</note> read "of every universal sphere" <note>original: πάσης καθόλου σφαίρας</note> as suggested by Hultsch.

131, 4: for "and someone" <note>original: δέ τις</note> read "and still someone" <note>original: δ’ ἔτι τις</note>?

131, 6: "[into] equals" <note>original: 〈εἰς〉 ἴσα</note> suggested by Hultsch.

138, 9: for "or" <note>original: ἤ</note> read "and" <note>original: καὶ</note> as suggested by Hultsch; compare page 152, line 4.

150, 2: for "these things" <note>original: ταῦτα</note> read "the" <note>original: τὰς; feminine plural</note> as suggested by Hultsch; or perhaps "themselves" <note>original: αὐτὰ</note>?

153, 11: for 98 <note>original: ϟη'</note> correct to 90 and 8 <note>original: ϟ' η''; a correction of the Greek numeral notation</note>.

157, 15: "let them be joined" <note>original: ἐπεζεύχθωσαν</note> suggested by Hultsch.

171, 3: "inverted" <note>original: ἀνάστροφον</note> suggested by Hultsch.

171, 19: "[and] by the [optic?]" <note>original: 〈καὶ〉 τῇ οπ?</note>

178, in the note to line 2: correct to "added" <note>original: additum</note>.

180, 6: "apart from the" <note>original: χωρὶς τοῦ</note> suggested by Hultsch.

180, 18: "mechanical sphere-making" <note>original: μηχανικαῖς σφαιροποιίαις</note> suggested by Hultsch.

184, 3: "[it might become] according to the M" <note>original: 〈γένηται〉 κατὰ τὸ μ</note> suggested by Hultsch.

184, 24: "and by the motion of the wandering stars <note>planets</note>, that is, the spheres, being according to nature" <note>original: καὶ κατὰ φύσιν οὔσῃ φορᾷ τῶν πλανωμένων ἤτοι σφαιρῶν</note> suggested by Hultsch.
